### Book Eight — Refuting the idea that souls move from one body to another, and answering its proponents

#### Chapter 1 — A proof from the highest realm

This question is a place where people easily lose their footing and go astray. The reason is that some statements by the earliest prophets and spiritually accomplished people seem, at first, to support the idea that souls move from one body to another. Some accounts of what Plato, Socrates, and other ancient philosophers said also seem to support it. We will show that these statements have a sound meaning that does not involve transmigration. By God's grace, we have also been given a strong proof that rules out every kind of it, whether a soul is supposed to move down into a lower kind of body or rise into a higher one.

As we have explained many times, the soul is essentially connected to its body. Soul and body form a natural unity, and each goes through deep change in its very existence. When a soul first comes into being, it is not yet developed in any of its later states, as is its body. At every stage of life, it takes on a new state: childhood, youth, old age, and so on. Soul and body move together from what it is still capable of becoming toward what it has fully become. For as long as the soul remains connected to a body, each stage of its development corresponds to a stage in that particular body's development. During its bodily life, every soul moves from what it is still capable of becoming toward what it has fully become. Its good or bad actions also give it a more definite state of existence, whether it moves toward happiness or misery.

Once a soul has become actual in one kind of being, it cannot return to a state of pure potentiality and become the soul of another kind of body. That would be like a fully formed animal turning back into a drop of semen or an embryo. This is a deep, essential movement; it cannot be reversed by force, by nature, by choice, or by chance. If a soul that had left one body were to enter another body, whether that body were an embryo or something else, then one side of the new union would be actual while the other remained only potential. The same thing would have to be actual and potential at once. That is impossible, because soul and body make one natural whole, and a natural whole cannot be made from one part that is actual and another that is merely potential.

Here is the argument in the form philosophers use. In every natural whole made of matter and form—whether it is a soul and a body, a natural power and its material basis, or some other pair—the two are joined so closely that one cannot cease to exist as matter or form while the other remains in that same role.

Matter is incomplete. It exists as a definite thing only through its form, which brings it from an undeveloped ability into a fully realized state. A form, considered as the form of a particular thing, also depends on its matter: that matter gives it its individual character and supports the particular states and actions that belong to it. So when a portion of matter comes into being as a definite thing, its corresponding form comes into being with it. When that thing is destroyed, its form as the form of that thing is destroyed with it. The form is the thing's completion and perfection; an incomplete thing cannot exist without the completion that makes it what it is.

The same is true of the body's relation to the soul that belongs to it. The soul, as the form of this particular living body, gives it its kind and makes it a living being. The body, as the matter of that soul, is the material basis of that living being. While the soul is still weak and its existence still depends on its body, it cannot exist apart from it. Their existence is bound together in the way we have described.

There is one qualification. A form can also exist in its own right, rather than as the form of a particular thing. In that case, it can exist without matter. And matter can sometimes receive a different form and exist in a different way. For example, a portion of a general kind becomes a particular kind through the form that joins it: if that form disappears, the portion of matter that was one with it also disappears as that particular thing. The same principle applies to the soul and its particular body.

The human soul, however, can come to have a way of existing beyond its dependent, bodily existence. Once it has reached that separate existence, the loss of its role as the form of a natural body does not destroy its whole being. It has become something that can exist apart from bodily matter. We have therefore established a general proof against every form of transmigration: a soul cannot leave one body and enter another separate body, whether the proposed move is downward, upward, or of some other kind.

Later we will explain the difference between transmigration and bodily resurrection, and also between transmigration and what happened to some of the people of Moses, whom God made apes and pigs.

By transmigration we mean that a soul leaves one natural, material body and enters another body that is separate from the first. This is impossible whether the soul is said to move downward or upward. A downward move into a human body is called *naskh*; into an animal body, *maskh*; into a plant, *faskh*; and into an inanimate body, *raskh*. An upward move would reverse that order. Some scholars even held that certain souls, such as those of people who were simple-minded or spiritually incomplete, entered a celestial body after leaving their earthly body at natural death.

But a soul's passage from earthly life into the life of the afterlife, where its settled character and habits give it an appropriate animal or other form—beautiful, bright, and happy, or ugly, dark, and miserable—is not contrary to the truth. It is proved by reason, affirmed by those who claim direct spiritual insight, and taught by the true religions. The Qur'an and the Prophet's traditions repeatedly say that souls take on forms shaped by their characters, habits, intentions, and beliefs.

The Qur'an says that the creatures of the earth and the birds that fly are communities like you; nothing has been left out of the Book. It speaks of people who were made apes and pigs and who served false gods, and of a group told, “Become despised apes.” It says that people's hearing, sight, and skin will testify against them for what they used to do, and that their tongues, hands, and feet will bear witness to their actions. The testimony of the limbs comes through the forms their repeated actions have given them. A dog's form, its tongue, and the sound it makes can testify to the evil in its character and habits; the same is true of the limbs of other animals that have been shaped by harmful actions. The Qur'an also says that some will be gathered on their faces, that Hell has roaring and groaning, and that its people will be told, “Stay there in disgrace, and do not speak to Me.” These are among the passages that describe a change of form.

The Prophet is also reported to have said that people will be gathered in different forms, each suited to their actions, intentions, and settled traits. He is reported to have said, “As you live, so you will die; and as you sleep, so you will be raised.” Another report says that some people will be raised in forms that make even apes and pigs look better by comparison. A tradition says that someone who deliberately opposes the prayer leader's movements will be raised with the head of a donkey. The idea is that a person who persists in foolishly opposing the leader becomes fixed in that foolishness and takes on a fitting form. Another report describes hypocrites as wearing the skins of sheep while having the hearts of wolves.

All this describes the inner self changing from human nature—either potentially or in actuality—into another nature, such as that of a grazing animal or a predator. It is a change in the soul's inner state, not a soul's transfer to a separate natural body.

The idea that spirits take forms suited to them in the afterlife is found in every religion. That may be why people say that every religious tradition has some trace of transmigration in it. I believe that when Plato and other great philosophers, such as Socrates, Pythagoras, Agathodaemon, and Empedocles, are said to have insisted on transmigration, they meant what religion teaches about the afterlife.

The same may be true of what is reported about Aristotle changing his mind and adopting his teacher Plato's view. He may have meant that human souls that are morally corrupt and incomplete—whether incomplete in knowledge and action, or only in knowledge—take on forms suited to them in the next life. Those who are guilty and miserable take on the forms of animals that match the bad habits and character that ruled them in this life. Those who are sound, middling in both knowledge and action, or incomplete in knowledge but complete in action take on beautiful forms suited to their character.[^V09-1] On the familiar account held by transmigrationists, a soul moves from one earthly body into another. That account has been proved false.

As for souls that have reached full understanding—those who know, whether their actions are also complete or remain middling—everyone agrees that they are free from bodies, whether earthly or afterlife bodies. They belong to the company of intellects free from bodies, dimensions, motion, and matter. This is so whether the transfer spoken of by the ancient philosophers is true or false.

#### Chapter 2 — Refuting the forms of transmigration and its proponents' arguments

Some transmigrationists say that human souls move from their human bodies into animal bodies whose character and actions resemble their own. A small group of philosophers known as transmigrationists held this view.[^V09-2] They claimed that no soul can become separate from its body after death, because it is material. It must therefore keep moving among animal bodies and other bodies. They found this view easier to accept.

But a soul must be either impressed in a body, as a form or quality is, or separate from bodies. Neither option supports their view. First, as we have shown, a human soul cannot be impressed in a body. And even if it could, this would conflict with their own account: an impressed form or quality cannot move from one separate body to another. We need to say “separate body” because natural forms can change and develop within one continuous material thing. Bodies, too, can change in size, quality, and even kind while remaining part of one continuous process. We have explained elsewhere that this kind of change is possible. The transmigrationists mean something different: a soul leaving one body and entering a different, separate one.

Second, a separate soul could not keep moving endlessly among bodies. God's care for all things means that each reaches its proper end and perfection. A soul's intellectual perfection is to become filled with the forms of all things it can know. Its practical perfection is to break free from bodily attachments, leave behind bad character and actions, and clear its inner mirror of what clouds it. If the soul were forced to move among bodies forever, never reaching the next life or drawing near to the highest realm, it would be kept from its proper perfection forever. God's care for creation does not allow this.[^V09-3]

There are two other forms of the doctrine. The first is attributed to the “Easterners.” They say that the first home of the human soul—the “Lordly Light”—is the human body, which they call the “Mother of Forms,” because it gives life to all animal and plant bodies. This is the view of Yudhasaf, who taught cycles and ages. He is said to have predicted that Noah's flood would take place in his land and to have warned his people. Some say he established the Sabian religion for the king Tahmurath.[^V09-4]

They say that those who are complete and happy join the intellectual world and the highest assembly after death, and receive a happiness beyond anything seen or heard, or even imagined. The souls of those who are not complete—whether they are middling or deeply deficient—and of the various kinds of the miserable move from this body to another and govern it. They disagree about what kind of body the soul can enter. Some allow only another human body; some allow an animal body; some also allow a plant body; and some allow even an inanimate body. The Brethren of Purity seem to have leaned toward this last view.

The second form is an upward transmigration. Its proponents claim that a plant is the first and proper recipient of a newly given soul. A human body, however, is balanced and refined enough to call for a nobler soul, one that has already passed through plant and animal life. They therefore say that a soul first enters a plant and then rises through plant kinds, from the lowest to the highest. It reaches a kind near the lowest animal, such as the bee,[^V09-5] then passes into a low animal, such as a worm, and climbs through the animal kinds until it reaches the human level.

We will give arguments against both views. Some rule out all transmigration; others address one or the other form. This will help anyone seeking the truth about the afterlife to see the issue clearly. Here is the general proof.

When a body's mixture becomes so disordered that it can no longer support the soul's activity, the soul leaves it. It must then do one of four things: pass into the world of intellect, enter a form in the afterlife, move into another natural body, or remain inactive. These are the only options.[^V09-6] The last two are impossible. Inactivity is impossible, as we have already established. Transmigration is impossible because, if the soul began to govern another body, that body would first have to be ready to receive a soul. We have already shown[^V09-7] that souls begin to exist, and that everything that begins to exist—especially something that exists in its own right—must ultimately depend on causes separate from matter.[^V09-8] A body cannot cause a soul, and neither a natural form nor another soul can cause it. Qualities such as bodily mixtures only prepare the body to receive the effect of an intellectual cause.

So, as soon as a body is properly balanced and ready to receive a soul, the giver of forms brings a new soul into it, with no delay—just as sunlight falls on a surface as soon as it is turned toward the sun. If we suppose that a soul has entered the body by transmigration, a new soul must also come from the source of forms; the nearest cause in that chain is the Active Intellect. One body would then have two souls. This is impossible. We have shown that every body has one soul, especially on our view that the soul is the body's very way of existing and what makes it an individual. One individual existence cannot belong to two selves. Each person is aware of one self and one soul.

Nor can the transmigrationist say that the transferred soul prevents a new soul from arriving. There is no reason why one should block the other. We have compared the body's readiness to a wall's readiness to receive sunlight. If a screen is removed and another polished surface reflects sunlight onto the wall, both the direct and reflected light fall on it. The reflected light does not prevent the direct light from reaching it. The same applies here. But two souls in one body is impossible, so transmigration is impossible, whether upward or downward.

The claim that a human body cannot be ready to receive a new soul directly, because plant souls must first pass into animals and then into human beings, does not work.[^V09-9] If those changes happen as one continuous development within the same matter, as a human form develops from its earliest beginning to its complete state, then they are not transmigration.[^V09-10] Our question is about a soul moving between separate bodies. A plant's matter, with its own balance and readiness, may be ready for a soul; but the matter of a human body, with a more balanced and refined readiness, is even more ready for a human soul.

They might object that claims about what is “more ready” are unreliable in a world of motion and chance. Some things have hidden causes that we cannot know. Even if everyone tried, we might not discover what mixture of qualities makes a magnet attract iron. So no one can say that a human body's mixture should attract a soul more readily than a plant's mixture simply because it is nobler.

We answer that a nobler, more complete bodily mixture calls for a nobler, more complete kind of being. Nobility in existence depends on how near something is to the highest source of being and how far it is from first matter. The more separate something is from bodily conditions and material attachments, the more complete its existence is and the closer it is to the source of existence, which has no deficiency or need. Attracting iron is not a higher kind of perfection that a human being possesses more fully than an inanimate body.

They might say that a nobler bodily mixture must call for a nobler soul, one that has passed through plant and animal life, so the soul entering a human body must be the soul that moved up from one of those earlier bodies.[^V09-11] We answer that this is only a claim, with no proof. The souls of the heavenly bodies are extremely noble, yet philosophers generally do not say that they came from animal or human souls.[^V09-12] Even if we grant that a soul has passed through those stages, it need not have moved from one body to another. It may have developed continuously in its own being.

A human soul begins with the body's earliest formation and develops through its stages, from the seed and embryo until it reaches the human level. It passes through plant-like and animal-like stages as part of a natural development that takes place in both the bodily matter and the soul. As we have said, the human seed changes through stages of creation: first an inanimate body, then a plant, then an animal, and then a human being. This happens through continuous development, not because one form is destroyed and replaced by another form whose existence is separate from it.

As we have explained, it is impossible for one natural cause to hand control of its matter over to another separate natural cause. The same is true when one natural cause is supposed to hand its action over to another, unrelated natural cause. A single cause cannot pass from one natural action to a separate action with no continuity between the two. That is what transmigration would require, so it is impossible.

Here is another general proof. The moment a soul leaves its first body cannot be the very same moment it enters the second. Time must pass between the two. The soul would therefore be left without a body to govern during that interval. But a soul cannot remain inactive. This argument follows from our view that being a soul is the soul's own way of existing, not an accidental connection it can simply pause and resume.

A special argument against downward transmigration is this: if it were true, the time when every human body perishes would have to match the time when some animal body comes into being. That result follows from their theory, but it is false.

Here is why their view requires this. They say that the first home of the “Lordly Light,” the immaterial soul, is the human body, which is born fully equipped with its powers and organs. They call the human body the “Mother of Forms” and the gateway through which life enters all material bodies. On their account, earthly animals receive life when human souls move into them. There would therefore be no animal other than the human being. Some people would be transformed into animals and remain there; others would be renewed in this world if they were incomplete, or raised to the world of light if they were complete. This is the teaching attributed to Yudhasaf and earlier philosophers of Babylon and Persia.[^V09-13]

They say that a person's dominant habits and dark inner traits determine what animal body the soul enters after the human body dies. Each bad habit corresponds to animal kinds suited to it. Each animal kind has its particular measure of the habit: the greed of an ant is not the same as the greed of a pig, and individuals of one kind also vary. The same is true of other bad traits, which differ in strength and in how they combine. On their view, the differences among animal kinds reflect differences among human beings in good and bad character. All those traits began in the first home, the human being, and moved from there into the animals through souls that passed from one body to another without any pause in between.

The conclusion is false for two reasons. First, there is no known necessary link that would make the time when a human body dies match the time when an animal body is born. To say that this timing is controlled by a hidden pattern in the stars—like one person's loss being timed with another's gain—is to rely on a remote possibility. Such a possibility gives no sound belief and cannot overturn a proof. If we allow explanations like this, no one could confidently tell when two events are necessarily linked and when they are not.

Second, their theory would require the number of animal bodies being born at every time to match the number of human bodies dying at that time. If there were more souls than animal bodies, several souls would crowd into one body. If they prevented one another from entering, some souls would remain without a body, which is impossible on their view. If there were more animal bodies than souls, one soul might have to govern more than one body, so that the same animal would be different from itself. If a new soul entered some bodies while an old soul entered others, there would be no reason to make this distinction. And if some bodies ready for new souls received none, they would remain without a soul. Every option is impossible.

The number of animal births can be far greater than the number of human deaths. In one day, more ants may be born than the number of people who have died over many years, let alone the number of greedy people who die on that one day. At other times, the number of human deaths may be greater, as in a widespread plague or flood.

A reply is that animal births need not match human deaths on the same day. A soul might enter an ant only after moving through many bodies over thousands of years. A greedy soul would not enter an ant after its first death. It would first enter the body of a large animal suited to its strongest trait, then descend through successively smaller animals until that trait had faded. It would then enter the largest body suited to its next strongest trait and gradually move through smaller animals again. Only after all these traits had faded would it leave the world of generation and decay and reach the first stage of Paradise, freed from dark bodily ties and bad physical traits.

Nor, they say, is it certain that births will always outnumber deaths. That would follow only if souls also moved upward from animal bodies into human beings. The number of animals is much larger than the number of plants, and the number of human beings is much smaller than the number of animals. Without a universal plague or flood, the numbers would not match.[^V09-14] The deaths in a universal plague or flood might be balanced by births among sea animals or insects. They even suggest that each human occupation might have a corresponding group of land or sea animals, with similar character and way of life. For example, some soldiers' character and way of life may resemble that of predators. Their souls could move first into the largest predators, then into smaller kinds over long periods, and finally reach Paradise after those traits had faded.

The main reason downward transmigration is impossible is that all natural forms, including souls, are directed toward their own ends. They move from what it is still capable of becoming toward what it has fully become. As long as the soul remains in its body, it grows in its very being and becomes more actual. This is true whether the person will be happy or miserable in the next life. As existence grows stronger, it becomes more independent and needs its underlying matter or support less. What was connected can become separate; what was joined can become independent.

It is impossible, then, for the human soul to begin as independent of matter in its own nature but dependent on a body in its actions, and then—when that body perishes—to become material in both its nature and its actions, as the transmigrationists say happens to the miserable. This contradicts the principle that things naturally seek their own ends and are drawn toward what completes them. A backward movement from stronger to weaker existence, from greater power to less, is impossible.[^V09-15]

Someone may object that becoming an inferior animal need not be a natural development; it could be forced.

We answer that this kind of force cannot be permanent or happen in most cases. Yet the proponents say that downward transmigration happens to most human souls, while only a few reach the higher realm.[^V09-16] They also say that a soul's essential movement toward fuller existence cannot be stopped by force, compulsion, or chance for as long as the soul exists. Only what destroys or ends the subject can stop that movement. Once the soul has ended its earthly existence, it does not begin another movement or development by falling back to an earlier state.[^V09-17]

Someone may then ask: if all souls move toward greater existence—even the sinful, ignorant, and lowly—how can there be misery in the afterlife?

Misery, too, is a kind of completion. The soul becomes independent in its existence, but in this life it may have grown used to shameful desires and anger, or become fixed in false beliefs. When it leaves this world and the veil is lifted from its sight, it suffers from those habits, from its longing for low and worldly pleasures, and from its false beliefs and deep ignorance.

Every human soul exists independently after its natural death from this body. Through its actions and deeds, and the character and settled traits that follow from them, it takes on a definite state of existence. This may be angelic and noble, or demonic, predatory, or animal-like. During its earthly life, if it moves out of the original potentiality of its nature and becomes actual through the traits it has acquired, it takes on the form of one of these kinds. This is not a natural or material form. When it leaves this world, it is a form without matter and an actuality without potentiality. Whether happy or miserable, it experiences the results of its character and actions: the good traits and deeds bring delight, while bad traits and deeds bring torment.

Someone may object: if every form without matter is a pure intellect, then it must be a perfect being in the sacred realm. How could such a form be miserable?

We answer that “matter” means the subject that undergoes change and motion in the world of generation and decay. Being free of such matter does not mean being free of all dimensions or shape, as intellectual forms are. The afterlife bodies that match a soul's character are not matter that carries its potentialities and traits. They are image-like forms and shapes that belong to the soul as a shadow belongs to the object that casts it.[^V09-18] They come from the soul's own active powers, not from a passive subject or material movement. What comes from the soul in this way is not matter for it, nor is it a body that governs and moves the soul as this earthly body does. It belongs to the soul as its shadow belongs to it.

The thing that casts a shadow is not completed by the shadow, changed by it, or dependent on it. In the same way, advanced souls do not attend to their afterlife images as objects of desire; they are absorbed in the world of meaning and in the Creator of meaning and form.[^V09-19] So the transmigrationists' claim that souls need to enter another earthly body is overturned. During its natural life, each soul becomes an independent being, with an existence other than its material, bodily existence. It becomes a new kind of being, actual in its inner reality.

Someone may object: human beings are one kind, and all human souls belong to that kind.

How, then, could they become different animal kinds? Whether this happened here or in the next life, would that not be an impossible change in their nature?

We answer that natural human beings are one real kind. Human souls are likewise one kind when considered in their bodily, dependent existence, because the soul is the source of the defining feature that completes the body's kind: the capacity to understand universal ideas. The human soul is indeed the form and completion of this natural kind. Yet the same soul is also able to take on different kinds of form through another, non-natural mode of existence. This is like first matter, which is one in itself but takes on many kinds as it moves from potentiality to actuality in the natural world.[^V09-20] There is no contradiction in a thing being one in one respect and many in another.[^V09-21]

A human being in the natural world is one kind of being. It is made of bodily matter with a balanced human mixture and a form of completion: the soul, which preserves that balance and enables the person to act and understand. Its natural definition comes from its matter and its soul, which is the body's first completion. In this earthly existence, all human souls belong to the same kind. They are all open to habits and settled traits, however, and these develop through different actions. Each set of traits suits another kind of inner form.

Once those traits become settled in individual souls, the souls become actual in those traits, take on their form, and enter a new kind of existence in the next life. They then become different kinds of being, with different inner realities. They may take forms suited to angelic, demonic, animal-like, or predatory character. These different forms belong to the next life, not to this earthly life; transmigration between earthly bodies remains impossible.

This account—that human souls begin as one kind but become many kinds in a second creation—is not commonly attributed to the philosophers. It is what God has inspired us to see and what the proof leads us to. The Qur'an supports it: “People were once one community, then they differed”; “You think them united, but their hearts are divided”; and “God is the protector of those who believe. He brings them out of darkness into light. Those who disbelieve have false gods as their protectors; they bring them out of light into darkness.”

The Qur'an also indicates that people of knowledge are inwardly unlike the rest of humankind, animals, and livestock: “Among people, animals, and livestock, their colors differ in the same way. Only those of His servants who have knowledge truly stand in awe of God.” The contrast suggests that people who are heedless and ignorant inwardly resemble animals, while those with knowledge are different in kind.[^V09-22]

As long as a soul has not yet developed and settled into a particular inner form, it can acquire any level or station for which it is suited.

Once it has become shaped by an inner form and that form has grown firm, however, it settles into that level. Its readiness to move from one state to another then comes to an end. It cannot return from this second creation to its first state. Wishing to become matter or dust again cannot make the impossible possible.

Here is a proof from the highest realm. The human soul has three kinds of perceptual life. The first is the natural, sensory form of existence, which is expressed through the five outer senses. It is called “this world” because it is nearer and comes before the other two.[^V09-23] It is also called the visible world because the senses can perceive it. Its good and evil are plain to everyone.

In this life, everything that exists is subject to change, and its form cannot be separated from its matter. The second[^V09-24] kind of existence is made up of images and forms that the outer senses cannot perceive. It is expressed through the inner senses and is called the hidden world or the next life, in relation to the first. “First” and “next” describe each other, like two things defined by their relation. As the Qur'an says, “You know of the first creation; why do you not take heed?” The next life has two regions: Paradise, where the happy dwell, and Hell, where the miserable dwell. The roots of happiness and misery there are the noble or bad character traits that have become settled in the soul.

The third kind of existence is intellectual. It is the home of those brought near to God, the realm of intellect and what the intellect understands. It is expressed through the human power of reason when that power becomes actual intellect. This realm is pure good and pure light.

The first life is a realm of possibility and readiness; it is the field where the seeds of spirits, intentions, and beliefs are planted. The other two are realms of completion, actuality, and harvest. The soul has a pure readiness for inner traits only at the beginning of its earthly life, before its potential has become actual in its beliefs, habits, and character. Once a trait has become actual, that pure potential cannot begin again in a second life in this world, through attachment to a different animal body. Such a return would require the soul to be stripped of all its settled inner qualities, which is impossible. It would also contradict the transmigrationists' own claim that bad traits cause souls to move to animal bodies.

A special argument against upward transmigration is this. If animals do not have separate souls but only souls impressed in matter, they cannot move from one body to another. An impressed form or quality cannot leave its subject and enter a separate one. If, on the other hand, an animal does have a separate soul, how could it rise to the human level? Its organs and powers are suited to bodily needs—to appetite, anger, and revenge. These are the powers that animals perfect. Appetite and anger are also the roots of criminal behavior and of becoming attached to bodily life. When either dominates a human being, the person falls toward an animal kind suited to that trait, whether this change is imagined in this life, as transmigrationists claim, or in the next life, as those with direct spiritual insight affirm. If appetite and anger drag a soul down, they cannot be the source of its rising from animal life to human life, where the soul's perfection comes from restraining them.

Someone may say that a horse, for example, keeps some stable feature throughout its life even though its body is always changing. Its body parts break down and are renewed, little by little, through inner heat and the outside air. We cannot say that its equine nature or soul is always changing. Animals also remember painful experiences: if a horse was struck with a stick once, it may run away when someone later drags a stick toward it. This seems to show that it holds a general idea of the stick and its danger. Animals of the same kind also differ in how close they are to human beings. Some, like the ape, imitate human actions; others, like the parrot, imitate human speech. Some seem to have practical intelligence.[^V09-25]

Others have a practical power close to theoretical reason. The remarkable traits and habits of some animals—the lion's pride, the camel's resentment, the bee's skill in building, and the camel's delight in hearing music—suggest that animals have souls separate from matter that should be able to rise to human life.[^V09-26] We see them, by their instincts, moving toward some kind of completion. If their goal is a higher state, it should be the human level and then the angelic. If their instinctive movement has no goal, it would be contrary to divine generosity to deny them the completion they deserve.

We answer that each kind of animal—and of plant—has a guiding spiritual source that directs it toward the actions and perfection proper to its kind. The Persians called this source the “lord of the image.” We have already explained how separate forms guide material kinds: every kind of natural body has an independent source that gives it its existence and care. Kinds differ in nobility because their luminous sources differ in how strong their light is and how near they are to the Light of Lights. On the account given by Aristotle’s followers, remarkable animal senses and movements come from the help of natural and celestial powers, or from inspiration from the heavens. The camel's love of sound, for example, may arise from a bodily power suited to its mixture.

Even if animals have souls that are separate from matter and directed toward perfection, that does not mean their perfection is intellectual, or that their individual souls must enter the world of intellect.[^V09-27] Many people do not reach an intellectual life in the next world, yet they still have imagined pleasures and animal joys that are the highest happiness available to them. They do not long for intellectual realities or share in the highest realm.[^V09-28] Not every being with a soul is directed toward intellectual perfection. Moving toward some form of completion does not mean becoming human. Nor is any being denied its proper perfection: the highest perfection may not suit or be possible for every individual or kind.

Even if animals are open to intellectual completion, they need not pass through the human level to reach it. There are many paths to God and to the world of the divine kingdom. As the Qur'an says, “Each has a direction toward which it turns,” and “There is no creature but He holds it by its forelock. My Lord is on a straight path.”[^V09-29] This is the human path that leads to the Lord of Muhammad, Hud, and the other prophets, the name that gathers all the divine names. Other paths lead to the lords of different kinds and the names that belong to them, such as the Avenger, the One who humbles, the Compeller, and the One who leads astray.

#### Chapter 3 — Addressing the remaining doubts and answering them

Know that transmigrationists have other arguments that we need to present and answer. The issue is easily confused and hard to follow. That is why people widely believe that the ancient philosophers, despite their great wisdom, taught transmigration. In our view, this is simply a false accusation based on confusing the resurrection of souls with their transfer into other bodies.

One argument is that souls need bodies because, at the beginning of their existence, they are potential and incomplete. The sinful and ignorant who have rejected God's gifts, however, may become even more incomplete and base than they were at the beginning. They should therefore need bodies more and be drawn to bodily matter more strongly. Some philosophers raised this objection but could not answer it. What God has shown us is this: at the beginning of its existence, every soul is potential in its intellectual, imaginative, and other inner qualities, even though it already gives a material body its living form and completes it. For that reason, it cannot yet exist without matter, as the transmigrationists also say.

But in its bodily life, the soul acquires good or bad character, and true or false beliefs. It becomes actual in one or another of its inner states. It becomes happy in the afterlife if it gains good character and true beliefs, joining the company of angels and the good. It becomes miserable if it gains bad traits and false beliefs, resembling devils and evildoers. In short, a soul that was originally receptive in its inner qualities and dependent on matter becomes actual through the settled traits it acquires. It takes on an inner form and another kind of existence in the next life. In this earthly life, it is already complete as the form and perfection of a natural body. A single thing cannot be both form and matter, or actuality and potentiality, or completion and incompletion in the same respect and in the same life.

The soul needs a body only while it remains merely receptive and potential in its inner qualities. Once one of those qualities has become actual and has shaped the soul's own inner form, the soul loses the pure potential it once had. It separates from the body and exists as a form without matter and without readiness to become something else. Souls whose true knowledge and good character have become dominant enter the sacred realm, the home of the angels. Souls dominated by ignorance, misguidance, and false beliefs join the devils; those dominated by appetite and anger join the animals and predators. This is the meaning of the verse about people being made apes and pigs and serving false gods.

A soul's reaching a settled state does not mean that it becomes happy. Rebellious and disbelieving souls may become worse and more miserable during their earthly life; nevertheless, they lose their original potentiality and reach a definite state, even if that state is complete misery.[^V09-30]

Another argument is that when sinful or ignorant people sleep or become ill, their senses may grow quiet and they may see hidden things. This is supposedly because their souls have joined the sacred realm. If they can join its sources while they are still committing sins and indulging their desires,[^V09-31] then they should be even more likely to join them when the body dies and its sensory distractions end completely. Where, then, would the promised misery, punishment, and torment be? The argument concludes that sinners must remain tied to bodies and that their souls must enter animals whose forms and habits match their own, so they can be punished for their actions.

We deny that the souls of miserable people join the highest sources when bodily distractions lessen. At most, the souls of the ill, sleepers, and those like them reach some of the intermediate realms between this world and the sacred realm. Nor does it follow that a soul that reaches such a realm in this life, when its senses are quiet, must also reach it in the afterlife. The dead may be so absorbed in terrors and calamities that they cannot turn their attention to anything else. They are bound in chains, kept from the world of light, as the Qur'an says: “We have placed a barrier before them and behind them, and covered them, so they cannot see”; “No indeed, what they used to do has covered their hearts”; and “No indeed, they will be kept from their Lord”; they will also be separated from what they desire.

They also cite the ancient philosophers, such as Plato and those before him, the words of prophets who cannot be mistaken or lie, and verses from the divine Book. They point to the verse, “Whenever their skins are burned through, We replace them with other skins so that they may taste the punishment”; to “We created the human being in the finest form, then brought him down to the lowest of the low”; to “Our Lord, You caused us to die twice and gave us life twice, and we have admitted our sins. Is there any way out?”;[^V09-32] to “Our Lord, take us out; if we return, then we will be wrongdoers”; and to “Stay there in disgrace, and do not speak to Me.”

We have already given the ancient philosophers' claims about souls entering animal bodies a different meaning from the one the transmigrationists give them. The Qur'anic symbols and the Prophet's words also have sound meanings that do not support this doctrine. Most of what divine law promises happens in another world, not this one. The next life is not limited to a world of intellect without size, shape, or form. It includes a luminous realm of intellect and a sensory realm divided into a home of delight and a home of torment. The luminous realm is for those brought near to God; Paradise is for the happy and righteous; Hell is for the miserable, sent to the lowest realm. The confusion arises from treating resurrection as if it meant transmigration.

The confusion also comes from overlooking the intermediate world between nature and intellect. There, people are raised in forms that match their character. The philosophers who discussed this explained that every bad trait or settled inner disposition has animal forms suited to it. Arrogance and rashness suit the lion; cunning and deceit suit the fox; imitation and mockery suit the ape; vanity suits the peacock; and greed suits the pig. Every degree of a trait, strong or weak, has its own fitting form. One person may have many bad traits, each in a different degree. The combinations are too many to count, so the soul's fitting form varies according to the strength and mixture of its traits.

When a trait disappears—or a strong degree of it weakens—the soul takes on a form suited to the next most deeply rooted trait. In that way, its afterlife forms may change over long periods until all its bad traits have disappeared, if they can disappear at all. These changes take place in the next life, not in this world. The verse about replacing skins when they are burned through refers to changes in afterlife bodies in this way, not to a soul moving from one material body to another in a world where bodies are still developing.

Someone might object that our argument against transmigration also rules out a soul's connection to an afterlife body. If an afterlife body were ready to receive a soul, would not the giver of forms create a new soul for it? Then the transferred soul would make two souls in one body, which we have said is impossible.

We answer that afterlife bodies do not exist as potentials in matter and do not come into being through the gradual readiness, motion, and development of matter, or through remote causes. They are attached to their souls as shadows are attached to the things that cast them. The First Reality brings them into being through the soul's active powers, without any contribution from matter's readiness to receive a form. Every separate soul gives rise to an image shaped by its character, habits, and inner traits. This does not depend on a body's preparation or gradual movement, as it does in this world.

An afterlife body does not exist before its soul. They come into being together, without one being made after the other. They are like a thing and its shadow: neither comes first, and neither has to prepare the other for its existence. The same is true of an afterlife body and the soul connected to it.

Someone may say that the Qur'an teaches that every person's afterlife body is the very same body they had in this life. We agree, but the body is the same in form, not in matter. A thing is complete through its form, not its matter.[^V09-33] A person's body remains the same individual throughout life, from childhood to old age, but not because its matter stays the same. Its matter is always changing. What preserves the person's identity is the soul-form that maintains its unity and particular character. The same is true of every limb, despite changes in size, shape, and quality.

What matters in the resurrection of the body is that it remains the same in its form and identity, not in the same particular matter, which is always changing. The soul and an indeterminate basis of matter preserve the body's identity from childhood to old age, despite changes to its qualities, limbs, and organs. We do not say that an old person punished for a crime committed in youth is a different person from the one who committed it. In the same way, the soul and an indeterminate basis preserve the identity of the earthly and afterlife bodies. The soul is rewarded or punished for actions it carried out through its earthly limbs, even though its body takes another form in the next life.[^V09-34]

Reports also support the claim that afterlife bodies change form. The people of Paradise are described as young and beardless, appearing around thirty years old.[^V09-35] The first group to enter Paradise will shine like the full moon, and those who follow them like the brightest star in the sky. The people of Hell are described as having a molar as large as Mount Uhud, a thigh as large as Mount al-Bayda, and a place in Hell three days' journey across.[^V09-36] One report says that a disbeliever's skin will be forty-two cubits thick, and his place in Hell will stretch between Mecca and Medina. Another says that a disbeliever's tongue will trail for a mile or two and that people will trample over it. There are many such descriptions of the two groups in the next life.

Someone might argue that the world with sickness, injury, punishment, evil, and pain is this material world. It should therefore be the Hell of the miserable and the wicked, while the animals into which their souls enter should be its pits. The wicked would repeatedly be sent back into those bodies because their ignorance and bad character keep growing. The happy, by contrast, would not taste death again after the first death; their souls would never enter the suffering bodies that are Hell's gates. Since their souls do not enter material bodies that come into being and decay, they taste only the first death, when they leave their human bodies.

We answer that the presence of great punishment in one realm does not mean that it is the best of all realms. The realm where the miserable suffer and the happy receive great reward is a realm of perception, subtler and clearer than this dense material life. It is the next life, the true home of life.[^V09-37] The things that delight or hurt us are not, in truth, outside objects; they are the perceptual forms present to the soul.[^V09-38] What gives pleasure or pain is not an external sensation received by the outer senses, but the inner senses of the next life, through which we see, hear, taste, smell, and touch things that have no existence in this material world.

Nature's intoxication and the soul's numbness in this life[^V09-39], caused by its preoccupation with the body, keep it from clearly perceiving the injuries and pains that follow from its actions and bad character. Its bodily senses distract it; they make it forget. When death lifts the veil, the person sees what their actions have brought about. Someone with bad character, evil actions, and false beliefs then faces terrible pain and disaster. As the Qur'an says, “We have removed your veil from you, and today your sight is sharp.”

Though the next life is subtler than this world's pleasures, it is the proper place for the misery and pain of the wicked. Its subtlety allows clearer perception, which is what pain depends on. The verse “Hell surrounds the disbelievers” points to this: the causes of their pain already surround them in this life, but they do not perceive them because their senses are dulled and their inner sight is blind. Consider the terrible distress that people can experience in dreams, or through thoughts without any outside cause. It can be as painful as, or worse than, anything caused by the outer senses. This shows that the pain of the next life is more lasting and reaches the soul more deeply than the pains of this world.

Someone may ask: if the punishments promised by the religious law—such as being trapped in false beliefs—befall a spiritual being separate from bodily matter, how can that being have these limited images without the power of imagination?[^V09-40] False beliefs require images and judgments that do not match reality, and these come from imagination. A separate soul receiving its existence from the higher sources should receive only what matches reality. The source of falsehood and ignorance is a material state. If a separate soul is the subject of pain and hardship, how can false belief torment it? At most, habits and traits would remain in the soul. Without new images, these would disappear. The soul would have no desire for something it could not picture, no power of desire, and no pain from longing for worldly pleasures. Once sensory distractions ended, it would be content in the divine mercy of the sacred realm.

If the human brain is the basis for imagination, it is destroyed at death, along with the powers of imagining, memory, and thought.

If some other body, perhaps a celestial body, is the basis for imagination, what relation begins at death between that body and the soul?[^V09-41] What would make this particular soul turn toward this particular body, rather than toward other bodies and the souls that have left them? Why would it enter that body's region and not another region of the same kind?

The body that serves as a basis for the soul's images would have to be moved by the soul, with bodily motions that follow its inner movements and changes, as the brain changes when the soul acts through it. If the body did not change in response to the soul's thoughts and feelings, the soul would have no way to act on it, and there would be no reason to connect that body to this soul rather than to any other. A celestial body cannot be moved by another mover; it is moved by its own soul in one continuous, uniform motion.

Some people say the basis of imagination is a smoky body below the celestial spheres and above the sphere of fire.[^V09-42] That view also fails. This body would not have a balanced mixture that could receive a soul and be governed by it.[^V09-43] If it were near the fire or a hot-moving sphere, the heat would quickly change it into fire. If it were far from them and in the air, it would either grow thin and rise with heat or grow dense and sink with cold. It would have no firm, dry boundary to keep it from cooling and mixing with its surroundings, as the brain has. It would also need a dry part to preserve images and a moist part to receive them.

Since the souls that leave human bodies are without end,[^V09-44] their theory would also require them all to gather on one of the world's bodies. Either the number of those souls would be limited, or that one body would contain an unlimited number; both are impossible.

The objection assumes that a soul without a body has no power to imagine. But this is not true of souls that are still incomplete or midway in their development. They may be free of material bodies but remain connected to image-like forms. If the objection means that they are free of sense bodies but remain connected to such images, there is no problem. We will explain this in the discussion of the afterlife. The soul can have particular, image-based perceptions and a power of imagination that arises from its own being, because, as we said earlier, the soul itself has hearing, sight, inner perception, and the power to move.

Its power to act and its power to perceive are one in its unified existence. Its ability to move belongs to its ability to perceive; its imagination belongs to its inner sight.[^V09-45] All the outer powers are shadows of aspects that exist together in the soul's own being. In this life, they have separated and multiplied, stretching into the matter of the body and taking the shape of its organs. In itself, the soul has sight, hearing, taste, smell, touch, and the active power to present what it perceives directly, as if it were present before it.

Our knowledge and imagination in this life are seeds of direct vision in the next. The difference between imagining something now and seeing it there is a difference in strength. The soul encounters whatever it longs for. If its thoughts and images in this life have been good, kind, and pure, they become gardens, satisfaction, peace, fragrant plants, and angels to keep it company in this life and bring it joy in the next. If they have been evil, ugly, and false, they become snakes, scorpions, fires, a wrathful keeper of Hell, and devils. They harm the soul in this life and distress it in the next.

The argument that an unlimited number of souls would have to gather on one body does not apply.[^V09-46] First, the discussion concerns only the souls of the miserable, not every soul.[^V09-47] Second, some souls do not connect to bodies, while others connect to image-like forms. Even if their number were unlimited, they would not crowd one material body. Image-like forms do not compete for one material place, so their number need not be limited. There is therefore no need for the number of those forms—or of material bodies—to be either finite or infinite in the way the objection claims.

I am surprised[^V09-48] that some people who are said to understand divine truths and look toward the lights of the heavenly realm—such as the author of *The Intimations*—could accept the idea that a celestial body is the basis for the images of groups of happy and miserable souls. Their souls, they say, have not reached the intellectual world and have not broken their ties to bodies; they remain potentially intellects and need a bodily relation. The author calls this a good explanation. Yet elsewhere he rejects the idea that the souls of the miserable could be attached to noble celestial bodies with luminous souls. He says that their potentiality requires bodily images, and proposes a solid, spherical body beneath the Moon and above the sphere of fire, serving as an in-between realm for their imagined fires, stinging scorpions, snakes, and bitter fruit.

He says that this account answers the remaining objections to transmigration. He adds that, based on his own spiritual exercises, he is certain that ignorant and sinful people would reach the greater spirit if they were freed from a bodily power that recalls their states, preserves their ignorance and traits, and shapes their images.

But a soul's imagining something through a celestial body, or another kind of body, requires a direct relation to it or a relation through something that is directly connected to the soul. The soul would have to be the form that completes that body, through its own being or through one of its own powers. In short, the soul would have to act on the body in some way. At the very least, the body would have to act as a surface or mirror does for some perceptions: it would need a positional relation to the material body that receives the soul's actions and is the basis for its powers.

A celestial body, however, is not acted on by anything separate from its own source. As we said earlier, the heavenly bodies are not affected by anything other than their own intellectual sources, and cannot receive strange, forced influences from outside.

Those who say that celestial bodies host the images of separated souls give those souls no bodies that could have a positional relation to the celestial bodies. The soul's relation to a celestial body would depend on its natural relation to its own body. Once death ends the natural relation to the body, the dependent positional relation must also end. When the root is gone, so is the branch. Their account is therefore false.

Moreover, if the celestial body were like a mirror for the soul's images, the images would be the ones already present in that body, formed by its own soul—not images created by the souls of the dead. It is impossible for a celestial body to receive dark and painful images unlike those its own noble soul forms. A pure receiver can take only true images from a noble source. The unhappy souls are tormented by their own bad traits, false images, and corrupt beliefs, not by images that match reality. So the account does not work.

The same problem applies to the proposed special, created body whose kind exists only in one individual, placed below the Moon. It would have to be a fifth kind of heavenly nature, even though it was said to be beneath the Moon. It would also be finite and smaller than the Moon. If the number of miserable souls is without limit, how could all of them use that one body for their endless images? At the very least, the body would need an unlimited capacity, which is plainly impossible.

The truth, as we have established, is that the images that delight the happy and torment the miserable in the next life exist in another realm, not in a material body or a bodily power. They exist in their own right, and the souls of the happy and miserable reveal them through their own activity. The images that appear in a mirror are not part of the mirror; the mirror reveals them by receiving them. It is possible for the soul to create something through one aspect of itself and to be affected by it through another. The soul has an intellectual, active side and a natural, receptive side. Just as its activity can produce health or illness in the body and the soul then feels pleasure or pain from them, so its actions produce the good or bad images that follow from its obedience and disobedience, and it experiences delight or pain from them.[^V09-49] These two aspects are present in the soul before it becomes pure intellect. At that stage, it acts and perceives through one side, as we have explained before.

Everything that reaches the soul in the next life comes from what it has brought forth. Souls that are not complete in knowledge and action, or only in knowledge—whether they are middling in happiness, incomplete in both knowledge and action, incomplete in knowledge, or miserable—may be free of earthly matter and natural bodies but remain attached to afterlife images. These images give delight or pain more intensely and last longer than changeable earthly bodies. The Qur'an says, “The punishment of the next life is harsher and lasts longer.” Even the fire and pain of this world are effects of the fire and punishment of the next life, or the same thing in a diminished, clouded, and weakened form. The Prophet is reported to have said that our fire is part of the fire of Hell, washed seventy times and then sent down to this world. The same is true of light: earthly delight is small compared with the delight of the next life.

Here is a reminder that further supports this view and explains why the afterlife images that bring the happy delight and the miserable torment do not exist in celestial or other bodies. They are image-forms belonging to the soul, present in another realm, and produced through actions and deeds in this life. Those deeds shape habits and character in the soul, and those in turn bring about the images.

Ibn Arabi, a leading authority on spiritual unveiling, says that the realm of images in which spirits dwell after leaving this world is not the same as the realm between separate spirits and bodies. Existence descends and rises in a circle. The realm before earthly life belongs to the descent and comes first; the realm after earthly life belongs to the ascent and comes last.[^V09-50]

Ibn Arabi also says that the forms spirits encounter in the final realm of images are the results of their past actions, unlike the forms of the first realm.[^V09-51]

The two realms share the fact that they are spiritual and luminous, not material.

He explains elsewhere that the first realm is called the “possible unseen,” because what is there can appear in the visible world. The second is called the “impossible unseen,” because what is there cannot return to this earthly world, except in the next life.[^V09-52] Few people have direct insight into that second realm; many can see or gain knowledge of the first and learn what events may occur in the earthly world. He also says[^V09-53] that a person may see, in sleep and after death, qualities that are usually temporary appear as independent forms that speak to them. They may seem like bodies carrying spirits, and the dreamer or the dead person has no doubt that they are real. Someone with direct insight can see while awake what the sleeper sees in a dream and what the dead person sees after death. In the next life, a person sees forms of actions, even though actions are normally passing qualities; death itself may appear as a white ram, even though it is the separation of things that were joined.

Some people perceive these images through the senses while awake; others perceive them through imagination. In dreams, they are certainly perceived through imagination. Ibn Arabi also says that after death, in the imaginal realm, people perceive things through the same images they had in this life. Each person there is held by what they have earned, confined within the forms of their actions until the resurrection in the next life.

#### Chapter 4 — How the soul relates to the body, and the difference between natural death and death from an outside cause

Know that the soul carries the body; the body does not carry the soul, as most people think. They hear that the soul is the product of the elements and the balance of bodily qualities. They suppose that the soul comes from the body, grows stronger when the body is nourished, and grows weaker when it is not. This is mistaken. The soul brings the body into being and gives it its form. It moves the body in different directions.[^V09-54] The soul is present with the body, its powers, and its limbs, guiding them as it wills and taking them wherever it wishes. It can move the body down or lift it up, despite the body's heaviness. When the soul wants to lift it, the body rises and its heaviness gives way to lightness. When it wants to lower the body, it moves down and its lightness gives way to heaviness.

But the soul cannot rise to the heavenly world, the home of spirits and the blessed, or to the highest dwelling place with this dense body. It rises with a body of light, of the same kind as that world, after leaving behind this dark bodily frame.[^V09-55] And the soul cannot rise to the home of those brought near to God, or to the realm of perfect beings and separate intellectual forms, until it has left behind the limits of bodies and dimensions, detached itself from images and shapes, and risen beyond both worlds.

The point is that the soul is too exalted, in its journey through these realms, to depend on the body for its existence. At some lower stages, the body is instead one of the things that depend on the soul. This principle disproves both transmigration and the view that natural death happens because the body runs out of strength, loses its inborn heat, wears out its tools, and falls apart, as many doctors and natural philosophers have thought.

The truth is that the soul gradually becomes independent in its existence. Little by little, it leaves this natural life for another kind of life. As we have explained, the very being of material things is in motion. The soul changes from one state to another and grows stronger in its existence. As it grows stronger, it gives less of its power to the body, because its attention is turning elsewhere.[^V09-56] The body and its powers then grow weaker and wither in a natural way. When the soul reaches the limit of its strength and independence, it completely ends its connection with the body, its care for it, and its giving of life to it. The body's death follows. This is natural death. It is different from death brought on by an outside accident.

The body's decline after the age when growth stops, followed by old age and death, comes from the soul's changes as it approaches the next life: a life in which it is on its own, apart from this natural body and this world, and exists independently. The bodily stages we see in a person—childhood, youth, gray hair, old age, and death—follow corresponding stages in the soul, but in reverse. As the soul gains strength and becomes more fully itself, the body grows weaker and less able. At last the soul stands on its own and the body dies. The soul's departure brings the body's ruin; the body's ruin does not cause the soul to depart.

A poet puts it this way: “The soul meant to leave. I said, ‘Don't go!’ It answered, ‘What can I do? The house is falling down.’” That saying applies to death caused by an outside accident, not to natural death. In general, people have not understood the soul's return journey toward God, a journey we have shown to be present in most things.[^V09-57] They have therefore offered mistaken explanations of death. What we have explained also shows why this death is called natural, rather than for the reasons they give.

What we have said does not deny that some souls are miserable, or that they will suffer in the next life in Hell, in fire, and under God's anger. They will face these things as payment for their deeds and the results of their actions, bad habits, and false beliefs. We will explain this more fully later.

Once we understand that natural death begins when the soul turns by its nature toward the next life and draws near to God, we can see that every kind of transmigration is impossible. After the death of its earthly body—whether its life ended naturally or through an outside cause—the soul cannot enter another natural body or move from one body to another. If it moved into another body, it would have to enter at the body's beginning, when it was a drop of fluid or an embryo in the womb, as supporters of transmigration admit. This would contradict what we have established: the stages in the soul's growth and the body's growth move together in reverse. Remember this, traveler on the path to God.

Use your insight to see that from the day you came into being as a drop in a safe place, you have been moving toward your Lord. You have passed from a lower state to a better one, from a less complete stage to a higher and nobler one. You will continue until you meet your Lord, see Him, and have your deeds weighed. Then you will remain with Him, either joyful and at peace forever, among the prophets, the truthful, the martyrs, and the good—excellent companions—or grieving, suffering, and punished by God's blazing fire, which reaches into the heart, among unbelievers, devils, evildoers, and hypocrites. What terrible companions they are.

We have already explained that all things in this world are moving toward God, though most do not know it because their veils are thick and their darkness has piled up. In a human being, this inward journey is clearer, especially in the complete human being who travels the whole upward arc, from creation back to God. Those with direct insight know this: their inner sight is not covered by blind imitation, doubt, desire, or attachment to this world. This is God's favor, which He gives to whom He wills.

The human body in this world is like a well-built ship at sea, carrying many powers and workers that serve it by God's permission. A ship can move from place to place only when the wind of its will blows. When the wind dies down, the ship stops. “In God's name it sails and comes to rest.”[^V09-58]

If the wind, whose relation to the ship is like the soul's relation to the body, stops, the ship comes to a halt before any part of it breaks or any tool fails. In the same way, when the soul leaves, the human body and its organs can no longer sense or live, even if its limbs and bodily matter have not yet decayed. What has gone is the breath of life, like the wind that moved the ship.

The proof is clear: the wind is not part of the ship. The ship's movement follows the wind; the ship does not carry the wind. By God's permission, the wind carries and moves it, and it sails in God's name. Those on board, with all their different powers, cannot bring the wind back by a trick or a device. The same is true of the soul and its life-giving breath. They are not made from the body's substance, and the body does not carry the soul. No bodily power or mixture of bodily qualities can bring the soul back once it has left.

This example shows that the body's life and movement follow the soul; the soul does not follow them. It also disproves transmigration, which claims that a soul can return to a body after leaving it, because that body's mixture and material are ready to receive it. As we have explained, the soul itself preserves the body's balance, brings its parts together, and keeps its elements joined. The soul follows no bodily mixture or elements.

The difference between natural death and death from an outside cause can also be shown by the ship. A ship can be destroyed in two ways. First, its hull can be damaged or its structure can come apart. Water then enters, and the ship sinks with everyone aboard if they have failed to notice the damage and repair it. The body and its powers can likewise be destroyed when one bodily quality overwhelms the others, because a person has neglected the body. When its balance is lost, its order breaks down, its parts are no longer in proper proportion, and its tools grow weak, the soul no longer remains with it. The wind, however, has not vanished; it remains in its own source, just as the soul remains in its own realm after the body is destroyed. This is death from an outside cause.

The second way a ship can be destroyed is when a violent wind strikes it with a force that the ship and its tools cannot bear. The tools weaken and break because they cannot withstand the force. So too, when the soul grows stronger in its very being and its inborn heat grows more intense, the body can no longer bear it. Its structure breaks down, its tools dry up, and its moisture is used up as the heat takes over.

In our view, the inborn heat of old people is stronger than that of the young. Its effect is less visible because the body that carries it is weaker and its material has dried up. This is the opposite of the common view that older people have less heat than the young. Natural death, then, comes from heat overpowering the body and consuming its moisture. The body's own heat finally fails, and death follows. This is a necessary result of the soul's own growth and purpose. Here, that purpose is the soul's strength and perfection: the spirit becoming more complete, and the life-giving breath growing stronger. In the ship's example, this is like a storm whose force overwhelms the ship.

The people on this ship, caught in such a storm, respond in one of two ways. If they understand God's plan, they are at peace and surrender themselves to Him before the veil is lifted. As they leave this world, they urge one another to be patient, not to despair, and to look with longing toward the home they are about to reach. Once they understand this divine order and work with it through reason and faith, their grief and worry come to an end, and they reach lasting happiness.

But if they do not understand God's plan—that all that the Wise One does is good and right—and do not accept the call to trust and surrender, their reward is Hell. They are kept from happiness and far from the pleasure of God, the All-Knowing and All-Wise.

#### Chapter 5 — Every person has one self: the soul itself is the living, perceiving, and thinking being

It is also the being that hears and sees, nourishes and grows, and reproduces. In a sense, it is the natural body that moves, grows, and senses.

We have already proved this in earlier chapters, but we want to explain it more fully because it matters greatly to understanding how all actions come from the First Reality. Each of us knows, before reasoning it out, that our self is one thing, not many. At the same time, we know that we are the one who thinks, perceives, senses, desires, becomes angry, occupies space, moves, and rests. We are described by many names and qualities. Some belong to reason and thought, some to sensation and imagination, and some to the body and its states and reactions.

This is something we know directly. Yet most people cannot recognize it through careful study and deny that the self is one when they begin to investigate—except those whom God has given light.

If someone cannot see the unity of their own self, how can they see the unity of their Lord? The early thinkers divided different actions among different powers. They assigned each action to a different power, then had to explain whether one power among them was the source and the others depended on it. Let us review the views reported on this question and the arguments for each.

Some said there is one soul. They disagreed about how its powers work. Some said the soul performs its actions through different bodily tools, with a particular action coming from each power. This is the view of Avicenna and those in his school. Others said that the soul is not one: the body has several souls, some for sensation, some for thought, some for desire, and some for anger.[^V09-59]

Those who deny that the soul is one argue as follows. A plant has a power for nourishment, while an animal has powers for nourishment and sensation, but no power for thought or reason. Since a plant's nutritive soul exists without an animal's sensitive soul, and an animal's sensitive soul exists without a rational soul, they say these must be different souls. If one soul did all these things, they argue, each power would have to be present whenever any one of them was present.

They also say that since these powers are separate in some beings, they must be separate souls when they are found together in a human being. But this argument is weak. Some simple kinds of thing contain different features in one and the same existence. For example, one feature may be present where another is absent. That does not prove that the features have separate existences in the thing they form.[^V09-60]

Also, the nutritive power in a plant is not the same kind of power as the nutritive power in an animal. Nor is the sensitive power in a nonthinking animal exactly the same kind as the sensitive power in a human being. They share a broad meaning, like “able to sense,” when we consider that meaning on its own, apart from the other things that complete it.

When “able to sense” is complete and independent, as it is in animals other than humans, it is already a full kind of living power. It does not wait for another power to complete it. But when it can become real only by being joined to something more complete—such as the rational power in a human being—it is different in kind, even though the two share a broader meaning. So the sensitive soul in other animals is different from the human soul that can think. In a human being, they are one. The same applies to the nutritive power in plants and the nutritive power in animals and humans.[^V09-61] Remember this rule; it will help you.

Those who say the soul is one argue that we have already shown that different actions of the soul come from different powers. Each power, in its own role, can produce only a certain kind of action. The power of anger is not moved by pleasure, and the power of desire is not affected by painful things. The power that perceives is not affected in the same way as either of them.

These powers can sometimes work together and sometimes oppose each other. When we sense something, we may desire it or become angry. But if we focus on thought, our senses may grow weaker; if we focus on sensation, our anger or desire may grow weaker.

If there were no shared source to direct all these powers, they could not cooperate or conflict in this way.[^V09-62] For each power has its own tool, and its actions are not linked to the actions of the others. If there is a shared source, it must be a body, something present in a body, or something that is neither a body nor present in one. The first two options have already been ruled out. So, they conclude, all these powers must come together in one thing that is neither bodily nor dependent on a body: the soul.
