I say that this argument does not help us solve the problem. Someone could reply: how do you know that the soul connects these powers?

If you mean only that the soul causes them to exist, that is not enough to show that the soul itself is the one that senses, nourishes, rests, writes, and laughs. Causing a power to exist does not make the powers work together or get in one another's way.[^V09-63]

If one cause creates separate powers in separate places and gives each its own tool, each power remains separate from the others and does not need them. How could one power's particular action stop another power from acting? You say that the Active Intellect is the source of every power in the body. Would that mean that all these powers help or hinder one another simply because they come from one source?

If by “connecting” the powers you mean that the soul itself sees what is visible, hears what is audible, and desires what is desirable—that the soul itself is the place where these powers are present and the source of their actions and qualities—then this is the truth, which nothing can overturn.[^V09-64] But who has established this carefully and answered all the doubts that stand in its way?

If the soul itself does all these things, how can we still say that its powers are many and that they sometimes conflict? If they are one substance with one identity, this view also seems to undermine the separate powers that Avicenna and other philosophers place in different parts of the body. If the soul itself sees, hears, and desires, why claim that a seeing power is located in the spirit where the two optic nerves meet, or a hearing power in the spirit spread through the ear?

It would also mean that a person sees and hears through acts that belong to someone else, rather than through powers that belong to the person.

There is a second way to say that the soul connects its powers. When the power of sight perceives a particular person, the rational soul recognizes that someone exists with this color, this shape, and this position. But it takes all these qualities in a general way. Even if we add a thousand general qualities to something general, it does not become a particular individual.

The sense perception of that individual prepares the soul to know it in a general way. The soul's general understanding then prompts a general desire to obtain that thing. The desire becomes particular when it is received by a particular faculty. That particular desire is what we call appetite. The same explanation would apply to anger and the other particular states attributed to the human soul. This is the strongest account that can be given of the soul as a bond that gathers bodily powers, according to Avicenna and his followers.[^V09-65]

I say that this account also fails. The soul's relation to particular desires, anger, sensations, movements, and other individual states is not like the relation of one separate thing to another. It is not as though the soul understands, in a general way, everything that the other powers sense in a particular way. If that were so, the Active Intellect would also have desire, anger, sensation, and movement, just as a human being does. But we know that it is free of these concerns and reactions.

We know directly that we are one self that thinks, senses, understands, moves, desires, becomes angry, and undergoes other reactions. The one in us that knows general truths is the same one that perceives particular things. The one that desires is the same one that becomes angry, and the same is true of other opposing qualities. This unity cannot be explained by a mere chain of relationships, like the relation between a ruler and soldiers or between a householder, children, and servants. We need a natural unity: one being with many different powers. This is known to those who have reached a deep understanding of the soul and its stages, and of the true existence and divine qualities made known through the names taught by God to Adam: “He taught Adam the names, all of them.” We have explained in several places that the stronger and simpler a being's existence is, the more meanings and effects it gathers within itself.

There are three worlds: the world of intellect, the world of the animal soul, and the world of nature. The first is entirely free of multiplicity. The second is free of division into physical places and parts. The third is where multiplicity, conflict, and division among material things appear.[^V09-66]

The human soul, unlike other animal souls, has three levels: intellect and holiness, soul and imagination, and sense and nature. Every quality and action it has at one level is also present at the others, but in a way that fits each level.[^V09-67]

The senses and powers of perception and movement exist in the body as many separate powers because matter can be divided and can contain differences and opposites. The place of sight is not the place of hearing. Desire is not located where anger is, and the power for forceful movement is not in the same organ as the power for walking. One part of the body can feel the pain of a torn muscle while another feels the relief of a wound healing.

All these powers also exist in the realm of imagination, the animal soul, as distinct powers, but they are united in one nonphysical field. There is no division into places as there is in the outer senses. An inner sense can see, hear, smell, taste, and touch particular things without splitting these acts among separate body parts. In the same way, it can desire, become angry, feel pain, and feel joy without bodily injury or healing. These powers are many in the world of the outer senses, but not divided by place in the world of inner sense.[^V09-68]

We also find all of them in the realm of intellect. There they are free of multiplicity, detail, physical division, and particular images, yet none of their meanings is missing.[^V09-69] The intellectual human being is spiritual. All its parts are present in one being that is one in itself but rich in meaning. It has an intellectual face, sight, hearing, and limbs, all together without physical separation, as Aristotle explains in his *Theology*.

You may ask: if the soul itself perceives everything and performs every action, why do we need many powers—some for perception, such as hearing and sight, and others for movement, such as attraction, repulsion, desire, and anger?

We answer: the soul is indeed all these powers. But for them to appear in the material world, they need separate bodily tools in different places.[^V09-70] The bodily world is divided into parts. One physical organ, with one bodily nature, cannot give rise to many different qualities, such as hearing and seeing, or many different kinds of movement. The existence of an organ is not complete enough to gather all these meanings. A spiritual being, by contrast, can gather them within itself.

The one soul is the source of every action carried out through its scattered powers and different organs, while remaining one in itself. In the same way, the one intellect gathers all the powers and meanings found in the other powers of the soul, senses, and body, but in a higher and more perfect way, as we have explained many times.

You may also ask: how does the separate intellect differ from the soul in this ability to gather powers? The soul needs many different powers, bodily tools, and organs in order to express these qualities. Why does the intellect not need many materials and tools to carry out its actions and show its meanings?[^V09-71]

We answer: the intellect gives its qualities to external matter by bringing things into being. It is not changed, affected, or made more complete by them. The soul, by contrast, cannot stand on its own or become free of change and reaction until it becomes pure intellect, with no lack or unrealized potential.

Until then, the soul passes through different stages and needs all of them: sometimes it is at the level of sense and nature, sometimes at the level of soul and imagination, and sometimes at the level of intellect and what it understands. This is its condition as long as it is connected to the natural body. Once it leaves this world, it is at the level of separate intellect if it has reached full maturity in knowledge and action. If it has not, it remains at the second, imaginal level of the afterlife. There it takes on forms that fit its habits and states, which differ from one soul to another.

#### Chapter 6 — A weakness in the argument of the author of The Book of Discussions

As the light of truth has dawned on you from its horizon, as we have reminded you many times, consider the argument of one who argues for many sources of human actions, some bodily and some spiritual. The argument, in brief, is this: if the soul and its powers were one thing, the soul would have to be joined to matter when it nourishes the body, makes it grow, reproduces, and senses bodily things. Yet it would have to detach itself from matter when it understands abstract truths.[^V09-72] But that is impossible.

They argue also that if one power held all the images in its storehouse, it would always see them, as the intellect does when its knowledge is fully present.[^V09-73]

The author of *The Book of Discussions* answers that the imagination is bodily, as the ancient philosophers admit, though there are issues that do not fit our present discussion. The outer senses also play a part in imagination.[^V09-74]

As for the claim that the soul must mix with matter in order to nourish the body and do similar things, perhaps this does not follow. A being that is not joined to matter might still act on nourishment or other bodily processes in some way. Perhaps one power continues its work after another power stops. And perhaps reproduction and growth fail in certain people or at certain times because the body's mixture is no longer ready for them.[^V09-75]

For example, the balance of the spirits in the body's cavities can change, preparing different powers for different actions. A person's bodily mixture also changes with age, so it may become ready to receive many effects from one source, one effect from many sources, or one effect from one source in one particular way.[^V09-76] The body may stop growing at a certain age even while the source of growth—the soul or intellect—remains.

The argument goes on to say that certain actions attributed to these powers can come only from the Giver of Forms.[^V09-77] If all forms come from that source and powers merely prepare the body to receive them, then perhaps readiness comes from powers that are not themselves living substances.

That is the end of his statement.

I see several problems with this argument.[^V09-78] First, his agreement with the ancient philosophers that imagination is a bodily power is mistaken.[^V09-79] As we have shown, imagination can exist apart from the natural body, even by the principles of those ancient philosophers.

Second, it is wrong to allow that a separate soul could act on nourishment in a way that depends on nourishment to keep the soul itself alive. A separate being may govern lower things, but it is not changed or made more complete by them.

Third, the claim that reproduction and growth stop at certain times because the body is no longer ready for them confuses the regular course of a kind of living thing with accidents that affect one individual. What belongs to a whole kind cannot be explained by the body's accidental conditions, because effects caused by those conditions would not happen always or even in most cases.

The regular course shared by a whole kind comes from its active source. The fact that growth and reproduction stop with old age, and that natural death follows, is of this kind. These events come from causes that regularly lead to certain results: either directly, as when a soul reaches a new stage of completion, or indirectly, as when one power weakens because another has grown stronger, or when a soul turns away from one action in order to move toward another kind of life.

As the soul enters a new life, it turns its power away from the bodily world, and the body dies as a result. You now know that each stage in the body's development follows the soul's progress through its own essential stages and changes. At each point, the soul has a different way of existing, and this brings with it a different action. The body's readiness follows the source that acts on it; it does not determine that source.

An active source may remain while its effect is absent only when something blocks it. That happens rarely and by accident in a few individuals of a kind. It cannot explain why growth and reproduction regularly stop in old age, as they do in everyone. The fact that growth stops at that stage is therefore not caused by an accidental bodily obstacle.[^V09-80]

Another problem is the claim that actions come only from the Giver of Forms. This rests on the view that there are no substantial forms, a view we have already shown to be weak. The same is true of denying real powers. On the view we are criticizing, nourishment, growth, and reproduction in plants and animals come neither from their souls and powers nor from the human soul, but from a separate substance.

We have explained in several places that the direct sources of motion and change are the natural powers present in bodies. The separate intellect is the source of all things, but it does not act on each one directly.[^V09-81] The power of fire to heat and of water to cool, and other powers of this kind, are not merely conditions that prepare for an effect. They are true causes of the effects that regularly come from them.[^V09-82]

The same is true of the powers of plants and animals, as we explained in our discussion of forms. This is why we return to what we have said about the unity of the soul.

#### Chapter 7 — The body's powers are shadows of the soul's inner powers

Know that the powers present in the body and its organs belong to the natural human being. They are shadows and likenesses of the guiding soul and its powers—the human being of the next life. That imaginal human being, with its own powers and organs, is in turn a shadow and likeness of the intellectual human being, with its own aspects and qualities. The natural body, its organs, and its qualities are therefore shadows of shadows and likenesses of likenesses of what exists in the human intellect.[^V09-83]

The bodily powers of the natural human being are like an outer covering around the animal soul that will be raised in the next life, the true home of life. God says, “The home of the next life is truly life, if only they knew.” We can see this because when our outer powers and bodily senses fall quiet during sleep, illness, or other states, we often find that we can still hear, see, smell, touch, grasp, and walk.[^V09-84] We have these senses and powers within us, without missing any of them. But they are not fixed in this world of outward sense and sight. If they were, everyone with sound senses would see them. Their true home is another world: the unseen world within the soul.

The inward powers and tools are themselves shadows of what exists in the human intellect. The intellect is simple and undivided, yet it gives rise to these many powers in the soul and, through them, in the body. If it did not contain different aspects suited to its nature, one simple thing could not give rise to so many different things. Every high and noble being is the source, in a unified way, of the many meanings found in the beings below it.

We have quoted the First Teacher, Aristotle, as saying plainly that the intellectual human being has all the organs of the human being who senses, but in a form that suits the intellect. Elsewhere he says that the bodily human being contains both the human soul and the intellectual human being. He does not mean that they are identical, but that the bodily human being is connected to them: it is an image of them. It carries out some actions of the intellectual human being and some of the animal soul. The bodily human being therefore gathers both kinds of meaning, though only weakly and in a limited way, since it is an image of an image.

This shows that the first, higher human being does sense, but in a better and more complete way than the lower human being. The lower human being receives its senses from the higher, intellectual human being, as we have explained. This is a remarkable account of the force of Aristotle's insight into divine matters. He reached a height that later thinkers could not match. Most Muslim philosophers, including Avicenna and those like him, did not grasp what he meant. In *The Healing*, Avicenna dismissed the view he attributed to Plato and Socrates: that there are two human beings, one that perishes and one that is separate and everlasting. What would he have said if he had heard that every human being contains three beings, different in their degree of existence: a bodily being that perishes, an intellectual being that lasts, and an imaginal soul between them?[^V09-85]

The bodily human form, with all its organs, is an image of the imaginal human being. The human being as it exists in the world of images is in turn an image of the human being understood through intellect. The imaginal human being is raised either among the happy, in Paradise, or among the miserable, in Hell. The intellectual human being, however, is in a seat of truth with a powerful King.

The imaginal human being senses things and judges them through its own being, without using a bodily tool. It perceives things outside itself through images present within it. It perceives them through those very images; if it needed another image to perceive each image, this would lead to an endless chain. Its own being is sight for seeing, hearing for hearing, smell for smelling, taste for tasting, and touch for touching. As we have learned, the sense and the thing sensed are joined in the act of perception.

The imaginal human being also judges things through its own being, whether these judgments come from imagination or another inner power. It desires what is pleasant and feels anger toward what conflicts with it. It does not need a separate desire or anger added to it. The same is true of the intellectual human being in all the corresponding ways.

The greatest of the philosophers says: “These senses are weak intellects, and those intellects are strong senses.”[^V09-86] Someone might object: if the human being in the higher world can sense, and sensation is what distinguishes animals, then sensation must be a reaction to a physical form. How can a noble, higher being have a sense that belongs to a lower one?

The answer, as we learn from the philosopher, is that the relation between ordinary sense and intellectual sense is like the relation between a flesh-and-blood animal and the intellectual animal. The same is true of what is sensed here and what is sensed in the higher world. The sense found in this lower world is not like the sense found in the higher one: there, the sense is directed toward things that are themselves higher. That is why the sight of this lower animal is connected to the sight of the higher animal.

I say that the same is true of hearing, smell, taste, and touch. Their connection is like the connection between material things and their intellectual forms in the higher world, and in God's eternal knowledge.

The Prophet, peace be upon him and his family, said that this fire had been washed seventy times with water before it was brought down to us. This points to the fact that the fire we know is one stage in the descent of an intellectual fire. The Prophet also said that he spent the night with his Lord, who fed him and gave him drink.[^V09-87] He said, “I can smell the Breath of the All-Merciful coming from Yemen.” He said, “The earth was gathered and folded up for me, and I saw its eastern and western ends.”[^V09-88] He also said, “God placed His hand on my shoulder, and I felt the coolness of it between my breasts.” These reports describe perception through the subtle senses of the next life, not through the ordinary senses that wear out.

The Prophet also said, “The sky groans, and it has every right to groan: there is no space in it the size of a foot without an angel bowing or prostrating.”[^V09-89] In short, the intellectual human being shines its light on the lower, bodily human being through the imaginal human being, which stands between them.

The soul's effect on an image already present within it is like sunlight falling on a body in one way, but different in another.[^V09-90] The sun makes an object visible; the soul brings its own imagined forms into being. When the soul imagines something, it gives rise to its image in its own world, not in the outer world, and perceives it through its own being, not through another image.

The soul also perceives things that are visible outside it. As we explained earlier in our discussion of sight, the material thing and its position in relation to the eye prepare the soul to bring forth an image like that thing. The image is separate from matter and its material conditions, and exists in the soul's own realm, like an imagined image. The difference is that an image of something seen depends on an outside object and its conditions, while an imagined image does not.

#### Chapter 8 — What the soul first connects to

The soul acts on the body's dense, material organs only through a suitable medium that connects the two. This medium is a fine, light substance called the spirit. It flows through the organs by way of the nerves in the brain. The claim that the soul first acts through this spirit—said to be formed from the finer, vaporous part of the bodily fluids, while the body is formed from their thicker, coarser part—is close to obvious and needs no proof. The levels of existence are arranged from fine to coarse, just as they are arranged from noble to low.

Within an animal, one part is finer than the rest.[^V09-91] This is the vital spirit carried in the arteries.[^V09-92] Another part is coarser than the rest, near the hip. Between the finest and the coarsest are suitable intermediate levels, arranged one above another by God's wise design, like the layers of the heavenly bodies and the elements.

The finer a thing is in its substance, the more actively it acts and the less it is acted on. The coarser it is, the more it is acted on and the less it acts. For example, the body's vital spirit naturally acts on the blood, which belongs to the same level as itself. The blood in turn acts on the moisture of the brain, and that moisture acts on the muscles. This continues from one part to another until it reaches the hardest parts, which are wholly receptive and do not act on anything else.

The same order should be understood among spiritual beings, just as we understand it among bodily things. The same is true of the great world, as it is of the small world of the human being. Among bodily things, one part is the finest—the highest celestial sphere—and another is the coarsest—the earth below. Between them are orderly stages, each between the finer and the coarser, set in place by divine wisdom.

The evidence shows that the finer a thing is, the more it acts; the coarser it is, the more it receives. This is why prayers turn toward the sky when people ask for what they need, seek good things, or ask for their prayers to be answered. In truth, turning toward the sky means turning toward God.[^V09-93] This is also what some mystics mean when they say that God's light is one, but appears in different forms according to the places where it shines. The being that flows from God is one in its reality, though it has different levels according to how near or far it is from Him.

Once we understand this, we can see that the soul belongs to the world of pure intellectual light, while the body is dense, dark, and material. The soul cannot act on the body in a way that makes them one natural thing unless there is a medium between them. That medium is the fine substance doctors call the spirit. There is also a suitable medium between each of these levels and the one next to it.[^V09-94] For example, an imaginal, intermediate form lies between the rational soul and the animal spirit, while pure, fine blood lies between that spirit and the body.

There is evidence that this substance, called the spirit, exists.[^V09-95] If the nerves are blocked, sensation and movement stop beyond the blockage, on the side away from the brain. The blockage prevents only the passage of bodily substances. Medical experience also shows that an abundance of spirit brings joy, energy, strength, courage, and desire. A lack of it brings epilepsy, fainting, grief, sadness, and melancholy. If it leaves the heart, death comes suddenly. The soul is connected to the body through this spirit. And since the soul is one, it must first connect to one organ through this subtle spirit, then reach the brain, liver, and other organs through that first organ.

People disagree about which organ is the main one, giving life and power to the rest: the heart or the brain. Most doctors and natural philosophers say it is the heart. It is the warmest part of the body, while the brain is not. The heart is therefore the right place for the warm, inborn spirit.

Also, intense thinking heats the brain. If the brain were not cool by nature, it might burn when the heat of the spirit and the heat of thought came together. The heart is also the first organ to form, the first to move, and the last to stop. So it is the soul's first point of connection to the body. From there, it reaches the brain, the liver, and the other organs in order.

One might object: if the heart is the main organ and the source that gives the others their powers, and the soul's spirits flow from it to the brain, then the nerves should begin in the heart, not in the brain. Tools should begin at their source, not at the place that receives them. But the nerves begin in the brain, so the claim about the heart must be wrong.

We answer that some commentators on the *Canon of Medicine* say no conclusive proof has ever shown that the nerves begin in the brain.[^V09-96] Even setting that claim aside, someone could deny that a tool must begin at the source of its power. Why could a tool not begin in the organ that receives its power, after the power has reached it from the source? The spirits that carry the powers of sensation and movement could then pass through the brain.

A full treatment of this question belongs in medical books.

### Book Nine — The human soul's traits, actions, reactions, and stages

Some of the soul's powers exist at a higher level than others and are less open to division. This book has several chapters.

#### Chapter 1 — The human being's distinctive traits

One distinctive human trait is speech. Consider the wisdom God showed in making human beings from the elements: He gave them a system of words shared by agreement. We need others to live in this world and depend on help from our own kind. If one person lived alone, with no one else of the same kind or family around, and no access to anything beyond what nature provided, that person would soon die or live badly.

We need things that nature does not provide in a ready-to-use form, such as prepared food and made clothing. Natural food is not suitable for us to eat until it is prepared, and natural coverings are not useful until human work turns them into clothes. We need to learn crafts to live well. But one person cannot do every kind of work. People must help each other: one bakes bread for another, someone else weaves cloth, another carries goods, and each gives a share of their work in return.

For these and similar reasons, we need a way to tell another person what is in our mind. The best way is with sound. Sound can be divided into letters and joined into many combinations without putting strain on the body. We already need to make sounds in order to breathe and cool the heart. Sound also does not last; it disappears as soon as it is made, so it does not remain for someone who has no need to hear it. A gesture can also communicate, but it is less useful: it can guide only someone who sees it from a particular direction, and the person making it must keep moving the eyes to direct it. Gesture is therefore less useful and takes more effort. Sound has none of these limits. So nature gave the human soul the ability to combine sounds and use them to communicate what is in the mind. This became our shared system of words.[^V09-97]

Other animals do not need speech in the same way. They find food in nature or need little work to prepare it; they come with their own coverings, such as skin and hair, or shelter in holes and caves. They still make sounds, and some of their calls show a little thought and tell others something of their feelings. But these calls give only a broad signal, enough to seek what is pleasant or avoid what is harmful. Human speech gives much more detail. We need to express almost endlessly many things, but we could not be born with a different sound for each one. So we need to create words and agree on their meanings.

Another remarkable human trait is the ability to invent unusual crafts. Animals also build, especially birds, and bees build their hexagonal cells. A hexagon holds more space than other shapes after the circle, and resembles a circle in how much space it encloses. Bees do not use circles because gaps would be left between the cells. Squares would leave wasted corners inside. Their cells therefore fit together closely in a shape suited to their builder.

Even so, animals do not invent these crafts through their own individual reasoning. Otherwise, their work would vary from one animal to another. Instead, their work follows an inspiration or guidance given by the beings that govern their kind, such as the angels. Most of these crafts serve the whole kind or some other kind, such as human beings, who seem to be the special purpose of animal life and its crafts. Only a small part of an animal's craft serves that particular animal. Human beings are different, especially those who have reached their full development: what each one does and makes serves that person's own life. Each person can continue as an individual in the next life and through their own intellect, while other animals continue as a kind, not as separate individuals.

Another human trait is that unusual things can make us feel wonder and then laugh, while painful things can make us feel distress and then cry. Human beings also learn to take part in one another's interests. They are taught from childhood not to do certain things and to do others. This makes them firmly believe they ought to avoid one kind of act and choose another, for the sake of a well-ordered life. By God's care, they call the first kind shameful and the other good and beautiful.[^V09-98]

Other animals may also avoid certain acts. A trained lion, for example, may not eat its keeper, and a noble horse may not mate with its mother. But this does not come from a belief about right and wrong. Every animal naturally loves what suits and pleases it. It may grow attached to the person who feeds it and so refuse to eat that person. A horse raised by its mother from birth may grow so attached to her that this keeps it from mating with her. Other habits may come from an inspiration given to the animal, such as the love every animal feels for its young.

If a person realizes that someone else has seen them do something shameful, they feel embarrassment. A person may also think that something harmful will happen in the future and feel fear, or think that something good will happen and feel hope. Other animals usually feel these things only in response to what is happening now.[^V09-99] If they prepare for something, they do not do so because they clearly understand what may happen later. They act through an instinct given to their kind, as when ants quickly carry grain into their nest before rain. Perhaps they imagine some harm or rain is coming, just as an animal runs from an enemy because it imagines it will be struck. Or perhaps they are guided by an inspiration from the angel that governs their kind.

In general, human actions that show judgment and reason come from each human being as an individual. In other animals, they come from a general plan for the kind.

A human being can also think about future choices and decide whether to act. At one time, the person does what their judgment recommends; at another time, they hold back, even from something they could do before, because their judgment has changed. Other animals cannot do this. They have fixed patterns of behavior, whether or not those patterns lead to good results.

Another human trait is recalling things that have left the mind. Other animals cannot do this in the same way; they do not deliberately bring back and think over what they once knew.

The most distinctively human ability is to grasp general meanings fully apart from matter, as we have explained in our discussion of intellect and intelligible things. We can also use what we already know to discover what we do not yet know, both by reasoning about facts and by forming ideas. Most distinctively of all, some human souls can join the divine world so completely that they let go of their own self and live through God. Then God becomes their hearing, sight, hand, and foot, and they take on God's qualities.

These are some of the human being's traits. Some belong to the body, but they occur through the soul, as with laughter and tears. Some are states of the soul that arise through the body, such as fear, hope, embarrassment, and grief. Others are purely intellectual, such as the theoretical and practical wisdom of the soul. Among all created beings, and especially among animals, the human being gathers together bodily, soulful, and intellectual qualities.

Avicenna says[^V09-100] that a human being deals with particular matters and general matters. We can form beliefs only about general matters. Even when we believe in a general way how a house should be built, that belief alone does not directly produce a particular house. Actions deal with particular things and come from particular parts of us.

So a person must have one power that works with general ideas and another that thinks about particular choices:[^V09-101] what should or should not be done, what helps or harms, and what is good or bad. This power uses reasoning and thought, whether sound or mistaken. It produces a judgment about one possible action in the future. People do not deliberate about what must happen or cannot happen, or about something that has already happened.

When this power reaches a decision, it moves the power of desire, which moves the body.[^V09-102] Or the practical power may draw on the power that handles general ideas, taking its general premises from it. The example is: “Every good act should be done; telling the truth is good; therefore, telling the truth should be done.” The practical power then applies that general judgment to a particular case: “This is telling the truth; telling the truth should be done; therefore, this should be done.” Theoretical and practical reason are two levels of one intellect, not two separate beings.[^V09-103]

The first power is called theoretical reason, because it seeks knowledge. The second is called practical reason, because it guides action. Theoretical reason deals with truth and falsehood; practical reason with good and bad in particular cases. The first deals with what is necessary, possible, or impossible; the second with what is good, shameful, or allowed. The first begins from first principles; the second usually begins from widely accepted views, claims people accept, or experience that may not be fully reliable.[^V09-104]

Each of these two powers has its own belief and judgment. A firm belief is a conclusion held as certain. A leaning belief allows that the opposite may still be true. Not everyone who leans toward a view believes it firmly, just as sensing is not the same as thinking, and imagining is not the same as forming a judgment. A human being therefore has a judge of sense, a judge of imagination, a judge of theory, and a judge of practical matters. The powers that move the body include imagination, practical reason, desire, and anger. Animals have three of these kinds of judgment.

Practical reason needs the body and bodily powers in all its actions. Theoretical reason also needs the body and its powers, but not always or in every way; it can sometimes stand on its own. Neither of these powers alone is the human soul. The soul is the single being that has them. It is a single substance, ready for different actions—some that require bodily tools and full attention to them, and others that require no tools at all. We will explain this in its proper place.

There are many points in Avicenna's account that could be questioned, but that would take too long to discuss here. What we have explained about the soul—its nature, stages, and unified existence across its different levels—is enough for a thoughtful reader.[^V09-105] If we think carefully about Avicenna's claim that a person has a judge of sense, imagination, theory, and practical reason, we can see that the soul itself is all these powers. It is not merely a thing that owns them.[^V09-106]

Everyone knows that each person is one being with one self, and that all their actions come from this one being. Still, anyone who has studied the subject knows that a judge of sense must belong to the level of sense and what is sensed, and a judge of imagination must belong to the level of imagination. The practical judge belongs to the level of an immaterial power connected to particular images, as we explained in our discussion of imagination and imagined things.[^V09-107] The theoretical judge belongs to the level of the intellect that is separate from matter.

This shows that the soul develops from one stage to another in its very being, as we have claimed. Avicenna's further point—that the intellect needs the body and its powers at first, but can later stand on its own—also supports our view that the soul begins with the body and survives as a spirit. Avicenna does not accept the deep change in the soul's being that we have described. For the soul's need for the body is not like a craftsperson's need for a workshop for some extra task. At first, it needs the body and its powers in order to exist. The soul itself is a way of existing, not something added to a completed self.

Once the soul becomes an active intellect, no longer needing the body, it becomes one of the beings of the intellectual world, like intelligible forms that understand themselves whether or not another being understands them. How could a bodily, material form become a form that can be understood in itself, whether or not someone thinks of it? It can do so only by changing and growing stronger in its very being, moving from the lowest stages to the highest. This is what is shown by the Prophet's ascent, as he passed through every level until he reached the highest and saw the greatest signs of his Lord.

The same is shown by Abraham's vision: “We showed Abraham the kingdom of the heavens and the earth,” and by his words, “I have turned my face toward the One who created the heavens and the earth.” How could the human stage described by the words “I am only a human being like you” be the same as the divine stage described by the Prophet's saying, “I have a time with God when no angel brought near or prophet sent can reach me”?

#### Chapter 2 — The main qualities of the soul and the roots of good and bad character

Most differences in human character come from differences in the soul's strength and nobility, or in its weakness and baseness.[^V09-108] We have already seen that existence itself has different degrees of strength and weakness, and that these differences change the nature and qualities of things.

Human souls vary greatly in completeness, nobility, and deficiency. A strong soul can carry out great actions in many different areas. This is because it gathers many levels of existence within itself. A weak soul cannot do as much.

We see weak souls that can attend to only one thing at a time. When they focus on thought, their senses grow weak; when they focus on their senses, their thought falters. If they devote themselves to moving the body, their ability to perceive is disrupted. Other souls are strong enough to combine different kinds of perception and action, especially the inner qualities that make up good character.[^V09-109]

Strength and weakness can belong to the soul as a sensing being or as an intellect.[^V09-110] The first kind appears in people with vivid powers of imagination, such as a diviner or someone thought to have the evil eye. The second appears in people with great knowledge.

A soul is noble by nature when it resembles higher, immaterial beings in wisdom and freedom. Wisdom may be inborn or learned. Inborn wisdom means that a person's judgments and beliefs tend to be true. This is the first natural capacity for developing learned wisdom. Human beings differ in this gift. The highest degree belongs to the holy, prophetic soul. The Qur'an describes it as oil that “almost gives light, even if no fire touches it.” This soul does not need a human teacher to gain knowledge. At the other extreme is a dull soul that gains nothing even from a teacher.

Freedom means that the soul is not naturally obedient to bodily desires and the pleasures of animal life. A soul that obeys them is not free. In everyday language, freedom is the opposite of slavery. Desires can overpower and command incomplete souls, making them servants to their appetites whether or not they give in to them. A person who longs for something but cannot have it may be more troubled than one who has it now, even if the first person may fare better in the end. Turning away from what is desired may eventually lessen the longing.

True freedom, then, is an inborn quality of the soul, not something produced by habit or teaching, though those too can help a person become virtuous. Aristotle calls freedom a settled quality that guards the soul from within, not a skill imposed on it from outside.[^V09-111] In short, the weaker the soul's ties to the body and the stronger its ties to reason, the freer it is. The opposite makes it more enslaved to desire.

Plato points to this when he says, “Base souls dwell in the realm of nature and its shadow; noble souls dwell in the realm of intellect.” If you understand wisdom and freedom, you can see that all the virtues of the soul come back to these two: a power to understand many things, and freedom from material attachments.[^V09-112] The many bad traits likewise come back to their opposites. It is not enough to cleanse the soul of only some of them. If even one remains in control, it may draw the person toward the others. No one escapes their harm except the one who comes to God with a sound heart. God says, “Successful is the one who purifies the soul, and lost is the one who corrupts it.” The Prophet, peace be upon him and his family, said, “I was sent to perfect noble character.”

Just as a person's outward appearance is beautiful when all its parts are well formed and balanced, the inner self is beautiful only when all its parts are sound. Then good character, wisdom, and freedom can develop. The inner self has four powers: knowledge, anger, desire, and reason. Justice keeps them in balance.[^V09-113] When these powers are balanced, the many traits of character that grow from them also fall into balance.

The power of knowledge is at its best when it can tell truth from falsehood in speech, right from wrong in belief, and good from bad in action. When this power is balanced, without excess or neglect,[^V09-114] it gives rise to wisdom. In truth, wisdom is the root of all good and the source of the other virtues. God says, “Whoever is given wisdom has been given much good.” He also says, “That is God's favor; He gives it to whom He wills. God is the possessor of great favor,” after saying that He teaches people the Book and wisdom. This wisdom—the knowledge of things as they are—is a fruit of the first kind, moral wisdom. The more knowledge a person gains, the better; this is not true of moral wisdom, which must remain balanced.[^V09-115]

#### Further note

The power of anger is balanced when it follows wisdom and religious law. The same is true of desire. Justice is the power that keeps anger and desire under the guidance of reason and religion. Theoretical reason is like a wise adviser. Justice is the power that carries out its instructions. Anger and desire are the powers that act on those instructions. They are like a hunting dog's and horse's movements: they should move, stop, hold back, or reach out when the guide directs them.

A balanced power of anger is called courage. God loves the courageous. Excess becomes recklessness; deficiency becomes cowardice. Courage gives rise to generosity, bravery, self-respect, patience, steadiness, control of anger, dignity, and other good traits. Excessive anger gives rise to recklessness, arrogance, extravagance, rage, pride, and conceit. Too little anger gives rise to cowardice, humiliation, meanness, lack of concern for one's family, lack of protective feeling, and a small heart.

A balanced power of desire is called chastity. Excess becomes greed; deficiency becomes dullness. Chastity gives rise to generosity, modesty, patience, forgiveness, contentment, piety, freedom from greed, and kindness.[^V09-116] Excessive desire gives rise to greed, shamelessness, waste, hypocrisy, indecency, flattery, envy, delight in others' suffering, fawning on the rich, and looking down on the poor.

A balanced power of reason gives rise to sound judgment and planning, a quick mind, good sense, and the ability to notice small details in people's actions and the hidden dangers in the soul. Excess becomes cunning and deceit. Deficiency becomes dullness, stupidity, foolishness, and gullibility.

These are the main roots of good and bad character. The account of the hypocrite's punishment in the grave describes them as the four heads of a dragon.[^V09-117] Good character in all four powers and in all the traits that grow from them means finding the middle ground between excess and deficiency. The best course is the middle one, and both extremes are blameworthy.[^V09-118] God says, “Do not keep your hand chained to your neck, nor stretch it out completely.” He also says, “When they spend, they are neither wasteful nor stingy, but follow a course between the two,” and, “They are firm against the unbelievers and kind to one another.” If any one of these powers departs from the right course, good character is still incomplete.

It is also important to know that strength of soul and nobility of soul are not the same thing.[^V09-119] Each can be present without the other, though they sometimes come together.[^V09-120] Courage, for example, may come from strength: a person looks down on an opponent and expects to defeat them. Or it may come from nobility: a person refuses to accept humiliation. A philosopher says, “Noble souls refuse to live in humiliation. They see that life in humiliation is death, and death in it is life.”

When courage comes from strength, it must also be guided by wisdom. Courage then means that the soul follows the sound judgment of reason in both acting and holding back. Cowardice follows reason in holding back, but not in acting. This may come from ignorance or weakness. Recklessness follows reason in acting, but not in holding back; it comes from strength without knowledge.[^V09-121]

A desire to rule may come from a noble soul or from a strong but ignorant one. The ignorant person mistakenly believes they deserve what is beyond their reach, and their strength drives them to seek it. Self-sufficiency may come from strength and confidence in one's ability to meet a need,[^V09-122] or from nobility and a lack of attachment to present things or worry about what is absent.[^V09-123] A feeling of poverty may come from weakness and fear of being without what one needs, or from baseness and greed. Justice belongs to a noble soul, especially when it is strong; injustice belongs to a low and grasping soul. Truthfulness goes with nobility, lying with baseness. Generosity comes from strength joined to nobility; foolishness from weakness joined to baseness. High ambition belongs to a strong and noble soul, while failure and small ambition belong to a weak and low one.

#### Chapter 3 — The stages of a person, according to the powers of the soul

The inner self of every human being is a mixture of different powers: animal, predatory, devilish, and angelic. The animal power gives rise to appetite, greed, and unrestrained desire. The predatory power gives rise to envy, hostility, and hatred. The devilish power gives rise to cunning, deception, schemes, pride, the love of status, boasting, and the desire to dominate. The angelic power gives rise to knowledge and purity. These four are the roots from which all character grows. They are mixed together so deeply within a person that they are hard to separate.

The soul escapes the darkness of the first three through the light of guidance that comes from reason and religious teaching. The animal power appears first in a child, bringing appetite and greed. Next the predatory power appears, bringing hostility and a tendency to quarrel. Then the devilish power takes hold. It first brings cunning and deception, as the animal and angry powers lead a person to use intelligence to satisfy desire and anger. Later it brings pride, conceit, boasting, and the desire to rise above others.

After this, the intellect develops. It brings the light of faith. It belongs to God's side and to the angels; the other powers belong to Satan's forces. The intellect's growth is complete around the age of forty, though it begins to appear at puberty. The other powers have reached the heart before puberty. The soul grows familiar with them and follows their desires.

When the light of the intellect arrives, a battle begins in the heart. If the intellect is weak, Satan takes control, and the person is gathered with Satan and all his forces. If the intellect grows strong through knowledge and faith, the other powers become subject to it and join the ranks of the angels. No soul knows in what land it will die.

#### Chapter 4 — How perception rises from the lowest level to the highest

Know that every universal idea we understand has a nature that can be shared by many. Take human nature. Considered simply as human, it can belong to many people. By itself, it does not tell us whether there is one person or many, whether it is known through reason or sense, or whether it is universal or particular. If it did, it could not apply to the opposite case.[^V09-124]

When this nature exists in external matter, it comes with certain qualities, size, place, position, and time. These are not part of what it means to be human, but they are part of each person's particular way of existing. They help make that person an individual.[^V09-125] Ordinary thinkers suppose that human nature exists outside the mind in one way, while its individual features have another existence. They do not understand the different ways existence can appear.

In the world outside the mind, a human being exists as a particular individual with some size, qualities, place, and other features. These are not separate things added to the person's existence. People see that such features change while human nature remains and conclude that the person's existence is different from the nature of humanity. They miss that one nature can exist in many individuals without losing its unity as a kind or as an idea in the mind. A single person, too, can change from one state of existence to another while remaining the same individual through gradual, continuous change.[^V09-126]

Ancient philosophers wrote that the senses, imagination, inner judgment, and intellect arise as we leave behind different bodily conditions. People thought this meant that perception removes some features and leaves others behind. But every act of perception is a way of existing. The object moves from one level of existence to another while its nature and identity remain.[^V09-127]

A material thing exists with a particular matter, size, qualities, and place. A sense-image[^V09-128] exists as a form with no physical place, but it depends on an outside object and a similar form in the sense. If the material object were absent, the sense would not receive its image.

An imagined image, by contrast, does not depend on the continued presence of material.

A sense-image arises only when something is sensed, and remains a particular image, not an idea that applies to many things.

An image formed by imagination is still a particular image, not a general idea. An idea understood by the mind can apply to many individuals: the general idea of a human being, for example.[^V09-129] The intellectual human being also has intellectual forms of hearing, sight, hands, and other organs. These many meanings do not divide the simple intellectual being, because one simple being can contain many different meanings.

We have often explained that things with a nature move toward their perfection and purpose. Of all natural beings, the human being is special: one individual can rise from the lowest levels to the highest while remaining the same individual.[^V09-130] Other natural things do not follow this path. The matter that carries their form takes on another kind of form, separate from the one it had before; it does not preserve either the individual or the kind.[^V09-131]

A human being, by contrast, can have several modes of existence: bodily, imaginal, and intellectual. Each has many levels—not an endless number of separate things outside the mind, but levels we can distinguish in thought.[^V09-132] The person moves from a lower level to a higher one, from a less complete state to a nobler one. The person completes the stages of one mode of existence before passing to the next.

From childhood until bodily maturity, a person is first a bodily human being. As this mode of existence develops, it becomes clearer and finer, until the person gains imaginal existence, the second human being. This imaginal human being has its own organs, but they do not need to occupy separate places as bodily organs do.[^V09-133] In the bodily world, sight and hearing need different organs. Taste and smell also need different organs. Some powers are more divided and depend more on matter than others. Touch is the lowest level of animal life, so every animal has it, even one as low as a shellfish or worm. In the imaginal human being, the senses are gathered together in one inner sense.

The powers that move the body are also spread across different organs: some are in the liver, some in the brain, some in the heart, some in the reproductive organs, and others elsewhere. In the imaginal human being, they are gathered together. If a person—rarely—rises from this imaginal existence to the intellectual level and becomes an active intellect, they are an intellectual human being, the third human being, with intellectual organs as we have said.

Looking up from this world, the order is bodily, imaginal, and intellectual. Looking down from the world of intellect, the order is reversed. Aristotle's *Theology* says that the first, intellectual human gives its light to the second, imaginal human being, who in turn gives light to the third, bodily human. So the bodily human contains the meanings of both the imaginal and intellectual human, but only faintly. It is an image of an image.

There is no contradiction between saying that some people rise from bodily existence to imaginal existence and, in rare cases, intellectual existence, and saying that the bodily human contains both higher meanings. The first statement is about what has become actual in a person, not about a connection that remains only as a possibility.[^V09-134]

A human being is the soul and body together. Though they differ in rank, they exist as one being with two ends: one changes and passes away like a branch, while the other remains like a root. As the soul becomes more complete, the body becomes finer and clearer, more closely joined to the soul, and more united with it. When the intellectual level is reached, the two become one without distinction.[^V09-135]

People generally think that, when the soul changes from its worldly state to its afterlife state, it leaves the body behind like someone taking off clothes. They think the body it managed and moved was the corpse left after death. This is wrong. The dead body is no longer the thing the soul manages directly.[^V09-136] It is like waste or sediment cast off by the body's processes, or like hair, wool, horns, and hooves that nature produces outside the living body's essential activity. It is like a house built to protect someone from heat and cold—useful in this world, but not itself alive.

The true body is the one in which sensation and life flow as part of its very being, not by accident. Its relation to the soul is like the relation of light to the sun.[^V09-137] When the soul reaches a higher level, it is like an object that has become sensed, then imagined, then understood. Abstraction does not mean that some features are removed and others kept. It means that the object's lower, darker existence changes into a higher, brighter, and more complete one. In the same way, the human being's passage from this world to the next is a change from one mode of existence to another.[^V09-138]

The soul does not become an intellect by losing some powers, such as sensation, while keeping others, such as reason. As its existence grows stronger and more complete, all its powers grow stronger and more complete with it. The higher it rises, the less divided it is and the more unified it becomes.[^V09-139]

Aristotle supports this account. He says that the first human being is sensitive, but in a higher and better way than the lower human being. The lower human being receives its senses from the human being in the higher intellectual world. The higher one does not receive anything from the lower; instead, the lower depends on the higher.

The senses and the things perceived in the higher world are not bodies. Nor does the higher human being see and sense in the same way we do. Its senses are stronger and reach more things because they perceive what is nobler, clearer, and more complete. Its sight understands what many beings share, while our bodily sight sees individual things. Our sight is weaker because it sees lesser things: bodily images of those higher realities. As Aristotle says, “These senses are weak intellects, and those intellects are strong senses.”[^V09-140]

Aristotle also says that if the soul contains the forms of active causes, the human soul contains the sources of life and speech. A soul that exists in this way before entering a body is already a human being there.[^V09-141] When it enters a body, it becomes the image of another human being, as much as that body can receive. It is like a sculptor making a human figure from material, trying to make it resemble a real person. The figure is only an image. It does not have the true person's powers, life, movement, states, or abilities.[^V09-142]

In the same way, the bodily human being is an image of the first, true human being. But here the sculptor is the soul.[^V09-143] It makes the bodily human resemble the true one, though the body's human qualities are weak and faint. The true human being's powers and life are strong.[^V09-144] If you want to see the first, true human being, you must be good and virtuous, and have senses strong enough not to shrink from the bright lights shining upon them.

This is the human being Plato describes in the teaching of divine forms. He says there are two human beings: one that can be sensed and will perish, and one that is lasting and never disappears. But Aristotle adds to this account by saying that the true human being uses the body and acts through bodily tools. What this means is that the soul uses the body indirectly, through the animal soul, not as its first and direct means.[^V09-145] When the animal soul reaches the imaginal world, the rational soul follows it and gives it a higher, more noble life. The rational soul does not leave the intellectual world to descend; it remains connected to this life from above.[^V09-146]

Someone might object: if the soul in the higher world can sense, how can sensation belong to noble, higher beings when it also belongs to lower beings?

We answer that sensation in the higher, intellectual world is not like sensation in this lower world.[^V09-147] The higher being senses according to the kinds of things that exist there. Its sight is connected to the sight of the lower human being, as one fire is connected to another higher fire. The lower human being receives the power of sense through this connection. If there were bodies in the higher world like these bodies here, the higher soul would sense them too. The second human—the image of the first in the bodily world—senses bodies because it carries traces of the first human being's intellectual form.

#### Chapter 5 — Why some powers of the soul are more divided than others, and how they remain powers of one person as they change

As we have seen, beings differ in how complete they are. The argument from possibility shows that every level between the highest and lowest must be realized in the world.[^V09-148] We explained this when we described kinds of inanimate things, plants, and animals: they exist at different levels, and the highest level of one kind connects to the lowest level of the next.

Any whole made of joined parts, such as a human being, includes a higher part—the soul—and a lower part—the body's matter. There must be a fitting link between them, and between each part and that link.[^V09-149] The human being therefore has many powers. Some take things in; others move us to act. The powers of perception include intellect, inner judgment, imagination, and the senses. They differ in strength and in how much they depend on matter. Intellect is least tied to matter, followed by inner judgment, imagination, and the senses. The five senses also differ: touch is most tied to matter, then taste, smell, hearing, and sight.

Each of these eight kinds has its own range of strength and weakness. The powers that move us—anger, desire, and the powers of plant life—also differ in how much they depend on matter. The more a power depends on matter, the more it can be divided. The less it depends on matter, the less divided it is. The soul itself cannot be split in its immaterial nature, but through its connection to the body it can be described as divided.

The great philosopher says that the soul is divided only indirectly. When it is in a body, it is divided along with the body. We can speak of its thinking part as distinct from its animal part, or its desiring part from its angry part. The soul itself is not divided; the body it inhabits is. When we say that the soul is divided, we mean that it is present in each part of the body and is divided along with it. The body's organs show this: an organ can sense only because a power of the soul is present in it. Since the soul's power of sensation is present in all the sense organs, it is called divided along with them. Yet it is complete in every organ. It is not split as the organs are. It is divided only in the way just described.

These many powers are gathered together in the soul but spread throughout the body. They are spread out because each needs a suitable organ. An organ that serves sight, for example, cannot serve hearing in the same way. The bodily place suited to anger is not the one suited to desire.[^V09-150] Touch is the lowest and least refined power, close to the most basic qualities of matter, so many organs can serve it.

The philosopher says that each power has a particular place in the body. It does not need that place in order to exist; it needs it so that it can act through a part prepared for its work. The soul prepares an organ in the way needed for a power to act through it. Its powers differ according to the differences among the organs.[^V09-151] But the soul is not made up of separate powers. It is one and simple, and it continually gives powers to the body. Since the soul causes these powers, their qualities belong more properly to the soul than to the body, especially when they are qualities better suited to the soul.[^V09-152] The soul's powers must have places in the body. Otherwise, there would be no difference between being inside the body and being outside it. The body's movement and sensation would have nothing to do with the soul, and we could not explain how the soul acts through bodily organs.

How can powers whose existence keeps changing still belong, as one and the same individual, to one personal soul? The answer is that changing things remain one through a single, unified reality, as the body is held together by the soul and matter by its form. Matter and form make one being; so do soul and body. The soul completes the body, and a thing's completion is that thing in a fuller and stronger way. Each lower power, as it changes, keeps its identity through a higher power that remains stable and is its source. This continues up the scale until we reach something fixed in every way.

This also answers a problem about touch. Touch works only when the part of the body that touches is changed from its usual state by receiving a quality opposite to the quality it senses. It seems, then, that the first quality must disappear when the opposite one arrives. Since sensation depends on the sense's power, that power should disappear too. But perception requires the image perceived to remain.

The answer is that the lowest power of touch may disappear and be replaced by another like it. The power of perception above it gathers what came before: it uses the earlier state to perceive the later one and holds them together, even when the earlier state has passed away.[^V09-153]

The philosopher divides the soul's powers into two kinds. Some are divided along with the body, such as growth and desire, which extend throughout a plant or animal. A higher power gathers these divided powers together. A power that is spread through the body can therefore be undivided in the higher power above it. The bodily senses are spread among separate organs, but one inner sense gathers them together. It receives what the senses report and recognizes and compares their reports at once. This inner sense is not divided because it does not act through a bodily organ. Its greater freedom from matter allows it to take in and distinguish all the sensed things together.[^V09-154]

I say that this is a valuable insight. It helps explain many things: how bodies are raised in the afterlife;[^V09-155] how what we know remains connected to its causes;[^V09-156] and how a person remains the same through change from one moment to the next.[^V09-157] Avicenna could not grasp this, despite his quick mind and sharp understanding. When Bahmanyār asked whether the self could change, Avicenna replied, “I do not have to answer you, since I will not be held to account when you ask me.”

These subtle truths cannot be reached by memorizing rules and definitions alone. They require inner insight and direct spiritual experience. Such insight comes through spiritual practice and effort in solitude, with distance from other people and from worldly desires, ambitions, and false hopes.[^V09-158] Much of this philosopher's writing shows his deep insight, inner light, and closeness to God. He may have taken up worldly duties—governing, improving people's lives, and building cities—after completing his spiritual practice and perfecting himself, when no one concern distracted him from another. He may have wanted to unite worldly leadership with spiritual leadership, and to complete both forms of life.

He then taught people, helped them improve, and guided them toward the right path, seeking closeness to God. Avicenna did not take up worldly affairs in this way. It is striking that whenever his inquiry turned to the nature of existence itself, rather than general questions and rules, his understanding seemed to fail him. This happened in many places. For example, he rejected change in the very nature of a thing. He thought that if Zayd changed in his humanity, he would have to stop being human and become something else. Yet any change requires something to remain through it. He missed the different ways one nature can exist, some more complete than others, and how one person can pass through many levels of being. If existence were only an idea we drew from things, as many later thinkers believed, Avicenna would have been right. But existence is not merely an idea.

He also rejected Plato's forms that exist apart from matter. He rejected the idea that the knower and what is known can be one, and that the soul can be one with the Active Intellect. He could not accept the idea that basic matter longs for form, or that the forms of the elements can change into a single, balanced form. He could not prove that bodies are raised in the afterlife. He also believed that the heavens and stars had always existed as individual things, each with its own form, matter, size, shape, color, and light. He thought that their movements and positions had existed forever only as kinds, not as individual movements. He also believed that the matter of earthly things had always existed, though we have already shown that it began to exist.

Bahmanyār once asked Avicenna why some powers of the soul can perceive while others cannot, even though all belong to one self. Avicenna replied, “I have not worked this out.”[^V09-159] In his writing about the soul, Avicenna had already said that it thinks, imagines, senses, and moves. Bahmanyār also asked whether Avicenna could identify something that stayed the same in animals and plants. “If only I could,” Avicenna replied.[^V09-160] He was asked whether animals other than humans are aware of themselves. He said he would have to think about it.[^V09-161] Perhaps they are aware only of what they sense or imagine, not of themselves; perhaps they know themselves through bodily tools; or perhaps they have a shared kind of awareness among their inner images.[^V09-162]

I have explained earlier that animals with active imagination do not have material souls. They know themselves in a particular way, because their existence belongs to them and not to something else. Whatever exists in itself, rather than in another thing, is aware of itself. This does not mean animal souls are intellectual beings. Being free of matter does not always mean being an intellect.

A related question asks: if an animal power can know that it should flee from a wolf, or that something is dangerous, why can it not know general ideas? These ideas also have no size and cannot exist in a body.

Avicenna's answer was that fear and flight become intellectual only after a certain kind of separation from matter.[^V09-163] This does not answer the question. Fear, flight, desire, anger, love, and similar states are meanings that cannot be divided.[^V09-164] They are not things with size, shape, or position. How, then, can they exist in a body or in a power that itself depends on a body? Their condition before they are separated from matter is already like that of many things after they are separated. The right answer is that the power that knows these simple meanings must be an animal power that is not located in a body. It need not be an intellect.

Avicenna was also asked how a thing can become an intellect when universal forms come to be in it. This seemed strange: how could something that is not yet an intellect be made into one by receiving a form that makes it an intellect? Avicenna said that “becomes” did not mean it changed at that moment; it only showed that the thing was already an intellect.[^V09-165]

I say that Avicenna gave the word this strained meaning[^V09-166] because he believed that the human soul is an intellect free of matter from the moment it begins in the fourth month of pregnancy. But as I explained earlier, the soul does not begin that way. At first, imagination is active and intellect is only a possibility. Through repeated perception, the soul draws general ideas from particular images and moves from a possible intellect to an active one. Its very nature changes as it passes from imaginative power to intellectual power.[^V09-167] The many statements by ancient philosophers on this subject are clear and cannot be dismissed as metaphor.

In one of his letters to a student, Avicenna writes:

“I have thought deeply about this problem. It is new to me. I have answered some parts with a convincing argument and others only by hint. I may have failed to answer some. A stable principle in animals is easier to explain, and I have explored it at length in the Eastern studies, first by raising doubts and then by seeking insight. But the case of plants is harder.

“If nothing stable remains in a plant, then its identity is not a kind but an individual. But how can it remain the same individual if its change can be divided endlessly in thought, with no one division more basic than another? How can infinitely many changes happen in a limited time?

“Perhaps the matter is what remains. But how can matter stay the same when its size keeps changing? Growth does not happen in one matter alone; new matter enters through nourishment. Perhaps one plant form[^V09-168] can take in and wear more than one portion of matter. But how can one form belong to just one portion of matter?

“Perhaps one form stays with one portion of matter[^V09-169] for the whole life of the individual. But how can that be, when the parts of a growing thing increase alike? Each similar part becomes larger than before. The power is present everywhere in the body, so no one part has a stronger claim than another to be the original form.

“Perhaps the earliest power is the original one[^V09-170] that remains. But its relation to the earlier stage is like the relation of a later stage to the next. Perhaps a plant is not really one individual in number: each part added at one time is a new individual, joined to the first. Or perhaps the first part is the source from which a second, similar one comes. When the first disappears, the second then disappears too. Perhaps this applies to animals, or to most animals, but not to plants, since they cannot divide into parts that can live on their own.

“Or perhaps an animal or plant has a root that is not mixed with its body, though the body is mixed. But this would go against what I believe. Perhaps what seems alike to the senses[^V09-171] is not alike in reality. Or perhaps a plant is never one individual, except during the pause in growth that must occur at times. These are puzzles and traps. If the mind circles around them, branches into them, and examines their different sides, I hope it will find a way out, by God's help, toward the truth.”

I say that these alternatives show that Avicenna could not explain how a plant—or even an animal—can grow in size and still remain the same individual. He could not identify something stable in them through change, because he thought their souls were part of their bodily matter. But it is striking that one of his possible answers was the truth: an animal or plant has a source that is not mixed with its body. This is certain for animals, since they have a soul distinct from the body. It is also true of plants, whose changing powers depend on a stable power above them.[^V09-172]

We have also shown that an indefinite amount of matter is included in the identity of a natural being with a complete form.[^V09-173] Because matter enters this identity only in an undefined way, changes in the body's size do not break the person's identity.[^V09-174]

Avicenna also struggled with whether simple human souls survive the body. Since he could not prove that imagination is free of matter, he sometimes said that such souls cease to exist, although he accepted that a nonphysical substance does not perish when a body does. At other times, he said they survive by knowing a few first principles and general truths. But anyone with a firm grounding in philosophy knows that the next life is a life of knowledge. Rational souls have their being through understanding intellectual realities. A thing cannot exist as a mere general idea: it must have its own definite reality. What happiness[^V09-175] could the soul find in grasping only the idea of a thing or of possibility, whether taken in its broad or narrow sense, or in knowing that a whole is greater than its part and that things equal to the same thing are equal to one another? In this world, nothing exists merely as “substance in general” or “quality in general.” It must be a definite kind with its own form. The same is true in the world of intellect:[^V09-176] nothing can exist there merely as “some thing,” “some possibility,” or “some substance” without becoming a distinct intellectual reality.

Avicenna claimed that celestial souls have no further perfection to reach except an easier goal: gaining spatial relations to their bodies.[^V09-177] To a thoughtful observer, this is a weak and mistaken view. As long as the soul is incomplete, it needs a body as a tool to reach its perfection. It uses and moves the body so that it can become more complete in its very being, not to gain some extra relation outside itself. How could a clear-thinking person believe that an intellectual soul is trapped in a body and has left the world of light for the world of darkness just to gain spatial relations? Avicenna himself says that higher beings do not turn their attention toward lower ones.

Avicenna rejected the idea that a thing can change in its very nature. Yet he agreed that when the soul is complete and free of the body, it becomes an intellect and is no longer called a soul. He did not see that being a soul is not an added relation, like being a king or a ship's captain. The soul's very mode of existence makes it a soul. When its existence grows stronger and it becomes an intellect, its mode of existence changes. This is precisely a deep change in what it is—the kind of change he rejected.

He also failed to understand the simple intellect.[^V09-178] He said it means knowing many ideas all at once, without moving from one idea to another in time, but in an order of causes and effects.[^V09-179] He quoted himself in *Notes*:

“Simple intellect means grasping all ideas at once, in their proper order and through their causes, without moving from one idea to another as the soul does when it learns them one by one. It knows each thing together with the causes that lead to it. If we say that the First has intellect in this simple sense, we mean that it knows things through their causes and sources, which are present to it because things come from it.
