We do not mean that the ideas of things are parts of its essence. Rather, the ideas flow from it, and it is more properly called intellect than the ideas that flow from its act of knowing.

“Simple ideas are present to it all at once in their order: each is related to the ones before and after it, and to the First. Here is an example. You read a book, and someone asks whether you know what it contains. You say yes because you know its contents and can explain them in detail. This is what simple intellect means.[^V09-180] There is no human intellect like this, except perhaps that of a prophet. Intellectual knowledge is not laid out in time; knowledge through the soul is.”

Avicenna repeated this account in *Notes*, but he did not go beyond the idea that our knowledge comes in a sequence in time, while the First's knowledge follows the order of causes and effects. He did not understand that a simple intellect can contain every intelligible without being divided.[^V09-181]

He also denied that separate intellectual forms could be God's detailed knowledge of things. He thought these forms were separate from God and had a different nature. If so, how could they be necessary to God? And if they were not necessary, something else would have to come before them and produce them, leading to an endless chain.[^V09-182] He therefore treated God's knowledge as qualities that exist within Him, though he said that they neither affect Him nor make Him more complete.

You have already seen the error in this view. The intellectual forms are not separate beings that exist apart from God.[^V09-183] These and similar mistakes come from overlooking what existence is and how it works, and from spending time on subjects that are not needed for this path: grammar, detailed arithmetic, music, the many treatments used in medicine, lists of simple drugs and mixtures, antidotes, poisons, ointments, laxatives, and the treatment of sores and wounds. God made different people for different fields; the person seeking God should not lose themselves in all these details.

When Socrates was asked why he turned away from the practical sciences, he replied, “I am occupied with the noblest science”—meaning the knowledge of God. In short, for someone with a clear mind, a gentle nature, sharp intelligence, sound judgment, and strong ability, the key is to avoid worldly concerns and the pursuit of status. They must turn from people, seek solitude, find ease in God, and place their attention and thought on divine realities. First they need some knowledge of language, logic, nature, and character—enough for those who seek God through study, though not for those drawn to Him from the start by a divine pull as strong as the efforts of all people together. Without either path, how could someone reach inner insight into God, the beginning and end of all things, the soul, and its journey to God while caught up in worldly ties?

Avicenna says in a letter to a student who asked him to rewrite a book that had been lost during one of his journeys:

“Who is this person forced to begin again, to move from falsehood to truth, from this world to the next, from empty details to what matters? Fate has caught me in its claws. I do not know how to escape. I have been given tasks that do not suit me. I have lost touch with knowledge; I can only glimpse it through a thick curtain. Still, I thank God: through changing conditions, great trials, and repeated journeys, He never leaves me without a flash of light that gives my heart life and steadies my steps. I praise Him for what helps and harms, what grieves and delights.”[^V09-184]

I say: Even if these duties had been forced on Avicenna, without his choosing them, who wrote so much about particular sciences and crafts? Did his deep immersion in them not keep him from turning toward God, staying close to Him, and seeking greater light and knowledge from Him?

### Book Ten — The spiritual afterlife, intellectual happiness and misery, and other forms of happiness and misery

#### Chapter 1 — What true happiness is

Existence is good and a source of happiness. Being aware of existence is also good and brings happiness.[^V09-185] But beings differ in how complete they are. The more complete a being's existence, the less it is marked by absence and limitation, and the greater its happiness. The less complete its existence, the more evil and misery are mixed into it. The First Reality, God, is the most complete and noble of all. Below God are the intellects free of bodies, then souls. Lower still are natural powers and bodily forms. At the lowest level are basic matter, time, and motion.

Each thing finds pleasure in existing. It would find even greater pleasure in existing through its own cause and what completes it, since that is the source of its being. The more complete a being is, the greater its happiness, which comes from awareness of its existence. The intellectual powers exist at a higher level than the animal powers of desire and anger, so their happiness, pleasure, and love are greater. When our souls become complete and strong, break free of their ties to the body, and return to their true selves and their Creator, their joy will be beyond description and beyond comparison with bodily pleasures.

The causes of pleasure are awareness, the thing perceived, and the power of perception.[^V09-186] Awareness of a thing is stronger when the perceiving power is stronger, and when the thing perceived is more complete. The intellect is stronger than the senses, and its objects are more complete. It reaches the true nature of what it understands and attains pure light, good, and being—the source of all that exists and of all goodness. The intellectual beings that follow God are also beautiful and beloved in themselves, whether anyone else loves them or not.

Sensory perception, by contrast, comes through powers that are weaker and less complete. It reaches only the outer parts of things, not their full nature. The senses perceive food, touch, smells, colors, and similar things. Intellect can know all things; the senses know only some things, and only those that can be sensed. Not every sensed thing is pleasant, but everything understood by the mind is suited to the intellect and completes it. Sensible things can conflict with one another because they are limited and can be harmed. Intellectual beings have room to exist without crowding one another.

When the intellect understands an intellectual form, it becomes more complete through that form. In a sense, the form becomes part of what the intellect is. Some of these forms were already present as part of the intellect's nature, but it had not noticed them because it was occupied elsewhere.[^V09-187] When it turns inward and becomes aware of them, it sees beauty and splendor in itself. As the First Teacher says: “I went within myself and left all other things behind. There I found a beauty and splendor that words cannot describe.”

This pleasure resembles the joy the First Reality has in itself, and the joy of those close to God in themselves and in their source. The angels' joy in their spiritual awareness is greater than a donkey's joy in mating or eating. While we are tied to our bodies, however, we do not feel or desire these higher pleasures as we feel bodily ones. We learn about them through proof. We know the beloved intellectual beings only through general ideas that apply to them, but an idea of a thing is not its actual existence. The idea of sweetness is not sweet. Knowing what kingship is does not make you a king.

Reason and proof tell us that these intellectual pleasures exist, and that knowing them in this life prepares us to experience them in the next. Knowledge is the seed of direct vision.[^V09-188] What blocks us from intellectual beings is either that we have not learned about them, or that we are too busy with the body, material things, and the powers tied to them.[^V09-189] The soul can approach these beings by learning what they are. As its knowledge becomes firmer, it becomes ready to receive their forms from the intellect that does not depend on a body. When the soul is separated from the body at death, it no longer turns toward bodily concerns. Its knowledge then gives it access to what it knows, and it takes pleasure in it.

Bodily perception is different. Seeing a pleasant object does not always bring pleasure, because a physical body may be blocked from reaching it. The parts of a body are separate, and the body is not fully present to itself or to anything else. The qualities and states that depend on a body are limited in the same way. A body, simply by being a body, is not alive or aware. A living body is alive because something else in it—the animal soul—is alive in itself. If bodily existence itself were life, every body would be alive. A being that is not a body, even if connected to a body, can have life simply by existing. This is true of God, the intellects, and the souls of the celestial bodies, humans, and animals. Life is not something added to existence; life is existence itself. The same holds for place and relation in a body, and for light in immaterial beings.

The point of this discussion is that bodily existence always carries death, distraction, separation, and loss, whether on the side of the perceiver or the thing perceived. The more a being depends on a body, the less present it is to itself and to other things, and the weaker its awareness becomes. We were more aware of ourselves before we became attached to our bodies. Most people, lost in their material bodies and concerns, forget themselves—as God says, “They forgot God, so He made them forget themselves.” Our sense of self while we are tied to the body is mixed with our awareness of it[^V09-190], because soul and body are joined like light and shadow, flame and smoke, or a person and their reflection.

When the soul's tie to the body ends, it sees intellectual forms directly. Awareness becomes presence, knowledge becomes direct sight, and perception becomes inward vision. The pleasure of intellectual life is greater than any other good or happiness. As we have seen, true pleasure comes from existence, especially intellectual existence, which is free from the trace of nonexistence. The truest beloved and fullest perfection is God, who is existence itself and contains every kind of being. Joy in God is the greatest joy and rest: it is rest without pain.

#### Chapter 1 — How the soul reaches this happiness, and why it is kept from it in this world

The soul reaches this joy and happiness through actions that purify it, clear away its inner stains, and polish the mirror of the heart. It also reaches them through thought and study that help it understand the forms and natures of things. When the soul has learned what things are and its intellect has become active, it no longer needs the senses, body, or bodily powers. Yet the body and its powers still distract it and keep it from full union with the intellect and from reaching its source.[^V09-191]

When the body's demands, the whispering of inner judgment, and the play of imagination fall away, the barrier lifts and union continues. The soul remains; the active source remains; its giving is constant; and the soul is ready to receive it. Both barriers—the inner one and the outer one—must be removed. The inner barrier is the soul's weakness and potential state, and its deep entanglement with the body. It is removed when the soul becomes actual in its own intellectual nature and clearly distinguishes itself from the body and its powers. The outer barrier is the body and its senses, like a cover around a polished mirror. It is removed when the soul leaves the body at death.

The body and senses are needed at first. Through them, the soul gains sound images and learns to draw general meanings from them, waking to its own world, its source, and its return. At the beginning of life, the soul can recognize truths only through the senses. That is why people say, “Whoever loses a sense loses knowledge.” The senses help at first but hold us back at the end, like a fishing net or a mount that carries us to our destination. Once we arrive, the very thing that helped us can become a distraction.

Each power is happy when it gets what suits its nature, without an obstacle, and reaches its full strength. Desire is fulfilled when it gets what it wants; anger when it wins or takes revenge; inner judgment when it hopes and imagines what might be. Each sense is fulfilled by the quality it can perceive. Touch perceives a balanced bodily state; taste perceives flavors suited to the body, such as sweetness and richness; smell takes in pleasant scents; sight, light and color; hearing, harmonious sounds and pleasing melodies. Imagination forms pleasing images, either from sense images or from intellectual meanings presented through images. The intellect, in its own nature, reaches pure intellectual realities and receives the divine forms and the order of all that exists, from the First Reality down to the lowest beings.

The soul's happiness through its link to the body and its life in this world comes from justice. It must act in a balanced way between opposing traits—between what it wants and does not want, and between anger and restraint—so that it is not controlled by the body and its powers.[^V09-192] While the soul remains in this world, it cannot be entirely free of the body's influence. But balance makes it act as if it were free of that influence. It does not give in to the body's powers. Instead, it rises above them, directs them, and makes them obey. Giving in to the body and being shaped by its powers brings misery and blocks happiness. At first, the soul and body affect one another. Sometimes the soul overcomes the body, with God's help. At other times, the soul gives in to bodily powers, led astray by Satan through the tricks of inner judgment and the body's desires. If the soul gives in again and again, it becomes deeply shaped by the body and forms a bad habit. Then it is harder to resist the body's harms than it was before.

If the soul repeatedly restrains the body and rises above its demands, it gains inner strength and finds it easier to do what once was hard. This strength comes from acting with balance in the ways the body requires while we live in it, without excess or neglect. A person becomes weak and submissive by going to extremes. Balanced actions, by contrast, leave the soul free from bodily control. They are like water that is neither hot nor cold. This inner strength suits the soul's nature. The soul is naturally drawn to being independent and free of matter; obedience to bodily desires is foreign to its rational nature. The soul's true happiness and completion lie in independent, immaterial existence, in knowing things as they are, and in seeing intellectual realities and beings of light. These pleasures cannot be compared with the dim, passing pleasures of the senses and bodily desires.

Why do we fail to enjoy the knowledge we already have? It is like an illness that dulls the sense of taste. The person cannot enjoy sweet or tasty things that are there for them.[^V09-193] If the mind could hold the truths proper to it while free from bodily distractions and the pull of the senses, its pleasure would be beyond description. Those who know the truth do not feel a strong desire for these pleasures now because they do not yet fully taste them. True pleasure comes from the actual presence of something suited to us, not from an idea or a thought of it. What we have in the soul now is only a weak mental form of intellectual knowledge. Its weakness may come from bodily distraction, or from the weakness of the knowledge itself, such as knowledge based on guesswork rather than proof. But the realities themselves are strong, clear, and bright.

If the soul's knowledge is weak only because the body clouds it—not because the soul has failed to understand or lacks sound proof—then once the body's veil is removed, that knowledge can lead the soul to see the beings brought close to God and be with them. Full knowledge in this life is the seed of full vision in the next. A soul that has gained true knowledge and is free of bad traits may also take on the pleasant forms promised in revelation—not as empty daydreams, but as forms shaped by its good actions and character.[^V09-194] This is the paradise of those who live well without reaching full perfection, and the happiness of the righteous. The paradise of the perfected and those brought near to God is of a higher kind. In short, joy follows vision. The less clearly we see, the less joy we feel. This is also true of the happiness we know about but cannot yet see for ourselves.

We can tell that higher happiness is real from the joy we feel when we remember God, reflect on His qualities and acts, and understand the order of existence as the best and most complete order. This points to a happiness we can reach when bodily concerns fall away. Our happiness will be greater still if we understand these questions fully, as those firmly grounded in knowledge do.

At a minimum, to deserve this true happiness[^V09-195], a person should learn about the highest source of being, the kind of existence that belongs to it[^V09-196], and how it knows all things without being changed by them.[^V09-197] A person should learn how it can do all that can be done without changing or becoming many, how it cares for all things, and how its mercy and generosity reach them. Its true qualities do not add to its being, though its relations to things do. Names such as the All-Powerful, the All-Knowing, the Provider, and the Creator are different ways of speaking about one simple relation. Just as all God's true qualities refer to the fullness of existence, all these other names refer to His being the complete source of all that exists.

A person should also know the intellects that bring other beings into existence,[^V09-198] God's complete and everlasting words, and His angels of knowledge; and the universal souls, which are His books and angels of action. They should understand the order by which existence flows from God to intellect, then to souls and natural powers, down to matter and bodies; and how existence rises again through minerals, plants, and animals to the acquired intellect, returning to where it began. Whoever gains this knowledge has won a great victory and a large share of happiness. They escape the misery that comes from turning away from knowledge despite having the ability and readiness to learn, from turning away from wisdom while wanting to gain knowledge, or from denying these truths out of arrogance and stubbornness. The more complete this knowledge becomes, the greater the person's happiness.[^V09-199] I know people who have studied this field so deeply that they understand aspects of existence beyond the reach of even sharp minds, and unlike anything written by earlier or later philosophers. Praise and thanks be to God.

We have seen that true nobility and happiness come to the soul through its theoretical side, which is the root of its being. What comes from its practical side, which is tied to the body and its needs, brings safety from hardship, purification from faults and stains, clarity instead of weakness and vice, and freedom from punishment and painful torment. By itself, though, this does not bring the soul true nobility or intellectual joy. Still, the righteous and those who live simply have a kind of happiness suited to them, as we will explain later.

#### Chapter 3 — Misery opposed to true happiness

This kind of misery does not belong to animal souls, to souls that remain simple, or to ordinary people who have not yet developed a desire to learn the intellectual and theological sciences or a love of status and leadership. Most people who earn their living through crafts, ordinary people, women, and children fall into these groups. If they suffer, their suffering is of another kind; if they are happy, their happiness is of another kind too. Those who suffer fall into two groups. The first are those who were destined to go astray and against whom the decree was set from eternity. They belong to darkness, worldly life, and a veil that seals their hearts by nature. God says, “I will surely fill Hell with jinn and people,” and, “We have created many jinn and people for Hell. They have hearts with which they do not understand.”[^V09-200]

The second group are hypocrites. They were originally able to receive the light of knowledge, but they strayed from the path.[^V09-201] Their hearts grew veiled by stains they acquired through bad habits, wrongdoing, animal desires, and cruel impulses. They kept turning to the devil's tricks until dark ways of acting took root in them. Their minds filled with false arguments, mistaken ideas, empty images, and lies. They were left doubtful, lost, and turned away from the truth. Their actions came to nothing, and their souls were bound in painful misery, chained to matter and lost in the darkness of worldly desire. They had lost the powers and tools that once let them pursue these desires, and now they were stung by the scorpions and snakes of their own bad character. They would remain there as long as the heavens and earth endure. The call of truth had reached them, but they ignored it, went astray, turned away, and denied it. God's anger fell on them. It was said of them:

“Whoever turns away from My reminder will have a cramped life, and We will raise him blind on the Day of Resurrection. He will say, ‘My Lord, why have You raised me blind when I could see?’ He will say, ‘This is how Our signs came to you, but you forgot them; so today you are forgotten.’”[^V09-202]

One of their greatest pains is that they are kept from their Lord. Their hearts are covered by what they did, their sins surround them, and they remain in the lowest part of the Fire. These people are in a worse state than the first group: the first are veiled from the truth, while the second are punished.[^V09-203] The Qur'an speaks often of both groups and of each one separately. One example appears at the start of the chapter of the Cow. The first group is described in the words, “Those who disbelieve—it is the same to them whether you warn them or not,” through to, “They will have a terrible punishment.” They have no way out of the Fire; they are truly its companions.[^V09-204] Their nature has been strengthened by repeated sin and desire. This is what is meant by, “Your Lord's word has come true against those who rebel: they will not believe,” and, “Your Lord's word has come true against those who disbelieve: they are the companions of the Fire.”[^V09-205]

They have been shut off from the path and every door has been closed to them. The heart—the power of reason—is the inner sense where inspiration and guidance are received. Their hearts have been sealed, so they are cut off from it. The senses, especially hearing and sight, are two great doors to understanding and reflection. But these too have lost their use, because meaning can no longer reach the heart through them. They have no way to reach true knowledge inwardly.

Nor can they gain the outward, conventional kinds of knowledge.[^V09-206] The Qur'an says, “We have created many jinn and people for Hell. They have hearts with which they do not understand.” They are imprisoned in darkness. How great their pain is. The second group is described in the words, “Some people say, ‘We believe in God and the Last Day,’” through to, “They will have a terrible punishment.” Their hearts are sick and their souls suffer from resentment, envy, pride, hypocrisy, hatred, foolishness, and showing off. They are given to scheming, betrayal, deceit, discord, and manipulation. They may also hold false views and beliefs. God protect us: they may memorize religious teachings and formal knowledge, then use them to gain status and power, claiming to believe in God and the Last Day though faith has never entered their hearts. Whether or not these inner sicknesses involve false beliefs, they all cause pain to the soul and torment to the heart. False beliefs and corrupt knowledge cause even greater torment. They burn within the people who hold them, and their pain is worse than that of the fire of this world. God says, “It is God's kindled fire, which rises over the hearts; it will close in on them, in towering columns.” These beliefs are painful in themselves, and they also lead to other sins that torment the soul for a time. Both kinds are forms of intellectual misery.[^V09-207] The bodily forms of happiness and misery will be explained later,[^V09-208] God willing.

To sum up, afterlife misery comes either from bodily habits formed by sins of desire and anger, or from inner habits such as envy, showing off, denying the truth, and rejecting true knowledge. The first kind forms when repeated surrender to bodily impulses becomes fixed. It keeps the soul from reaching its happiness in the afterlife and blocks it from its reward. It also causes pain because the soul loses the familiar things it longed for and has no distraction to keep it from remembering them. While the soul is turned toward the body, this attention keeps it from feeling how ugly these habits are and how much they oppose its true nature. In the afterlife, when that distraction ends, the soul feels their pain. God says, “We have lifted your veil, and today your sight is sharp.” When the veil is lifted at death, the soul feels great pain, like someone who has a painful illness but is too distracted to notice it; once the distraction is gone, the full pain becomes clear. These habits are foreign to the soul's true nature, as are the hellish forms that grow from them. So, God willing, they may fade over time, in a period that depends on how many obstacles remain. This may be what the true religion points to when it says that a sinful believer will not remain in the Fire forever.[^V09-209]

The second kind of intellectual misery is a deep, painful disease.[^V09-210] A person may be able to learn and long for intellectual knowledge and completion, yet fail to make the effort needed to develop the intellect. The soul then loses its natural readiness and takes on twisted habits and false ideas that oppose reality. It speaks from stubborn loyalty to a view and denies what is true. This is a disease that even the doctors of the soul struggle to cure. What is impossible cannot be achieved. The pain it causes is the opposite of the pleasure that comes from intellectual completion. That pleasure is greater than any bodily pleasure; this pain is worse than any bodily pain, whether burning in fire, freezing in bitter cold, beheading, or losing a limb. People in this world may not feel it for the reason already explained: they do not feel its opposite pleasure either. Children do not understand the pleasures and pains known to mature adults. They may laugh at adults, enjoy what is not truly pleasant, and dislike what adults enjoy. The immature in understanding are like children in this respect.

#### Chapter 4 — Why some souls lack intellectual knowledge and miss happiness in the afterlife

The part of a person that receives knowledge is a subtle power that guides the body's organs and puts its senses and abilities to work. By its nature, it can receive every kind of knowledge, just as a mirror can reflect any color or visible thing. But five things can prevent it from receiving these forms of knowledge, as one scholar explains through the example of a mirror.

The first is that the soul is not yet developed enough. A child's soul cannot clearly receive knowledge because it is still weak and only has the potential to know. This is like an iron mirror before it has been melted, shaped, and polished.

The second is that the soul has become cloudy and dark. Desires and the layers built up through repeated sin block the heart's clarity and keep truth from appearing in it. This is like rust or dirt on a mirror. Every action leaves a mark on the soul. Actions driven by desire or anger hold it back from the perfection it could reach; acts of thought help it grow. Every time the soul is caught up in bodily, worldly concerns, it is like a dark spot on a mirror. When these spots gather, they distort the mirror and keep it from reflecting what it was made to show.

The third is that the soul is turned away from the truth it needs to see. The souls of the righteous and obedient may be free from the dirt of wrongdoing and from deceit, yet still fail to see the truth clearly because they are not seeking it in depth.[^V09-211] They have turned away from the path to the spiritual world—the careful study of true knowledge, reflection on God's signs, and awareness of His presence. Instead, they may focus all their effort on bodily acts, religious routines, and set prayers, without thinking about their meaning or purpose.[^V09-212] They see only what they have set out to see: how to perform acts of worship correctly and avoid mistakes in prayer and other practices.

If a person's fixed attention on good deeds can prevent a clear view of truth, then worldly desires and attachments will do so even more. In the mirror example, this is like turning the mirror away from the image. The fourth obstacle is a curtain placed between the soul and the truth. A person may control desire, seek knowledge, and think carefully, yet still fail to see what they seek because an accepted belief, learned in childhood and held on trust, blocks the truth from view.[^V09-213] This is a major barrier for many theologians, sectarian loyalists, and even people known for learning and piety. Beliefs accepted without examination can take such a firm hold that they block the way to truth. In the mirror example, this is like a curtain placed between the mirror and the image.

The fifth obstacle is not knowing which way to look for the truth. A learner cannot find the answer by any path at all. They need to recall what they already know and put the right ideas together in the right order. When they do this in the way used by careful thinkers, they find the right path and the answer becomes clear. The knowledge needed for happiness in the afterlife is not inborn. We reach it through earlier knowledge. Any new piece of knowledge depends on two earlier pieces being joined in a particular way, much as a child comes from the union of male and female. Every possible being has its own cause, and nothing that depends on a cause can come into being without it. In the same way, we can know a thing only by learning its causes. Not knowing the first principles or how to put them together blocks us from knowledge.[^V09-214]

In the mirror example, this is like not standing at the right angle to see the image. If someone wants to see the back of their head in a mirror, they need two mirrors. One is placed behind them and the other in front, so the first reflection appears in the second mirror. The mirrors must be set at just the right angle. The paths to knowledge are even more subtle. Few people on earth know how to follow them. These are the obstacles that keep the rational soul from knowing what things are. By its original nature, every soul is able to know the truth, because it is a noble, God-given reality, different from all other things in this world. The Prophet is reported to have said, “If devils did not circle around the hearts of the children of Adam, they would see the kingdom of the heavens.” This refers to the soul's natural capacity and to the barriers that block it from the spiritual world.

When those barriers are lifted from the heart—the rational soul—the forms of the spiritual and material worlds appear within it. The soul then sees itself in a paradise as wide as the heavens and the earth, or even wider. The visible world is limited, but the spiritual world and its intellectual truths have no end. The visible and spiritual worlds together are called the Divine Presence, because God encompasses all that exists. What appears to the heart is, for some, paradise itself; for others, it is what earns them paradise. The breadth of their paradise depends on the breadth of their knowledge and on how much of God, His qualities, and His acts they come to see.[^V09-215]

All acts of worship and bodily practice aim to clear the heart, purify the soul, and improve its practical side. “Successful is the one who purifies it; lost is the one who corrupts it.” Purity alone is not true perfection, since it is the absence of something bad. The true aim is the light of faith: the knowledge of God, His acts, His books, His messengers, and the Last Day. God says, “When God wills to guide someone, He opens their heart to Islam, and they are in a light from their Lord.” An open heart is the goal of practical wisdom; light is the goal of theoretical wisdom. The true philosopher joins both. In the language of religion, such a person is a true believer. This is the great success.

#### Chapter 5 — How the Active Intellect becomes present in our souls

The Active Intellect exists in itself, and it also exists in our souls. The soul reaches its full development when the Active Intellect is present within it, connected to it, and united with it. Nothing can be the natural goal of another thing unless the two can become one. What is a thing's natural goal at one time can become its form at another, unless something blocks its way. We explained this union in the general discussion of knowledge and the relationship between intellect and what it understands. That account is enough for those who can grasp it through insight and experience, though it will not help those who deny it. If someone asks how one thing can be both a cause that comes before another thing and a goal that follows it, they should return to the argument already given and follow the same path.[^V09-216]

We showed by proof and direct insight that the highest source is both the first and the last. Its being extends everywhere; its unity gathers everything; its light shines with full strength. All individual beings flow from it, and all that exists comes from it. Its complete being is the source of things and their goal and completion. It is the root and fruit of the tree of existence. Its unity is unlike other unities, which result when separate things are counted. Nothing is equal to its unity; nothing is like its existence. Its pure oneness gathers many things, runs through all unities, and its light reaches every being. As Imam Ali said, “He is with everything, but not by being joined to it; He is other than everything, but not by being apart from it.” Its existence as the source of all things does not rule out its also being their goal. All beings descend from it at the beginning and return to it in the end.

The same is true, in their own way, of the beings of the sustaining lights and the separate divine forms. The Active Intellects are like rays of God's existence, and their unity reflects His unity. Their reach and completeness reflect His, though in a lesser way. They are the means by which things flow from God and return to Him. They are first in relation to the beings that come from them and last as the goal toward which those beings return. They exist through God's existence and remain through His permanence, like rays of His light and necessary expressions of His being.[^V09-217]

The Active Intellect's existence in itself is one question; its presence in our souls is another. We have already shown that it exists in itself. Now we ask how it completes the human soul. At first, the soul is already a soul, but its intellect is only a possibility. By understanding intellectual forms and learning to notice the ideas that follow from them, it can become an active intellect. It can then bring to mind what it knows whenever it wishes, without having to begin the work of learning all over again. It has moved from a possible intellect to an actual one.

Whenever a capacity becomes real, something already capable of acting must bring about that change. If the cause were something nonintellectual, such as a body or a bodily power, then something lower in being would cause something higher. That would mean something other than intellect could produce intellect, which is impossible. If the cause is itself an intellect, then either it is an intellect by its original nature, or it too needs something else to make it actual. If so, we must ask the same question about that cause, leading to an endless chain. So there must be an intellect that is actual from the beginning: the Active Intellect, free of possibility and the weakness that comes with it.

There is another way to show that it exists. A person may know something but not have it in mind at every moment. The knowledge is stored, and the person has the power to recall it. So the power to understand and the power to preserve knowledge are different: one receives knowledge, while the other keeps it. Since the soul receives, the source that preserves what it knows must be a higher being. This kind of being is the Active Intellect. It is clear that it cannot be a body or a power in a body, since neither can understand or be understood as an intellect does.[^V09-218]

Bodies affect other things through their position and contact. In spiritual beings, the matching relation is not physical nearness but a connection of meaning and intellect. God is the source of existence and gives it to every being able to receive it. But each kind of created thing also needs a fitting intermediary: a separate intellectual form or being. These are the close angels, called “lords of kinds” by the ancient philosophers, Platonic forms by the Platonists, and divine forms in this account. They are God's detailed knowledge of things, through which things beyond Him come to be. The intermediary most suited to completing human souls is their holy intellectual guide, called Gabriel and the Spirit of Holiness in religious teaching. In Persian, this being was called the Life-Giver. Several Qur'anic verses say that people and prophets receive knowledge from a powerful angel or from the All-Knowing through an angel.[^V09-219]

Sense images come to the imagination through the senses. From these images, the soul draws general meanings by noticing what things share and how they differ. At first, these meanings are vague and faint, like objects in a dark room. But as the soul grows ready through purification, freedom from inner stains, repeated perception, and careful thought, the Active Intellect's light shines on the soul and its imagined images. It makes the soul an actual intellect and its imagined ideas actual objects of thought.

The Active Intellect acts on the soul and its images as the sun acts on a healthy eye and on the things before it. Before the sun rises, the eye can see only in potential, and the things around it can be seen only in potential. When sunlight reaches the eye, the eye actually sees and the things before it become visible. In the same way, when this sacred light reaches the soul and its imagined ideas, the soul becomes an actual intellect, able to think, and its imagined ideas become actual objects of thought. The intellect then distinguishes what is essential from what is accidental, what is real from what merely accompanies it, and what is basic from what follows from it. It understands these things apart from their numbers and individual instances. The human being then becomes an intellectual human being: its existence is no longer confined to separate bodily parts and attachments.

As the light that forms the soul grows stronger, the soul unites with the universal human spirit, called the Spirit of Holiness and the Active Intellect. This source is wide enough to include all human beings, just as a universal nature can be said of many individuals. But the universal nature is only a general idea of those individuals,[^V09-220] while this source is their real foundation: it contains them and keeps them. General natures, too, are signs of real, luminous beings and sacred intellectual forms. In short, when the soul becomes intellect, its sense images become actual objects of thought[^V09-221] and are themselves known, for everything that is actually understood is also an actual knower.

#### Chapter 6 — The Anqa, the spiritual being of the mystics, described through symbols

The Anqa is a real being to the mystics. They are as certain of its existence as they are of whiteness.[^V09-222] It is a sacred bird, and its home is Mount Qaf.[^V09-223] Its whistle wakes those asleep in the beds of darkness. Its call stirs those who have forgotten God's signs, though few listen closely or recognize its echo.[^V09-224] Many have shut themselves off from hearing and turned away from their Lord's signs. They are deaf, mute, and blind, so they do not understand. The Anqa is with all creation, though creation is not with it. As the Persian poet says:

You are with us, yet not one of us;
you are beyond us, yet never apart.

Anyone suffering from dropsy or an illness of black bile may drink from its shade.[^V09-225] Those suffering from melancholy also benefit from hearing its call. Its wings and feathers have qualities that can heal many different illnesses and diseases.[^V09-226] But there is one deadly illness it cannot heal: deep, stubborn ignorance. It can fly up and down without moving, and come and go without any change or journey. It can be near or far without distance or place. Colors appear through it, though it has no color; the same is true of tastes and smells. Whoever understands its language understands the languages of all birds and knows every truth and secret.[^V09-227][^V09-228]

Not an atom in the far west is without it. All things are occupied with it, while it is free of them all. Every place is full of it, yet it has no place. The sciences and crafts come from its call; pleasing melodies, remarkable songs, and instruments of music draw their principles from this sacred bird, blessed in its nature and name. The Persian poet says:

If you have never seen Solomon,
how can you know the language of birds?

Whoever protects themselves with one of its feathers may pass through fire without burning and cross water without drowning.[^V09-229] Through the support of this sacred bird, all beings reach the forms of perfection suited to them, and those who travel the path reach their aims and needs.

#### Chapter 7 — Sensory happiness and misery in the afterlife, apart from true intellectual happiness and misery

Philosophers have disagreed about whether souls that know only simple ignorance, and have not taken on any intellectual form or even basic knowledge, continue to exist after death. Alexander of Aphrodisias, one of the greatest students of Aristotle, is said to have believed that such souls perish. Avicenna also leaned toward this view in some of his writings. We have already explained that if the next life consisted only of intellectual existence, these simple souls would have to perish, because in any realm a thing exists through its form, not through its matter. But beyond this changing, bodily life there are two lasting realms of forms: one of things understood through intellect, and another of images that can be perceived without depending on matter.

Only the soul preserves these sensory images there, because their existence is awareness itself. A sensed thing exists for the soul that senses it; the soul keeps these images alive without any material body, just as the intellect, by God's permission, keeps the soul alive without a bearer or body.[^V09-230] The things of this world need only their active causes and the ends they serve; they do not need the material causes and bodily powers that prepare them here. So the soul remains after the body. If it has not acquired bodily habits or harmful traits that conflict with its original, luminous nature, it will not be tormented by them. Nor can it be left inactive, since nothing in existence is entirely inactive. Its share of pleasure and happiness is proportionate to its share of existence.

As we have seen, existence brings pleasure, and fuller existence brings greater pleasure. Because this soul does not depend on matter, it exists in its own right, not merely as a feature of something else. Its pleasure therefore depends on its existence and on what is present to it, so long as nothing blocks or disturbs it. But its pleasure is weak because it has not reached its full development. It has moved beyond mere potential by learning basic truths and widely accepted ideas, yet it has not developed a settled desire for intellectual realities or a firm longing for intellectual completion. If such a soul was good, then after leaving this body it may have a kind of happiness, though not the highest kind. It may experience the images it once imagined and longed for: companions in paradise, palaces, thornless lote trees, clustered fruit, spreading shade, trees and rivers, and all the other things it heard described in religious teachings.[^V09-231] This presents no difficulty for us, because afterlife images do not need a material subject to exist. We will explain this through careful reasoning later.

The philosophers, however, report a view from Avicenna's *Healing* that he considers possible and cautiously stated. He says that when these souls leave their bodies, they may still be bodily in their nature and have no interest in anything beyond bodies. This keeps their attention on bodily things. Their nature may still make them long to be connected to some kind of body, though not to a human or animal body, for reasons he gives. Such a body would have matter and form, but would not be a human body. It might be a celestial body.[^V09-232] The soul would not become the soul or ruler of that body; it would only use it to form images, since the body's matter could support imagination.[^V09-233] The soul could then imagine the things it had believed in while alive. If it believed that goodness and good actions bring happiness, it would see beautiful things and imagine that it had died and been buried, along with all the other things its faith taught about the reward of the good.

Avicenna also allows that this body could be made from air and smoke, joined to the subtle bodily spirit that natural philosophers say carries the soul's influence through the body, especially in the heart, brain, liver, arteries, nerves, and veins.[^V09-234] If this spirit could remain intact after separating from the body and its fluids, Avicenna says, the soul might stay with it in a lasting bond. The opposite would happen to evil souls: they would imagine the punishments described in their teachings. Their happiness or misery would depend on bodies because imagination and inner judgment use bodily powers. Each group would become happier or more miserable through contact with similar souls. The truly happy would enjoy one another's company and understand themselves and those joined to them. Their connection would not be like the crowding of bodies, where space runs out. It would be a meeting of one intellect with another, which grows more spacious as more join it.

This is as far as Avicenna and those like him reached in explaining sensory happiness and misery after death, and the paradise of the happy and the fire of the miserable. We have already shown, in the third chapter of the eighth book on the soul, how weak this view is and how many problems it creates. Here is one of them: how could anyone trained in natural philosophy—let alone divine philosophy—think that, once separated from this body, the soul might imagine through a body made of air and smoke? A smoky body made from the elements would scatter and dissolve at the slightest cause unless something kept it together and continually replaced what it lost, as the body's vital spirit does. If it remained prepared for the soul's use in this way, it would itself be a kind of plant or animal, since it would be the seat of imagination.

Would this not be transmigration? The smoky body would be an animal other than a human, and a human soul would enter it, leaving its human nature for another animal form. To be human, a being must have the power to understand intellectual truths, either already or at least as a real possibility. But this smoky body would have no such power: death has ended its earlier possibility without making it an actual intellect. If it did regain the power to understand, it would be another human being. So how could transmigration be avoided? Avicenna and the philosophers who follow him already reject it. They cannot avoid the problem merely by saying that the soul uses this body without becoming its soul. A soul is a knowing substance that uses a living animal body as the subject of its perceptions—especially according to their own view that sensory and imagined forms exist in the bodily organs that serve them.

This is a mistaken view. It was forced on them because they did not know that the imaginative soul and its images are free from natural matter. It is also surprising that Avicenna says that truly happy souls enjoy an intellectual union with one another, like one understood thing joined to another, even though he did not understand this kind of union and denied the unity of the knower and what is known throughout most of his writings. He strongly criticized Porphyry and those who followed him for defending it.

At the end of *The Healing* and in *The Adhawiyya*, Avicenna writes that imagined forms are no weaker than sensory ones. In fact, they may have greater effect and clarity, as we see in dreams. What we dream may even be more important than things we sense while awake. Images in the afterlife will be more stable than dream images because there will be fewer obstacles, and the soul and the capacity receiving them will be clearer. The forms we see in dreams and while awake are all images within the soul.[^V09-235] The difference is that dream images begin within us and descend into the outer sense, while waking images begin outside and rise inward.

The image that appears in the soul[^V09-236] is what truly gives pleasure or pain, not the thing outside it. Whatever takes form in the soul has an effect, even without an outside cause. The image within us is the direct cause; the outside thing is only an indirect cause, or a cause of that cause. So the soul feels pain or pleasure only through the image that appears within it. A beautiful sight that remains only in matter and does not enter the soul does not bring us pleasure.

This is as far as the ideas of the Muslim philosophers—Avicenna, al-Farabi, and those of their time and their followers—have gone. Their account falls short of a sound investigation. It does not reach a useful conclusion, and, as we have already shown, it is false in itself. We will return to the heart of the matter when we examine the afterlife of souls that have not reached perfection or the rank of the highest beings.

It is striking that the account Avicenna presents, which he attributes to the scholar Abu Nasr al-Farabi, as al-Tusi explains in his commentary on *Pointers and Reminders*, is better than the accounts found in other Muslim writings. Al-Ghazali chose it in many of his works, as passages in *The Revival of the Religious Sciences* and elsewhere show. In his well-known treatise *The Work Kept Hidden*, he says that we should believe in the promised bodily pleasures of paradise, such as eating and sexual pleasure, because they are possible. As we have said, pleasures are sensory, imaginal, or intellectual. Sensory pleasure is clear enough, and it is just as possible in the next life as it is in this one, once the soul is returned to the body; we have already shown this by proof.

Imaginal pleasure is like the pleasure of a dream, except that dreams do not last. If they did, we would not be able to tell imaginal pleasure from sensory pleasure. We take pleasure in an image because it is present to our imagination and senses, not simply because it exists outside us. If something existed outside us but left no impression on our senses, it would bring us no pleasure. And if the image remained in us after the outside thing had disappeared, the pleasure would continue.

The imaginative power can invent images in this world. But an invented image is not something we can see with our eyes. If we imagine a beautiful person in perfect detail and picture them standing before us, we do not feel great joy, because we cannot actually see them as we can in a dream. If imagination could place the image before our sight as it places it within itself, our pleasure would be much greater. The image would then be like something really present. In the next life, the power to present an image to the sight is complete. Whatever a person longs for appears before them in the imagination and is seen.

This is what the Prophet's saying points to: “There is a market in paradise where images are sold.” The market[^V09-237] means God's grace, the source of our power to create images that answer our desires. This power is broader[^V09-238] and more complete than the power to bring something into being outside the senses. It is fitting to understand the next life in the way that most fully answers our desires. The fact that its pleasures exist in our senses, rather than outside us, does not make them any less real. They exist for our enjoyment, and our share of them is fulfilled when they appear to our senses. Their existence outside us would be an extra, not something we need. In this limited world, outside things are the route to what we want; in the next world, that route is open and unrestricted.

There is also a third kind of existence, the intellectual. On this level, things we perceive through the senses are images of higher realities that the senses cannot perceive. Just as there are many different kinds of things we can sense, there are many kinds of intellectual realities. Each pleasure of the senses can stand as an image of a different intellectual pleasure, and its rank among pleasures of the senses corresponds to the rank of that intellectual reality.[^V09-239]

The view of the afterlife held by theologians such as Fakhr al-Din al-Razi falls below this account. They say that resurrection means gathering scattered material parts—especially the supposedly permanent parts of the original limbs—and giving them a form like the body's former shape, so that the soul can be attached to them again. They do not see that this would be a resurrection in this world, not in a new realm; it would return us to the first home, where we work and learn, rather than the next home, where we are repaid and brought to completion. How would this avoid transmigration? What, then, is the meaning of God's words: “We are able to replace you with others like you and bring you into a form you do not know”; and “We have set death among you, and nothing can prevent Us from replacing you with others like you and bringing you into a form you do not know”; and “We created them and strengthened their bodies, and, if We will, We can replace them with others just like them”?

Anyone with insight can see that the next life is another mode of existence, different from this one made of earth, water, and clay. Death and resurrection begin the soul's return to God, or its drawing near to Him,[^V09-240] not its return to a dense, dark body made from earthly matter.

In his great commentary, Fakhr al-Din al-Razi tries to prove the meaning of resurrection that he has understood from Qur'anic verses about the Day of Judgment. He interprets them in a way that suits his own view. He says that verses in the chapter *The Event* answer the objection of the deniers, who ask: “When we are dead and become dust and bones, will we really be raised? And our forefathers too?” The verses show that resurrection is possible in four ways.

First, God says: “Have you considered the seed you emit? Do you create it, or are We its Creator?” Al-Razi's argument is this. Semen, according to the old medical account, is the finest residue of the body's final stage of digestion. It spreads through the limbs like dew, and all the limbs contribute to it. This, he says, is why ritual washing follows sexual pleasure: the body's parts have all been relieved of their contribution. God then gives desire the power to gather these scattered, dew-like parts into a bodily structure. At first they were spread across the body, even as far as its extremities.[^V09-241]

God gathers them in the animal's body and then in the vessels that hold semen. He brings them out as flowing fluid and places them in the womb. If He can gather parts that were so widely scattered and form a person from them, why should He be unable to gather them again after death?

Al-Razi says this is the argument, and that it appears in several places in the Qur'an. In *The Pilgrimage*, God says: “O people, if you doubt the resurrection, We created you from dust,” and continues to say that God is the truth, gives life to the dead, has power over all things, and will raise those in the graves. In *The Believers*, after describing the stages of creation, God says: “Then after that you will die; then on the Day of Resurrection you will be raised.”

The same argument appears in *The Resurrection*: “Was he not a drop of semen emitted, then a clinging form, which God created and shaped?” And in *The Night Visitor*: “Let the human being consider what he was created from,” followed by the words that God can bring him back.

The second argument comes from God's words: “Have you considered what you sow? Do you make it grow, or do We make it grow?” Al-Razi's reasoning[^V09-242] is that seeds come in many shapes: split or whole, round, three-sided, square, and more. When a seed is placed in the ground and surrounded by water and soil, one might expect it to rot, since either of these would be enough to cause decay, let alone both together. Yet it remains intact. When it absorbs enough moisture, it splits in two. One part grows upward, while the other takes hold in the soil. Even a hard date stone splits in half by God's permission. One part grows upward and the other downward. Though both parts share the same nature and elements, one is light and rises while the other is heavy and sinks. This shows a complete power and wisdom beyond the reach of human understanding. How, then, could this power be unable to gather scattered parts and assemble limbs?

The third argument comes from God's words: “Have you considered the water you drink? Did you send it down from the clouds, or did We send it down?” Al-Razi gives several reasons. First, water is naturally heavy, so making it rise goes against its nature. There must be a powerful agent that overcomes its natural tendency, directs it, and makes what normally falls rise. Second, the scattered particles of water come together after being dispersed. There must be an agent that gathers them, drop by drop. The One who gathers tiny droplets of water for rain can gather the scattered particles of earth for resurrection.

Third, the water is moved by the winds. Fourth, it is sent down where it is needed, onto parched land. All this, Al-Razi says, points to resurrection.

The fourth argument comes from God's words: “Have you considered the fire you kindle? Did you bring forth its tree, or did We bring it forth?” Al-Razi says that fire naturally rises while a tree grows downward. Fire is bright, while the tree is dark; fire is hot and dry, while the tree is cool and moist. God holds the bright parts of fire within the dark tree. By His power, He brings together things with opposite qualities. If He is able to do this, how could He be unable to assemble an animal's parts and limbs?

That, then, is the furthest the theologians' understanding and the formal scholars' reasoning have gone in trying to establish the next life, the resurrection of bodies, and the raising of spirits and souls. Even setting aside the weaknesses in their argument and their distortion of the Qur'anic verses—whose meaning and purpose we will explain later—the account they give does not establish another realm or explain belief in the Day of Judgment at all.

What their account establishes is only that scattered parts, spread across different places in this world, could be gathered in one place and arranged together, then given a form like the one that had disappeared. After a long period of rest in its immaterial, holy realm, the soul would return to this world and become attached once more to a dense, dark body. They call it the Day of Resurrection because the soul then stands up from its natural body, no longer needing it and existing through itself and its Creator. In the next life, the body depends on the soul; here, the soul depends on the natural body, because its existence here is weak and its existence there is strong.

In short, Al-Razi's account sounds more like the words of those who deny the next life than those who affirm it. Many naturalists and materialists said much the same: the elements gather through winds, rain, and the rays of the sun and moon, producing human beings, animals, and plants. These beings die, and their forms break apart. Later, the same parts gather again in the same or a similar arrangement, producing more beings like the first—either with the same souls, as those who believe in transmigration say, or with new souls replacing the old ones.[^V09-243]

Who, I ask, has denied that the same water, earth, and matter can produce a form like the first one more than once? The point is not simply to show that God has the power to do this. The people who argue in this way, with polished words and endless disputes, have missed the purpose of religious duties, revealed laws, messengers, and scriptures. Their purpose is to complete human souls and free them from this world of opposites, from the grip of desire, and from the limits of place and direction. This completion and freedom come only when we pass from this changing, temporary life into a lasting, unchanging one.

To make that passage, we must first know about the lasting realm and believe that it is real. Second, we must know that it is the true goal of human life, toward which our nature would turn if ignorance and wrongdoing did not lead us astray. Third, we must act in keeping with that goal, follow the paths that lead to it, and remove whatever blocks the way.

The purpose of the Qur'anic verses that point to the truth of the afterlife is to awaken us to another mode of existence and guide us toward a world hidden from our senses and beyond the sight of creatures. This is the unseen world; our present world is the visible one. The first is the world of spirits, the second the world of bodies. Just as the spirit is hidden within the body, the next life is hidden within this one.[^V09-244]

It is difficult for most people to grasp a mode of existence unlike this natural world, or another realm hidden within the one we see. So they deny it. They are also so attached to their bodies, desires, and pleasures that they find it hard to leave them and seek a life unlike this one. They do not reflect on what the next life is or how it works. Instead, they turn away from it and from its signs. God says: “How many signs in the heavens and earth do they pass by, turning away from them?” They are content with this life, at ease in it, and cling to the earth, as God says of one who “inclined toward the earth and followed his own desire.”

We have seen many people associated with learning and religious law shrink from affirming a world beyond matter. Their hearts recoil at the words intellect, soul, and spirit, and at praise of that world or criticism of bodies, their passing pleasures, and their eventual end. Most imagine that the next life is like this one, only richer, longer, and more lasting. They are drawn to it and perform acts of worship for its sake, hoping to satisfy the desires of the belly and the sex. That is why the Qur'an repeatedly speaks of the next life, resurrection, and rising from the dead: to wake people from ignorance and heedlessness, turn them toward the next life, free them from the body's chains and this world's attachments, cleanse them of what stains them, and make them long to meet God, live near those brought close to Him, and join the holy ones.

Know that the purpose of the verses we have cited is not to prove, as Al-Razi explains, a random power of the sort attributed to the Ash'arites—a power that denies wisdom and rejects the connection between cause and effect. The strange thing is that whenever these people try to establish a basic religious belief, such as God's power, prophecy, or resurrection, they feel forced to deny the natural powers God placed in things, to reject rational connections between them, and to deny the ordered structure of existence that follows from their natures. God's way does not change. Yet they use this approach to establish many of their beliefs, as did the leading debater and theologian Al-Razi.[^V09-245]

You may ask: What, then, do these verses mean, especially the ones that describe semen and the beginning of creation? Do they not mean that God, who can create something for the first time, can also create it again?

I answer: the main purpose of these verses about resurrection is to point to two sound ways of explaining the return of souls and bodies. The first establishes it through the goal toward which things are directed and the goals built into their natures. The second establishes it through the agent that brings them about.

A proof is strongest when its middle step is the cause of its conclusion—that is, when the middle step explains why the conclusion follows. This is called a demonstrative proof. A weaker argument uses a middle step that is itself an effect of the point it is trying to prove. Of all proofs that proceed through causes, the strongest are those that show either the acting cause or the final goal. We can now see this in the verses.

The verses about semen and its stages describe it changing from a lower form to a higher one and from a lesser state to a fuller one. Their purpose is to show that these stages and changes have a final goal. Human beings naturally move toward completion. We have an inborn, God-given path toward the active source of life and the completion fitting for us. But that completion is not found in this lower world. It is found in the next life, where we return and reach our final end.

Once a person has passed through all the natural stages of growth—matter, plant life, and animal life—and has reached bodily maturity, their worldly, animal existence is complete. They must then turn toward the next life, bringing what has not yet developed to completion, and moving from this world to the next. Their Lord is the ultimate goal, the end of every longing and movement. This is what the verse means when it says, “O people, if you doubt the resurrection, We created you from dust, then from a drop of fluid,” and continues by promising that God will raise those in the graves—the graves of bodies and the graves of spirits, meaning their bodies.[^V09-246] The other verses that describe the stages of creation and the development of semen make the same point: human life and its natural movement have a final goal in the next life.

You may ask: These stages of growth also occur in things other than human beings, such as plants and animals. Must they, too, have a next life?

I answer: they pass through some, but not all, of these stages. Their nature is less complete than human nature, so their goal lies beyond the stages they have reached. An animal that has not reached the human level cannot enter the spiritual world or approach the holy realm, just as a plant cannot reach the human level without first passing through animal life. This is not transmigration from one body to another, but a different kind of passage,[^V09-247] understood by those with insight and wisdom.[^V09-248] And not everything that moves toward a goal is sure to reach it. Something outside it may block the way. The point is that each nature has a goal fixed by its original constitution, even though obstacles may prevent it from arriving.

The verses that argue from the creation of great heavenly bodies and the kinds of nature establish the next life through the acting cause.[^V09-249] Many people think that nothing can come into being without a material source. They deny that another realm with new forms and bodies can begin, because they assume it must be made from some existing matter. They do not understand that the beings of the next life are brought into existence through the power of an agent, not fashioned from prior matter prepared to receive them.

God shows that His primary way of acting is to bring things into being directly, not to shape and assemble them from earlier matter. The heavens, the earth, and the basic kinds of beings were created in this way. They were not made from some earlier matter. In the same way, paradise, hell, and the bodies raised in the next life are brought into being. This answers those who ask what matter the next life will be made from,[^V09-250] or in what region, place, or time it will exist. They demand a particular place in this world and ask when the Hour will come to rest, as though it must occupy some place among these bodies and some fixed time in this worldly age. They do not understand that the place and time of the next life are not the same kind as the place and time of this one. Nor does it come into being in the same way as beings in this world. Its existence is stronger, because it comes directly from God's creative act.

This is a subtle and noble way of reading the verses. It is more fitting and easier for the First Cause[^V09-251] to bring forms into being without prior matter than to assemble and combine scattered parts. Direct creation is God's special act, higher and nobler than gathering what has been scattered and joining what has been separated. Gathering and joining are the work of limited powers and natural forces, which are weaker in their ability to act.

If God first brought human beings into existence by combining the elements and their mixtures, He can bring them into existence again without matter. You already know that all existence comes from Him. The intermediaries do not create; they specify and direct His act or multiply the ways His gift of existence reaches things. What is simpler and less divided is closer to the true source. We have already said that beings in the next life are purer than combinations and mixtures, and closer to perfect unity than the beings of this world.

So just as God's special act in the beginning was to bring the first realm into existence, not to combine different things and gather scattered parts, the true resurrection is the direct creation of the second realm. This is easier for Him than creating beings in this world through movement and change in matter. The next life is better, more lasting, and higher; so it is more fitting to come from the Highest Source, as God says: “That is easier for Him,” and “His is the highest likeness in the heavens and earth.”[^V09-252]

Know that the next life is called “next” only in relation to our own development. In itself, it is earlier and more basic, because in the order of existence it comes before the natural world in rank and nobility.[^V09-253] It comes after the natural world only as we experience it and grow toward it.[^V09-254] This also shows that the next life already exists, and that paradise and hell were created long ago,[^V09-255] as many verses and reports say.

Another sign that creating the beings of the next life accords more closely with God's way of direct creation is this: beings in this world come into existence and reach completion gradually. They develop over time, and the many members of each kind do not all appear at once. Some are born before others, and one generation must end before another begins. God says: “Every community has its appointed term; when its term comes, they cannot delay it by an hour or bring it forward.”

The forms of the next life, however, are brought into being all at once, without birth from parents or gradual development. God says: “The first and the last will surely be gathered together at the appointed time.” Their existence comes in a single act, by God's permission, when Israfil breathes their spirits into the forms of their bodies, which come from those spirits. It is one blast,[^V09-256] and suddenly they are all there.

#### Chapter 8 — The different views about resurrection

Among the common errors and ignorant views is the claim that neither souls nor bodies can be raised, and that resurrection is impossible. This is the view of atheists, naturalists,[^V09-257] materialists, and some natural philosophers and doctors whose opinions carry no authority in religion or philosophy. They assume that a human being is nothing more than this visible body, its bodily balance, and the powers and qualities that depend on it. They say that all these things disappear at death, leaving only scattered matter. A human being, like any animal or plant, is gone once dead; happiness and misery are limited to bodily pleasures and pains in this worldly life. This view contradicts reason, as the careful philosophers have shown, and revelation, as the careful religious scholars have shown.

Galen is reported to have hesitated over the afterlife because he was unsure what the soul is. If it is only the body's balance, he thought, it will perish at death and cannot be raised. But if it is a being in its own right that does not depend on matter, it will survive death and can be raised. Some who cling to the authority of scholars add that something once destroyed cannot be brought back, so a human being whose body has perished cannot be restored. The theologians answered this in two ways. Some denied that what has been destroyed cannot return; others denied that the human being perishes when the body's structure breaks down. They said that some parts of the human being remain, either as divisible pieces or as indivisible ones, and interpreted the scriptural accounts of resurrection as referring to the gathering of those scattered, surviving parts.

To make resurrection fit their view, the theologians have accepted one of two strange and far-fetched ideas. Neither is required by reason or revelation. Both reason and revelation tell us that the person raised in the next life is the same one who acted, worked, and was bound by intellectual and religious duties.

The root of the deniers' doubt will not be removed until we uproot their basic assumption: that a person is destroyed at death and nothing remains, because a human being is only a body with a certain balance or a form that inhabits it. We have already refuted that assumption at length. The leading philosophers and religious scholars agree that the next life is real and that there is a lasting realm, though they disagree about how resurrection happens.

Most Muslim scholars, jurists, and traditionalists say that resurrection is bodily only. They regard the spirit as a body that flows through the limbs as fire moves through coal, water through a rose, or oil through an olive. Most philosophers, following Aristotle's school, say that resurrection is spiritual or intellectual only. They say that the body, its form, and its qualities perish when the soul's connection to it ends, so the same body cannot return. Since the soul exists in its own right without depending on matter and survives death, it returns to the realm of immaterial beings when its natural bond to the body is cut.

Many of the greatest philosophers, mystics, and theologians—including al-Ghazali, al-Ka'bi, al-Halimi, al-Raghib al-Isfahani, and several leading Imami scholars—say that both kinds of resurrection take place.[^V09-258] They hold that the immaterial soul returns to a body in the next life. Most Christians and those who believe in transmigration also affirm a soul's return to a body, but there is an important difference: the careful Muslim scholars say that souls begin to exist and are returned to bodies in the next life, while those who believe in transmigration say that souls have always existed and return to bodies in this world. They deny the next life and bodily paradise and hell.

Those who affirm both kinds of resurrection also disagree about whether the body raised is this very body or only a similar one. They also ask whether sameness or similarity must apply to every limb, shape, and feature. It seems that no one requires every detail to be the same. Many Muslim scholars appear to hold that the body raised will differ from the first in its form and shape. They sometimes point to reports describing the people of paradise and hell: the people of paradise are said to be smooth-skinned and beardless, while an unbeliever's tooth is said to be as large as Mount Uhud. They also cite God's words: “Whenever their skins are burned away, We replace them with other skins, so that they may taste the punishment,” and “Is the One who created the heavens and earth not able to create others like them?”

You may ask: On this account, would not the person rewarded or punished with bodily pleasures and pains be someone other than the one who performed the good or evil acts?

The answer is that what matters is the one who experiences them. That is the soul, whether it experiences them directly or through bodily instruments. The soul remains the same. We say that a person from childhood to old age is the very same person, even though their form, size, shape, qualities, many bodily parts, and powers have changed. We do not say that someone who did wrong in youth and is punished in old age is being punished for another person's crime.

That is how the different views are stated. The truth, as you will learn, is that the person raised in the next life is this very same person, both in soul and in body. The soul is this same soul, and the body is this same body: if you saw it, you would say, “I have seen this very person—the one who lived in this world.” This is so even though the body has undergone many changes, to the point where we might say, “This has become gold,” or “This has become iron,” and eventually both may become one, pure intellect.[^V09-259] Whoever denies this rejects religious teaching and falls short in wisdom; they would also have to reject many clear passages of the Qur'an.

#### Chapter 9 — Arguments of those who deny resurrection

Those who deny resurrection argue, first, that something destroyed cannot be brought back. They also ask what happens if one person eats another.[^V09-260] If the eaten person's parts are restored to the eater's body, then the eaten person has not been raised. If those parts are restored to the body they came from, then the eater has not been raised. If they are restored to both, the very same parts would be enjoying reward and suffering punishment at once, as when a believer eats an unbeliever.

Theologians have answered the first objection by denying that what has been destroyed cannot return, or by denying that resurrection requires something destroyed to return. They answer the second by saying that God preserves the original parts of each person's body and does not let them become parts of another body in the same way.
