There have been long arguments between those who affirm resurrection and those who deny it. Repeating them would take time without bringing us closer to the truth. The theologians' arguments do not force their opponents to accept their view. How, then, can we discover the truth? Anyone who relies only on debate should first ask the deniers what they are claiming. Do they say resurrection is impossible, or only that it is not possible? If they claim impossibility, the burden is on them to prove it. They have no proof. If they only deny that it is possible, then God's revelation and the Prophet's reports—coming from one free of error and falsehood—stand in place of mathematical proof.

Some thinkers have tried to explain how the body can be restored. Their argument goes like this: a person is made this particular person by the particular parts of their body and soul, not by the exact arrangement of those parts. The parts themselves remain the same, even when their arrangement breaks down at death. If those same parts are later joined again in the same general way, the original person returns. This is what one writer says.

A later scholar gives a similar account. Some theologians, he says, allow that what has been destroyed can be restored, while philosophers and other theologians deny it. Yet those who affirm bodily resurrection without allowing restoration of the destroyed body do not say that bodies vanish. They say that the parts scatter and can no longer serve their former purpose. In his notes on *The Guide*, he says this view depends on denying that a body has a basic bodily form, and treating it instead as a collection of indivisible atoms, as theologians do. It also depends on the view that a body is a continuous shape that remains the same even when broken apart. If we accept that bodies have a basic form, he says, it is enough for bodily resurrection that the material parts be the same; the body's form may change, so long as the new form is close to the one that disappeared.[^V09-261]

If someone objects that this is transmigration, the reply is that what they reject is a soul moving into a body made of different matter. Here, the body would be made from the very same matter as before, and its form would be close to the old one. If someone calls that transmigration, the disagreement is only over words. They must prove that the thing itself is impossible.

The accounts of these two respected scholars are badly reasoned, though they come closer to the truth than the accounts of other theologians. There are several problems with them.

First, they assume that when a person named Zayd dies, only the relations among the body's parts and the arrangement of its limbs disappear. That would make life a kind of relation, which is plainly false.

Second, even if Zayd's body were made only of indivisible atoms, it would not follow that any arrangement of those atoms would be Zayd. If any arrangement were enough, then a solid ball made from those atoms would be Zayd. If only one special arrangement counted, then a dead person would sometimes become alive whenever their parts happened to fall back into that arrangement. The scholar al-Tusi, and other reasonable thinkers who deny a separate underlying matter, did not say that an individual body survives when its parts are scattered. What they say remains is matter in general, the kind of thing that continues through joining and separation—not a particular body like this one. A body as a general kind[^V09-262] cannot exist on its own in the world. It exists only through a form that gives it a particular nature.

A human being is not simply a body. If being human meant only having a body, then every body would be human. Nor is Zayd simply a certain amount of matter. If it were, then scattered parts of his body, spread to opposite ends of the world, would still be Zayd. No reasonable person would say that a particular horse remains that very horse after it is cut into pieces and its parts are scattered.

Third, the problem of transmigration clearly remains. The same body would have two souls. If the scattered parts could still receive the soul while they were separated, then the soul would never have left them; Zayd would still be alive at death, which contradicts what we assumed. If they could no longer receive a soul, they would need something new to prepare them. Once that new condition made them ready, a new soul would have to come from the Giver of life. If the returning soul then joined the parts as well, one body would have two souls, which is impossible.

What special link could remain between the soul and a few scattered pieces of earth, rather than any other parts of matter? Even a weak link must arise because the two are especially suited to one another. We often see matter with a balance close to that of an animal, and so ready to receive an animal's form. It is more fit for a soul than gray, scattered parts that are no longer suited even to a lifeless form, let alone to plant life, animal life, or human life—and still less to being Zayd.

Anyone who looks within knows that a soul is not drawn to one body or some of its parts rather than others unless those parts have a quality or shape that fits it. Tell me, thoughtful reader: what bond could the soul have with parts scattered in the air, buried in the ground, or lost in water, when neither sight nor imagination can distinguish them from other parts? Even if God knows them as different, that difference creates no special link. Nor, according to these thinkers, does the soul remember the body after its powers break down, so memory cannot single out the parts of its former body. And even if memory did remain and could identify that body, it would explain only why this soul returned to this body rather than another. It would not explain why this same soul, rather than another soul, returned to it.[^V09-263]

These problems and far-fetched explanations disappear only through the truth we have established. God has given us this share of the higher wisdom, along with other rare insights. Anyone who follows God's path and receives the light of divine knowledge can recognize its truth and worth, just as a skilled jeweler can tell a bright gem from other stones.

#### Chapter 10 — How people differ in their understanding of the afterlife

Know that people who believe in the truth of resurrection and the raising of bodies hold different views, each suited to a different level of understanding.

The first and lowest level is the ordinary Muslim's belief. It is the simplest view and the least open to harmful doubts: everything described about the next life—the punishment in the grave, its pressure, the questioning by Munkar and Nakir, snakes, scorpions, and more—really happens as something physical that the eye could see. God simply does not allow people to see it while they are alive, for a wise purpose. Some verses and reports appear to support this view.

The second view is that the promised rewards and punishments are like what we see in dreams. They are imaginal forms, with no material existence outside the soul, just like the snakes and scorpions a person sees in a dream. Yet a dream can cause real pain. The sleeper may cry out, sweat, and toss about. Dreams can also bring intense pleasure, sometimes greater than anything felt while awake. The sleeper sees forms, acts, and responds, while the body lies still. No snake is actually nearby, yet for the sleeper the snake and its bite are real. If the pain comes from the bite, it makes no difference[^V09-264] whether the snake is in a dream or before the eyes.

The same is true of the gardens, trees, rivers, pleasant places, and gracious people we may see in a dream. They are real for the sleeper as objects of inner awareness, even though they do not exist outside or appear to the waking senses. Avicenna leaned toward this view when he discussed the paradise of people whose knowledge is incomplete and the fire of those whose actions fall short. Al-Ghazali followed him, as we have seen.

The third view accepts the images promised for the Day of Resurrection, but understands them in one of two ways.

The first is that the forms perceived through the senses and described in religious teaching point to separate intellectual forms in the world of pure intellect. Plato and his followers held that every kind of thing perceived through the senses has a corresponding intellectual form, or pattern, in that world.[^V09-265] Seeing the next life in this intellectual way is possible only for people of deep knowledge and complete understanding. It is not for those with simple minds or still, undeveloped souls, who make up most of the people in paradise. A report says that most of its people are simple folk.

Al-Ghazali says in one of his treatises that all these views are possible. A person may receive what suits their readiness. Someone whose love of religious teaching has made them deeply attached to its outward forms will not find a path to the deeper truths, or see the intellectual forms. Those who know the truth and look down on bodily images and pleasures will receive subtle joys and intellectual pleasures suited to them. These will satisfy their longing. Paradise gives each person what they desire. Desires differ, so gifts and pleasures may also differ. God's power is vast; human beings cannot take in all its wonders. Through the prophets, God has made known as much of it as people can understand. We must believe what they understand, and accept that there are other matters beyond the reach of our knowledge.

The second way to understand these promised images[^V09-266] is that they stand for the kinds of joy or pain that follow from them. Al-Ghazali explains this by comparing them to dreams. If someone dreams of green fields, flowing water, a beautiful face, rivers, rain falling as milk, honey, or wine, trees decorated with jewels and pearls, palaces of gold and silver, or attendants waiting to serve, a dream interpreter would explain the dream as a kind of joy. They would not take it as a literal report about another kind of joy. Some joys come from knowledge and discovery; others come from power or from seeing friends. They may all be called pleasure, but they differ in rank and quality. We should understand intellectual pleasures in the same way, even when no eye has seen them, no ear has heard them, and no heart has imagined them.

Al-Ghazali then explains how the promised images may stand for intellectual pleasure and pain. A snake itself does not hurt you; its poison does. The poison itself is not the pain either. You suffer from the effect the poison produces in you. If the same effect came about without poison,[^V09-267][^V09-268] you would still suffer. We can describe this kind of pain only by linking it to the usual cause. In the same way, if a person felt sexual pleasure without the familiar act, we could describe it only by comparing it to that act. The effect is present even if its usual cause is not. A cause exists for the sake of its result, not for itself.

At death, destructive traits turn into things that hurt the soul. Their pain is like a snakebite even when there are no snakes or scorpions. A quality can turn from something pleasing into something painful, just as love becomes painful when the beloved dies. What once brought joy can become such a source of suffering that the lover wishes they had never known love or union. This itself is one kind of torment after death.[^V09-269]

The fourth level is held by people firmly grounded in knowledge, who join direct experience with proof. They know with certainty that the forms described and promised in religious teaching really exist. They are real and stable. In existence, strength, permanence, and effect, they surpass the forms of this world so greatly that there is no comparison.

These forms have different levels. Some are intellectual forms: the paradise of those who know God's oneness and are close to Him. Some are pleasurable sensory forms: the paradise of the people of the right, the safe, and the faithful. Others are painful forms: the hell of those who turn away, go astray, or deny the Day of Judgment. But these things are not sensed with the failing eyes and bodily senses of this world, as some literal-minded Muslims believe.

Nor are they merely imagined forms with no real existence, as some followers of the Stoics and others say.[^V09-270] Nor are they only intellectual ideas, inner states, or qualities of the soul, with no bodily shapes or measured forms, as most followers of Aristotle believe. They are real forms that exist in themselves. They are not in this material world, and the natural senses cannot perceive them. They exist in the next life and are sensed by the senses of that life. The relation of those senses to what they sense is like the relation of our present senses to what they sense here.

The next life is not one single world. It contains many worlds, each higher and nobler than this one. Human beings, too, have many forms of life and many sets of senses beyond this material, changing life. God says, “We will replace you with others like you and bring you into a form you do not know,” and, “The next life has greater ranks and greater excellence.” After describing the body's development from a drop of fluid, a clinging form, and a lump of flesh, He says, “Then We brought it into being as another creation. Blessed is God, the best of creators.” This points to the higher life of the spirit. God also says that He created people in stages. Those who attain sainthood enter another, higher stage beyond the soul's first life; the stage of prophecy and its special gifts is higher still. God brings these stages into being, raises the dead, opens the books and records on the Day of Rising, and brings out what is in people's hearts and graves. Resurrection means bringing the dead to life, drawing forms out of matter, and freeing spirits from bodies by giving them another kind of life—higher, brighter, and more knowing. Ignorance is the greatest death; knowledge is the noblest life.

God speaks of knowledge and ignorance in His Book as life and death, and also as light and darkness. He says: “Was one who was dead and whom We brought to life, giving him a light to walk among people, like one lost in darkness?” Know that each blessed soul has a vast kingdom in the next life, a world greater and wider than the heavens and earth. It is not outside the soul. Its kingdom, servants, attendants, gardens, trees, companions, and young attendants all exist through the soul, which preserves and brings them into being by God's permission and power.

The things of the next life resemble what we see in dreams or in some mirrors, but their true nature is different.[^V09-271] What they share is that neither kind of image exists in the matter of an ordinary body or in a place or direction belonging to this world. Images do not crowd one another, and they do not take up room that would prevent other things from being in the same place. A sleeper may see great skies, wide fields, and distant deserts, all much like the things we see while awake. Even though the dream images are separate from the outside things, they do not crowd or block them. In the same way, a person in the grave may see more than the small space of the grave could hold if these were bodies made of worldly matter.

But the next life is different because its forms are real, powerful, and lasting. They have a strong effect, bringing great pleasure or pain. They are stronger than the things of this world, and far more than the weak images we see in dreams or mirrors. The next life is to this one as waking is to dreaming, as the Prophet said: “People are asleep; when they die, they wake.”

We have discussed in several places how these forms exist. A full proof of them, laid out step by step, needs a set of firm starting points and general rules. Once these have been explained, only a closed mind or a willful refusal to understand could still deny the conclusion. We will soon set out that proof, God willing.

Here is the main point. The human soul belongs to the spiritual world and has the power to create forms without matter. But while it is tied to this dense body, made from conflicting elements, it is busy with the tasks of protecting the body, meeting its needs, avoiding harm, and managing its affairs. The forms it creates then have weak, incomplete, and unstable existence. They do not produce the full effects they could, and they keep changing. They appear through a vapor in the brain, which is always breaking down and being renewed as the brain's condition changes. If these forms were stronger and more lasting—if the soul were free from distractions and could gather its attention, while the body's other powers were quiet—the forms would have their full effects. There would then be no difference between an imagined form and one seen outside us: imagination would be sight, and its imagined object would be something actually seen.

When the soul rests from the work of maintaining a body whose parts are always pulling apart, and the outer senses stop working—during sleep, fainting, or when the soul turns away from them toward a higher world through an inborn or learned power—it takes the chance to return partway to itself. It cannot return completely, because the body's natural and plant-like powers must keep working, or the body would die and its fluids would rot.

As it turns inward, the soul creates images and sees them through senses within itself, without help from the body. It must have hearing, sight, smell, taste, and touch within itself. Otherwise, how could we see, hear, smell, taste, and touch in sleep or fainting, when the outer senses are no longer taking in what is around us? The body's five senses come together in one inner sense, the common sense. In the same way, all the soul's ways of sensing, understanding, and acting come together in its one, light-giving self, through God's permission and power. As the soul returns from this world to itself, its awareness of things becomes its power to bring them about.[^V09-272]

If the soul can create images this way while it is only partly turned inward and still partly active in the body, imagine what it can do when every tie and obstacle has been removed and it has fully returned to itself and its Creator.

Also consider this: the stronger and more complete a soul is, the less its powers get in one another's way. This may happen because its powers are weak, as in the sick or confused, or because they have been freed in a holy way, as with prophets and saints. It may also happen when something overwhelms the senses, as with soothsayers and magicians. In such cases, the soul meets hidden forms and sees them more clearly. Its response—desire or fear, pleasure or pain—becomes stronger.

Some souls rise above worldly ties and shake the dust of bodily concerns from themselves. They look at this world with care but do not let it distract them. Nothing keeps them from remembering God and the next life. Their power can hold both worlds in view. Their very being can create forms that they see as they arise. A mystic's vision of the next world may sometimes prevent them from seeing the present world while awake. Though this also places a veil before them, it is rare. The next life has hidden the present one from their sight.[^V09-273]

All these states come from the power of the next life becoming visible in some souls. This gives us a first glimpse of the soul's state there. When a soul's bond to the body ends at death and it leaves the ruined house behind, its inner senses grow stronger and more able to perceive the things of the next life. It sees the real forms that exist there. This is not limited to one kind of soul, just as the power to sense through bodily senses in this life is not limited to one kind of person.

When the soul's bond to this body ends, by force or by choice, the things suited to its actions, deeds, intentions, and beliefs are revealed to it. God says: “We have lifted your veil from you, and today your sight is sharp.” If its actions and intentions were good, it will have rest, sweet provision, and a garden of delight. If they were evil and its beliefs corrupt, it will face boiling water and enter the fire.

#### Chapter 11 — The nobility of knowing the afterlife and the greatness of understanding the resurrection of souls and bodies

Know that this subject includes the grave, rising, resurrection, the gathering of all people, judgment, the record of deeds, the balance, the place of judgment, the bridge, paradise and its levels and gates, and hell and its gates and depths. It is a great part of faith and a central subject in wisdom and true understanding. It is among the most subtle, noble, and difficult fields of knowledge. Only a person with clear insight and a heart lit by God can find its way. Few of the greatest philosophers, past or present, have understood it.

Most philosophers, even after using all their effort to understand the First Reality—to affirm God's oneness and perfection, and deny flaws and change in His being, qualities, acts, and effects—could not understand the stages of the afterlife and the places where people will be gathered. They did not draw the light of wisdom from the prophecy of the final Prophet, peace and blessings be upon him and his family. Their leading figure admitted that reason alone could not prove one kind of resurrection.[^V09-274] This is the kind most people need and that almost every soul will experience, except for a few people who are close to God in each age. They also could not explain the afterlife of souls in between these levels; some were forced to say that such souls attach themselves after death to the bodies of the stars. As for souls that are still tied to matter, Alexander of Aphrodisias went so far as to say they perish.

Most philosophers therefore affirmed only a spiritual afterlife. In their view, paradise and its delights, companions, palaces, trees, and rivers are nothing more than understanding ideas and taking joy in them. Hell, its chains, burning water, and bitter fruit stand for bad character and harmful qualities, especially ignorance and the pain it brings.

Most Muslim scholars, however, think that a human being is only this visible body: flesh, blood, bones, veins, and other bodily parts, together with the qualities and powers that belong to it. They identify the human form with the body's special arrangement, or with the matter that makes up the body. In truth, they do not know what resurrection is or grasp the Day of Judgment, even if they affirm it aloud. For them, the Day of Judgment means only restoring these bodies, with the same qualities they have in this world.

Many people today say that the soul does not depend on matter, but they still know little about what the soul is, how it rises through different levels, or where it comes from and returns to. It may shine brightly in the highest realm, or be darkened and turned downward toward the lowest. Whoever knows the soul's true nature and how it first joins the body, then rises and grows stronger, separates from it, becomes an actual intellect, and finally returns to God—first a natural being, then a soul, then an intellect, then a light absorbed into the light of divine oneness—is a true knower of God. Whoever does not know themselves or the truth of their own being does not understand either kind of resurrection, and knows their Creator even less.

It is surprising that so many people who claim to be learned have settled for the level of ordinary people and children. They spend their lives on rare disputes about religious rules, but never seriously ask what the soul is, what will happen to it, or where it is going. They have left aside a duty each person must fulfill and spent their time on duties that can be shared among many.

This simple belief in the afterlife is suitable for unlearned people, ordinary workers, craftspeople, and traders who cannot study the deepest truths or reflect on the first causes and natural goals of things. A familiar religious account of the afterlife can encourage them to do good, avoid wrongdoing, turn away from sin, worship, keep trusts, avoid betrayal, honor agreements, deal fairly, offer honest advice, and treat others well.

The more advanced view held by the careful scholars of religion and the best Muslim thinkers is that, alongside these bodies, there are other beings: finer, brighter spirits. At death they leave these bodies. Resurrection happens when they return to these bodies, or to other bodies like them, so that people can be raised, rewarded, or punished for what they have done. This view is better[^V09-275] and closer to the truth. Those who have reached a higher level of knowledge and trained deeply in wisdom believe that resurrection is something whose full nature is known only to God and those firmly grounded in knowledge.

The revealed books given to the prophets differ in their outward descriptions of rising and the Day of Judgment, though their inner meaning agrees. The subject is subtle and hard to grasp.[^V09-276] The Torah says that the people of paradise will remain there for fifteen thousand years and then become angels; the people of hell will remain for a similar or longer time and then become devils. The Gospel says that people will rise like angels: they will not eat, drink, sleep, or have children. Some Qur'anic verses say that each person will be raised alone, as in “Each of them will come to Him on the Day of Judgment alone,” or will return as God first created them: “As He began your creation, so you will return.”[^V09-277]

Other verses speak of people as having bodies: they will be dragged on their faces through the Fire; the gold and silver they stored will be heated in the Fire and used to brand their foreheads, sides, and backs. Abraham asks God to show him how He brings the dead to life, and God tells him to take four birds. The Qur'an also tells of Ezra, who wondered how God would bring a ruined town back to life; God caused him to die for a hundred years, then raised him. The Companions of the Cave slept for three hundred and nine years. God says that He revealed their story so people would know that His promise is true. Some passages, then, speak of the souls being raised; others speak of bodies. God's words and the books He sent cannot contradict one another. We will explain how they agree. Similar accounts also appear in prophetic reports and in the writings of the greatest philosophers, who drew their knowledge from prophecy and revelation rather than mere debate.

Socrates, Plato's teacher, says that those who committed great crimes are sent to Tartarus and never leave. Those who regret their sins while they are alive, but whose sins were not too great, are sent there for a year and burned. Then a wave carries them to a place where they call out to those they wronged and ask to be allowed to make amends. If the victims agree, they are freed from suffering. If not, they return to Tartarus and remain there until their victims are satisfied. Those who lived well leave these places, escape these prisons, and settle in a pure land.

The translator explains that Tartarus is a deep chasm, with rivers flowing into it, though Socrates describes it as a place of burning fire, like the Qur'anic “sea set ablaze” and “the sea filled with fire.” Plato, the leading philosopher, says that when the soul rises but does not fully reach the higher world, it remains between the two worlds, between what is known through intellect and what is perceived through the senses. It is partly intellect and partly sense. If it wants to rise, it can do so with little effort. But a soul in the lower world finds it hard to climb toward the intellectual world.[^V09-278]

How can we make the different passages and reports agree? The bodies of the next life stand between the spiritual and material worlds. They unite qualities of both and lack many of the qualities of bodies here. The afterlife body is like a lasting shadow of the spirit, a living image of it. The body and spirit there are one in existence, unlike these changing, perishable bodies. Paradise, its trees and rivers, its rooms and homes, and the bodies within it are all living forms of awareness.[^V09-279]

We have often explained that a sensed form exists in itself as something sensed, and it exists for the sensing being in the same way. A form understood by the intellect exists as something understood and for the intellect as one reality. The same is true of afterlife bodies and souls: there, they are one. This is why God says, “The next home is truly life,” meaning that life belongs to those bodies by their very nature. The bodies of this world are lifeless and dark in themselves. They are unknown to themselves until a form of awareness comes to them from outside. In our account of intellect and what it understands, we have already shown that an intellectual form is one with the intellect that understands it. In the same way, a sensed form is one with the being that senses it. The bodies of the next life are souls; the bodies of this world are not.

There is another way these accounts agree. The existence of the blessed and the people of paradise is higher than the existence of the wretched and those in hell. The people closest to God are higher still. Some have a spiritual existence; others have a bodily existence; the most complete have both.

Now we can begin the promised proof of bodily resurrection and the raising of bodies, together with the related conditions and ranks of the afterlife. We will lay it out through certain starting points and clear proofs, as suits those who seek to understand. We ask God and His spiritual world for help.

### Book Eleven — Bodily resurrection and the states of the afterlife

#### Chapter 1 — Principles needed to establish this subject

These principles and starting points have already been explained in full throughout this book. I bring them together here so that we can keep them in view and consider what follows from them.

**First principle.** In anything that exists, existence is what makes it real; what a thing is follows from its existence. A thing's true reality is its own particular way of existing, not its definition or the idea of it. Existence is not, as many later thinkers claimed, merely an abstract idea formed by the mind, with nothing real corresponding to it outside the mind. Existence is a concrete reality. No idea in the mind is its equal, and it can be pointed to only through direct, inward awareness.[^V09-280]

**Second principle.** A thing is this particular thing, distinct from others, through its own existence. Existence and individuality are one in reality, though we use different words and ideas for them. What are called the “individuating features” of a thing are only signs and consequences of its individual existence. They need not all be present together: one or many may change, and in time all of them may change, while the individual remains the same.

**Third principle.** Existence itself can be stronger or weaker, though it is simple and has no parts, either in reality or in thought. Its individual instances do not differ because each has a different essential feature, an added quality, or a separate marker of individuality. They differ through their own degrees of strength and weakness, priority and lateness, fullness and lack.[^V09-281][^V09-282] The general ideas we form about these instances—what are called their essences—do differ by kind, species, or other qualities. That is why people say that kinds of existence differ, and that the stronger and weaker levels are different kinds.

**Fourth principle.** Existence can become stronger or weaker; in other words, it can undergo a gradual increase in intensity. A being can also change in its very way of existing, not merely in its outward qualities. We have already shown that the parts and stages of one continuous change do not each exist separately and all at once. They are all present through one continuing existence.[^V09-283] So the essences that correspond to those different levels of existence do not each exist in full detail as separate things. They are present together in a unified way, as the parts of a boundary or limit are present in what we have explained earlier.

**Fifth principle.** A composite thing is what it is because of its form, not its matter. A bed is a bed because of its form, not the wood it is made from; a sword is a sword because of its shape and sharpness, not because it is iron; an animal is an animal because of its soul, not its body. Matter carries a thing's potential and allows it to undergo change. The relation between matter and form is like the relation between what is incomplete and what completes it: the incomplete depends on the complete, but the complete does not depend on the incomplete.[^V09-284]

For a composite thing defined by a broader kind and a defining feature, its final, defining difference—such as “rational” in the definition of a human being—is the basis of its specific nature. The other broader kinds and differences are requirements that follow from that basis; they may appear in the definition because the thing is being defined as a composite.[^V09-285] A composite nature can be considered in two ways: as many parts set out in detail, or as one unified whole. If we define it in detail, we must mention the meanings that correspond to all its parts. If we define it as one whole, through its complete form, we define it by the features that follow from that form. The form itself is simple and has no parts, either in reality or in thought. These features are not like ordinary consequences that exist separately from the thing they belong to. They all follow from the complete form itself, without anything being added to it. Yet they are not part of its essence, because its only essence is its very existence: the complete forms of things are pure ways of existing, differing in how fully they exist. As we have already explained, existence itself has no definition in the usual sense.[^V09-286]

Defining a simple form by the features that follow from it is no worse than defining a composite by its broader kind and differences. Those parts are one with the thing and are drawn from it. This is what Avicenna means when he says in *The Eastern Philosophy* that simple things can be defined by features that lead the mind to their inner reality, and that such a definition is no less useful than one made by broader kind and difference.[^V09-287] This is another place where a definition includes more than the thing being defined. The same method applies when we define the basic principles of existence or explain a power through what it does.

From this we learn that a human being has two ways of existing: a detailed existence and a unified existence. The detailed existence depends on substantial matter, an extended bodily form, the life-giving power of growth and nourishment, the power of sensation and voluntary movement, and the rational soul. We may therefore define a human being as a substance that has dimensions, grows, senses, moves by choice, and understands universal ideas. These aspects come in an order, from the lower and less complete to the higher and more complete. Whatever belongs to the earlier, lower level also belongs to the later, higher level, but in a subtler and simpler way. The unified existence of a human being is the rational soul itself, in which all these meanings are present in a simpler and higher form.

**Sixth principle.**[^V09-288] The individual unity of a thing, which is its existence, does not take the same form in every kind of thing, just as existence itself does not. In continuous quantities, unity is their continuity and extension. In time and other flowing forms of existence, it is their continual passing and renewal. In number, it is the actual presence of many units. In natural bodies, it is the potential for division into many parts. Unity also differs between beings that do not depend on matter and those that do. A physical body cannot contain opposite qualities at once, such as black and white, sweet and bitter, or pain and pleasure. Its existence is too limited for that. In the human body, sight, hearing, smell, and taste each have their own place. But the soul, though one, can hold the forms of black and white and other opposites together. The more a person becomes free of matter, stronger, and more complete, the more things they can take in and the more opposites they can hold together. The soul keeps developing until it gathers within itself, in one unity understood through intellect, a likeness of all existence.

It becomes a world understood through intellect, corresponding to the whole world we sense, witnessing absolute goodness, truth, and beauty, and joined to them through its own form.

This is also shown by the fact that it is the same directing soul that perceives through the senses, imagination, and intellect, and acts through the natural, animal, and human powers. It can descend to the level of the senses and bodily instruments, and rise to the Active Intellect and beyond,[^V09-289] all at once. Its being is wide enough and its light reaches far enough to do both. By God's command, it takes on different states and descends to the stations of the body's powers and organs. When it enters the realm of matter and bodies, it acts as the bodily powers do. When it returns to its own inner reality, once it is complete, it gathers all things into one. This principle shows how one and the same thing can at one time be bound to matter and at another be free of it. The familiar claim[^V09-290] that these two ways of existing can never belong to one thing has not been proved—unless it means that they cannot belong to it at the same time and in the same respect.

**Seventh principle.** A body's identity and individuality belong to the living self, not to its mass of matter. Zayd, for example, is Zayd because of himself,[^V09-291] not because of his body considered as matter. This is why he keeps his identity as long as his soul remains in him, even as his parts change and his qualities—where he is, how large he is, what qualities he has, and how he is positioned—keep changing throughout his life. The same is true if his natural bodily form changes into an imaginal form, as in a dream or in the world of the grave and the intermediate state before resurrection, or into an afterlife form. Through all these changes, the human being remains one and the same, because the changes unfold through a continuous, unified process.

The particular substances and limits that appear along the way in this change in the being's very existence are not what matter most. What matters is what continues through it: the soul. It is the complete form of a human being, the root of its identity and nature, the gathering point of its essence and reality, and the source of its powers, instruments, parts, and organs. It preserves them for as long as the body belongs to this natural world, then gradually replaces them with spiritual organs. In time, if God's plan and grace bring the person to intellectual perfection, the soul becomes a simple intellect. If some barrier prevents that final perfection, the point at which the process stops depends on causes that would take too long to explain.

If someone asks[^V09-292] whether Zayd's body in youth is the same as the body he had as a child or will have in old age, the answer is both yes and no, depending on what is meant. If “body” means the particular matter that makes up the body at a given time, then it is not the same: that matter changes. If “body” means the general kind of body, without specifying its particular matter, then it is the same and can be said of Zayd throughout his life. In the first sense, the body's matter is a part of Zayd but is not something we can predicate of him as a whole. In the second sense, “body” is predicated of him and is one with him. The change of matter does not destroy the whole composite, because matter belongs to it as a general, indeterminate kind, not as a fixed set of pieces. The difference between broader kind and matter is like the difference between a general essence and that same essence taken on its own. A similar distinction applies to the defining feature and form, and to a quality and the thing that has it: each is said of a thing in one sense, but not in another.

**Eighth principle.[^V09-293]** The power of imagination exists in its own right. It does not exist in any bodily organ, nor does it occupy a place in the natural world. It is separate from this world and belongs to an intermediate world of its own, between the world of pure intellect and the material world. We have established this through clear and decisive arguments, as explained earlier.

**Ninth principle.** Imagined forms, and perceptual forms more generally, do not exist inside the soul as things placed in a container. They exist through the soul as an action exists through the one who performs it. The same is true of sight. Seeing is not caused by an image of the object being stamped onto the eye, as natural philosophers say; it is not caused by a ray leaving the eye, as mathematicians claim; nor is it simply a relation of knowledge between the soul and an external image, as some illuminationist philosophers think. We have shown that all these accounts are mistaken. The first two have long been refuted in well-known books. We have also refuted the last account. Abū Naṣr put it forward as a way to bring the other two views together, and the philosopher known as the Martyred Shaykh adopted it in his *Philosophy of Illumination*.

While the soul is attached to the body, seeing and sensing require outside material and particular conditions. Imagination does not. Once the soul leaves this world, the difference between imagination and sensation disappears. The imaginative power—the storehouse of the senses—grows stronger and is freed from the body's dust and weakness. The powers unite and return to their common source. The soul can then do through imagination what it did through the other powers; it sees through the eye of imagination what it once saw through bodily sight. Its power, knowledge, and desire become one. To perceive what it desires is to have the power to bring it before itself. Paradise contains what souls desire and choose, as God says: “There you will have what your souls desire,” and “There you will have what souls desire and what delights the eyes.”

**Tenth principle.** Bodily forms, shapes, and arrangements can come about through an active cause's understanding and power, without any contribution from matter.[^V09-294] They can also come about through an active cause working with receptive matter and its readiness. For example, the heavens and stars come from intellectual principles through their acts of understanding; there was no earlier matter before the first bodies. In the same way, the soul can produce imagined forms through its power to form images.[^V09-295] These may include enormous bodies and landscapes, larger than the whole visible sky: wide deserts, vast plains, great mountains, cities, gardens, trees unlike any found on earth. These forms are not in the brain, nor placed inside the imaginative power, nor in the universal world of images we described earlier. They exist in the soul's own domain, beyond this material world.

We have already explained that the forms the soul creates through imagination and sees through its inward sight do not exist in this world; otherwise, everyone with healthy senses would see them. They exist in another world, unseen from here. The only difference between them and the forms we see with our bodily senses is that imagined forms do not last as long and are less firmly rooted, because the soul is busy with other things and its attention is divided by outside events.[^V09-296] If the soul were free from the work of its other natural and animal powers and focused entirely on imagining and forming images, those forms and bodies would become fully stable and real. Their effect could be stronger than the effect of material things, as is told of people with extraordinary gifts.

If the soul can form things this way while it is still in this world and attached to its body, imagine what it can do when all its worldly ties have ended, its powers have grown stronger, and it has become fully active. Every human soul that leaves this world free of inner disease, bad character, and fixed habits that hurt it or keep it from returning to itself will have a world of its own, containing whatever it wants and desires. This is the lowest rank of the blessed. Each of them has a paradise as wide as the heavens, while the homes of the righteous and those brought near to God extend far beyond it.

**Eleventh principle.** However many worlds and stages of existence there may be, they fall into three broad kinds, even though existence itself is one and all things are connected. Lowest is the world of natural forms that come to be and pass away. In the middle is the world of sensed and imagined forms, free from matter that carries potential and can take on opposite forms. Highest is the world of intellectual forms and divine patterns.

Of all beings, the human soul alone passes through all three kinds of existence while remaining the same individual. A person begins in childhood with natural, bodily life. In this respect the person is a human being in bodily form. They then gradually become more refined and complete until they reach a second mode of existence: a spiritual, afterlife form suited to resurrection, with its own spiritual organs. This is the second human being, the human soul in its afterlife life. From there, a person may gradually rise to intellectual existence and become an intellectual human being with intellectual organs. The leading philosopher describes this in *The Theology of Aristotle*. These changes are the path one person takes toward the highest goal. No other kind of being makes this full journey. All things turn toward God's presence, but only the human kind follows the straight path all the way to the final end. If another kind were to follow it, it would first have to change into the human kind.

The order of these three stages in the soul's return to God is the reverse of their order in the descent from God, though the two processes are not alike. The descent begins through creation, without time or motion; the return takes place through motion and time. Human beings also have modes of existence before their individual bodily birth. For this reason, the divine Plato held that human souls have an intellectual existence before the body comes to be. Our true religious tradition also teaches that human beings had distinct individual lives[^V09-297] before their natural existence, as in God's words: “When your Lord took from the children of Adam, from their backs, their descendants…” Many reports from the Imams say that the spirits of the prophets and their successors were created from the clay of Illiyyin[^V09-298] before the heavens and earth, while their bodies were created[^V09-299] from other clay. The same is true of the spirits and bodies of their followers and supporters. The hearts of hypocrites, and the hearts of those who follow them, were created from the clay of Sijjin.

This and similar reports show that human beings have modes of existence before this one. By their original nature, people move gradually toward the afterlife and their final goal. They begin with material existence in this world and move toward the image-bearing existence of the next. This world is to the next as what is incomplete is to what is complete, or as a child is to an adult. While we are here, we are like children who need a cradle—which is place—and a nurse—which is time.[^V09-300]

When a person reaches the height of their substantial development,[^V09-301] they leave this worldly existence for an afterlife existence and become ready to leave this home for the lasting home. This stage—when the soul has its own independent, image-bearing existence, a change called the blowing of the Trumpet and bringing about natural death—is shared by believer and unbeliever, one who believes in God's oneness and one who worships other gods, and even one who denies God. This kind of existence, and freedom from the body's matter, can coexist with misery and punishment in the Fire. Indeed, it may make them more severe. A stronger existence frees the soul from material coverings and makes it more aware of pain, harm, and the results of its own evil deeds, illnesses, and inner defects. In this world, nature's dullness and a veil over the heart's sight keep the soul from noticing them. Once that veil is gone, the suffering is there.

Until the soul has passed beyond all the limits of nature and the soul's lower life, it has not reached God's nearness or earned a place in His presence. Death is the first stage of the next life and the last stage of this one. After leaving this world, a person may remain for a long or short time in the intermediate world between this life and the next. Some rise quickly through the light of knowledge, the strength of worship, a pull from God, or the intercession of those who intercede. The last to intercede is the Most Merciful.

#### Chapter 2 — What follows from these principles

Anyone who reflects carefully on the ten principles and rules whose foundations we have laid with clear and decisive arguments, especially throughout this book, and whose nature is free from confusion, envy, stubbornness, partisanship, and pride, will have no doubt about resurrection and the raising of souls and bodies. They will know with certainty that this very body will be raised on the Day of Resurrection in the form of a body. They will see that resurrection means the return of this same soul and body, with the same identity and individuality. The body raised on that Day is this very body, not some other body made of different earthly matter, as some Muslim thinkers say, or a separate imaginal body, as the Illuminationists say.[^V09-302][^V09-303] This is the sound belief: it agrees with the revealed law and with reason. Whoever believes and accepts this truly believes in the Day of Judgment.

To believe less than this is to fall short of knowledge and understanding. It also means believing that most of the body's powers and natural capacities will fail to reach their proper ends, perfections, and results. It would make the natural drive toward perfection that God placed in all things pointless and wasted. Every power, in the soul and elsewhere, has a perfection of its own, with pleasures and pains, attractions and repulsions suited to it. Whatever it has gained or done brings a natural response and return, as the philosophers have established when they showed that every power, high or low, has an end toward which it moves.[^V09-304]

So compete in doing good. Wherever you are, God will bring you all together. “Each has a direction toward which they turn.” This is also what is meant by God's words: “There is no creature but He holds it by its forelock. My Lord is on a straight path.” Every natural thing is a creature in motion, as we have explained. God holds each soul and nature by its forelock, turns it toward Himself, and draws it to Him. Anyone who understands this knows that all things must return, and will not mistake the matter. This follows from wisdom, from the fulfillment of God's promise and warning, and from the natural return and recompense of things.

We have written a separate treatise on this subject, showing clearly and with sound principles that all things that come to be—including lifeless things and plants—are raised in the next life and return to God. Nothing in nature is left without an end, and nothing in creation is still. Everything moves toward its proper goal,[^V09-305] but each kind is raised in a way suited to it: human beings in their way, devils in theirs, animals in theirs, and plants and lifeless things in theirs. God says of the people: “On the Day We gather the mindful before the Most Merciful as honored guests, and drive the guilty to hell like a thirsty herd.”[^V09-306] Of devils, He says: “By your Lord, We will surely gather them and the devils.” Of animals: “When the wild beasts are gathered,”[^V09-307] and “The birds, too, are gathered; each returns to Him.” Of plants: “The stars and trees bow down.” He also says: “You see the earth barren, but when We send water down upon it, it stirs, swells, and brings forth every kind of lovely plant”—then He says that God will raise those in the graves.

God also says of all things: “On the Day We move the mountains and you see the earth laid bare, We will gather them all and leave no one behind. They will be brought before your Lord, lined up.” And: “We inherit the earth and all who are on it, and to Us they will return.” And: “As We began the first creation, so We will bring it back.”

#### Chapter 3 — Answering the doubts of those who deny the resurrection of bodies

The principles we have set out establish this conclusion and also answer the objections raised against it. It is worth setting out the objections and showing how they are answered.

One objection, mentioned earlier, asks what happens when one person becomes food for another. Are the eaten parts restored to the eater's body, or to the body of the person who was eaten? Either way, one of them would not return in full. If the eater is an unbeliever and the person eaten is a believer, then either the obedient person would be punished and the unbeliever rewarded, or the same person would be both an unbeliever who is punished and a believer who is rewarded, since they would have one body.

The answer is clear from what we have said: each person's individuality comes from their soul, not their body. A body, considered as matter, is indefinite; it receives its particular identity only through the soul. So it does not follow that when Zayd's body is raised, the matter that became food for a lion or another person must also be raised as Zayd. Whatever body is connected with Zayd's soul is the very body that was his. To believe in the resurrection of bodies means that bodies will be raised from their graves so that, on seeing each one, a person can say, “This is so-and-so himself,” or “This is the body of so-and-so,” as we have already explained.[^V09-308]

This does not mean that the body must keep the same outward appearance and identity in every detail. A person need not be raised deformed, with missing limbs, blind, or old, just as they were when they died. Reports speak of this. The theologians have all answered the objection in other ways, but those answers are too weak to be worth repeating.

Another objection says that the earth has a fixed, limited size, measured in miles and cubits, while the number of souls is unlimited. The earth cannot provide enough material for an unlimited number of bodies. The true answer follows from the principles already explained: the body's particular material is not what matters for its identity. What matters is that there be a body of some kind, whatever its dimensions. The body of the next life comes from the soul and its qualities; in this life, by contrast, the soul arises from matter with its forms and readiness.

You could also answer that one kind of matter can take on greater size and be divided into more parts. Matter, considered apart from any particular form, is simply a capacity to receive forms: it has no size of its own and is not limited to a fixed boundary or number of parts. Size and division come to it from outside, while in itself it can be divided without limit.[^V09-309] Nor does it have to keep its earthly form in order to become a body. It may change from an earthly form into other kinds of body as God wills. And not every soul must be raised in a body; some souls leave bodies behind and rise into the holy world, joining those brought near to God. The first answer is the main one.[^V09-310]

Another objection asks: if paradise and hell are physical, where are they? In what direction and place in the world do they exist? If either is beyond the outermost limit of directions, then there is a place in what has no place and a direction in what has no direction. If they are inside the layers of the heavens and earth or between them, then either one heaven must overlap another or they must be separated, and both are impossible. This also seems to conflict with the verse that says paradise is as wide as the heavens and earth.

This objection and its answer are clear to anyone who reflects on the principles we have explained. The theologians have not entered the subject through its proper starting points, so they have not been able to resolve objections of this kind. At times they say empty space is possible; at others, that paradise and hell have not yet been created; or that the heavens will split open to make room for paradise. I wish they had been content with simple faith and tradition, and had not been too proud to say, “We do not know. God and His Messenger know best.”

What is more astonishing is that many of them discuss abstract matters without knowing what can be sensed, speak about God while knowing nothing of nature, and make arguments without understanding logic or mathematics. They know little of religious learning beyond disputed questions. Their aim is not to improve the soul, train the inner self, and cleanse the heart of bad qualities and habits. They seek status and influence, and want people to submit to their legal opinions and judgments. That is why they hide hostility and oppose people of wisdom and insight. One of the greatest trials is that, with such limited understanding and shallow views, they quarrel with wise and spiritually perceptive people more fiercely than with unbelievers, Jews, or Christians. They call this defending religion and protecting Muslim belief. Their defense amounts to saying that wisdom is error and misguidance, that learning it is an innovation and a source of harm, that astrology is false at its root, that the stars are lifeless objects, and that the heavens have no life or speech. They say that learning is useless, geometry has no truth, and most natural science is unbelief and heresy, and that its students are atheists and infidels. These claims are full of deception and confusion, and most of them conflict with God's Book and the Prophet's teaching, which honor wisdom and its people, speak highly of the stars and the sky, and praise those who reflect on their creation while condemning those who turn away from God's signs.

When these people try to defend religion against questions like this, they are helpless, like a donkey stuck in mud. Let us return to the point we set aside and explain how to answer the objection about where paradise and hell are. This is the hardest objection raised by those who deny bodily resurrection and one of the strongest against the existence of paradise and hell, both of which are affirmed by the true religious law brought by the prophets and by wisdom grounded in firm principles. The objection assumes that paradise and hell must occupy a place in the extended, spatial directions of this world, and must exist at some time within its passing sequence. It assumes they are inside the heavens and under the outermost boundary of the world.

The principles we have laid out show that the whole basis of this objection is mistaken. The afterlife[^V09-311] is a complete world, containing all that belongs to its own kind of existence. A world that is complete in this way does not exist in a place. The whole of this world also has no place to which it could be pointed from inside or outside: a thing has a place only through its relation to something separate from it in position and outside it in that relation. Nothing of the same kind lies outside the whole world; otherwise, it would not be complete.

Nor does anything inside it stand apart from the whole when it is considered complete. So there is no sensory direction from which to point to the world as a whole, whether from within it or beyond it. It has no “where” or spatial position. This is why the leading philosopher says that the world as a whole has no place. A complete world cannot be assigned a place. The confusion comes from treating a part as if it were the whole, and mistaking what is incomplete for what is complete.

Even if we set this aside for a moment and ask whether this world and the next form one connected whole, the answer is still clear. If both worlds were parts of one whole, then asking where the whole is would make sense. But if each is a complete world, distinct in its essence and existence from the other, then they are not parts of one spatial whole, and the question does not apply. The truth is the second view. The worlds may be called one in another sense: they are joined and interwoven in meaning and existence, not in spatial position and extension. Each remains a complete world.

People everywhere speak of “this world” and “that world,” as they have learned from earlier generations. If the two together made up one world, this way of speaking would be false. It is not like calling the realm of the elements, the realm of the stars, and the realm of animals “worlds”; those are figurative names. If this world and the next were not each complete worlds, then there would be no complete world at all. They could form one whole only if one were the inner reality and the other its outward appearance, as we have suggested, but that is a different and subtle question.[^V09-312] Since they are distinct in existence and are not parts of some further world that contains both, each must be complete in itself. Religious teaching accordingly speaks of two worlds: this world and the next.

The afterlife is also unlike this world because it is lasting. There people speak with God, while this world is passing away and its inhabitants perish. The difference in what follows from the two worlds shows that their underlying existence differs. The prophets' conversations with God, and the Messenger's conversation with Him on the Night Journey, come from the afterlife's power becoming visible within their hearts. God says: “We will bring you into a form you do not know.” This makes clear that the life to come is unlike this life. In short, the way the afterlife exists is unlike the way this world exists. If the next life had the same basic reality as this world, then this world could not be destroyed while the next remained as the lasting home. This world is worldly by its very nature and existence, not because of temporary features that distinguish it from other things. Otherwise, every year—or even every day—would be another world, since shapes, conditions, and individual features keep changing. Resurrection would then mean rebuilding this world after its destruction, which no one believes. Everyone agrees that this world comes to an end and will never be rebuilt as it was. So the two worlds differ in the very kind of existence they have; they are not parts of one spatial whole. There is no reason to ask where the next world is. Anyone with sound judgment can see this from the arguments in this book; we have gone into detail only to help those who look for religious practice mainly as a way to satisfy bodily appetites in the next life. In truth, they seek this world while imagining that they seek the reward of the next and nearness to God.

Another objection is that bodily resurrection would amount to transmigration of souls. As we have said, this is a serious objection that is difficult to answer unless one has learned and followed our approach.

The answer given by other writers is extremely weak. No Muslim thinker, so far as we know, has offered a solution that can be relied on; some have even accepted transmigration, though its impossibility has been proved. The best-known answer appears in a treatise on resurrection. It says the human soul has two connections with the body. Its first and primary connection is with the subtle, life-giving spirit; its second is with the dense bodily organs. When the spirit's balance is disturbed and it is close to becoming unfit for the soul, the soul's secondary connection with the organs grows stronger and singles out certain parts. At resurrection, when the body is gathered and formed again and the life-giving spirit returns, the soul resumes its original connection. This secondary connection prevents a new soul from arising for those parts, so the soul that survived death returns to receive its reward.

We have already shown why this fails. A soul's connection to a body is natural and depends on the matter being fully suited and ready for that particular soul, rather than for another. That special readiness must belong to this one matter in relation to this one soul; otherwise, a soul would be assigned without a reason, or one soul would be connected to two bodies. You also know that a soul arises through the substantial development of matter: it gradually takes on higher forms until the sequence of bodily forms reaches the soul. That is why the body can no longer have the original natural bond to its scattered earthly pieces. Even apart from all we have established, anyone who understands this tradition knows that the body linked to the soul—especially the rational soul, the highest stage of bodily nobility and perfection on its return toward the Active Intellect—must have a special readiness, balance, and subtle life-giving spirit, like a heavenly body.[^V09-313][^V09-314]

Natural bonds that belong to a thing in itself are not like voluntary bonds a person forms for some outside benefit, arbitrary desire, or habit. Someone may turn back toward a ruined house where they once lived, and try to rebuild it because they remember the pleasures they enjoyed there, or because they see some benefit in it. None of this applies to a natural bond between a thing and its end.

When someone says that the soul is linked first to subtle spirits and second to dense organs, this means that its essential connection is only to those subtle spirits, which are more like the soul in their nature. The soul is connected to the dense organs only indirectly, because they are like the shell, covering, and vessel that protects the subtle body. That body has the balance and delicacy needed to be inhabited by the holy dove and the bird of the spiritual realm. When the net is torn apart, turns to dust and ash, and the heavenly bird escapes from that narrow space, what connection remains between it and the scattered pieces and particles? What bond could the soul have to dust and ash, even if the pieces were somehow gathered again? If it did, then every kind of dust and ash would have a soul, since they are all alike in being dust and ash. Nor does the soul remain linked to those parts because they once belonged to it: the past time in which the connection existed no longer exists.

**A revealing note.** Al-Ghazali says in several places that bodily resurrection means that the soul, once separated from its body, becomes connected to another body. He rejects the return of the first body's parts. He argues that old Zayd is the same person as young Zayd, who was the same person as the infant in the womb, even though the matter did not remain the same. Resurrection, he says, is like this. Those who insist that the parts return are merely following others without understanding.[^V09-315]

I say that this statement is far too brief, and it does not show how resurrection differs from transmigration. We have explained that true resurrection is the return of the very same body with the very same soul, just as clear religious teaching says without reinterpretation and sound reason confirms without evasion. Al-Ghazali then says that this is not transmigration: resurrection brings back the first person, while transmigration produces another person. He says the difference is this: if a soul becomes connected to a new body and the first person is thereby restored, that is resurrection, not transmigration.[^V09-316]

But his account of bodily resurrection—as the return of the person without the body's return—seems to contradict his statement that a person is the soul and body together.[^V09-317] His distinction between transmigration and resurrection is even more puzzling. He simply declares that the second person in transmigration is different, while in resurrection it is the same person. That distinction is unsupported, since the soul remains one in transmigration too and is what makes a person the same individual.[^V09-318] Stranger still, elsewhere he says that the soul returns to a body other than the first, with none of the same parts.[^V09-319]

He then says: “If someone objects that this is transmigration, we concede the name; there is no dispute over names. Religious teaching permits this kind of transmigration and forbids another.”[^V09-320] Many learned people have accepted this. Perhaps they think the difficulty of resurrection is only that it would be called transmigration, as a matter of meaning or terminology. Or perhaps they think the problem is that two souls would be together in one body, whether the process is called transmigration or resurrection. Elsewhere he says: “Those who deny bodily resurrection also reject the return of souls to bodies. But no certain proof establishes that this is impossible. The earlier philosophers' arguments against it are not sound proofs, and religious teaching says it will happen, so we must believe it.”[^V09-321]

He argues that this was not proved impossible because Avicenna, the greatest of the later philosophers, said in *The Salvation* and *The Healing* that it is possible for a heavenly body to become the object of a soul's imagination after death. He also reports the view of a highly respected scholar who said it was not impossible. Ghazali takes this as evidence that Avicenna doubted the principle and had no proof of its impossibility. If Avicenna had thought it impossible, he would not have called its supporter careful in speech. Someone might reply that he said this only to be polite or cautious, since in his discussion of transmigration in *On the Soul* he says that one soul cannot move between bodies. But that argument against transmigration is also meant to disprove bodily resurrection.[^V09-322]

I say that Avicenna's argument against transmigration is not a sound proof either. He says that if a soul returned to a body and the body became ready to receive it, a new soul would flow from the Giver of Forms, since matter that is ready is entitled by its nature to receive a form. The new soul would join the returning soul, leaving one body with two souls, which is impossible. This argument could also be used against the resurrection of bodies, but it is weak. One could answer that bodies may have different degrees of readiness: matter might be suited to the soul that was already connected to it and need no new soul. If two drops of semen in a womb were ready at the same time to receive souls, two souls would flow from the Giver of Forms, each belonging to one of them. A soul is not inside a body as an accident is inside its subject. Rather, it is linked to one body because the qualities of that body suit it more than the other. If two similar souls can be assigned this way, why could the same not be true of souls that have left bodies? If the returning soul suited the body and received its due, why should the Giver of Forms send a new soul as well? Avicenna's own argument, then, does not prove that bodily resurrection is impossible.[^V09-323][^V09-324]

I say that the impossibility of transmigration has been proved, as we explained earlier. As for what Avicenna allowed, and reported from scholars he considered careful, it means only that a heavenly body may serve as a setting in which the soul's familiar forms and desires appear.[^V09-325] He states clearly that this does not mean the soul separated from its body becomes the soul of that heavenly body. Otherwise, that body would have two souls, which is impossible both in itself and even according to those who believe in transmigration. How could a thinker like Avicenna allow something no reasonable person accepts? What we have explained and established is a different matter, and a deep, subtle path that many people may overlook.[^V09-326]

Nor does the claim that bodies have different degrees of readiness solve the problem of transmigration. If we reflect carefully, it makes the problem clearer.[^V09-327] Matter becomes ready to receive a form only after it has taken on a prior form that prepares it for the next. That prior form is the closest thing to the form that follows. Matter becomes ready for a soul only after it has passed through all the lower forms and stages of readiness. This is supported by proof, experience, and direct awareness. For this reason, understanding how bodies are raised is harder than these arguments suggest. The way to know bodily resurrection is closed to anyone who does not follow our method—the path of those devoted to God and firmly grounded in knowledge and faith, who bring direct insight together with proof and draw wisdom from the light of prophecy. God is most generous.

Another objection says that restoring the dead for no purpose would be pointless and arbitrary, which is unworthy of a wise God. If the purpose were for God, it would imply that He has some lack, which must be denied of Him. If it were for human beings, it would imply inability and conflict with wisdom and justice. If the purpose were to inflict pain, that would be unworthy of a wise and just being, let alone the Creator of wisdom and justice. If the purpose were to give pleasure, then pleasures—especially sensory pleasures—would only relieve pain. A full person does not enjoy food, however tasty; only a hungry person enjoys it. The same applies to other bodily pleasures. Scholars and physicians say that they all relieve pain.[^V09-328] It would follow, then, that God first makes a person suffer so that He can give them bodily pleasure. That would not be wise or just. Who would claim to do someone a kindness by cutting off one of their limbs, wounding them, and then applying ointments so that they could enjoy the relief?

The Ash'arites answer that God's actions do not need a purpose, and that it is not wrong for them to have none. They also deny that the only purpose could be to give pleasure or pain, and deny that pleasure is simply relief from pain. Finally, they say that afterlife pleasures are not like worldly pleasures, so they need not be relief from pain. The Mu'tazilites answer that the purpose of resurrection is for people to receive their reward and for the truth of the prophets to become clear.[^V09-329]

I say that we have already explained the difference between an aim that follows by nature and a purpose that fulfills a need. Actions have natural ends and necessary consequences. Death is a transition from this mode of life to a higher one. The pleasures and pains of the next life are consequences of what souls have chosen and acquired, whether through deliberate action or habit. God is above being affected by the goodness of those who obey or the evil of those who do wrong. God's rewarding the blessed and punishing the condemned is not like the actions of kings and rulers, who have needs and are affected by what their friends and enemies do. They honor a friend to gain respect and avoid shame, and take revenge on an enemy to satisfy their anger. God's action toward people is more like a doctor's care for the sick: the doctor tells them to avoid harmful things and take medicine, and warns them not to neglect their care or do things that worsen the illness. Those who follow the doctor's instructions recover and are saved.

Those who rebel suffer and perish; the doctor is unaffected by whether the patient obeys or disobeys. In the same way, souls in this world are like patients, the earth is a place of illness, and the prophets are doctors sent by God. Some people obey; others do not. “God is free of the idolaters, and so is His Messenger.” “The truth is from your Lord: whoever wishes may believe, and whoever wishes may disbelieve.”

The pleasures and pains of the next life are not of the same kind as pleasures and pains here. Worldly pleasures are effects the soul receives from outside and is changed by. Afterlife pleasures are the soul's joy in its own being, its qualities, and its actions, precisely because they belong to it. Acting and being acted upon are different categories and share no essential feature. So the soul's active joy is different in kind and definition from the passive pleasure it feels here; the two cannot be measured against each other.[^V09-330]

In paradise the soul has every kind of pleasure in its fullest form. The senses, too, have pleasures of a higher, fuller, and nobler kind. These rest on leaving behind the passing pleasures of this world, which can turn into pain and harm if we become too absorbed in them. A person should refine and clear the soul, turn away from worldly attachment, and build the power to recognize good and long for lasting happiness. Then, when potential has become active, the incomplete has become complete, and the soul has emerged from its coverings, shells, graves, and tombs, all that is within it will be revealed. Everything it hopes for and desires will be present before it. At the Resurrection, the soul encounters what it has intended and sought. If its aims were sound and true, it will win a great victory. If it desired passing, impossible things—bodily cravings, anger, or Satanic fantasies—those desires will fail, the forms they took will break apart, and their existence will come to an end. What remains is grief, regret, bitterness, and painful suffering, because the soul had clung to them and grown used to them.

You know how the whole world and everything in it fades beside the next life, like ice melting when the sun rises and its light and heat grow strong. Those attached to worldly pleasures are like bats that have grown comfortable in the dark. When the sun of the next life rises at the dawn of Resurrection, after setting in the horizon of the bodies that come to be in the night of this world, they are seized by a fear and confusion like that of bats in daylight. This comparison is meant to make the matter clearer, or to help minds too limited to grasp the reality of different worlds and modes of existence. The claim itself is proved by reason and does not depend on an image. The same is true of the comparisons in the Qur'an and the teachings of the prophets: they help ordinary people understand the afterlife.[^V09-331][^V09-332]

Perhaps complete philosophers used examples in their books on logic for the same reason: people with limited understanding can grasp the spirit of an idea only when it is shown through something concrete that resembles it. Each individual human being is a concrete likeness of human nature. Natural things we sense are images of their intellectual kinds. This world and everything in it is an image of the next life and its contents, because the worlds correspond to one another.
