If we try to describe another mode of life to someone who has not yet entered it or moved beyond the life they have now, we can do so only through examples. True religious laws therefore use such examples, since they are addressed to everyone.

**A correction and a conclusion.** Some Muslim philosophers, after opening the door to allegorical interpretation, have reinterpreted clear verses about the resurrection of bodies and turned descriptions of bodily events in the afterlife into descriptions of spiritual states. They say these teachings address ordinary people, rough Arabs, and Jews, who cannot understand spiritual realities, and that Arabic is full of metaphors and figures of speech. It is surprising that this philosopher, like most philosophers, overlooked the existence of another bodily world, with afterlife bodies, shapes, and qualities, as we have described. How could he then interpret clear Qur'anic statements about bodily resurrection as referring only to intellectual realities, while still believing in the Messenger and the Qur'an? The reason, I say, is that he could not prove this subject by reason, or even show that it was possible, because it is so difficult to understand. Anyone who reflects on what we have explained can see this. He was therefore driven to allegorical interpretation, as other Muslim scholars have been when faced with verses about the beginning of creation that seem to describe God in human or physical terms. What he did does not make him an unbeliever, just as similar interpretations by others do not make them unbelievers.[^V09-333]

Some later thinkers objected that if this philosopher were right, then the guide who leads people to truth would actually mislead them and teach them falsehood. Ordinary Arabs and Jews, he says, understand these words only in their outward sense, which he claims is not intended. That would undermine guidance and contradict the claim that the Prophet is truthful and the Qur'an is true.

I see two problems with this objection. First, the Qur'an contains benefits for this life and the next: “Provision for you and your livestock.” Its benefits in the next life need not go to those who are coarse and hard of heart. They belong to people who have become pure and open-hearted. The foolish people just mentioned are far from guidance or misguidance alike, as God says: “We have created for Hell many of the jinn and humankind…” Second, the outward descriptions in the Qur'an would mislead only if they were not examples and accounts that picture realities and represent the events of the next life. But that is their purpose. Otherwise, every use of an example would be misleading.[^V09-334]

Another objection comes from most natural philosophers and materialists who imitate philosophers. They say that humans and other things made from the elements arise only through heavenly causes, earthly material, the ordering of celestial movements, and material changes that take a long time. This is the limit of their knowledge: their view has not risen beyond the natural world to another mode of existence in which forms arise through active causes alone. They deny that things can come into being in any way except through movement and the preparation of matter. They do not understand that human beings have two modes of existence, so they judge the second life by the rules of the first.

God has repeatedly shown in the Qur'an that bringing about the next life and giving afterlife things their existence is like recreating this world at the beginning, through God's own act of giving existence: “As We began the first creation, so We will bring it back.” The heavens, stars, and elements did not come into being through movement, time, or preparation by receptive matter; they came through God's care. The same is true of the next life. Its beings are forms that exist on their own, without matter. Their only source is the Giver of Forms. Those forms exist through the one who gives them, not in a material recipient, as we have explained many times.

Another objection asks whether some bodies will be raised or all of them. If only some, then some would be chosen without reason, since everyone is subject to reward and punishment. If all are raised, then people's bodies, records, and reckoning would crowd one another. God alludes to this when the deniers ask: “Will we really be raised, along with our earliest forefathers?” He removes their doubt through the words He taught His Prophet: “Say: the first and the last will all be gathered at the appointed time of a known Day.”[^V09-335] God shows that bodies do not crowd one another in the mode of existence they have in the next life. Those forms do not exist in matter fixed in place and direction; they arise from the soul's own acts of imagination, just as the images in our minds do not crowd one another. They exist in a different way.

The time appointed for the next life and the Hour of Resurrection is also a known Day with God and with those among His servants who know. Minds veiled from the next life and bound to the places and times of this world cannot grasp it. Our present senses are not suited to perceive the realities of the next life. The events of Resurrection are hidden from this human world, and no one can fully understand them while still bound by the senses and the limits of imagination. Most objections to resurrection come from judging the next life by the rules of this one.

When the deniers ask, “When will this promise be fulfilled, if you are truthful?”, they ask a question that cannot be answered in the terms they expect. If the Hour lies outside the sequence of time, and comes about through an act of creation as swift as the blink of an eye—or swifter—then “when?” cannot be answered by naming a particular worldly time. The only brief answer is that its knowledge belongs to God. It is like a blind person asking how colors are perceived after we describe them. The right answer is that sight knows them. The right answer to those veiled from the next life who ask when the Hour will come is God's own answer: “Say: its knowledge is with God”; “With Him is the knowledge of the Hour.” Whoever is freed from the veil of this world, returns to God, and is gathered into His presence will necessarily know the reality of the Hour.[^V09-336] This is why the most knowledgeable of people said: “The Hour will not come while anyone on the face of the earth is saying, ‘God, God.’” Whoever remains on the face of the earth has not yet been gathered to God.[^V09-337] Resurrection lies within the veils of the heavens and earth, and this world stands in relation to it as a womb stands to the child inside it. Until the human soul is born a second time and leaves the womb of this world and the body's placenta, it has not reached the open space of the next life and the kingdom of the heavens and earth. As Christ said: “No one enters the kingdom of heaven unless they are born twice.” The fully realized knowers undergo this second birth through a chosen death; others undergo it through natural death. As long as a traveler remains outside the veils of the heavens and earth, the Hour has not risen for them, because it lies within those veils. God is within the veils; with Him is the hidden reality of the heavens and earth and the knowledge of the Hour.

Once the traveler has passed through these veils and has settled into the presence of God, the hidden reality of the Hour becomes open to them, and knowledge becomes direct sight.[^V09-338]

#### Chapter 4 — The true grave: a garden of paradise or a pit of Fire

I have already explained that death is real. Natural death marks the end of our turning away from this world and the beginning of our return to God.[^V09-339] When the spirit leaves the natural body, it remains connected to the body in some way. This connection is not to the body's material parts, as some later thinkers claimed—a view we have already disproved. Rather, it is to the body as a whole: to its form and structure as preserved in memory. When the soul leaves the body, it takes with it the power that perceives particular meanings on its own and bodily forms with the help of imagination. We have shown that the imaginative power exists independently and survives the body. It needs the physical body at the beginning of life, but not in order to continue. After leaving the body, it can perceive particular things and bodily forms just as it did in this life.[^V09-340]

When someone dies, the soul imagines itself as having left this world. It pictures that same person who died and was buried, in the form they had at death, and finds their body in the grave. It feels the pains that reach it as bodily punishments, as the true religions teach. This is the punishment of the grave. If the soul is blessed, it imagines the promised feasts in forms suited to it and in keeping with what it believed: gardens, rivers, orchards, young attendants, companions, and a cup of pure drink. This is the reward of the grave. The Prophet said, “The grave is either a garden from the gardens of paradise or a pit from the pits of Fire.” The true grave consists of these forms, and its punishment and reward are what we have described.[^V09-341]

Resurrection is the soul's emergence from the haze of these forms, like a child leaving the secure place of the womb. The difference between the state of the grave and the state after resurrection is like the difference between a person in the womb and that same person after birth. The grave offers a first glimpse of the events of Resurrection. But because the soul has only just left this world, its power to reveal the next life in full has not yet grown strong within it.[^V09-342]

As long as the soul remains in this weak state, with an awareness like that of someone asleep, it is said to be in the world of the grave and the intermediate state. This also explains the pressure of the grave, even when the dead body lies still in the earth or is in the air or water. What directly hurts and affects the soul is not the physical event that happens to the body, but the form of that event as it reaches the soul through its connection with the body. If the same form reached the soul by another means, without a physical cause, it would affect it in the same way for as long as the soul remained connected to the body—whether or not that body still existed. The pressure and punishment of the grave work in this way, as do its reward and comfort. The grave's spaciousness or narrowness depends on whether the soul feels open and at ease or constricted.

#### Chapter 5 — A brief note on the punishment of the grave

One of the scholars said: Anyone who looked within with the light of insight would see the self filled with harmful things and predators: desire, anger, deceit, envy, resentment, self-admiration, and showing off. These keep tearing at the soul whenever it loses sight of them, even for a moment. Most people cannot see them. But when the veil is lifted and a person is placed in the grave, they see these traits take on forms that match their meanings. Scorpions and snakes surround them. These are the person's own habits and qualities, now present in the soul and revealed in their true inner forms. Every meaning has a fitting form. This is the punishment of the grave for the unhappy; for the blessed, the opposite is true.[^V09-343]

#### Chapter 6 — What remains of the human being

We have said that when the soul leaves the body, something of the body remains, though it exists only in a weak way. A report from the Prophet calls it the “tailbone,” and scholars have disagreed about what this means. Some say it is the material intellect; others say it is matter itself. Al-Ghazali says it is the soul, from which the life of the next world grows.[^V09-344] Abu Yazid al-Waqwaqi says it is an indivisible particle that remains unchanged. Ibn Arabi says in *The Meccan Revelations* that it is the person's fixed, individual essence. The theologians say it is the body's original parts. We say it is the power of imagination: the last level of existence reached in this life through the natural, plant, and animal powers as they arise one after another in human matter, and the first level reached in the next life.

Here is why. Nothing from this world—not its matter, forms, or powers—can pass into the next life unchanged. It must first undergo change and pass through new stages of existence. A person becomes ready for resurrection only through a completing power: the final form reached by the natural powers and material forms. A human being develops through the elemental and lifeless stages, then plant life, then animal life. The senses arise in order: first touch, then taste, smell, hearing, and sight.

After the earliest elemental and lifeless stages, the first power to arise is nutrition. Next comes touch, through which we sense qualities such as heat and cold. Then come the powers that sense taste, smell, sound, color, and other visible things such as light. Along with the senses comes the power that moves us toward what we want and away from what we dislike.

At last, a power arises that can retain images from the five outer senses after the senses no longer perceive them. It keeps these images from vanishing even when the external material things that held them—like bodies for those images—have decayed. The images it preserves exist in a different way from the things they represent, just as the human body exists in a different way from the spirit that survives its decay. This power remains after the body has fallen apart. It can serve as the basis of the next life because it is the final form reached by forms in this world and the completion of the natural powers that arose one after another. It is also the first basis for the forms and powers of the next life, which differ in rank and closeness to the Active Principle. This power can join this world and the next: it is the roof of this world and the floor of the next.

#### Chapter 7 — The basis of the next life's lasting forms

The finer a thing's underlying matter is, the more readily it can take on a form. The simpler and more spiritual it is, the nobler the forms it can receive. We have already explained that matter and form are joined as one, like a general class and the feature that sets a kind apart. So they must have a close likeness that allows them to unite.[^V09-345] Here is an example. Fresh water is finer than earth, so it changes more readily and takes on shapes, colors, tastes, and other qualities more easily. Air is finer and nobler than both, and flows more freely, so it changes faster and takes on what it can receive more easily. Its forms—smells and sounds—are finer than those taken in by earth and water. Rays and light receive forms still faster, at once; the colors and shapes they receive are more spiritual and closer to the world of spirit.[^V09-346]

Likewise, the life-giving spirit that rises from the heart's hollow to the brain's hollow is finer and nobler than the three things just mentioned. It takes in all the qualities, tastes, smells, and colors they do, but in a finer and nobler way. Here the fineness of natural bodily matter reaches its limit.[^V09-347] Many who study the sciences overlook this point.[^V09-348] They do not give it close attention. If they did, they would see that the soul has levels of strength and weakness, and of greater or lesser subtlety. Even the soul's least subtle level is far subtler than physical light. That is why the soul can receive impressions of everything sensed, imagined, and understood, as these arise through the levels of sense, imagination, and thought. The first basis ready to receive the next life's forms is the soul's power of imagination. Those forms take shape in it, apart from the matter they came from.

There are other differences between what underlies worldly forms and what underlies the forms of the next life. Worldly forms arise only after matter has been prepared over time and made ready by suitable conditions and outside causes. The basis of the next life is different: its forms flow from the First Giver through the soul's own inner qualities, causes, and powers.[^V09-349] When these have become firm, the forms appear as a necessary result. Another difference is that if a worldly form is removed from matter, an outside cause must act on the matter again to restore it. For example, once whiteness or sweetness has left something, the thing cannot restore it by itself. But if an image disappears from the soul, the soul does not need new learning or effort to bring it back. Its own nature and inner make-up are enough. This is what God points to when He says, “That day, each person will have enough to occupy them.” It shows that outside chance causes will not operate in that world. Creation will flow from what is within, from the First Source—not from outside events and tools that only prepare matter through change, as they do here. God also says, “Sovereignty that day belongs to God,” and, “There will be no family ties among them.”[^V09-350]

#### Chapter 8 — Resurrection

Resurrection is the soul's emergence from the haze of the forms surrounding it, like an embryo leaving its secure place in the womb. As we have already said, the true grave is the soul's being sunk and confined, after the body's death, among the forms that surround it. Between death and resurrection, the soul is like an embryo or a sleeping person: its powers and senses are not yet strong enough to perceive the things of the next life.[^V09-351] When the time to rise comes, the human being emerges from this state and moves toward God, turning toward the Divine Presence. One person comes free of attachments and the prison of desire, joyful in their own being and glad to meet God: “Whoever longs to meet God, God longs to meet them.” Another comes held fast by forces that restrain and overpower them, unwilling to meet God: “Whoever dislikes meeting God, God dislikes meeting them.”

#### Chapter 9 — How people are gathered

We have already said that in this world human beings are one kind, and its members are alike. In the next life, however, they become countless kinds. The soul's form is a basis that can receive many different forms of the next life, according to the habits and traits a person has acquired. On the Day of Resurrection, God clothes each soul in forms that fit those traits, and the person is gathered in those forms and through them.[^V09-352] God says, “When the Trumpet is blown, you will come in crowds.” People will be gathered in different ways according to their actions, habits, and beliefs. Some will come as honored guests: “On the day We gather the mindful to the Most Merciful as honored guests.” Others will be brought for punishment: “On the day He gathers the enemies of God to the Fire.” Some will be raised blind; some with blue faces; some with chains around their necks, dragged through boiling water and then thrown into the Fire. Others will be honored and welcomed: “Peace be upon you. You have done well, so enter and stay forever,” and “Enter in peace, safe and secure.” Still others will be seized and bound: “Take him and chain him, then burn him in the Fire.”

Then put him in a chain seventy cubits long.[^V09-353] In short, each person is gathered to the end of their effort and action, and to what they loved and desired. If someone loved a stone, they would be gathered with it. God says, “You and what you worship besides God are fuel for Hell,” and, “Gather those who did wrong together with their pairs.”[^V09-354] Repeated actions create habits. Any habit that takes hold of the soul appears at Resurrection in a form that suits it: “Everyone acts according to their own way.” The rejected and unhappy people act from low aims, at the level of animal life between this world and the next. Their thoughts are limited to animal desire and the anger of beasts of prey, which rule their souls. So they are gathered in the forms of those animals. The visible bodies of the next life give outward form to the soul's inner habits and qualities. God points to this when He says, “When wild beasts are gathered,” and, “Company of jinn, you have drawn many human beings to yourselves.”[^V09-355]

The Prophet also said, “People will be gathered in the forms of their intentions,” and, “Some people will be gathered in a form beside which monkeys and pigs look beautiful.” Here is a further point about the next life. Within each person's physical body is an inner animal form, complete with its own limbs, powers, and senses. It exists in its own right and does not die when the physical body dies. It is this form that is gathered on the Day of Resurrection, in a shape that suits its inner meaning; it is the one that receives reward or punishment. Its life is not like that of the physical body, whose life comes from outside and is only temporary. Its life is like the soul's own life. It stands between the animal life of the mind and the animal life of the senses. At Resurrection it takes the shape of the habits and traits the soul formed through its own actions.[^V09-356][^V09-357][^V09-358] This also explains the meaning of transmigration as reported by ancient philosophers such as Plato, Socrates, and Pythagoras, as we have already mentioned. It also explains what the prophets taught and the verses that point to transmigration.

God says, “When the sentence falls upon them, We will bring out for them a creature from the earth that will speak to them, because people did not believe in Our signs.” He also says, “On that day We will gather from every community a group of those who denied Our signs, and they will be held back,” and, “On that day they will be divided.” All these verses point to a change in the very being of souls. They emerge from silent groups and communities, and from the graves when their contents are brought out and what is in people's hearts is revealed. They appear in different forms: predators, harmful creatures, herd animals, wild beasts, and devils.

Here is a reminder that may help explain this. There are three worlds or stages of life. The first is the world of nature and matter, where things come into being and then decay. The second is the middle world of forms with size and shape, which can be sensed but have no physical matter.

The third is the world of intellectual forms and patterns that exist apart from matter. The first world passes away; the other two remain, especially the third. It is the home of those who have reached full maturity and the return of those nearest to God. A human being can potentially gather all three worlds within themselves through three ways of awareness: the senses, which begin in nature; imagination, which begins in the soul; and thought, which begins in the intellect.

When the human soul first comes into being, all three ways of awareness are only potential. Before it exists, it lies hidden in possibility, as God says, “I created you before, when you were nothing,” and, “Was there not a time when the human being was nothing to be mentioned?” Whatever begins as a possibility must develop gradually in its existence and abilities, rising from lower to higher and from weaker to stronger. In each of the three worlds, it passes through three levels: potential, readiness, and full ability. Writing offers an example. A young child has the potential to write. An adult who has gathered the tools and learned what is needed, but has not yet learned to write, is ready. A skilled writer has the ability and can write whenever they wish, without learning the skill again, because it has become firmly established. That ability may still be blocked by something outside the person, just as a healthy eye cannot see when a covering is placed over it. When the covering is removed, the person can use the ability freely. The same levels apply to the senses: a fetus has them only as potential; a newborn child has the needed organs and is ready to use them; and a growing child reaches the full ability to sense.

Imagination and reason develop in a similar way. The mind begins with the ability to understand, develops the readiness to exercise it, and comes to understand fully. The traditional names for these stages distinguish an intellect that has not yet developed, one that has begun to understand, and the higher intellect from which understanding comes. Across these stages a person moves from an undeveloped ability through readiness to understanding, and then beyond. These levels arise one after another according to a person's nature. Someone dominated by one of the three kinds of development—sensory, of the soul, or intellectual—will end up in the world that matches it, with its conditions and consequences. Someone dominated by the senses, who seeks bodily pleasures and familiar things in this world, will after death be caught in anguish and painful torment. This is not because the world and its pleasures are unreal. Rather, they pass quickly. They are effects the soul experiences when they arise, and they soon fade. What remains is the mark they leave and the habit of loving and longing for them. Whoever becomes attached to them is like someone who loves something that no longer exists and desperately seeks what cannot be found. With no beloved to meet and no result to gain, they remain in lasting anguish. This is also the state of those who long for worldly things, though while they live in this world they mistake them for lasting realities. They eat and enjoy themselves like cattle, and the Fire is their home.

When the sun of the next life rises and stands, the shapes of these passing things disappear in its light, just as shadows fade and ice melts in the summer sun.[^V09-359][^V09-360] The person who loved the world and its physical pleasures is left burning in the Fire, in painful torment, and is gathered to the home of ruin and the refuge of the wicked. But if a person's strongest hope is for the next life, and they long for what God and His Messenger promised—paradise, its joy, its companions, and its palaces—and fear the pain and fire of Hell, then act on that promise and warning, their end is to reach the joys of paradise and escape the Fire. If the power of thought rules them, and they grow through understanding pure ideas and learning lasting truths by proof and sound reasoning, they will reach the world of divine forms and lights, join the ranks of those nearest to God, and stand among the highest angels—provided their beliefs are joined to true renunciation, free from selfish motives, personal desire, worldly cravings, and anything that distracts the heart from God. This is a great gift. True renunciation and pure intention, free of selfish aims, can be achieved only by the last group: those who know God deeply, not by people who worship without understanding.[^V09-361] The true purpose of worship is to free the heart from distractions, turn fully toward the First Source, and long for God's pleasure. How can someone long for and turn toward the First Source and the place of God's honor if they do not know or picture them?

Someone may object: “Your argument makes a circle. Longing for God and following the path toward Him depend on knowing Him, but knowing Him depends on following that path and longing for Him.” I reply: “Yes. Knowledge is the first and the last, the beginning and the end. But this is not an impossible circle, because knowledge can be weak or strong. The knowledge that first leads a person to act may be only a likely belief or a received teaching that settles in the soul. This can still lead to good action. The final knowledge, reached after a person acts and follows the straight path, is different: it is direct awareness and a real connection with what is known. Sufis call this ‘fading away in God's oneness.’”

#### Chapter 10 — The human soul passes through many resurrections, before and after this body comes into being

We have already said that the human soul has a way of existing before it becomes a soul in this world, as reports from the Prophet's family teach and as Plato famously believed. Some followers of Aristotle's school criticized Plato, as their books record, but they did not understand what he meant. They thought he meant that the soul existed as a soul, in the same state it has in this life, before the body. That would lead to transmigration, to one soul shared by everyone, or to many individuals of one kind with nothing outside them to distinguish one from another.[^V09-362] We have already shown that this was not Plato's claim. Before the soul becomes a human, thinking soul, it has other ways of existing. Some belong to this life: animal life and the natural powers of plants and other things. The changes from one of these states to another take place through time and movement, in matter that changes, comes into being, and passes away. Other states come before this life: some belong to the world of images, some to intellect, and some to God's decree. The transitions there are a downward flow of existence through God's giving and creation, without time or movement. After this life come further stages: animal life through the senses, life in imagination, and sacred intellectual life.[^V09-363] Anyone who reflects on what we have explained will see that the human being passes through many resurrections.

One thing that partly supports this is what Ibn Arabi says in chapter 284 of *The Meccan Revelations*. He writes: “God created the human spirit to govern a visible, bodily form, whether in this world, the intermediate world, the next life, or anywhere else.[^V09-364] Its first form was the one in which it pledged to acknowledge God's lordship. From there, it passed into this world's physical form and remained within it from the fourth month of the body's formation in the womb until death. When it dies, it passes into another form and stays there until the questioning. At the time of questioning, it is gathered into the form of its body—the body that people see as dead—and the spirit gives it life. Ordinary people's senses do not detect this, except for those whom God has granted insight, such as a prophet or a saint.[^V09-365] After the questioning, the spirit passes into another form in the intermediate world and remains there. That form is the intermediate world itself.[^V09-366] Sleep and death are alike in this respect, until the trumpet of resurrection is blown. The person then leaves that form and is gathered into the bodily form they left in this world, if they still have questions to answer. If they do not belong to that group, they take the form in which they enter paradise.[^V09-367] Those who are questioned on the Day of Judgment take the form of paradise or Hell once their questioning is over. Everyone in Hell is questioned.[^V09-368] When the people of paradise have entered and settled there, they are called to see God. They take on a form fit for that vision.[^V09-369] When they return, they take on a form fit for life in paradise again. With each new form, they forget the one before and are changed to match the form they enter.

“When a person enters the market of paradise and sees the forms there, whichever one they admire, they are gathered into that form.[^V09-370] They remain in paradise forever, passing from one form to another without end. This shows the endless reach of God's giving. They take from the market only forms that suit the way God will appear to them in the future; each form prepares them for that meeting. This is one of the deepest points of knowledge of God.

“If you could see clearly, you would know that this is already happening to you. With every breath you take, you are gathered into a form that matches your present state. What hides this from you is your familiar way of seeing things. You do feel the changes in your outer and inner life, but you do not know that each is a form your spirit enters, one that those who know can see clearly.[^V09-371] The one who knows God brings their Resurrection forward into this life of responsibility: they weigh their actions and call themselves to account here, before moving on, as the religious teaching urges: ‘Take account of yourselves before you are called to account.’ I have seen a great truth in this and have benefited from it.”

I have quoted Ibn Arabi at length because his account contains valuable points and agrees with much of what we have said about the ways people are gathered. We differ where the mystics rely only on personal experience. We do not rely fully on anything unless reason proves it with certainty, and we do not include it in our books on philosophy.

**A reminder.** My seeker of God, these bodily things and material forms are signs that point to spiritual realities and God's settled commands.[^V09-372] They point from an effect to its cause, from a result to its purpose, from what is incomplete to what completes it, and from an image to its reality and meaning. As we have learned, the worlds match one another closely. They are all stages on the way to God. They are also forms through which God's names appear. Those names are many when we consider their meanings, but one in their real being, with no difference between them. As explained earlier, they first appear in the world of sacred intellects, God's lights and complete knowledge; then in the soul's forms and images; and finally in material forms that take up space.

Since existence flows down from its highest source through these stages in this order, our return and resurrection must follow the same steps in reverse.

#### Chapter 11 — A person passes through countless resurrections

God has made the levels of sensing, imagining, and thinking into steps and ladders that a seeker climbs toward Him. The seeker must first descend into the world of physical things perceived by the senses, then into the world of images without matter, perceived by imagination as sense perception gives way to it. From there comes the world of forms beyond matter, as imagination matures into reason. Each of these three worlds has many levels, differing in how fine or dense they are.[^V09-373] The higher the world, the more levels it contains and the wider the gap between its lowest and highest. A person cannot reach even the lowest level of the final world without passing through all the levels of the middle world.

A person cannot reach even the lowest level of the middle world without passing through all the levels of the first world, the world of physical things perceived by the senses. All the worlds and their levels are stations on the journey to God. At each station, the seeker takes off one covering and puts on another; they undergo death and resurrection there, then are gathered into the next station. The number of these deaths, resurrections, and gatherings is beyond counting. Some say it is as many as the breaths we take. We consider this correct, because we have proved that no natural substance stands still—least of all the human soul, which changes within itself as it moves toward the next life and then toward the presence of God. The soul must first pass through the lowest levels of sense, then rise step by step until it leaves them behind. God says, “There is none of you who will not come to it. This is a decree your Lord has made.”[^V09-374]

Then God saves the mindful and leaves the wrongdoers kneeling there. By entering the changing world of nature, each person becomes liable to the Fire unless their nature changes and the harm in it is repaired by doing good and leaving selfish desire. God says, “Those who turn back, believe, and do good deeds—God will change their bad deeds into good ones.” Someone may say that this conflicts with the Prophet's saying, “Every child is born with a sound nature.” I reply that there is no conflict. That saying speaks of the spirit, which comes from a world of purity. We are speaking of the body's first formation and the soul's entry into it. The body's nature comes from material things, whose existence is low, dark, and far from God and His realm. A report from the Prophet says that God has not looked at bodies since He created them. What we have said also clears up the apparent conflict between two well-known reports: “Every child is born with a sound nature,” and “The fortunate person is fortunate in the womb, and the unhappy person is unhappy in the womb.”[^V09-375]

Then He sprinkled them with His light, as the report says.

My dear seeker, just as you must enter this world of the senses, you must also learn about the things you sense if you are to leave this world and its attachments behind. You can turn away from them only by giving them up, and you cannot give them up until you know how low and limited they are. This world and the next are linked as lower and higher, base and noble, first and last. Knowing one of these linked things helps you know the other; not knowing one means you do not know the other. If you do not know how low this world is, you cannot know the worth of the next. And if you do not know the worth of the next, how can you choose it or long for it? For this and other reasons, philosophers studied natural bodies and the things we sense, so they could move from them to what lies beyond the senses and beyond nature. God says, “You know how you were first created. Why, then, do you not remember?”

#### Chapter 12 — A reminder that death and resurrection are real

As we have explained throughout this book, every real thing has a natural movement toward the next life and a built-in longing for the sacred world. Its very nature is a kind of worship that draws it toward God.[^V09-376] This is especially true of human beings, the noblest kind among things that come into being and pass away. As we have often said, each person changes from within: from their first natural life to the end of bodily life, then through the stages of the soul, and finally to the end of intellectual life. The soul first seeks to complete this life of the senses and furnish the body's realm with powers and tools. The body is like a mount or a ship carrying the soul on its journey toward God, across the land of bodies and the sea of spirits.

When this first stage is complete, the soul leaves it and begins to build a second life in a station closer to its source and goal. It continues step by step, completing its inner self and strengthening its existence through God's care. As its spiritual life grows stronger, its outward form weakens and its life through the senses fades. When its inner movement reaches a doorway to the next life, death from this stage comes and birth into the next begins. Death ends the journey toward the next life and starts a new journey within it, toward a third stage.

We have also said that when the human soul first begins to form, it is like an embryo in the womb of this world, held in the body's covering. It grows little by little here, as an embryo grows in its mother's womb. Death, whether natural or chosen, means leaving this world's womb for the wide space of the next life. What we have explained makes this clear: death is natural for the soul. Whatever is natural for something is good and completes it; whatever is good and completes it is right for it. So death is right for the soul. The body's decay and destruction are only side effects, like other harms that follow from the good ends of nature. God's justice gives priority to what is nobler and better. A person's spiritual life is nobler than this bodily life.[^V09-377] And if you look carefully at the body as a natural body, you will see that the soul and its powers give it its shape, wholeness, and identity. The body is joined to the soul as matter is joined to form, and as an incomplete thing is joined to what completes it. If the body were left on its own, apart from the soul and its powers, nothing of its true identity would remain—only elements and parts falling apart, much as they were before death.

The bodies that souls and spirits control are weak and dependent in themselves. The nobler and stronger the soul, the weaker and less complete the body it uses. As the soul grows in strength, life, and completeness, the body grows weaker and closer to death and decay. When the soul reaches full independence, the body disappears altogether.[^V09-378] We can see something like this in nature: an inanimate thing has a stronger, firmer body than a plant; a plant's body is firmer than an animal's; an animal's is firmer than a human body's. This follows the different strength and fullness of the souls and spirits within them.

One of the knowers says that death happens when the Real appears to the speaking soul, just as God appeared to Moses. The mountain of the body's selfhood is then crushed.[^V09-379] God appears in the world of spirits to a soul whose tie to that world has grown strong. As the spiritual side becomes stronger, the bodily side fades, because they pull in opposite directions. This world and the next are like two sides of a scale: when one rises, the other falls. The heavenly spheres do not disprove this. Their perfection comes from their souls, not from their bodies. Their inability to split, their protection from outside matter, injury, decay, and change all come from their souls, not from any special strength in their bodies. They have no nature or power apart from what depends on their souls. The body of a sphere, considered just as matter, is among the weakest bodies. The whole sphere is almost all soul, apart from a faint extension of matter—or something a little more than that—which lets it take on positions and movements.[^V09-380] As we have said, the bodies of the next life are their souls. They exist through awareness and the soul, and their life belongs to them by nature, unlike the borrowed life of material bodies. **A point of wisdom:** God has placed in the soul a love of lasting life and a dislike of passing away.[^V09-381]

We know that existence itself is pure good and light. To remain in existence is to remain in goodness and light. Nature does nothing without purpose. Everything built into it must have an end it can reach. This shows that souls love to remain alive because they are meant to reach a fuller state, not a lesser one.[^V09-382] A soul's inborn love of lasting life shows that it has another life that will last forever. It cannot remain forever in this bodily world. If there were no other lasting life for it to enter, nature would have planted in the soul a longing for eternal life that could never be fulfilled. The philosophers who know God have shown that nature does nothing in vain.

Someone may ask: If the body's death in this passing life is the soul's life in the lasting one, and the soul naturally turns toward the next life, moves toward closeness to God, and seeks to leave the dark, bodily world, why does it fear death and shrink from laying down the body, which frees it from weight and confinement? I reply that the soul's dislike of bodily death has two causes: one that brings it about, and one that serves a purpose.[^V09-383] The first cause is that the soul's earliest life is this bodily life. As long as it remains joined to the body and directs it, this life rules the soul. It is affected by whatever affects a living body. So it suffers when its parts are torn apart or burned, not as a thinking soul or an intellect, but as a being that senses and is tied to the body.[^V09-384]

The soul's fear and dislike of bodily death belong to its share in this natural life. They are stronger when it is more deeply sunk in the body and more absorbed in it. We do not agree that a person fears the natural death that comes at the end of a full life, as they fear a sudden death that cuts life short. A clear mind, inner strength, faith in God and the next life, and the power of the spiritual world lead a person to welcome worldly death. They long for God and the company of those near Him; they shrink from worldly life, its darkness, and its harmful companions. A person who knows God avoids the company of this world's creatures as a living person avoids the company of the dead. The second cause—the purpose—is that the soul protects the body, its mount on the road to the next life. It keeps the body safe from passing harm so the soul can gain knowledge and good habits until it reaches its full potential.

God has also placed pain and fear in animals so they will protect their bodies from harm and illness. Bodies cannot sense danger or act to protect themselves. Without pain and fear, the soul would neglect the body and leave it exposed to danger before its life was complete. It would die before reaching the next form of life and developing its inner self. This would conflict with God's wisdom and care. The pain and fear in animals are not punishments for sins, as some who believe in transmigration have thought.[^V09-385]

#### Chapter 13 — How all beings, even plants and lifeless things, return to God, as the Qur'an teaches

As we have explained, all things that can exist fall into levels. At the highest are the intellects beyond bodies, the forms of God's knowledge. Next are the spirits that guide the heavenly bodies and earthly bodies as a whole. Below them are spirits that guide particular bodies, imaginal souls attached to lower bodies made of vapor, smoke, or fire, and some kinds of jinn and demons. Then come plant souls, the natural powers within bodies, and finally material bodies, the lowest and farthest from the First Source. All of them return to God. We will now prove this, both in general and in detail, with the help of our Lord, the Most High.

Here is the general proof. God created nothing without a purpose. Every possible thing has a cause and an end. Some things are made of parts and have four causes: what makes them, what they are for, the material they are made from, and the form that makes them what they are. Each end points to a further end. This chain cannot go on without end. It must reach a final end that has no end beyond it, just as every source leads back to a first source. Proof has shown that the Creator of all things has no aim in His action beyond Himself. He is the final end of all ends and the first source of all sources. The true end of a thing is what it can reach unless something outside it prevents it.[^V09-386] If a thing could not reach what we call its end, then “end” would only be a figure of speech, not its true end. But we have said that it is the true end, so that cannot be right. It follows that, by their natures and deepest inclinations, all possible things seek God and are carried toward Him by an inner movement. God placed this movement and longing within them, so they cannot be pointless or wasted.

Their ends must therefore be real and attainable, unless some obstacle holds them back. But anything forced against its nature cannot be held back forever. As we have explained, the force that holds it back must eventually end. The obstacles may remain for a long time, but when they pass, things return to their original ends. An end is always nobler than the thing that seeks it. The end of each kind of being is a fuller, stronger, and nobler kind of existence. We can trace this upward until we reach the highest end, with nothing nobler beyond it. To avoid an endless chain, this must be the end of all ends, the goal of all movement and longing, and the home of those who love God, long for Him, and seek His help. Thus all things return to God. That is what we set out to prove.

We will now give the detailed proof through several claims.

**The first claim: pure intellects return to God.** We have often shown that their separate identities are absorbed in God's identity, that they continue to exist through God, and that they return to Him. Here are several proofs.

**First proof.** These intellects are pure existence and pure light, without any trace of absence or darkness. The only difference between them and the Light of Lights is that they are less complete. Their differences from one another are likewise only differences of strength and weakness. Since this is so, they are joined by a real inner connection; their identities are not cut off from God's identity. For a thing reaches its fullness in what it truly is, and that fullness belongs to it more fully than anything else.[^V09-387][^V09-388]

**Second proof.** The principle that a higher, nobler being must exist before a lower one requires a real connection between the First Source and the being nearest to it. The same must be true between that being and the next, all the way down to the purest forms of existence and light. So all these beings are like one connected reality, with levels of greater or lesser brightness and fullness. One side rises without limit in intensity; the other has limited intensity but no limit in duration or number. Otherwise, an infinite series would be trapped between two fixed limits, as we explained earlier.[^V09-389] The only way out is to see these beings as levels of divine life and ranks of God's rule, as the verse says: “High above all ranks is He, the Lord of the Throne.” They are joined to God and return to His very being.

**Third proof.** Since no veil stands between the intellect and God, the intellect can see the First Reality through its own being. It cannot fully contain God or master Him; that is impossible. But there is no intermediary or barrier that separates them. So God reveals Himself to the intellect in His very being. The First Intellect is the form in which this revelation appears, since it has no other recipient than itself. What reveals, what is revealed, and the one to whom it is revealed are all one.[^V09-390][^V09-391] There are not two separate things here—the intellect and God's revelation to it. It is impossible for God, in one respect, to bring about two forms, the intellect's form and a separate form of revelation. Nor can one thing have two separate acts of existence. So the intellect's existence is itself God's revealing His own form to it. God's form is not something separate from God. The intellect is like a mirror in which the First Reality appears.[^V09-392] A mirror has no color of its own; it shows only the image before it. The image is not something separate from what it reflects. In the same way, the intellect has no identity apart from the form of the First Reality. That form is not separate from its source. It is an image and account of that source, and this is its own true being. God's essence is the reality of the intellect, and the intellect is its image. Everything that has a reality returns to that reality. The First Intellect, then, returns to God, and so do the other intellects, by the same reasoning: whatever returns to something that itself returns to God also returns to God.

**Fourth proof.** We have already proved that the one who understands and what is understood are united in the act of understanding. We have also shown that the separate intellects are God's own detailed knowledge of things, not extra images added to Him.[^V09-393] Since their being is God's knowing of all things, and knowing something is not separate from that thing, the intellects must return to God and become one with Him.

**Fifth proof.** We have already shown that the divine intellectual forms exist through God's own being. They do not exist in Him as things held inside a container. They are necessary expressions of His essence, which is not made by anything else and exists necessarily.[^V09-394] So they must return to God. These proofs establish that the world of intellect, the divine forms, and the lights beyond bodies all return to Him. They lose their separate selves and continue through God. He is their return, just as He is their source.

**The second claim: rational souls return to God.** These souls are either complete in intellect or incomplete. A complete soul has moved from the ability to think only potentially to thinking fully. It has become intellect in action. When it reaches this state, it passes beyond its former self and joins the intellect that completes it. It returns to that intellect. Whatever returns to an intellect that itself returns to God also returns to God. So complete souls return to Him.

An incomplete rational soul may or may not long for intellectual completion. Some do not long for it by nature, as is the case with animals; others lose that longing because they forget God. The Qur'an says, “They forgot God, so He made them forget themselves.” Such souls go to an intermediate world between the intellectual and material worlds: the world of measured images and forms perceived by the inner senses. These forms are patterns, expressions, and shadows of the intellectual forms. They give those forms a continuing presence. The way these souls are gathered is like the way animal souls are gathered. “They are like cattle; indeed, they are even further astray.” We will discuss the return of animal souls later.

Other incomplete souls still long for intellectual realities but have not reached them. They remain in Hell, suffering for a long or short time. Then their longing for intellect fades. This may happen because God's care draws them toward it, or through the help of an angel or a human being, when their longing is strong and their attachments are weak. Or it may happen after they have remained for a long time in the lower intermediate worlds and become used to them. Their suffering then ends, and they settle in their true home, either at a higher level or a lower one. They return to God in another way, without moving from one body to another, as we will explain.[^V09-395][^V09-396]

**The third claim: animal souls return to God.** An animal soul that has developed imagination, and does not stop at sense perception alone, does not disappear when its body breaks down. It remains in the intermediate world, with its own distinct identity and a form suited to its inner qualities.[^V09-397] The individuals of each kind remain distinct and take on the shapes and parts suited to that kind. Each animal kind is connected to the intellect that is the source and goal of its kind. Every natural kind has such an intellect, as we explained earlier. So the individuals of each kind return, while remaining distinct, to the intellect that is the source of their kind.

Some of these lower intellects act directly on the natural things they produce. Others act through an intermediate soul: a human soul for the noblest kinds, an animal soul for those below them, and a plant soul for kinds below those. The lowest intellects are the sources of lifeless natural powers. The intellects that are sources for animals with imagination receive the animal souls belonging to their kinds. Each animal soul keeps its own identity as it returns to the source of its kind.

Animal souls that have sense perception but no developed imagination or memory return to the intellect that guides them when they die, but they no longer remain distinct individuals. They become one with that intellect. They were like rays from one light, divided into many by the openings through which they passed. Once those openings disappear, their separate forms disappear too, and they return to the unity they had at their source. This is how the souls of animals that sense but do not imagine are gathered. It is like the body's separate senses coming together in the one shared sense. We can see that the five senses are spread across different organs but join in a single power of perception. This makes it easier to believe that animal souls, which depend on bodies for their existence, also depend on their source. One cause has one effect; the effect appears to multiply only because it has many receivers.

When the receivers break down, the effect returns to the unity it had in its source. This is a useful point to understand. The animal souls that do not exist independently are like the powers of one soul. Sight and hearing, for example, do not each have their own separate identity. If they did, each would be aware of itself. Rather, the soul, which is aware of itself, gathers the identities of all its powers and uses them to perceive other things. In the Resurrection and the lasting life, the soul that exists on its own remains distinct. Its other powers continue through it, because they are joined to it and share its unity. The same is true of animal souls that cannot stand on their own or be aware of themselves.[^V09-398]

When the many bodies that had made them seem separate are gone, these souls return to their source and become one with it, just as the senses and other powers return to the single power that gathers them when their organs and tools break down.

**A note on a mistaken comparison.** Some people claim that human souls, like the lesser spirits in animals and plants, merge into one after their bodies are destroyed. They compare them to water kept in jars: when the jars break, the water flows together into one pool. This is a false idea. It confuses two kinds of multiplicity: the divisions caused by separate receivers, and the distinct identities that come from the beings themselves.

Ibn Arabi discusses this question in chapter 302 of *The Meccan Revelations*. He says that people disagree about whether the spirits that guide the forms of the world exist before, after, or together with those forms.[^V09-399] He compares the spirits that guide the world's forms to the spirits of the human body's organs: the power of the hand, the spirit of the ear, and the spirit of the eye.[^V09-400] His own view is that the spirits that guide forms existed together in a general, undivided state, while each was already distinct in God's knowledge.[^V09-401] They were like letters that exist only potentially in ink.[^V09-402] They were not distinct to themselves, though God knew each one distinctly.

When a pen writes on a tablet, the letters appear separately, though before that they were together in the ink.[^V09-403] In simple forms, there are letters such as alif, dal, and jim; these are like the spirits of simple things.[^V09-404] In combined forms, there are words such as “he stood,” “Zayd,” “he went out,” and “Amr”; these are like the spirits of compound bodies. When God formed the world, or whichever world He willed, the universal spirit was like the writer's hand, the spirits were like ink in the pen, and the forms were like the letters written on the tablet.[^V09-405] The spirit was breathed into the forms of the world,[^V09-406] and the spirits appeared as distinct beings: this is Zayd, this is Amr, this is a horse, this an elephant, and this a snake. Everything has a spirit, though not everything is aware. Some say that spirits first arise from the body's mixture; others deny this. Each view has an argument. The best middle way, Ibn Arabi says, is the one he has chosen, as the Qur'an says: “Then We brought it into a new creation.”[^V09-407]

When God forms a body, He can join it to whichever spiritual form He wills: a pig, a dog, a human, or a horse. Someone ruled by dullness and animal traits has the spirit of a donkey and is called a donkey because that spirit governs him. In the same way, people may be called dogs, lions, or human beings according to the quality that rules them.[^V09-408] The human form is the most complete of these. God says, “He created you, shaped you, and made you upright; He formed you in whatever shape He willed.” Thus the visible form is shaped according to the spirit placed within it, and each spirit is distinct through its own form.

When spirits leave these bodies, Ibn Arabi says, people hold three views. Some say the spirits become entirely free of matter and return to their source, like sunlight reflected from a polished surface that later tarnishes. Some say the spirits no longer remain distinct, as water from broken jars flows back into a river. Others say spirits take on good or bad traits from their bodies, and keep those traits after leaving them, just as water can take on a vessel's color, smell, or taste. A third group says that spirits continue to guide bodies in this world and, in the intermediate world, guide imaginal bodies—the forms people see themselves in during dreams and after death. Then, on the Day of Resurrection, they return to the same natural bodies they had in this life.[^V09-409]

That is where the disagreement among Ibn Arabi's followers ends. We have quoted him at length because some of what he says supports the proofs of philosophy, though some does not. For example, he says that a bodily form exists before the spirits that guide it. But we have shown that spirits, or souls, exist in their own nature before bodies, in a certain sense.[^V09-410] The soul gives the body its features, shape, and organs, all suited to its nature and qualities. The body displays what the soul is and gives it a place to carry out its actions and develop its abilities. In its true existence, the body depends on the spirit, not the other way around, even though the spirit also needs the body to develop and act.

When the Qur'an says God forms a person in whatever shape He wills, the “shape” means the natural bodily form of this world. Ibn Arabi is right that a dull person may have the spirit of a donkey, but the body too is shaped by that spirit and follows it.[^V09-411] This can be true from a person's original nature in this life, or from the animal traits the soul has acquired and carries into the next life. In either life, the body's form follows the soul's form.

Ibn Arabi also seems to say that the body in which people rise on the Day of Resurrection is exactly the natural body they had in this life.[^V09-412] But that is not so. This world and the next have different kinds of existence. The form in which a person rises is not natural, though the next life's outward senses can perceive it, as we have explained. He should also have distinguished between general spirits and particular spirits, and among the three kinds of soul: rational souls, animal souls with imagination, and animal souls that have sense perception alone. Some remain distinct after leaving their bodies; others do not. The view that spirits merge like water from broken jars is right only for particular souls that rely on sense perception, and for plant spirits. It is not right for rational souls or animal souls with imagination. They exist on their own, rather than being spread through bodies, so their many distinct identities do not depend on the number of bodies. They remain many after the bodies decay. These souls, like all other things, also exist in another way above the worlds: together in an intellectual source, a sacred being called the Divine Pen.

There, all beings exist together in an undivided form, as we have already explained.

**The fourth claim: plant powers return to God.** Plant souls rank below animal souls because they have no senses, especially no finer senses. But they rank above the natural powers of lifeless matter, because their actions show some form of touch-like awareness.[^V09-413] This is why we call them souls. Their return is close to that of lower animals, since in this natural world they can rise, develop, and draw closer to the First Source.[^V09-414] Some plant souls live in animal seed and develop until they reach animal life. Some go further and reach human life; their return is more complete and their place in the Resurrection higher. They are closer to God. Other plant souls do no more than develop as plants. When their bodies decay, they return to a lower level, to an intellect that guides them below the intellects that guide animal kinds.

The great teacher of the philosophers asks: If a tree is cut down, where does the soul or power that lived in it go? It returns to the place it never left: the intellectual world. The same happens when an animal's body breaks down. Its soul moves toward the intellectual world, because that world is the soul's home. Intellect is not in a place, so the soul is not in a place either. It is above and below, and present throughout the whole without being divided into parts. The soul is everywhere and nowhere.

I would add that when a plant is cut at its root or dries up, its form first returns to the world of images and shapes without matter, then reaches the intellectual world. There, it becomes either one of the trees of paradise, if it had a pleasant taste and smell,[^V09-415] or one of the trees of Hell, if it was bitter and foul, like the tree of zaqqum, “the food of the sinner.” The roots of these trees reach the Lote Tree of the Furthest Limit, where the Garden of Refuge lies and where the tree is covered by what covers it.[^V09-416] In the same way, all souls first return to the Universal Soul, above which is the Universal Intellect. This is the home of universal souls and the end of particular souls.

The greatest teacher of philosophy says that every natural form also exists in that higher world, but in a better and nobler way. There, images of the sky, earth, air, fire, and water exist without matter. If those forms exist there, then plants must exist there too. Someone may ask: If there are plants, fire, and earth in the higher world, are they alive or dead? If they are dead, why are they there? If they are alive, how can they live there?

He replies that the plant here is already alive. A plant's form carries life, and if that life belongs to a plant made of matter, then it is a soul. The plant in the higher world must be even more alive, though in a nobler form. From this account, we learn that a natural plant has two other forms: one in the world of souls, as a sense image without matter, and another in the higher world, called the First Plant. We will quote more from him to show that earth, water, and other natural forms also have inner, soul-like forms. It is now clear that the forms of these apparently lifeless bodies, buried here in matter, are raised and gathered in another world. From there they return to the second life and then to the souls' final home. We will make this clearer.

**The fifth claim: lifeless things and the elements return to God.** My dear reader, if you wish to understand these deep matters—things that escape most great philosophers, let alone other thinkers—you must keep in mind the principles of existence that we have often discussed in this book. First, existence is one simple reality. It has no differences except in what comes earlier or later, and in what is more or less complete. The qualities that make existence complete—knowledge, power, will, and others—are part of existence itself, and appear in every thing in their own way.[^V09-417]

In God's one, unique being, existence is free from every trace of absence or limitation. The same is true of the second intellects and sacred levels. Their limits as created beings are overcome by their reaching a fuller, necessary existence. No possibility or poverty remains in them, because God's own unending perfection and light complete them. For this reason they are called “complete words.” Below them are incomplete beings, mixed with absence and limitation. These are all things other than God. As long as they are changing, each carries some lack of being and some absence in time. The lowest and most limited level is made up of natural bodies.

Insofar as these bodies exist, they share in knowledge, power, and life. But their parts are spread through space and matter. Their parts are far apart in time. They mix with absence and darkness, so they are unaware of themselves and forget their own nature. They are submerged in matter, scattered across the physical world. They cannot remember their original home of light and wholeness. Even so, their weak existence and small share of light come from existence itself. Through God's care and continued gift, they can receive some life and light. This frees them, in part, from the rule of absence and darkness, so they do not fall into complete nonexistence.

The first covering of form given to them by eternal mercy keeps them from scattering and flowing apart. Next comes the form that holds their parts together against forces that would break them apart. Then they receive what they need from outside to strengthen them and replace what they lose. Growth adds size and completes each individual. The power of reproduction then continues their existence through similar beings. After giving them these forms, God's care guides them toward closeness and union, as their capacities grow little by little, until they return to the world of the afterlife and the level of the intellect they have gained. This is one basic principle.[^V09-418]

Remember, too, that all bodies can receive a nobler life and a higher form of completion than the one already within them. What prevents some of them is their low position in the world of separation and opposition. Each is bound to a single, divided identity that conflicts with others and harms them, just as they harm it. When this force of division and conflict weakens, matter becomes ready for a higher form: one that is less divided and more united.

The elements are so low, divided, and far from unity—and their qualities and forms clash so strongly—that they resist the life of the soul and intellect. But when their opposed qualities weaken and their own existence grows firmer, this conflict fades. Then they can receive another kind of existence and a higher form. With greater balance, they stand between all the elements: in one way they are free of them; in another, they hold them together in a finer unity. As they move further from the opposed extremes, they become ready for a higher life and a more complete form. Step by step, they rise until their form becomes that of a soul joined to the Active Intellect, a light from God, a noble image of Him, a complete word, and a greatest name. This is another basic principle.[^V09-419]

Keep in mind that every complete form contains all the lower forms within it, but in a finer, nobler, and simpler way. An incomplete form can exist only through another form that completes it, surrounds it, preserves it, and brings it from possibility to reality and from lack to fullness. Without the complete form, the incomplete one would not exist. A lower thing cannot stand by itself; it depends on what is fuller. Possibility depends on what is actual, and what is actual on what must be. Completion comes before incompletion, what must be before what may be, existence before nonexistence, what is actual before what is only possible, and a defined form before something still undefined.

People are misled because in this world they see what is incomplete come first in time: a seed before a fruit, or a fetus before an animal. But what comes first in time is not the true cause of what comes later. It only prepares the material for it. Those earlier states of absence, power, and possibility must themselves depend on a real form that exists and gives them their place.

Once we understand these principles, we can see that every elemental and lifeless form must have another, more complete form closely joined to it, present within it though hidden from our senses. This form is not the Active Intellect acting directly, as followers of Aristotle say,[^V09-420] nor is it one of the separate intellects that rule the kinds of things, acting without any intermediate link, as the Illuminationists say.[^V09-421] As we explained, a higher cause can act on a lower thing only through something suited to both. Otherwise, another link would be needed between each pair, without end. So there must be a real inner connection between what gives a thing its form and what receives that form.

Every natural form therefore has an unseen form: this is the next life's image of it, just as its own image is the first life's form.[^V09-422] The next life has ranks, just like this one, from higher to lower. In the afterlife, each creature is returned to a place that matches its rank here: the nobler to the nobler, the lower to the lower. If a form changes here from a lower state to a higher one, as a lifeless thing becomes a plant or a plant becomes an animal, it returns in the next life according to the form it has reached. It is like a person who leaves disbelief for faith, or gives up wrongdoing and repents: their return changes from one level of Hell to a level of paradise, according to their state in this life.

Every natural thing therefore has a soul-form in the next life, and that soul-form has an intellectual form in a world above it. Each higher world has many ranks. Its highest level joins with the lowest level of the world above it. A form in the lower world is like matter for the form above it. As we have seen, matter and form are joined; the form completes the matter and is its return and final home. Sense forms, when made finer, become matter for soul-forms, which in turn become matter for intellectual forms. Sense depends on the soul; the soul on intellect; and intellect on the Creator.

The greatest philosopher says that the matter of intellect is noble because it is simple and intellectual, but intellect is even more complete and contains it. The matter of soul is noble because it is simple and belongs to the soul, but soul is more complete and contains it. With the help of intellect, the soul gives it wonderful effects. The soul is nobler than its matter because it surrounds it and shapes it into wonderful images.[^V09-423][^V09-424] We can see this in the world of the senses. Anyone who looks at it is amazed, especially by its size, beauty, nobility, constant movement, and the spirits that live within it: animals, air, plants, and all other things.

When you see the things we can sense in this lower world, use your reason to look toward the higher, true world, of which this one is only an image. There you will see the same things as lasting, intellectual realities, filled with virtues and pure life, untouched by impurity. They are full of knowledge and wisdom because light flows over them. Each seeks to rise toward the rank of the next and draw nearer to the first light that shines on that world. That world contains all lasting things that do not die, and includes all intellects and souls.[^V09-425] This account clearly supports what we are saying.

Another proof that natural forms have soul-forms, which are their inner reality and return, and intellectual forms above those, is found in our own perception. When we sense something outside us, its form appears in our senses in a way different from its outward form. Our senses belong to the animal level, which has sense perception but nothing higher. When the form enters a sense and completes it, another, finer, and nobler form arises from it. Our power of imagination takes on this new form. We have proved that imagination and the forms held in it are free of matter. From the form in imagination, yet another form enters our intellect. If each thing we sense had no inner connection to its image in imagination and its form in intellect, this progression could not happen.

The same is true in the opposite direction. When we understand an intellectual form, a matching image appears in imagination. When the image becomes strong, it affects our senses, and a form appears before them, as in the verse: “Then she saw him as a well-formed man.”[^V09-426] This is how the Prophet saw Gabriel and the angels in this world.

These proofs show that every natural form in the visible world has a soul-form in the unseen world. That form is its return and final home after its material support disappears. It is already joined to the natural form and sustains it. But because the natural form is buried in darkness and absence, and submerged in matter and bodies, only people of knowledge and insight can clearly see its return to the soul-form that gives it life.

When natural forms fall apart as their material support decays, they leave their bodily coverings and appear in that world of knowledge, insight, and certainty. They are gathered there, as the Qur'an says, “Hell will be brought into view for all to see,” and, “No indeed! They will soon know. Again, no indeed! They will soon know. No indeed! If only you knew with certainty, you would see Hell.” The image of Hell that people will see in the next life is the inner reality of this lower world.[^V09-427] Here, its fire burns bodies and changes their skin. There, it appears with its chains, shackles, scorpions, and snakes. Its pleasures and desires appear as fires that burn the heart and torment the soul. Its food and drink appear as the tree of zaqqum and boiling water, which increase the soul's hunger and thirst, heat and pain.

**The sixth claim: what happens to matter and material bodies, and the end of evil people and demons.** We have shown that all beings with a real form naturally seek their ends and the completion that leads them to higher ends, until they reach the one end with nothing beyond it. But some things exist only as possibilities or preparations for other things, which are their forms and completions. These include matter, motion, power, and time.

The same is true of bodily extension, which can be divided and broken apart and naturally tends to fade and flow away. If souls and natural powers did not hold it together and give it unity, its parts would separate from one another. Each part would be absent from the others; the presence of one would mean that another was away. What has no unity in itself has no existence. Time too is nothing but passing and running out.

So the end of these things is decay and disappearance. They cannot pass from this world, which is a source of evils, darkness, and absence, into the lasting world of life. If they did, what cannot remain would become lasting, nonexistence would become existence, change would become permanence, and death would become life. Thus matter, motion, and time, and the body considered only as something that changes and decays, return to nonexistence.[^V09-428]

The same applies to things in the soul. Cowardice, ignorance, dullness, and similar qualities end in destruction if they are simple and not mixed with active evil. If they are mixed with defiance, pride, or hypocrisy, they end in severe suffering and painful punishment.[^V09-429] As we explained in our discussion of cause and effect, people can be drawn toward false ends. These begin with confused ideas, empty hopes, and harmful desires. Each kind of corrupt desire has its own tyrant that leads a person toward a limited goal in this world. But those limited goals and imagined purposes are corrupt and short-lived.[^V09-430] Anyone ruled by such a tyrant, misled by desire and following it, is ruled by things that belong to this passing life. As this world fades, its tyrant and followers also fade. They move further into nonexistence and fall through lower states until they reach the home of ruin, the final resting place of those who do evil.

**A final word in support of our argument.** We have shown that God's power never stops giving existence to what lies below it. His power has no limit in strength, duration, or number. First, He gives existence to intellect and forms it in His image, before giving existence to anything else. His care does not allow a lower being to appear before a higher one. The higher comes first, then the next higher, step by step, until the lowest possible level is reached.

So the Creator first brought the First Intellect into existence, complete and perfect.
