But the intellect too is complete after the First, with no limit in how long or how many it can bring forth, though not in the strength of each one. It cannot stop at itself and produce nothing else. So it gives its light and power to the soul, as far as a giver and receiver can be close to one another.

When the soul is filled with light, power, virtue, and goodness, it too cannot stop at itself. These qualities it receives from intellect make it long to resemble and join with intellect. So it gives its light and virtues to what lies below it, but through change over time. If its existence were already complete, it would be intellect, not soul. Its light and beauty fill this world through the forms of kinds and the natures of bodies: spheres, stars, animals, plants, minerals, and elements.

Those forms too are made by nature to give to what lies below them and act upon it, following God's law and the pattern He has set. But material nature is the lowest of all forms. It can produce nothing beyond matter, which gives things only the ability to exist, and motion, which brings what is possible into being. Matter and motion therefore bring things into being, then allow them to pass away. They begin and end; they appear and disappear. This world must one day be ruined, and everything on earth must pass away, as God says: “Everyone on it will pass away, but the face of your Lord will remain, full of majesty and honor.” Nature then returns to soul, soul to intellect, and intellect to God, the One who rules over all. God asks, “Whose is the kingdom today?” and answers, “It is God's, the One, the All-Powerful.” He also says, “The Trumpet will be blown, and all who are in the heavens and on earth will fall senseless, except those God wills. Then it will be blown again, and they will rise and look around.”

When these things return to their original homes, leaving behind the world of motion, change, evil, pain, and bodily death—and when souls leave behind fear and collapse—God's mercy will give them life again, a life without death, and an existence that will not end. God says, “Then it will be blown again, and they will rise and look around,” and, “The earth will shine with the light of its Lord.” That earth of the next life is a soul-form, alive and ready to receive the light of intellect that flows from God.[^V09-431][^V09-432] It may also mean this earth after God takes it into His hand, when it becomes a soul-form that can be drawn into God's care, as He says, “The whole earth will be in His grasp on the Day of Resurrection.”[^V09-433] To take something into one's hand means it remains present but is raised to a nobler form, as food becomes part of the one who eats it. To fold something up, by contrast, means it no longer has its own separate existence. So God's taking the earth into His hand means that its natural form changes into a soul-form of the next life, as the verse says, “On the day the earth is changed into another earth.” Folding the heavens means they lose their separate selves and unite with intellect, which is God's right hand.[^V09-434] Pure intellect loses itself and remains through God.

Everything in the next life exists as an image perceived by the soul. It does not need a material subject or base, which has no life of its own, as matter and the bodies of this world do. Even the lowest thing in that world—the earth of the next life—is a living soul-form, higher than the sky of this world. A report says: “The ground of paradise is the Throne, and its roof is the Throne of the All-Merciful.” Water, air, fire, trees, mountains, buildings, and homes all exist there as soul-forms without matter, motion, or unrealized possibility.

If even the lower, earthly things in that world exist this way, imagine how its noble, heavenly beings exist. Everything in this world has a form in the next. The difference between the heavens and earth, animals and trees, stones and water, is much greater there, because those forms are the inner realities of these things. As God says, “The next life has higher ranks and greater excellence.” Everything there is alive in its own right. We have now shown that the forms of matter, including the elements, have lasting forms in the next life. The senses can perceive them there, and they do not fade, decay, or split apart. All of them exist within the soul as if they were one power, though they have many forms and appear as many things. This is one of the wonders that only people with insight can understand. These inner forms are the hidden realities of worldly things. After their existence in this world ends, they return to those forms, are gathered there, and live through the life of the soul.

The teacher of the Aristotelian philosophers says that fire has a quality like the earth's. Fire is a kind of active word within matter, as are similar things. Fire does not arise by itself without a cause, or simply through bodies rubbing together, as people think. Nor is matter itself fire in potential, or the cause that creates fire. Rather, an active word within matter produces the form of fire and all other forms. Matter receives this action. The word within it is the soul, which can shape fire and other forms. This soul is the life and active word within the fire; life and word are one.[^V09-435][^V09-436][^V09-437] Plato therefore said that every simple body has a soul, which produces the fire we sense.

If this is so, the thing that produces this fire is a kind of fiery life, a hidden fire. The fire above this fire, in the higher world, is even more truly fire. If it is a hidden fire, then it must be life. Its life is higher and nobler than the life of this fire, since this fire is only its image. So the fire in the higher world is alive, and its life gives life to this fire. The same is true of water and air there: they are alive, and their life is greater than the life they have here, because their life is what gives life to the water and air of this world.[^V09-438]

In another passage, the same philosopher says that the earth has a kind of life and an active word within it. He points to its many forms, which grow and bring forth plants, grasses, mountains, and minerals.[^V09-439] I must say that Ibn Arabi's point is very deep, and deserves to be written in gold, not ink. Life can mean the source of awareness and action, as when we say that a living thing is aware and active. This is the life people attribute to spirits. It belongs to spirits in their own right, but to bodies only through something else. Life can also mean real, simple, all-pervading existence. Think of how people call existence itself “the life that flows through all things.” We have often said the same in this book. In this second sense, life belongs to all existing things, including bodies. How could it be otherwise, when a body exists, and existence is life in each thing according to its degree?

I say, we must understand[^V09-440] that both direct spiritual insight and sound argument show that a body whose life belongs to its very being is not the changing physical body we see here. The bodies of this world—those in the heavens, on earth, and between them—come into being, pass away, and are renewed from moment to moment. I have explained this through reasoned proofs, scriptural evidence, and the teaching of great philosophers. How, then, could such passing bodies have life, praise, and speech[^V09-441] simply because they are physical matter? The body whose life is truly its own is a different body, belonging to the next life. Its existence consists in being present to awareness. It does not need material support, a guiding spirit, or a soul that must first bring it from undeveloped capacity into living activity. Its existence is already life and soul.[^V09-442] It does not require another soul to make it alive.

I have often said that such a body exists as an image present to awareness. For a form of this kind, existing and being perceived are not two separate things. By contrast, the forms of physical matter do not become objects of sensation or understanding until something capable of perceiving them receives or draws them from matter. We have also shown that a form actually sensed or understood is united with the being that senses or understands it. Thus these forms belonging to awareness are alive in themselves, unlike the physical bodies that continually come into being and perish. Ibn Arabi's statement cannot, then, be accepted in its most obvious sense. The Qur'an says, “There is nothing that does not praise Him,” and other verses speak of the life and speech of all things. Those verses show that bodies are alive and give praise, whether by their own bodily existence or through the spirit or angel joined to them. They do not prove[^V09-443] that changing matter, considered apart from any spirit or guiding form, itself speaks and praises.

You have learned that every body has a form belonging to the soul and a higher source that guides it. Through these it has life and speech, not through its dark physical matter,[^V09-444] which is mixed with absence and does not remain unchanged for even an instant. Yet there is a further subtle point[^V09-445] that can give Ibn Arabi's words a sound meaning. Within these changing, dark bodies is another body, radiant with light and spreading through them as light spreads through crystal. Through it they receive life and come under the direction of souls and spirits. I do not mean what physicians call the animal spirit, which they describe as rising from the blood in the heart and liver and flowing through the arteries. That is a compound physical thing, found only in some bodies, and it receives life from another source. The radiant body I mean is not made up of such parts. It extends through all bodies and lives through its own existence, an existence that is itself living awareness.

#### Chapter 14 — What “the Hour” means

God says, “They ask you about the Hour: when will it come? What have you to do with its mention? Its end rests with your Lord.” Some say it is called the Hour because souls are moving toward it,[^V09-446] not by crossing distances in space but by passing through breaths of time, through their inner movement and natural turning toward God and His realm, as we explained in the discussion of why death is necessary. When a person dies, their Hour arrives and their Resurrection begins. This is the “small Resurrection.” We can compare it with the Great Resurrection, just as one day contains many hours, or one year many days.[^V09-447] People with knowledge and certainty do not doubt the Hour. They know it is real.

They do not wait for it as heedless people do. Those who are veiled from the truth doubt that it will happen, think it unlikely, ask when it will come, and say, “When will this promise be fulfilled, if you are truthful?” People with certainty prepare to meet it. They see it as if it were already standing over them, happening now, or close at hand. God says, “How can you know? The Hour may be near. Those who do not believe in it demand that it come sooner, but those who believe in it are wary of it and know that it is true. Those who argue about the Hour are far astray.” He also says, “The Hour is coming; there is no doubt about it, but most people do not know.” And He says, “They ask, ‘When will this promise come, if you are truthful?’ Say, ‘I have no power to help or harm myself except as God wills. Every community has its appointed time. When its time comes, they cannot delay it by a moment or bring it forward.’”

#### Chapter 15 — What it means to blow the Trumpet

God says, “The Trumpet will be blown,” and so on. The Prophet was asked about the Trumpet and said that it is a horn of light that Israfil holds to his mouth. It is described as wide at one end and narrow at the other, though people differ about which end is which.[^V09-448] The Arabic word for the Trumpet can also mean “forms” when the vowel is changed. Al-Hasan al-Basri is reported to have read it in that way. In his great commentary on the Qur'an, al-Razi gives three meanings. First, it may mean an instrument that makes a loud sound when blown, like a horn used by a ruler to call troops. The verse would then use this instrument as an image. The report that it is a horn of light supports this reading, since light does not make a sound that the senses can hear.

Second, “forms” may mean all the forms together: to blow into them would mean to breathe life into them. Al-Hasan read the word this way, as did Abu Razin, who allowed both pronunciations. This reading supports the view that the word means a collection of forms. Third, blowing the Trumpet may be a figure of speech for raising the dead and bringing them forth.[^V09-449] Al-Razi prefers the first meaning because of the report. But the first meaning cannot be taken literally, as we have already shown, and the report itself supports that conclusion. Al-Razi also says that the phrase “then it will be blown again” shows that the verse does not mean breathing spirit into the forms, since that would not happen twice.

I say that God's blowing can only mean giving life, sending forth spirit, and bringing life into being. But life in a higher world requires death in a lower one. With the first blowing, bodies die and souls live. With the second blowing, souls rise through the Real, not through their own power.[^V09-450]

All the materials of the universe, with their natural forms, can receive light from spirits. They are like charcoal that can catch fire because of the heat hidden within it. Imaginal forms are hidden within natural forms, as heat and redness are hidden in charcoal. Spirits are hidden within those imaginal forms, as flame and light are hidden in heat. With the first blowing, natural forms die away, as blackness and cold disappear when charcoal becomes red and hot. The imaginal forms are then ready to receive light from spirits, just as heated, red charcoal is ready to catch fire.

When Israfil, who brings spirits into being, blows into the forms a second time, the spirits that now exist on their own light them up. God says, “Then they will rise and look around.” The Arab mystic Ibn Arabi says in *The Meccan Revelations* that there are two blowings: one puts out a fire, the other lights it again. Once the forms are ready, their preparation to receive spirits is like dry grass ready to catch fire. Imaginal forms are like lamps burning with the spirits within them. Israfil blows once, and that breath passes over the forms and puts them out. The next breath passes over the forms ready for the new life and lights them again. Then they rise, alive and speaking as God gives them speech. Some say, “Praise belongs to God.” Others ask, “Who has woken us from our resting place?” Others say, “Praise belongs to God, who gave us life after death. To Him we return.” Each speaks according to their state. They have forgotten their life in the intermediate world and think it was a dream, just as a person who wakes imagines their earlier life was a dream. When people die and enter the intermediate world, they wake there and think this life was a dream. In the next life, they think both this life and the intermediate life were dreams.

Ibn Arabi says elsewhere that when God takes spirits from natural bodies and places them within forms gathered in the great horn of light, everything a person perceives after death in the intermediate world is perceived through the very form the person has in that horn.[^V09-451] The two blowings are one that puts out the fire and one that lights it again. The first blowing ends the life of anyone thought to be alive, whether in heaven or on earth. God says, “The Trumpet will be blown, and all who are in the heavens and on earth will fall senseless, except those God wills.” He means those whose Great Resurrection has already happened. God also says, “Those for whom We have already promised the best reward will be kept far from it,” and goes on to say that the greatest terror will not grieve them and the angels will meet them, saying, “This is your day, the promise you were given.” Then He says, “On the day We roll up the sky as a record is rolled up.” This greatest terror is the same event as “all who are in the heavens and on earth will fall senseless.”[^V09-452]

I say that those already raised are not among the people of the heavens and earth, because their true being lies beyond bodies, bodily forms, and souls. Their identities do not change with time. They are absorbed in the sea of divine oneness and the power of God's light, like the angels who are near to Him. Their identities are hidden beneath the overwhelming light of God; they do not turn back toward themselves.[^V09-453] The second blowing brings life after death and lasting life after the end of passing life. This life is higher than the first, and its permanence is real and has no end.

#### Chapter 16 — The small and the great Resurrection

The small Resurrection is clear: when a person dies, their Resurrection has begun. The time of the great Resurrection, however, is hidden. God has set its time, but whoever claims to know exactly when it will happen is lying. The Prophet said, “Those who set a time are liars.” Everything that happens in the great Resurrection has a counterpart in the small one.[^V09-454][^V09-455]

The key to understanding the Day of Resurrection and the return of all creatures is to understand the soul and its different levels. Death is like birth: use what you know about birth to understand the afterlife, and compare the great birth with the birth we know. God says, “Your creation and your rising are like the creation and rising of a single soul.” The afterlife begins when veils are lifted, coverings fall away, and realities appear. The Real is revealed in true unity, and everything appears as it truly is. If you want to understand the great Resurrection—God's revelation of Himself in true unity, all things returning to Him and losing their separate identities, even the heavens, angels, spirits, and souls—consider God's words: “All who are in the heavens and on earth will fall senseless, except those God wills.”[^V09-456]

God also says, “To God belongs the inheritance of the heavens and earth,” “Everything will perish except His face,” “Everyone on earth will pass away, but the face of your Lord will remain, full of majesty and honor,” and “To God all things return.” A sound report says that God will bring every existing thing to death, even the angels, Gabriel, Israfil, Michael, and the Angel of Death, then restore them for judgment.[^V09-457] Think about the principles we have already discussed: every lower being turns toward what is higher; each thing returns to its source; each form returns to its true reality; natural and inner change reach their ends; effects return to their causes; and the souls of the heavens join with the intellects that complete them.[^V09-458]

Anyone whose heart is lit by certainty can see the parts, beings, natures, and souls of the world changing at every moment. Everything changes; its separate identity does not last. Every existing thing returns to God, even if many cycles and ages pass first. It returns through death, disappearance, change, transformation, or the overwhelming collapse that spirits undergo. Every movement must reach an end. That end has its own end, until all ends meet in a final end with nothing beyond it. For all things, this is one divine day—or even one moment, or less—containing every time and instant in which things reach their ends. All beginnings, too, came from one beginning and one source, from which every origin branches and every cause and effect springs.

The same idea becomes easier to accept when we see how all the human powers of awareness and movement, though many and different in kind, return to one simple spiritual self, are gathered into it, and then rise from it in the next life in a better and higher form. In the same way, all creatures return to God and then come forth from Him again, more complete than before. The human spirit sends the rays of its powers and senses to the body's different parts. At death, it draws their light back from those bodily channels; in the afterlife, it sends it forth again in a new way. The world is formed in the same way. All its beings—everything in the heavens, the earth, and between them—come into being, return to God as the heavens are folded up, the stars scattered, the mountains crushed, and the earth gathered in, and then begin again in a life that lasts forever.

God says, “He directs the affairs from heaven to earth; then they rise back to Him on a day whose measure is a thousand years by your reckoning.” When all things return to Him, He gives them existence again, and they rise into a life that will never end. God says, “Just as We began the first creation, We will bring it back. This is a promise We will surely fulfill.” If you have not tasted this truth yourself—whether you are a scholar who has not yet reached it, someone too sure of limited reason, or someone trapped by outward forms—then trust the prophets and saints. Believe what they say about what you cannot see, as a blind person trusts the guide who leads them. Do not deny any of it. May God protect us from weak faith in the Last Day and from rejecting the great news brought by those who know. Proof, the Qur'an's signs, and the light of insight all show that the world's beings are always changing: their separate identities fade and their natures renew at every moment. God is the end of every movement and change. “To God all things return.” So glory to the One who holds the kingdom of all things in His hand; to Him you will return.

#### Chapter — Which bodies are raised in the afterlife, and which are not

As long as spirits remain spirits, they always govern bodies. There are two kinds of bodies they govern. In the first kind, the soul acts directly and inwardly, without anything in between. In the second, it acts through another body that comes first. Our outward senses cannot perceive the first kind. They perceive only bodies like the ones that carry them: outer shells, such as those of water and air, animals and plants, fine vaporous bodies, or dense flesh and plant matter. Souls govern all of these only through an intermediary. The first kind of body—the one the soul governs directly—is luminous and alive through its own life, and cannot die.

It ranks above the transparent bodies we see in this world and above what is called the vital spirit. That spirit belongs to this world, though it is subtle and noble compared with other bodies. It quickly changes and fades away, so it cannot be raised into the afterlife. The bodies we are discussing are bodies of the afterlife. They rise with the souls, are joined to them, and remain as the souls remain.

As for the upper intermediate realms, the highest level of this world, called the Lote Tree of the Furthest Limit, is almost pure form without matter. It is like an imaginal body, and souls act on it as they act on the forms of imagination. The levels below it are like the spirits in our brains, since they are like the imagination of the greater world. Some people of spiritual insight have said that the luminous bodies of the heavens do not have imagination as a separate power; they are imagination itself. Just as human imagination is never without images, an angel's being is never without form. The form of a heavenly sphere is to the angel what the power of imagination is to a human being.[^V09-459] The blue sky we see is not the sky's true form, and the scattered lights we see are not its lights in the Day of Resurrection. On that day, this sky will be erased and broken apart, and its stars will be dimmed and blotted out.[^V09-460]

#### Chapter — The state of people of insight here

Some people see the events of the afterlife with inner sight. They witness Paradise and its people before the Hour arrives. They do not need to undergo physical death for the veils to lift from their hearts and inner vision. Their state before death is like the state of others after death. God says, “We have removed your veil, and today your sight is sharp.” This happens because their worldly state has changed into an afterlife state.[^V09-461][^V09-462]

When their state changes, their hearing, sight, and other senses change too. Then all the beings in heaven and earth change for them, because everything has two forms, one for this life and one for the next. Each kind of sense perceives the things that belong to its own world: the senses of this life perceive the things of this life, and the senses of the next life perceive the things of the next. God points to this change when He says, “On the day the earth is changed into another earth, and the heavens too, and all appear before God, the One, the All-Powerful.” He also says, “We have set death among you, and no one can stop Us from replacing you with others like you and bringing you into a form you do not know.” Through this change in being—whether it happens before death, at death, or after death—a person becomes ready to enter Paradise and the Abode of Peace.[^V09-463][^V09-464]

This also shows the difference between the people of Paradise and the people of Hell. The people of Paradise have lit hearts, open chests, purified bodies, and forms free from the impurity of natural matter. The people of Hell have not changed from their natural state. How, then, could they enter the Abode of Peace? God says, “Does every one of them hope to enter the Garden of Bliss? No. We created them from what they know”—that is, from impure seed. How could a being made from such matter be suited to the holy realm? Earthly dust is far from the Lord of all worlds.

This may raise a difficult question: all people share in having been created from seed; all have natural bodies. How, then, can they become fit to enter the Abode of Peace and live near the Lord of the worlds? What does dust have in common with the Lord? God answers, “By the Lord of the Easts and the Wests, We are able to replace them with others like them and bring them into a form they do not know.” In other words, their natural state changes as they gain knowledge, faith, and good works. This change in their very being makes them ready to enter the holy realm and the world of spirits.[^V09-465]

#### The Straight Path

As you have learned, every being has a natural movement and an inborn tendency toward the Cause of causes. Human beings share this general movement, but they also have another movement of their own.[^V09-466] It begins with a religious motive—a change in the soul's condition—and consists in following the way of unity and the path of those who affirm God's oneness: the prophets, the saints, and their followers. This is the path we ask God to show us when we say, “Guide us to the straight path.” It is the path described in God's words, “Say, ‘This is my way: I call to God with clear insight, I and those who follow me,’” and “You guide to a straight path, the path of God, to whom belongs all that is in the heavens and the earth.”

God commands His servants to stay firm on this path and walk it without turning aside. For this reason He sent them messengers and revealed books to them. The other paths followed by created beings are not this path, which belongs to those who seek God. Each of those paths ends somewhere else, not in meeting God or drawing near to Him, and not in the Gardens or the Abode of God's good pleasure. They lead instead to the levels of Hell and its fires.[^V09-467] The upward journey reaches God only by following the path of the fully realized human being. “Good words rise to Him, and good deeds raise them.” This is what God means when He says, “Stand firm, as you have been commanded, you and those who have turned back to God with you. Do not overstep.”

To turn away from this path is to fall from our original nature and follow our lower desires toward Hell.[^V09-468] It is said to be thinner than a hair and sharper than a sword because human perfection depends on two powers. The first is the power to understand: it reaches the truth and the light of certainty by following fine lines of reasoning, so subtle that they are thinner than a hair. The second is the power to act: it brings the three powers of desire, anger, and thought into balance. This gives the soul a middle state, as far as possible, between their extremes. Every extreme is blameworthy and leads to Hell, far from God, where the wretched are turned away.

You have learned that the true middle between opposing extremes is like being free of them. This freedom from the extremes is called justice. It is what saves us from Hell, and it is sharper than a sword. So the straight path has two sides: one is finer than a hair, the other sharper than a sword.[^V09-469] Turning aside from the first means falling away from our original nature: “Those who do not believe in the Hereafter stray from the path.” Failing to keep to the second means being cut off and split apart. This is the meaning of the words, “Are you content with the life of this world instead of the Hereafter?” and “They will be dragged through scalding water.” The reason is that justice is not a true perfection in itself. True perfection lies only in the light of knowledge, the strength of faith, and recognition of God.[^V09-470] Justice is a quality of the soul that consists in a balance between its powers. It is not a positive perfection in its own right. Relying on it and being satisfied with it can keep a person attached to this world, since justice too belongs to this world. Love of this world is the root of every sin.

#### A Qur'anic illumination

On the Day of Resurrection, this path will become visible to those who cross it. How clearly it appears, and how easily people make their way across it, will depend on the light of their certainty. The strength of their certainty determines how firmly and quickly they travel it. So the ranks of the blessed differ according to the light of their knowledge, certainty, and faith. We can draw near to God only through knowledge and certainty, and knowledge is light. Believers will make their way toward meeting God by the strength of their light and insight. God says, “On the Day you see believing men and women, with their light shining ahead of them and to their right, they will say, ‘Our Lord, complete our light for us…’”

A report says that some people will be given a light as great as a mountain, shining ahead of them. Others will receive a smaller light. Some will have a light like a date palm in their right hand; others will have less, until the last person is given a light only as large as a toe. It will shine for a moment and then go out. When it shines in front of him, he will walk; when it goes out, he will stop.

People will cross the path at different speeds, according to their light. Some will pass as quickly as the blink of an eye or a flash of light. Others will pass like a swift horse. The person whose light is no bigger than his toe will move on his face, hands, and feet, crawling and stumbling. The Fire will touch his sides. He will remain in that state until he finally gets across.[^V09-471] This shows how much people differ in faith. One person's faith may have thousands upon thousands of times the light and strength of another's.

Al-Hasan says that the path takes three thousand years to cross: a thousand years uphill, a thousand on level ground, and a thousand downhill. I think the first stage may mean the journey from creation to God, the second the journey within God, and the third the journey from God back to creation.[^V09-472]

Abu Talib al-Makki says in *The Nourishment of Hearts*: “It is reported that God created the Path from His mercy and brought it forth for the believers. The Path belongs to those who affirm God's oneness. The unbelievers have no passage across it,[^V09-473] for the tyrants among them have already been seized from the gathering place by the Fire, while the rest have followed into the Fire what they used to worship instead of God.”[^V09-474]

The light is divided among those who affirm God's oneness according to what they brought with them from this world. The Path grows narrower or wider according to their rank: narrow for sinners, wider for the Godfearing, and broad at its widest for the prophets and saints, like a wide carpet spread out before them. They cross at different speeds. The first pass as quickly as the blink of an eye; the last take as long as the life of the world—seven thousand years.[^V09-475] A foot slips and burns, then the person is pulled out and healed by mercy. Then the other foot slips, just as the first has healed.

“Islam came to them as mercy. When they accepted it but did not live up to it, God made a bridge for them from that mercy, so they could cross it. Whoever neglected any of the practices of Islam neglected the mercy shown to him, and his foot slipped. The Path's narrowness or breadth depends on the share of God's mercy given to each servant. The share of mercy God allotted to a person in this life determines how wide the Path will be for them there. The speed or slowness with which they cross depends on how near they are to God: the share they have of the light of nearness determines how quickly they move.

“The first group crosses in the blink of an eye, like a flash of lightning. They are the prophets. The second crosses like the wind or birds; they are the truthful and the saints. The third crosses as a swift horse or a fine mount would; they are the truthful ones who struggled with themselves until they were true to God in every movement and passing thought. The fourth crosses like a rider on his mount; they are the Godfearing. The fifth runs; they are the worshippers. The sixth walks; they are those who kept their faults hidden. The seventh crawls; they are those who openly sinned while affirming God's oneness.”[^V09-476][^V09-477]

Each group has its own light: the light of prophecy, sainthood, truthfulness, piety, worship, concealment, or the affirmation of God's oneness. Some have light that reaches as far as the eye can see; the last have light only as far as their toes. Light does not depend on how many deeds a person has done, but on how much light their deeds contain. A deed has greater light when the heart has greater light, and the heart has greater light according to its nearness to God. The nearer a person is to God, the brighter, greater, and more penetrating their light, and the more weight it carries. Many people with few deeds will reach Paradise before others whose deeds were multiplied many times over. Did you not hear the Prophet say to Mu'adh, “Be sincere, and a little action will be enough for you”? A servant can reach sincerity only through great light. That light makes their deeds pure and allows them to grow. It is said that a man from this community has surpassed people who lived a thousand years before him. A report says, “How good is the sleep and breaking of the fast of the wise! How far they surpass the night vigil and fasting of fools!” A mustard seed's worth of piety and certainty is better in God's sight than mountains of deeds done by the self-deceived. The difference in light comes from the heart's intention: some fast to obey, some to clear the heart—for hunger is a cloud that rains wisdom—some to understand the poor, and some to take on the quality of the Self-Sufficient, who does not eat. Some give to obey, some to develop generosity, some out of tenderness for the poor, and some to take on the quality of the Provider, who meets needs and provides for all creatures. The same applies to other actions.[^V09-478]

Al-Makki also says: “The straight path is the path of affirming God's oneness, the true religion followed by all the prophets, messengers, and their followers. All the noble states and stations of those traveling toward God and within God return to it. The knowledge of God's oneness is the most useful and highest of all knowledge. Indeed, it is their purest and clearest part. It is the highest aim and greatest goal. ‘Beyond Abadan there is no village.’ There is no hope of salvation except through gaining this knowledge, and no success in reaching the ranks except by harvesting its fruits. Because its rank is so high and its station so exalted, the inner sight is too weak to grasp it, minds grow ill, and eyes tire. It is a sea whose shore stops the mind; spirits and hearts cannot reach its depths.” This is the end of his words.

Al-Makki is right that only the knowledge of God's oneness can bring us to nearness to God and carry us along the way. Other kinds of knowledge and good deeds matter only when they help us gain that knowledge; they are tools, not ends in themselves.[^V09-479]

#### An unveiling insight

The straight path that brings you to Paradise is the image of guidance you create for yourself through the inward work of the heart and the states of the soul while you are in the natural world. When the veil is lifted and your heart's sight is opened, you will see that the blessed human soul itself is the image of God's straight path. It has its own limits and stages. Whoever travels through them, step by step and station by station, will reach the nearness of their Lord and enter Paradise. In this world, as with other things of the afterlife, the path is hidden from our eyes and senses.

When the veil is lifted at death and the covering is removed from your heart's sight, you will see it extended before you on the Day of Resurrection like a solid bridge over Hell.[^V09-480] It begins at the gathering place and ends at one of the gates of Paradise. Those who see it will know that it is their own work and construction. Then they will understand that, in this life, it was a bridge stretched across the Hell of their own nature. That nature is like the shadow with three branches, which is “neither cool nor does it protect from the flame.”[^V09-481] It is what leads the soul toward the blaze of its desires. The heat of that blaze will appear in the next life, though now it lies hidden within the covering of the body, like a live coal under ash. The fortunate person is the one who puts out this fire with the water of knowledge and piety.

Shaykh al-Saduq says in *The Beliefs*: “We believe that the Path is real, that it is a bridge over Hell, and that every creature must cross it. God says, ‘There is not one of you who will not pass over it; this is a decree your Lord must fulfill.’ The Path is also a name for God's proofs.[^V09-482] Whoever knew them in this world and obeyed them will be given a pass to cross the Path, the bridge over Hell on the Day of Resurrection.” The Prophet said to Ali, “Ali, on the Day of Resurrection, Gabriel and I will sit with you on the Path. No one will cross it unless they carry a certificate of your loyalty.” This is the end of al-Saduq's words.

I find it astonishing that the Path, the one who crosses it, the distance, and the movement along it are all one thing.[^V09-483] This is how it is on the afterlife journey taken by the human soul. The traveler toward God—the soul itself—travels within itself and crosses the stations and stages within itself by means of itself. At every step, it puts its foot on its own head,[^V09-484] or its head on its own foot. This sounds astonishing, but it is not astonishing once you understand what is happening.

#### Spreading out the records and bringing out the books

Words and actions do not last while they remain in the changing world of material things. But when someone acts or speaks, the act or word leaves an impression in the soul, a state of the heart that lasts for a time. When actions and words are repeated, their impressions take hold in the soul and become settled traits. A passing state differs from a settled trait only in degree: as a quality grows stronger, it can become a substantial form that gives rise to that quality. A little heat in coal, for example, can intensify until it becomes a burning flame. In the same way, a state of the soul can grow into a settled trait—a lasting form in the soul that easily produces actions of its kind, without thought or effort.

This is how skills and the roots of intellectual and practical learning take shape. If our souls were not first affected by actions, and if those impressions did not grow stronger day by day, no one could acquire a skill or a craft, and education and training would help no one. There would be no point in training children in useful actions before their habits became fixed in the opposite direction. It is hard to teach and discipline adults because animal qualities have become firmly rooted in their souls. Before that, their souls were open to learning and skill, like a blank page.

These effects that take shape in our hearts and spirits are like writing on pages and tablets. God says, “They are the ones in whose hearts He has written faith.” In the language of religion, these hearts and spirits are called the records of deeds.[^V09-485] Just as a page must be ready to receive writing, the writing also needs someone to produce it. Those who write this luminous record are the noble recording angels. Their own being and work are too noble for the lowliness of bodily matter; their nature rises above it.

These angels are a kind of angel appointed to human actions and words. God says, “Watchers are set over you, noble writers,” and, “Two receivers sit, one on the right and one on the left. Not a word does a person utter without a watcher ready beside them.” He also says, “On the Day We call every people with their leader, those who are given their book in their right hand will read it, and they will not be wronged in the least.” And He says, “As for the one who is given his book in his right hand, he will say, ‘Here, read my book! I knew I would meet my account.’”

The book of the right hand contains true knowledge and beliefs, and good character. Here “I knew” means “I was certain.” The book of the left hand contains lies, false beliefs, satanic traits, animal desires, and worldly ambitions. These burn the heart and torment the soul. A book full of lies, errors, and confused thinking deserves to burn in the Fire. God says, “As for the one who is given his book behind his back, he will call for destruction and enter a blazing Fire.”[^V09-486]

A report says that when someone does a good deed, God creates an angel from it who asks forgiveness for that person until the Day of Resurrection. God says, “Those who say, ‘Our Lord is God,’ and then remain steadfast—the angels descend upon them, saying, ‘Do not fear or grieve. Rejoice in the Garden you were promised. We are your protectors in this life and in the Hereafter.’” A good deed leads to another good deed until it becomes a settled trait.[^V09-487] The same applies in reverse to disbelief and bad beliefs. If someone forms corrupt beliefs about divine matters, clings to ignorance, and persists in disbelief, a devil descends upon them. It promises evil, deceives them through ignorance, and fills them with pride. It becomes their companion in this life and the next. God says, “Shall I tell you upon whom the devils descend? They descend upon every lying sinner who listens to God's signs, then persists in arrogant disbelief.” And He says, “Whoever turns away from the remembrance of the All-Merciful, We appoint a devil as his companion.”

A settled state of the soul that appears in a person on the Day of Resurrection is called a trait in philosophy. In religious language, a good trait is called an angel, and a bad one a devil.[^V09-488] In reality, these names point to one thing: the settled traits of the soul become substantial forms, active in the soul, bringing it pleasure or pain. Unless these traits were stable and enduring, there would be no reason for the people of Paradise to remain forever in reward or the people of Hell forever in punishment. If reward and punishment arose only from the action or word itself, both would have disappeared. The effect would remain after its cause had vanished, which cannot be right. How could a physical act performed over a limited time lead to a reward lasting forever? Such retribution would not befit the Wise, especially in the case of punishment. God says, “I am never unjust to My servants,” and “That is because of what your hearts have earned.”

The people of Paradise remain in Paradise, and the people of Hell in Hell, because their intentions and settled traits endure. Yet anyone who does even an atom's weight of good or evil in this world will find its effect written in the record of their own soul or in a record above it.[^V09-489] When the Resurrection comes and the distractions of this worldly life have ended, people will see the true face of their own being. They will look into the page of their inner life. This is what God means by, “When the records are spread open.” Someone who has ignored their own condition and failed to reckon with their good and bad deeds will say, when the veil is lifted and they come face to face with themselves, “What is this book? It leaves out nothing, small or great, but has counted it all.” They will find everything they did laid out before them, and “Your Lord wrongs no one.” God says, “On the Day every soul finds all the good it did brought before it, and all the evil it did, it will wish there were a great distance between itself and that evil.”[^V09-490]

Many reports on this subject have come to us from the Prophet and the Prophet's family. One of them is this report from Qays ibn Asim. The Prophet said, “Qays, with honor comes humiliation, and with life comes death. This world has a Hereafter, and everything has a watcher and an account. Every appointed time has a written term. You must have a companion who will be buried with you while it is alive and you are dead. If it is noble, it will honor you; if it is base, it will abandon you. Then you will be raised with it, and it will be raised with you. You will be questioned only about it, so make sure it is good. If it is good, you will find comfort with it; if it is bad, you will dread only it. That companion is your own action.”[^V09-491] Whoever reflects on this report will find in it the heart of the knowledge of the self and the afterlife, and hints at many important questions that there is no room to explain here.

Among these reports is the Prophet's saying, “Paradise is barren ground, and its plants are ‘Glory be to God.’” Another says that a person is held by their deeds, and another that “the unbeliever was created from the sin of the believer.” There are many reports of this kind.[^V09-492]

Pythagoras, one of the greatest ancient sages, is reported to have said: “Your actions, words, and thoughts will be brought before you. From every movement of thought, speech, or action, a spiritual or bodily form will appear. If it comes from anger or desire, it will become a devil that torments you in this life and keeps you from meeting the light after death. But if it comes from the intellect, it will become an angel. You will enjoy its company in this life, and it will guide you in the next toward nearness to God and the Abode of His honor.”

What happens to each person in the afterlife is the outcome and final form of what they did in this world. God says of Noah's son—reading the word as “a deed,” rather than “an act”—“He was an unrighteous deed.” Many verses of the Qur'an show that everything a person encounters in the next life, whether in Paradise—with its maidens, palaces, fruits, and other delights—or in the Fire, with its scorpions, snakes, and other torments, is the final form of their own actions and the effects of their character. Their reward there is their very own deeds, brought to their final outcome. God says, “You will be repaid only for what you did,” and “You are repaid only for what you used to do.” He does not say “for some of what you did.” He says, “The reward of God's enemies is the Fire.”[^V09-493]

To explain: the material of the people and things of the afterlife—the seeds from which its trees grow, the seed of its animals, and the underlying basis of its forms understood by the mind—is made up of inward thoughts, images formed in the soul, and ideas grasped by the intellect. The next life and everything in it are not of the same kind as this world. Things here have bodily matter that receives a form, or a soul that comes from outside, with the help of external causes and the movements and positions of the heavenly bodies. Animal bodies have a life that is added to them. But in the next life, the gardens, trees, rivers, and other things are spirits: they are images that exist on their own, and their life is their very being. Each human soul, along with its maidens, palaces, trees, and rivers, exists through one existence and lives through one life. Together they are one, yet contain many forms. When a person leaves this world, lays aside its garment, and has the veil lifted from their sight, their power to know becomes the power to see. What was hidden becomes visible. They see the outcome of their deeds and thoughts, witness the effects of their movements and actions, and read the record of their good and bad deeds.[^V09-494]

God says, “Every person has their bird fastened around their neck. On the Day of Resurrection, We will bring out for them a book they will find open: ‘Read your book! Today you are enough to call yourself to account.’”[^V09-495] This also shows that the person in the next life is not made from natural matter, but from the images of the soul and its power to know. God says, “We will bring you into a form you do not know.”[^V09-496] The Book of the righteous is in the highest realm, and what is there is not created from bodily matter. We know, then, that at the Resurrection a person takes form from what they know and believe. If they are filled with blameworthy desires, false hopes, and corrupt aims, they will be among the people of the Fire, burning in its torment, and their book will be in Sijjin. But if what they know is made up of holy realities and knowledge of God, His angels, His books, and His messengers, they will belong to the highest spiritual realm, the most exalted dwelling, the closest nearness, and the finest company.

#### The nature of reckoning and the scales

You may have realized from the principles we have repeated that, on the Day of Resurrection, everyone held accountable will see the total of all their scattered actions and words, and the final account of their good and bad deeds.[^V09-497] In their book they will find every deed, small or great. Nothing will be left out. They will find everything they did present, and their Lord will wrong no one.

An account is the gathering of separate numbers and amounts and the showing of their total. God can reveal the whole of everyone's deeds and the weight of their good and bad actions to all creatures in a single moment. He is the swiftest of those who take account.[^V09-498] God says, “We will set up the just scales on the Day of Resurrection, and no soul will be wronged in the least. Even if a deed weighs no more than a mustard seed, We will bring it forth. We are enough to take account.” He also says, “The weighing on that Day is true. Those whose scales are heavy will be the successful; those whose scales are light are the ones who have lost themselves by wronging Our signs.”

The Qur'an uses the plural “scales” to show that there are many kinds: some measure knowledge, others measure deeds. As for the measure of knowledge, God has set down a straight balance and a clear standard from heaven. Its different parts measure the portions of spiritual provision and weigh the nourishment of the soul. By it we can tell truth from falsehood, weigh the substance of intellectual realities, and distinguish sound ideas from counterfeit ones—the pure from the mixed. God taught us how to use it and recognize its five kinds through His angels and messengers.[^V09-499] He says, “Weigh with the straight balance.” Those who learn the measures God revealed in His Book to His Messenger are guided. Those who turn away from them and rely on guesses and opinions go astray and fall into Hell. You might ask: where are these measures of knowledge in the Qur'an? Is this not a false claim?[^V09-500]

We answer: have you not heard God's words in the chapter *The All-Merciful*: “The heaven He raised, and He set the balance. Weigh with justice, and do not fall short in the balance”? Have you not heard what He says in *Iron*: “We sent Our messengers with clear signs, and sent down with them the Book and the balance, so that people might uphold justice”? Do you think, thoughtful reader, that the balance God sent down with the Book and His messengers is only a device for weighing barley, wheat, rice, and dates? Do you think the balance set beside the heavens is a steelyard or a balance for weighing? That is a far-fetched mistake, and a shameful misrepresentation. Take care, reader, when interpreting the meanings of the Book. Do not force them, or stubbornly follow ignorance. I warn you not to be among the ignorant.

Know that what leads people like you—those who read the words of the Qur'an and the Prophet only in their everyday, outward sense—to take them as referring to ordinary things is their inability to think beyond bodies and physical things. If they would pause to consider the meaning of “balance,” and set aside the extra details and special shapes people associate with it, they would see that a balance does not have to have a particular shape or be a physical object. At its heart, a balance is anything by which one thing is measured against another. The things measured need not be physical. A steelyard or a two-pan balance measures weight; an astrolabe measures heights and times; a plumb line tests whether a column is upright; a ruler checks whether a line is straight. In the same way, logic measures reasoning in the theoretical sciences and shows sound thought from unsound thought. Grammar measures how words are formed; prosody measures poetry; the senses measure some things we perceive; and complete reason is a measure for everything. In short, the measure for each thing must be of the same kind as the thing measured.

The balance mentioned in the Qur'an should therefore be understood as the noblest of all balances: the balance of the Day of Reckoning. It is the measure of knowledge and of the deeds of the heart that grow out of bodily actions. When Ja'far al-Sadiq was asked about God's words, “We will set up the just scales,” he said, “The scales are the prophets and the saints.” The scale of the afterlife is what lets us recognize things as they really are: knowledge of God, His highest qualities and greatest works, His angels, His books, His messengers, and the Last Day and Resurrection—as taught by God's prophets, who learned from the angels, who learned from God. The first teacher is God, the second is Gabriel, and the third is the Messenger.[^V09-501]

God sent down from heaven five measures, drawn from the Qur'an, so that each person can know the extent of their knowledge and reason, and can measure their effort and deeds. They are the measure of equality, the measure of entailment, and the measure of contradiction. The measure of equality has three forms: the greater, the middle, and the lesser.[^V09-502] Together these make five. We explain their details, each form, and how to draw them from the Qur'an in our book *The Keys to the Unseen*. In truth, they are ladders by which the spirit rises toward the heavens and toward nearness to the Source of all things. The steps and principles set out there are stages on the spiritual ascent. A bodily ascent is beyond the power of most souls; it belongs only to the soul of the Prophet. In short, these measures of the afterlife let us weigh thoughts and judge insights in the true sciences, which are spiritual provisions for the people of the next life.

God sent them down from heaven so each person can know the extent of their knowledge and reason, measure their effort and deeds, and reckon their provision, their appointed term, the record of their life, and their hopes. Every creature has a particular provision, a known term, a written limit, and an account. Spiritual provisions, like bodily provisions, differ in kind and amount, and in how long they sustain life, how they benefit, and how they harm. The provisions of the afterlife differ even more than those of this world. God says, “The Hereafter has greater ranks and greater distinctions.” He also tells His Prophet, who warned and taught the people, “Call to the way of your Lord with wisdom and good counsel, and debate with them in the best way.” He commands the Prophet to offer different forms of spiritual provision to people, according to their different natures and dispositions. The Qur'an is like a table sent down from heaven to earth, spread with different kinds of food. Each group has its allotted provision and share of life. Wisdom and proof are food for some, counsel and public teaching for others, and debate and the appeal to common opinion for another group.

The Qur'an also contains food for other groups that is less subtle: it is like husks and bran, suited to people whose natures are coarse and low.[^V09-503] As God says, “There is nothing fresh or dry that is not in a clear Book.” Just as it contains the nourishment of sound reason for people of understanding, so it contains something like straw and husks for ordinary people, whose rank is like that of cattle. God says, “As a provision for you and your cattle.” Food must resemble the one who eats it. What is sensed nourishes the power of sensation; imagined or supposed things nourish the imaginative and estimating powers; and intelligible things nourish the intellect. Through them, the intellect is fed and strengthened, reaches its full development, and moves from a state of potential understanding to the active intellect, where it remains forever.

#### A note on the scales

One thing a traveler on the road to the afterlife must understand is how to compare the two worlds and weigh what exists in one against what exists in the other.[^V09-504] If God opens the heart to compare the visible world with the hidden world, it becomes easier to follow God's path and enter the Abode of Peace. The traveler will learn many of the Qur'an's secrets and levels, and see the realities and lights in its verses—things missed by most scholars who rely on formal learning and by famous philosophers who are thought to be wise.[^V09-505] This is a great opening into the true nature of things, especially the afterlife. It is also the first stage of prophecy. The prophets first begin to see in dreams what is in the spiritual world. The realities of things appear to them dressed in imagined forms, because a true dream is one part of prophecy.

Realities do not appear without the clothing of an image until the world of Resurrection,[^V09-506] where they exist on their own. In this world they are covered by sensory, material forms. The same is true in a true dream and in the world between death and Resurrection, though there the covering is thinner: realities appear in imagined forms. Whoever learns to compare these worlds—or the three worlds—will understand how to interpret sayings and dreams. Dreams are part of prophecy because they let the dreamer see what is in that world without the usual coverings. This ability belongs to the prophets while they are still human, but other saints gain it only after leaving this life.

Reflect on this, dear friend. Perhaps a window into the spiritual world will open for you. Otherwise, you will remain turned toward the coverings of animal habit and imitation, drawing your attention away from the lights of the spiritual world and relying on sensation and imitation. Then none of the hidden wisdom or secrets of the Resurrection will be revealed to you.

Know that you are traveling from this world to the next. Your life in this world is your capital; your trade is the knowledge you acquire, your provision for the journey; your profit is eternal life and the joy of meeting God and entering His spiritual realm. Your loss is the destruction of your soul through being kept from nearness to God and the Abode of His honor. Know that the judge sees clearly and accepts only pure gold—the knowledge and obedience that are free of dross. Weigh your good deeds with a true balance that does not tilt. Take account of yourself before your life ends and you are judged at a time when nothing can be set right. The scales are set up for the Day of Reckoning, when reward and punishment are given. Those whose scales are heavy will live in contentment; those whose scales are light will fall into the Abyss. “How will you know what it is? A blazing Fire.”[^V09-507]

As for the measure of deeds, every bodily action has an effect on the soul. Good deeds and acts of worship—prayer, fasting, pilgrimage, almsgiving, struggle, and the like—light up the soul, free it from the grip of desire, cleanse it of the darkness of bodily matter, and draw it from this world to the next and from the lower abode to the higher one. Each good deed has a particular effect in bringing light and purification. As good deeds multiply, their effects and light grow. Each bad deed likewise has a particular power to darken and cloud the soul, bind it to this world and its desires, and fetter it with chains. As sins multiply, darkness and heaviness grow stronger.

These effects remain hidden from people in this world. When the Hour comes and the veils are lifted, the truth will become clear. Each person will find the measure of their own effort and deeds, and see which side of the scale is heavier—the light of their obedience or the darkness of their rejection. In short, during life each person performs many separate deeds: good, bad, or mixed. On the Day of Resurrection, when the total of these deeds is brought together,[^V09-508] one side may outweigh the other, or the two may be equal. If the good deeds outweigh the bad, the person will be among the blessed; if the bad outweigh the good, they will be among the wretched. If the two are equal, the person will stand between the two groups until God decides whether to punish them or show them mercy. But mercy is more likely, since God is the Giver without limit.

These three groups are judged according to their deeds. But there is another group, higher than all the rest: those whose whole being is absorbed in witnessing God's majesty. They pay no attention to good or bad deeds. They have broken the two pans of their scales and left behind the world of deeds and measures, with its balance and extremes. They have entered the world of knowledge and inner states, and gaze on the lights of beauty and majesty.

In the beginning, then, everyone whose certainty, faith, and affirmation of God's oneness have not freed them from the chains of nature and the power of this world remains bound to their deeds. They are like a two-pan balance, pulled in opposite directions by the results of their actions. One pan tilts downward toward Hell according to the amount of passing worldly goods in it. The other tilts upward toward the Abode of Bliss according to the amount of afterlife provision it holds. On the Great Day of Gathering, when the two pans pull in opposite directions, God, the Most High and Great, will decide which abode each person enters, according to which pan is heavier.

The pan of good deeds is on the right, the eastern side; the pan of bad deeds is on the left, the western side. Once one side outweighs the other and judgment is carried out, the two pans become, in effect, one. The winning side overcomes and erases the other. The blessed will have both hands on the right; the people of the left will have both hands on the left. Understand this.

#### Chapter 22 — Groups of people on the Day of Resurrection

As you have learned, people in the next life fall into three broad groups: those brought near to God, the blessed people of the right, and the wretched people of the left. In terms of their final reckoning, they fall into two main classes. The first class enters Paradise and receives its blessings without an account. It contains three groups.

The first group is made up of those brought near to God, who have reached perfection in knowledge and freedom from worldly ties. Their high station and freedom from the concerns of books and accounts allow them to enter Paradise without being called to account. God says of people like them, “You are not responsible for their account, nor are they responsible for yours.”[^V09-509]

The second group is made up of some of the people of the right. They committed no sin or wrong in this world, and they sought neither power nor corruption on earth. Their consciences were clear, their original nature remained free from the stain of sin, and they were strong in doing good. They too enter Paradise without an account. God says, “We will make the home of the Hereafter for those who seek neither power nor corruption on earth. The happy ending belongs to the Godfearing.”

The third group has simple, unformed souls, and their records bear no trace of either good or bad deeds. They remain open to either possibility. God admits them to Paradise through mercy and grace, without letting the painful punishment touch them. Mercy outweighs anger; a soul that is open to good can receive it if nothing blocks it, and God is generous and kind. God says, “I am not unjust to My servants,”[^V09-510] “My mercy has gone ahead of My anger,” and “My mercy embraces all things.”

The other class consists, broadly, of those who face punishment. It too contains three groups. The first has no good deeds in its record; these people are complete unbelievers and enter Hell without an account. The second has done some good, but what applies to them is God's words, “Their deeds have come to nothing,” and, “We will turn to whatever deeds they did and make them scattered dust.” The third group is truly subject to an account because its members mixed good deeds with bad ones. This group divides in two.

Some people are examined closely, down to every small detail, because that is how they treated others in this world. They demanded every last part of what was owed to them and showed no leniency in their dealings. They will be treated in the next life as they treated people here. Others feared a bad account and dreaded the punishment of the Day of Resurrection. They will not be examined in every detail at the place of reckoning. How, then, could they be punished and kept in the place of punishment?

#### Chapter 23 — Events that take place on the Day of Resurrection

When the Light of all lights appears, the veils lift and the glory of God's sustaining presence is revealed. The rule of oneness overcomes all multiplicity. The powers that act and cause change grow strong as barriers disappear,[^V09-511] abilities that were only potential become active,[^V09-512] movements reach their goals, and realities emerge from the hidden places and material coverings that concealed them. Every being returns to its source; everything goes back to its origin; everything incomplete reaches completion. All affairs return to God, as He says, “To God belongs the inheritance of the heavens and the earth,” and, “To God all affairs return.” No one owns anything except by God's leave.[^V09-513] God says, “To whom does all power belong today? To God, the One, the All-Powerful.”

When every action is joined to its doer, every branch returns to its root, and everything that receives existence is gathered back to its giver, the stars no longer shine.[^V09-514] “When the stars are blotted out”; “when the stars are scattered”; the sun's light fades—“when the sun is folded up”—and the moon's light is erased—“when the moon is darkened.” There is no longer a spatial gap or separation between the light-giver and what receives its light. “The sun and the moon are joined together.” The soul joins with the spirits, and the divide between bodies and spirits disappears. That is why the bodies of the people of Paradise take the form of their souls, like a person and their shadow.[^V09-515]

The heavens and earth return to the state they were in before they were separated from their original unity. They return to a state of inward oneness, after having been divided in the natural world. The four elements also become one dark element. There is no sun or bitter cold to be seen there. The mountains, which came from sand, return to their state in the vision of the afterlife. God says, “They ask you about the mountains. Say, ‘My Lord will grind them to dust and leave the earth flat and bare, with no rise or hollow.’”[^V09-516] All material elements turn into one Fire, different from the elemental fire we know. All matter becomes a raging sea: “When the seas are set ablaze.” The same is suggested by what God says of Pharaoh's people: “They were drowned, then brought into a Fire.”[^V09-517]

In short, land and sea join, above and below become one, distances and sizes disappear, and barriers are removed. The veils thin for those in the intermediate world and at the place of gathering, where hidden things are exposed and witnesses stand. People are brought out from behind their veils to the place where secrets are revealed. God says, “Stop them; they must be questioned.” Those freed from the prisons of bodies and spirits turn toward the divine presence: “Then, suddenly, they will rush from their graves toward their Lord.”

One of the mystics who witnessed these things says: “When the earth brings out all its burdens, leaving nothing hidden within it, it is brought to the darkness beneath the place of gathering.” God says, “The earth and the mountains will be lifted and crushed in a single blow.” The earth is stretched out like a hide and spread flat, with no rise or hollow. It is called “the awakened plain,” because no one sleeps there. God says, “It will be only one blast, and suddenly they will be awake on the plain.”[^V09-518][^V09-519]

What lies beneath the inward curve of the sphere of the stars becomes Hell. It is called Hell because of its great depth; in Arabic, a well with a deep bottom may be called a *jahnam*. The Path is set from the earth upward to the outer surface of the sphere of the stars. Its inward face is the floor of Paradise, since all things of the next life lie within the veils of this world. That is why it is said that the earth of Paradise is the Footstool and its roof is the Throne of the All-Merciful. Scales are set up on the plain of gathering. After the general scale, every person held to account receives a scale suited to their deeds.[^V09-520][^V09-521]

In the special scales, records and pages are placed and weighed.[^V09-522] In this world, we weigh ideas and arguments with the proper measure so that we can tell sound thought from faulty thought and truth from falsehood. The last thing placed on the scale is a servant's saying, “Praise belongs to God.” The Prophet said, “Praise belongs to God; it fills the scale.” Each person's scale is shaped by their actions, and its weight or lightness depends on them.

Every remembrance and saying is weighed except “There is no god but God.” Every action has an opposite in the world of conflict, but the only opposite of affirming God's oneness is associating a partner with Him. The two cannot be together in one scale. Lasting certainty cannot coexist with its opposite, nor can both take turns in the same place.[^V09-523] So nothing can be set against that declaration to balance or outweigh it. This is shown by the report about the man whose records were weighed.[^V09-524]

Those who associate partners with God have no scale on that Day, because their good deeds have come to nothing. God says, “We will not set up a scale for them on the Day of Resurrection.” A wall called the Heights is placed between Paradise and Hell. It is a place for those whose two pans balance equally. The recording angels bring the books they wrote in this life. These books contain the actions and words of those held to account, but not the beliefs in their hearts. That is why God says, “Everything they did is written in the records,” not “everything they knew.” The books are hung around their necks or placed in their hands. God says, “Every person has their deeds fastened around their neck. On the Day of Resurrection We will bring out for them an open book: ‘Read your book! Today you are enough to call yourself to account.’” And He says, “Every soul will be repaid in full for what it did. He knows best what they do.”

Some receive their book in the right hand, some in the left, and some from behind their backs. These are the people who cast God's Book behind their backs in this world and sold it for a small price. They are the leaders of error. Each person is accompanied by a guide and a witness, whether angel or devil. God says, “Every soul will come with a driver and a witness.” Each soul has powers that move it and powers that perceive. The source of its moving powers is called the driver, whether that source is an angel or a devil; the source of its perceiving powers is called the witness. God also says, “Two receivers sit, one on the right and one on the left. Not a word does a person utter without a watcher ready beside them.”

Then God comes upon His Throne, carried by angels,[^V09-525] and they set it down on the earth shining with the light of its Lord. God says, “You will see the angels surrounding the Throne, praising their Lord. Judgment will be given between them in truth.” Paradise is on the right of the Throne, and the Fire on the other side. The angels of each heaven arrive in a separate row, apart from the angels of the other heavens and from all others. There are seven rows, with the greatest Spirit at their head.

Then the revealed Books are brought—the Books sent down to the prophets—and set there. God says, “The earth will shine with its Lord's light; the Book will be placed; the prophets and witnesses will be brought; and judgment will be given between them in truth.” Every community gathers with its messenger, both those who believed and those who did not. Individuals and prophets who were not sent with a message are gathered apart from the people. Messengers, unlike the other prophets, lead their communities, and have a special station of their own.

That Day, awe of God fills the hearts of everyone present—humans, angels, and jinn. They speak only in whispers. The veils between God and His servants are lifted. This is the meaning of “the uncovering of the shin,”[^V09-526] as in God's words, “On the Day the shin is uncovered, they will be called to bow down.” Everyone, whatever their religion, is called to bow in the familiar act of prostration. Those who bow only from fear or to show off will fall flat on their backs. This prostration makes the scale of the people on the Heights heavier—not because it is a required religious act, as some have thought, since religious duties belong only to this world, but because it is a natural act of submission, done without display or ulterior motive.

As mentioned earlier, mercy has the greater share when a person remains close to their original nature and does not turn away from what is true. They may change only by developing toward greater perfection, in keeping with the way God created them. Hell is brought to the gathering place in the form of an angry camel.[^V09-527] “On that Day, Hell will be brought near,” so that people can remember. Everyone present sees it plainly: “Hell will be exposed to all who see.” Its terrifying appearance makes all creatures realize that they are helpless, and they turn to God. If God did not hold it back through mercy, it would burn the heavens and the earth.

Death, which is the destruction of creatures through one side of an opposition, is placed between Paradise—the home of life—and the Fire—the home of ruin—in the form of a black-and-white ram. It is killed with a blade called Yahya, the image of life that will last. At Gabriel's command,[^V09-528] the source of life, the spirits give life to bodies by God's leave, so that the truth of eternal life appears: death dies, and life lives. A herald of Truth calls, “People of Paradise, you will live forever and never die. People of the Fire, you will remain forever and never die.” Their life is mixed with death, as God says, “They will neither die in it nor live.”

The people of Hell alone remain in the Fire. This is the Day of Regret, when each group sees the lasting home to which it belongs. When the people of Paradise see Death, they rejoice greatly and say, “May God bless you! You have freed us from the passing world. You were the best visitor to come to us, the best gift God sent.” The Prophet said, “Death is a gift to the believer.” The people of the Fire, who belong only to this world, see Death and panic. “You were the worst visitor to come to us,” they say. “You kept us from the good life and comfort we had.” Then they say, “Perhaps you can kill us now, so we can escape this suffering and pain.”

The gates of the Fire are then shut and never opened again.[^V09-529] They close over its people, and its parts are pressed together. Its lowest part becomes its highest, and its highest its lowest. People and devils are inside it like pieces of meat in a pot over a great fire, boiling like scalding water. The Fire and all within it turn over and over. “Whenever it dies down, We increase its blaze.” Their skins are changed, and they see Hell with the certainty of sight: “Then you will see it with the eye of certainty.” The people and stones that fuel it are those who have fallen from human life into the condition of stones. This Fire consumes itself, and its parts attack one another. It leaves bones as dust.

This bodily Fire[^V09-530] is not the same as the Fire that reaches the hearts. The bodily Fire may die down when the people of punishment sleep, easing their pain for a time. This is one meaning of “Whenever it dies down, We increase its blaze.” The verse also suggests a fire that can grow stronger or weaker. But true Fire cannot be described in this way. It may instead mean that when the fire consuming their bodies dies down through sleep or something similar, the punishment moves inward. They think about their disgrace and the horror of the Day of Resurrection, which is worse than bodily pain. Know that the Resurrection has many stages and places: the Path, the great gathering, receiving the books, setting up the scales, the Heights, and the gates of Paradise and Hell.

As for the public gathering and display,[^V09-531] it is like lining up an army so that its actions can be reviewed. All creatures meet in one place. The veils of time and the differences between places are lifted in that world. People who lived at different times and in different places in this world are all present together, in one open space on one day. God says, “The first and the last will all be gathered for the appointed time of a known Day.” The Prophet was asked about God's words, “He will be given an easy account.” He said, “That is the public display.” Whoever is examined closely in their account will be punished.
