Just as the soldiers in a parade are recognized by their uniforms and clothing, people there are recognized by their faces, their outward signs, and the forms of their intentions and character, as explained earlier. “The guilty will be recognized by their marks,” and seized by their forelocks and feet, like captives and animals here.

As for the books, the one who receives their book in the right hand will have an easy account and return happily to their people. This is the faithful and blessed person, because the book of their soul belongs to the highest tablets and honored, exalted records.[^V09-532] The one who receives their book in the left hand is a hypocrite and wretched, because their book belongs to low records and mistaken, physical writings that can be burned. God says, “The record of the wicked is in Sijjin.” The unbeliever, however, has no book. A hypocrite had the capacity to believe, so they were asked to accept faith and their outward submission was accepted, as it was for ordinary Muslims. But God says of them, “They did not believe in God, the Great.”[^V09-533] This includes those who deny God, those who associate partners with Him, and those who arrogantly reject Him. The hypocrite's inner state is one of these three. The outward form of Islam does not help them as it helps the weak and ordinary believers, for the reason explained above.

Those given their book from behind their backs are the ones who cast the Book behind them and sold it for a small price. On the Day of Resurrection they are told, “Take it from behind your back”—from the place where they cast it during their worldly life. This is like the words, “Go back and look for your light.” The book they threw away was the revelation sent to them through the Messenger, not their record of deeds. When they rejected it, they were certain they would never return to God.[^V09-534]

The scales have already been explained as far as we could explain their true meaning. The Path is the road to Paradise. It widens for some and narrows for others. In this world it is a meaning; in the next it has a visible form. Some people walk the straight path, while others turn away from it toward the road to Hell. God says, “This is My straight path, so follow it. Do not follow other paths, or they will separate you from His path.” When the Prophet recited this verse, he drew one straight line and several other lines beside it. The straight one is the path of affirming God's oneness, followed by all the prophets and their followers. The crooked lines are the ways of those led astray and the followers of Satan.

A hypocrite has no footing on the path of affirming God's oneness, but does have a footing on the path of existence. Someone who denies the Creator has no footing on the path of existence as a human being, but only as an animal or creature. God says, “There is no creature whose forelock He does not hold.”[^V09-535] Even a Muslim who sins will not remain in Hell forever. They may be held on the Path and questioned or punished there, on its surface above Hell, hidden within it. The hooks on the Path hold people there. Since the Path is over the Fire and there is no way to Paradise except across it, God says, “There is not one of you who will not pass over it; this is a decree your Lord must fulfill.”

The hooks and barbs described in reports are the image of a person's ties to this world and the worldly restraints that stop them from following the way of truth. On the Day of Resurrection, these ties hold them on the Path. Their faith and the light of their affirmation of God's oneness keep them from falling into the Fire until the intercession of those whom the All-Merciful allows reaches them. Whoever gets past this here will be spared there. If someone gives a debtor more time, God will give them more time. If someone forgives another, God will forgive them. If someone demands their full due here without leniency, God will demand their full due there. If someone treats this community harshly, God will treat them harshly. As the report says, “Your actions will return to you.” So hold fast to noble character. God will treat you as you treat His servants.

As for the Heights, they are a wall between Paradise and Hell. The side facing Paradise has a door, and mercy lies within it; its outer side faces Hell, and punishment lies there. Those whose scales are equal stand on it. They look toward Paradise with one eye and toward Hell with the other, but neither side outweighs the other enough for them to enter one of the two places. God says, “On the Heights are people who recognize each group by its marks.”

There is another interpretation of the Heights and its people, and careful thought shows that it is close to the one just given. While they remain in this natural life, the state of fully realized mystics resembles the afterlife state of those whose good and bad deeds balance. Their knowledge and insight, and the thinness of the veil around their bodies, nearly give them the joy of Paradise. But their dense physical bodies and their continued earthly life keep them from complete union and spiritual fulfillment. They occupy a middle state. Yet their essential nature and high souls stand in an elevated place. In Arabic, “the Heights” is a plural form meaning high places. A high place can see more clearly than a lower one, just as the crest of a horse or a rooster is high.

Commentators have offered two readings. Most say the Heights are the upper edge of the wall between Paradise and Hell. This is reported from Ibn Abbas, who also said that the Heights are the raised part of the Path. Hasan and al-Zajjaj offer another reading: “On the Heights” means “among those who know[^V09-536] the people of Paradise and Hell.” The verse means that some people recognize both groups by their marks. When Hasan was told that these were people whose good and bad deeds were equal, he struck his cheek and said, “They are people God has placed where they can recognize the people of Paradise and the people of Hell.” He then added, “By God, I do not know—perhaps some of them are with us.”

Those who follow the first reading disagree about who stands on the Heights. Some say they are the nobles and the most obedient people; others say they are angels who recognize the people of both abodes; others say they are prophets, whom God seats on the wall to distinguish them from the rest and let them see the people of Paradise and Hell and the degrees of their reward and punishment. Another view says they are people at the lowest rank of the rewarded. A fifth says they are martyrs. That last view is mistaken: the verse says that they recognize both the blessed and the wretched, but a martyr does not necessarily have this knowledge. If “recognize” only meant that the people of Paradise have smiling faces and the people of Hell dark faces and blue eyes, everyone at the Resurrection would know that, so it would not set the people of the Heights apart. The view that they are people of the lowest rank also fails, since they would not have the knowledge described. The second view fails because the verse calls them men, a word that usually distinguishes men from women; angels are neither. So “they recognize them by their marks” most likely means that, in this world, they recognized people of faith and goodness, and people of evil and rebellion. God places them on high stations suited to their rank, where they can see all people and testify about them. They know that the people of reward have reached their stations and the people of punishment have reached theirs.

Someone may object: the verse says that they have not entered Paradise, though they hope to do so. That does not suit the prophets or people like them.

We reply:[^V09-537] their not entering Paradise at the outset does not lessen their honor or the height of their rank. As for the statement that they hope to enter it, *hope* here means certainty. God reports Abraham as saying, “And I hope that He will forgive my fault on the Day of Judgment.” There can be no doubt that his hope here expresses certainty. The same is true in this passage.

In short, the description of the people on the Heights points to a great honor. They sit in a lofty place and recognize both groups by their marks. Such a distinction belongs to people of high standing, not to people whose good and bad deeds happen to balance. The stronger view, therefore, is that the people on the Heights hold an exalted rank.

This is supported by a report transmitted by Muhammad ibn Ya'qub al-Kulayni. Ibn al-Kawwa' asked Ali about God's words, “On the Heights are people who recognize each group by its marks.” Ali replied, “We are on the Heights. We recognize our supporters by their marks. We are the Heights, through knowledge of whom alone God is known. No one enters Paradise unless we recognize him, and no one enters the Fire unless he rejects us.”

These are some of the events of the Day of Resurrection. Many reports describe that Day, and scholars have recorded them in books. We have explained some of them as far as our strength and ability allow. The key to this knowledge is knowing the soul, for it brings forth and receives the realities of the next life. In truth, the soul is the Path, the Book, the Scale, the Heights, Paradise, and Hell, as reports from our Imams indicate.

Ibn Babawayh relates in *Meanings of the Reports* and *The Book of Divine Unity* that Ja'far al-Sadiq was asked about the straight path. He replied, “It is the Commander of the Faithful.” Ali ibn al-Husayn, the master of worshippers, said, “We are God's gates, and we are the straight path.” Ja'far ibn Muhammad said that God's words, “The path of those You have blessed,” refer to Muhammad[^V09-538] and his descendants.

Hisham ibn Salim also reports that he asked Ja'far al-Sadiq about the words, “We shall set up the just scales for the Day of Resurrection, and no soul will be wronged in anything.” He replied, “They are the prophets and their appointed successors.” And the Book mentioned in the verses about the record of the righteous in Illiyyin and the record of the wicked in Sijjin means the souls of the righteous and of the wicked, as explained earlier.

Ali also said, “You are the clear Book, whose signs reveal what lies hidden,” and, “Your cure is within you, though you do not see it; your illness comes from you, though you do not notice it.” These sayings show that the human soul is where the joys of Paradise and the pains and punishments of Hell take place.

#### Chapter 24 — What Paradise and Hell are

Paradise, as described in the Book and the teachings of the Prophet, and as confirmed by reason and spiritual insight, is the home of lasting life and peace. There is no death,[^V09-539] aging, sickness, grief, worry, decay, or loss there. It is the home of rest and honor. Its people feel no weariness or fatigue. They have whatever their souls desire and whatever delights their eyes, and they remain there forever. Its people are God's neighbors,[^V09-540] His friends and beloved ones, and those honored by Him.

They enjoy different kinds of blessing and stand at different ranks. Some take pleasure in praising, honoring, and magnifying God among the angels brought near to Him. Others enjoy the pleasures of the senses: kinds of food, drink, and fruit; couches and cushions; marriage with the wide-eyed companions; service from youths who remain young; sitting on soft cushions and rugs; and wearing fine green silk and brocade. Each person enjoys what they desire and seek, according to the aim of their soul. In general, things in the Gardens begin from acts of knowing and from powers that bring things into being. Matter and the causes that receive and support material things have no role there. Things in that world exist as forms without matter, movement, passivity, change, or transition.

Hell is the home of its people, where they face disgrace, sickness, grief, pain, hunger, thirst,[^V09-541] renewed punishment, and changing skins. They neither die nor truly live. Their punishment is not ended by death, and it is not eased for them. The true people of Hell are those who reject God and associate partners with Him; their faces are dark. People from among those who affirm God's oneness but have sinned are taken out of Hell through mercy and intercession.

A report from our Imams says that people who affirm God's oneness feel no pain in the Fire when they first enter it; God gives them pain when they leave it.[^V09-542] This fits our reasoning. A person who knows God's oneness has a soul lit by truth and certainty, lifted from the lower world toward the higher one. The Fire does not enter the place where knowledge and faith are. As the report says, “The Fire does not consume the place of faith.” In truth, the people of Hell are the unbelievers and those who associate partners with God. They will taste no coolness or drink there, except scalding water and foul fluid. If they ask for food, they are given the fruit of the tree of Zaqqum; if they cry for help, they receive water like molten metal that burns their faces. What a terrible drink and resting place! They call out from far away, but are told, “Be driven away, and do not speak to Me.” They call, “Malik, let your Lord put an end to us,” and he says, “You will remain here.”

You must know that the Garden from which Adam and his wife were sent out because of their mistake—the Garden of spirits,[^V09-543][^V09-544] which people of knowledge and religion call the place of the covenant and the source from which human descendants were taken—is not the same as the Garden promised to the Godfearing. That promised Garden is the Garden of the intermediate world. No one reaches it until their worldly life ends in death. All people reach it only after this world is destroyed, the heavens and earth pass away, and all movements reach their goals. The two Gardens agree in their true nature and level of being, because both are homes of life in its own right and of knowing through images. Neither undergoes change, decay, interruption, opposition, or conflict.

The beginnings and goals of existence face one another in a pattern of return. As you have learned, death begins the human soul's journey back to God, while natural life is the end of its descent from Him. The sages and mystics compare these two descending and ascending series to the two halves of a circle. This shows that the soul's returning journey bends back toward its source; it does not travel along the same route as its descent. Each rank on the upward curve corresponds to a rank on the downward curve, though the two ranks are not identical.[^V09-545][^V09-546]

Know also that there are two Gardens: a Garden we can sense and a Garden we can understand with the intellect. God says, “For whoever fears standing before their Lord there are two Gardens,” and, “In them are two of every kind of fruit.”[^V09-547] In the general study of existence, you have learned that there is an intellectual world containing intellectual forms and luminous patterns that match all the lights in the outer world. You have also learned that there is a world of images that can be sensed but have no material basis, even though they have direction and location. The Garden we can sense belongs to the people of the right; the intellectual Garden belongs to those brought near to God, the people of the highest realm.

There are also two Hells: one we can sense and one that is inward. The visible Fire belongs to unbelievers; the inward Fire belongs to hypocrites and the arrogant. One burns bodies; the other burns hearts. Each visible Garden and Hell is a world of forms. One is an image of God's mercy, the other an image of His anger. God says, “Whoever is overtaken by My anger has fallen.” Just as mercy is part of God's own being and anger arises as a response, reason shows—and God's words support—that mercy came before anger. “My mercy has gone ahead of My anger,” and “My mercy embraces all things.” In the same way, Paradise was created as an end in itself, while Hell exists as a secondary result. There is a secret in this.

You have learned that the next life does not occupy a place in this world, either above or below it. Everything that occupies a place in this world changes, decays, and passes away. Whatever is like this belongs to this world. The next life is the final home, not a home of ruin. It lies within this world, behind the veils of the heavens and earth, like a child in its mother's womb. Yet Paradise and Hell each have places in this world that act as mirrors and signs of their partial forms.

This is how we should understand the reports that place Paradise or Hell in particular locations. The Prophet said, “The space between my grave and my pulpit is one of the gardens of Paradise.” Another report says, “The believer's grave is one of the gardens of Paradise, and the hypocrite's grave is one of the pits of the Fire.” It is also reported that a spring from Paradise is on Mount Arvand. Abu Ja'far said that God created a Garden in the west, from which this Euphrates flows. The spirits of believers leave their graves every evening[^V09-548] and morning, fall upon its fruits, eat, enjoy its blessings, and meet and recognize one another. When dawn comes, they rise from the Garden and fly through the air between earth and sky. When the sun rises, they return to their graves.

God also created a Fire in the east for the spirits of unbelievers. At night, they eat from its Zaqqum and drink its scalding water. At dawn, they go to a valley in Yemen called Barahut, hotter than the fires of this world, where they meet and recognize one another. In the evening, they return to the Fire. This continues until the Day of Resurrection.

In *Al-Kafi*, Abu Basir reports that he said to Abu Abd Allah: “We have heard that the spirits of believers are in the crops of green birds, grazing in Paradise and resting in lamps beneath the Throne.” He replied, “No, they are not in the crops of birds.” Abu Basir asked, “Where are they, then?” He said, “In a Garden shaped like their bodies in Paradise.”[^V09-549] The same book reports that the Prophet said, “The worst Jews are the Jews of Banan; the worst Christians are the Christians of Najran; and the worst water on earth is the water of Barahut, a valley in Hadramawt. The skulls and remains of unbelievers go there.”[^V09-550]

Many reports place Paradise in the heavens. Mujahid says that God's words, “In heaven is your provision and what you are promised,” refer to Paradise and Hell; al-Dahhak says the same. It is reported that Abd Allah ibn Salam said, “The noblest of God's creation is Abu al-Qasim,” meaning the Prophet, “and Paradise is in the heavens.” Most say it is in the seventh heaven. Ibn Abbas is reported to have said that it is above the seven heavens, while Hell lies beneath layers of seas.

This is supported by the established report of the Prophet's night journey, which says that the Lote Tree was in the seventh heaven. The Qur'an says, “He saw him once more at the Lote Tree of the furthest boundary, beside which is the Garden of Refuge.” Abd Allah ibn Mas'ud is reported to have said that Paradise is in the fourth heaven, and that God will place it wherever He wills on the Day of Resurrection. Abd Allah ibn Umar is reported to have said that Paradise is folded up and hangs from the horns of the sun,[^V09-551] and that the spirits of believers are in birds like sparrows.[^V09-552] They recognize one another by the fruit of Paradise. A similar idea appears in the words of some ancient sages, who said that spirits descend into this world from the rays of the sun.

Some reports place Paradise in the lowest heaven. In the account of the Prophet's night journey, he saw Adam, the father of humanity, in the lowest heaven. On Adam's right was a door from which a pleasant breeze came; on his left was a door from which came a foul breeze. Gabriel told him that one was Paradise and the other Hell.

Other reports place it in one of the valleys of the earth. The Prophet is said to have reached a cool, pleasant valley on the way to Jerusalem and heard a voice. Gabriel told him, “That is the voice of Paradise.” Other reports say that Paradise and Hell appeared on earth at certain times. During an eclipse prayer, the Prophet said, “There is nothing you have been promised that I have not seen in this prayer. The Fire was brought near.” This was when people saw him step back, afraid its heat might reach him. He continued, “Then Paradise was brought near. That was when you saw me step forward and stand in my place. I reached out my hand, wanting to take some of its fruit so that you could see it, then I decided not to.” Muslim recorded this report.[^V09-553]

Some say that when the Prophet saw Hell during the eclipse prayer, he shielded his face from its heat with his hand and clothing, stepped back, and pleaded, “Did You not promise me, my Lord, that You would not punish them while I am among them?”—referring to God's words, “God would not punish them while you are among them, nor would He punish them while they seek forgiveness.” It is also reported that one day, after praying, the Prophet climbed the pulpit and pointed toward the front of the mosque. He said, “I have just seen Paradise and Hell, as if they were placed on this wall. I have never seen good and evil like I have today.” Al-Bukhari recorded this report. Most people say that Hell is beneath the seventh earth.

Other reports place Hell in the heavens. We have already quoted Mujahid and al-Dahhak on God's words, “In heaven is your provision and what you are promised.” In the report of the Prophet's night journey, he saw Malik, the guardian of Hell, in the lowest heaven. A path into Hell was opened for him, and he looked down into it until its smoke and sparks rose up to him from the door on his left.

Other reports place Hell in the sea. Ali is reported to have asked a Jew, “Where is the Fire in your Book?” He answered, “In the sea.” Ali said, “I think he is right,” because God says, “By the sea set ablaze.” Commentaries also say that the sea set ablaze is the Fire. Ahmad ibn Hanbal reports that the Prophet said, “The sea is Hell.” Abd Allah ibn Umar reports that the Prophet said, “No one should travel by sea except to fight in God's way or to make pilgrimage, for beneath the sea is a Fire,”[^V09-554] or, “beneath the Fire is a sea.”

Al-Tha'labi reports the Prophet's words, “The sea is a Fire within a Fire.” Ibn Abbas is reported to have said that Hell lies beneath a sea called Qays, beyond which is the sea called the Deaf Sea, beyond that the Covered Sea, then the Sea of Marmas, then the Still Sea, and then the Weeping Sea, the last of the seas and the one that surrounds them all. Each of these seas surrounds the one before it. Some early authorities explain God's words, “They ask you to hasten the punishment, though Hell surrounds the unbelievers,” by saying that Hell is the sea surrounding them. The stars are scattered into it, then it is kindled and becomes Hell.

Al-Dahhak explains God's words about Noah's people, “They were drowned, then brought into a Fire,” by saying that both happened at once in this world: they drowned on one side and burned on the other. This is like the earlier report from the sage Socrates that people who commit grave sins are thrown into Tartarus. The translator explains that Tartarus is a great cleft, a deep place into which rivers flow, meaning the sea or a deep whirlpool.

Other reports point to the Fire being in this very land, like the report about the valley mentioned earlier. Some say that part of Hell is on earth. Qatada explains God's words, “Was the one who built his building on the edge of a collapsing bank, which then collapsed into the Fire of Hell,” by saying, “By God, what is falling into Hell if not that?” Jabir ibn Abd Allah is reported to have said, “I saw smoke rising from the land of Darara.” Some say that Hadramawt is part of that region. This is close to the report about the valley of Barahut, which Ali described as the place God hates most. It contains the spirits of unbelievers and a well of foul black water where they stay. A man said that he spent a night in the valley and heard someone calling, “Dumah!” all night. He told a scholar, who said that Dumah was the name of the angel in charge of the spirits of unbelievers.

Al-Asma'i reports a man from Hadramawt as saying that a terrible, foul smell comes from the direction of Barahut, after which news arrives that a great unbeliever has died. Some of these reports speak of the spirits of unbelievers, without mentioning the Fire. But when we consider them together with the other reports and with God's words, “They are exposed to the Fire morning and evening, and when the Hour comes, ‘Bring Pharaoh's people into the harshest punishment,’” the connection to the Fire becomes clearer.

#### A note on these reports

To people who rely on outward readings, these reports may seem to contradict one another. To those who have reached spiritual insight, their inward meanings agree. Their knowledge rests on sound proofs and clear insight. They are as certain of these truths as they are of the sun at midday. Others, who rely on debate or repeat reports without understanding them, find the claims contradictory because they have not entered a subject through its proper door.

As you have learned, Paradise and Hell belong to another state of being and another world. Its beings are forms without matter, passivity, movement, or change. Everything in this world is created, corrupted, renewed, and passing away, with a place and direction. So, when a report places Paradise or Hell in a location in this world, it may mean that they are inwardly present in that place. When people say that Paradise is in the seventh heaven and Hell is beneath the heavens, they do not mean that Paradise is physically inside the heavenly sphere. They mean it is there in an inward way, as the soul is within the body. The same applies to Hell.

You have also learned that Paradise and Hell are to this world as a child is to the womb. The next life remains hidden until this world ends; the outward structure must fall before the inward world is fully revealed. Another meaning is that some places in this world act as mirrors that reveal particular forms of Paradise or Hell. A mirror shows a physical image, but the image is not inside the mirror. In the same way, some places show the forms of Paradise or Hell to those with spiritual insight. The space between the Prophet's grave and pulpit is one such mirror; a person with insight can see in it a garden of Paradise. The Prophet's mosque wall, the Euphrates, a spring on Mount Arvand, the valley of Barahut, and other places can also reveal forms of Paradise or Hell.

The sea mentioned in the report is not the visible sea. It means another, inward sea that the senses cannot perceive, as in God's words, “By the sea set ablaze.” The seven seas mentioned by Ibn Abbas and Ka'b al-Ahbar, or the Fire beneath seven seas, do not mean the seas of this world. They mean the levels of nature in their basic being. Nature itself is an unseen Fire: it burns bodies, melts forms, and changes skins.

#### Chapter 25 — Signs and forms of Paradise and Hell

Know that every real kind of thing and every basic meaning has a universal reality, particular examples, and physical forms that appear in this world. A human being, for example, has a universal reality: the universal human intellect, which gathers all human qualities and features in a higher and nobler way. This is the spirit God refers to when He says, “I breathed into him of My spirit.” There are also particular examples, such as Zayd and Amr, and visible forms, like images in mirrors or polished bodies.

Paradise has the same kinds of forms. Its universal reality is the spirit of the world and a sign of God's name, the All-Merciful. God says, “We will gather the Godfearing before the All-Merciful as honored guests.” Its universal image is like the Greatest Throne, the place where the All-Merciful is established.[^V09-555] Its particular examples include the hearts of believers, since “the believer's heart is God's Throne.” It also has visible forms in this world, such as the spring mentioned in the reports from Mount Arvand.

Hell also has a universal reality, shared by all its particular forms: distance from nearness to God and from His mercy. This is tied to His names the Compeller, the Avenger, and the All-Overpowering. Hell has a universal world of images, with seven levels beneath the Footstool. The Footstool is where the two feet are placed: the foot of the Compeller, which presses down on the people of Hell, and the foot of truth, which stands firm with God and belongs to the people of Paradise. There are roots of the lote tree there,[^V09-556] which is the tree of Zaqqum, food for sinners. That is where the deeds of the wicked and hypocrites come to an end.

Hell also has particular examples: each person's nature is the source of bodily suffering, and their soul and desires are the source of spiritual suffering. It has visible forms and places in this world, as in the reports about the valley of Barahut and other places. Paradise and Hell also have gates, as we will explain.

#### Chapter 26 — The gates of Paradise and Hell

The gates of Paradise are mentioned in God's words, “The angels will enter to them from every gate,” and, “The gates of heaven will not be opened for them, nor will they enter Paradise,” and, “Gardens of lasting life, whose gates are open to them.” The gates of Hell are mentioned in, “Enter the gates of Hell, where you will remain,” “When they come to it, its gates will be opened,” and, “It has seven gates, and each gate has an assigned share of them.”

There is disagreement about what these gates are. Some say they are the seven powers of perception in a human being: the five senses and two inner powers, imagination and estimation. One perceives images; the other perceives particular meanings.[^V09-557] These gates lead into Hell, but they also lead into Paradise when a person uses them for worship, good deeds, and understanding general truths through particular, sensory things. In short, they become gates to Paradise when used for the purpose for which they were created. Paradise also has an eighth gate, which is the heart.

Others say that the gates are the seven bodily parts subject to religious duties. Another view is that the gates of Hell are seven bad qualities, such as envy, stinginess, and pride, while the gates of Paradise are their good opposites, such as knowledge, generosity, and courage. It is possible that there are seven main kinds of bad qualities, each with many branches, and eight main kinds of good qualities, each with its own kinds, as described in books about character. But the first view is the best and fits the argument most closely.

Each of the seven senses can be a gate to worldly desires, which become burning fires and painful traits for the soul in the next life. But if a person uses these senses for good, they become gates to knowledge of reality, to understanding, and to good deeds. Through these, the person is rewarded, rises to the spiritual realm, and enters Paradise with the ranks of angels. Each sense has an outer side and an inner side. Its inner side opens toward mercy; its outer side faces punishment. The outer side opens toward Hell, or toward what makes a person deserve to enter it. The inner side opens toward Paradise, or toward what makes a person deserve to enter it.

When the gates of Hell are closed, the gates of Paradise are opened. They are like a door that opens into one room while closing off another: closing one set of gates is the same as opening the other. The exception is the gate of the heart, the eighth gate. It is always closed to those who are completely veiled from truth and reject faith, and open only to people of faith and knowledge.

The heart is sealed for the people of the Fire from eternity, as God says, “God has sealed their hearts,” and, “Their hearts were sealed, so they do not understand.” The Fire does not enter the heart or take control of it. It can only reach toward the heart without entering it,[^V09-558] just as the gate of Paradise is closed to unbelievers. The seven gates mentioned in scripture are those used by humans and jinn. The eighth gate stays closed to those who reject faith and remain veiled. Inside it are faith and servitude to God.

A report says, “Dirt does not consume the place of faith.” The heart's inner side is safe in this life and the next; suffering and punishment cannot enter it. The heart is like Paradise, surrounded by hardships. Its inner side holds mercy, while its outer side is exposed to the Fire that reaches the hearts.

The levels and openings of Hell correspond to the levels and balconies of Paradise.[^V09-559] A bad deed leads to another bad deed, just as a good deed leads to another good deed. The seven gates of Hell are named for the levels they open onto: the Gate of Hell, the Gate of the Blaze, the Gate of the Fire, the Gate of the Flame, the Gate of Saqar, the Gate of the Crusher, and the Gate of Sijjin. The eighth gate, which remains shut, is the barrier. Hell's openings are branches of disbelief and wrongdoing; Paradise's openings are branches of faith and obedience. Whoever does good will see it in the next life; whoever does evil may see it, though God may also pardon them.

#### A reminder

Know that a person's inner life in this world will be their outer form in the next. While they live here, the next life is hidden from them. When they move from this world to the next, what was hidden becomes visible to them.

Calling these outward senses the “gates of Paradise” is a loose way of speaking,[^V09-560] not the literal meaning. A house door or city gate opens directly into the place it leads to. Every door should be made of the same kind of thing as the place it opens into. These worldly senses are not like that. The senses that open into Paradise are the senses the soul will have in the next life, and they will remain as long as the soul remains.

You have learned that the soul itself has hearing, sight, smell, taste, touch, memory, and spiritual hands and feet. It has a seeing eye that looks toward its Lord, an ear that hears God's signs, the words of angels, the birds of Paradise, and the praises spoken by all things. It has a sense of smell for the scent of intimacy and the breezes of holiness; a taste for the foods and fruits of Paradise; and a touch by which it feels the companions of Paradise. These are the soul's inward senses.[^V09-561] They and what they sense belong to Paradise, unless something blocks or veils them.

The senses we use here, however, and the things they perceive, are temporary. They arise and pass away. They can cause painful suffering and a great veil, leading a person to ruin and burning them in the Fire. A soul that follows its desires, gives itself over to lust, and lets Satan rule it becomes a servant of its own appetite. God says, “Have you seen the one who takes his desire as his god? God has let him go astray despite his knowledge, sealed his hearing and heart, and covered his sight.” Each of the seven senses becomes a cause of distance from God and His realm, a gate through which the person serves desire and follows appetite, turns away from the path of guidance and truth, and falls into ruin. “Who can guide them after God? Will you not take heed?” Their state is as God describes: “As for the one who oversteps and prefers the life of this world, Hell will be his home.” This shows that each sense can become one of the seven gates of Hell.

But if the heart is lit by knowledge and faith, and it moves from possibility to full activity through discipline, worship, and cleansing from the dirt of sin and desire, it becomes like iron purified in fire. Its impurities are removed and it becomes a polished mirror. It then sees the higher spiritual world through each of its senses and finds in it one of the signs of its Lord. Each sense becomes a gate to knowledge of its Lord. Through the particular images it perceives, it draws out universal meanings and understands divine secrets. It reaches the Garden of those brought near and prepares for the highest happiness and nearness to the All-Merciful, in a place of truth before a King of great power.

The people who follow desire and ignorance, busy with worldly appetites and turning away from the next life and God's signs, persist in arrogance. God says, “He hears God's signs, then persists in arrogance as if he had not heard them.” Their gates are shut and their paths blocked, except for the road to Hell. God says, “We have placed a barrier before them and a barrier behind them, and covered them, so they cannot see.” They have no developed power of understanding that opens the way to divine knowledge and nearness to God. They have no sound heart or listening ear open to the words and warnings of God, so they cannot be saved from the punishment of the next life. Their state there will be just as they admitted when it was too late: “If only we had listened or used our reason, we would not be among the people of the Blaze.” They confess their sin. Away with the people of the Blaze!

It is now clear that all eight of these senses can become gates to Paradise for those who use them for the purpose God created them. God says, “As for the one who fears standing before his Lord and restrains himself from desire, Paradise will be his home.” The same senses become gates to Hell for those who use them to follow desire and worldly appetites: “As for the one who oversteps and prefers the life of this world, Hell will be his home.”

You might ask: a house door resembles the house it opens into. Paradise and Hell have completely different natures and kinds of existence. How, then, can the same human senses be the gates of both Paradise and Hell? We answer: the hearing, sight, and other senses of people guided toward happiness differ in their nature from the senses of people ruled by desire and misery. They may share the basic ability to sense, but the senses of the blessed are cleansed of material impurity and lit by knowledge and piety. The senses of the wretched are covered by the darkness of habit and dimmed by ignorance and desire. In short, the blessed person's hearing, sight, and heart have been changed in the next life. They have become suitable gates to Paradise, the home of good deeds and blessings. But the hearing, sight, and heart of the people of Hell have become more wretched, darker, and more impure than they were before. They are now suited to be gates to the home of darkness, ruin, punishment, and pain.

#### An unveiling insight

Know—may God guide you along the straight path to the next life—that Paradise, which those who belong there will reach, is already visible to you today in the place where it is,[^V09-562] though not in its outward form. You move through it in your present state without knowing that you are there. Your natural form keeps you from seeing it and the blessings prepared within it: its rooms, trees, rivers, food, and drink. Those with spiritual insight can see what is hidden from others. They can see this place as a green garden, or as Hell, with the toil and strain of its heat and cold, its fires and flames, its snakes and scorpions, its scalding water and Zaqqum.

People without spiritual insight and clear inner sight remain blind within their veil. They are like a blind person in a garden: the garden is not absent, but they cannot see it. In the same way, most of the people of Paradise are already in Paradise but do not see it. The people of Hell are already in Hell, surrounded by its walls, but do not know it. God points to this when He says, “Hell surrounds the unbelievers,” and, “A Garden as wide as the heavens and earth has been prepared for those who believe.” A sacred report says, “I have prepared for My righteous servants what no eye has seen…” You have learned that a believer's Paradise and an unbeliever's Hell are not things outside their own souls. If they are prepared now, they are already joined to the soul, even if a veil blocks a person from seeing them. God can stand between a person and their own heart—how much more can He stand between a person and the joy or pain within it?

Some people keep this spiritual sight throughout their lives; others do not. The second group may still be higher in rank, for God has hidden wisdom in His creatures. The people of God have eyes with which they see, ears with which they hear, and hearts with which they understand—but these are not the eyes, ears, and hearts of the body, as you learned earlier. The unbelieving and veiled are deaf, mute, and blind; they do not understand what comes from God and do not return to Him.

#### Chapter 27 — Further insight into what Paradise and Hell are

You have learned that the next life lies between the world of pure intellect and the material world of bodies. Everything there appears in forms that can be sensed and known by a power of the soul that is imagination here and sense there. When a person dies and is freed from this natural body, their small Resurrection begins. They are first gathered into the intermediate world, then into Paradise or Hell at the Great Resurrection.

The difference between the forms a person sees and takes on in the intermediate world and the forms they see and take on in Paradise or Hell at the Great Resurrection is one of strength and weakness, fullness and incompleteness. Both are particular forms that can be perceived but are not made of matter. In the intermediate world they are seen by imagination; in Paradise they are seen by sense. Yet the sense of the next life is not different from imagination. This is unlike the senses of this world, which are divided among five powers in five different parts of the body. Sight is in the eye, hearing in the ear, taste in the tongue. None can do the work of another: sight cannot hear, hearing cannot see, and neither can smell or taste. The same is true of the other senses. Even sight and touch are not in one place: sight is in the lens of the eye, touch in its outer covering.

The senses of the next life, however, are all in one place. They do not differ in location or direction, and each can do the work of the others. The relation of forms in the intermediate world to forms at the Great Resurrection is like the relation of a child or unborn baby to an adult.[^V09-563]

Ibn Arabi says in *The Meccan Revelations*, in chapter 355: “Between the two states of life, death is a time in the intermediate world. There, spirits build intermediate, imagined bodies, just as they built them during sleep. These bodies grow out of our earthly bodies, since imagination is one of their powers.” He continues: “When a person dies, their Resurrection has begun. It is a partial Resurrection. If you understand the Resurrection of one person, you understand the general Resurrection of every person who dies. The time in the intermediate world is to the next life as a woman's pregnancy is to birth. God develops the person there, stage after stage, through different forms of life, until they are born on the Day of Resurrection. This is why we say that when someone dies, their Resurrection begins: the next life starts to appear in the intermediate world, until they are raised from it on the Day of Resurrection, just as a child is born from the womb into the world.”[^V09-564][^V09-565]

“Think about how a person's body develops on earth while they are in the intermediate world. God shapes and adjusts it without following any earlier pattern, preparing it for the next life.”[^V09-566]

In chapter 374 of the same book, Ibn Arabi says: “Know that God has always appeared to hearts in this world, and different thoughts arise because His appearance takes different forms. The different thoughts in a person are themselves a form of God's appearance, though only the people of God know this. They also know that the different forms seen in this world and the next are all different forms of that appearance. He is the One who appears, and He is the inner reality of every thing.

“In the next life, a person's inner state is fixed, because it is the inner reality of their outward form. The change within them is hidden. It is the new creation that happens in every moment, though people fail to see it. In the next life, what was inward becomes outward. God's appearance is always active there, so a person's outward form changes in the next life just as their inner thoughts change in this life. The forms through which God appears color the person who sees them. This is the divine likeness within imagination. The difference is that in the next life it is outward, while in this life it is inward. The power of imagination stays with a person in the next life.”[^V09-567][^V09-568][^V09-569]

Something like this also happens in this world to some complete people, such as prophets and saints, and to others, such as fortune-tellers, the mentally ill, and people with brain fever. If someone's imagination is strong or their senses are weak, they may see something through imagination as a visible, tangible thing, like the other things they sense. They often confuse it with something in the outer world and wrongly believe that it is physically there.

Everything a person sees on the Day of Resurrection is seen through imagination. Those forms exist in that world, where they belong. But the things seen through imagination here, such as spiritual ranks, are not treated as lasting realities because they quickly disappear behind a veil. We cannot depend on them here because they soon leave our sight. This world is not where those forms exist. At death, the veil is fully lifted, and the vision of imagination lasts.

The forms the soul sees are not outside it; they are its own forms. In the next life and in the world of imagination, bodies are spirits and spirits are bodies. This is what it means for meanings and spirits to take bodily form. It happens only in that world. In this world, spirits are connected to bodies but do not take bodily form.

In chapter 373, Ibn Arabi asks about spirits that appear in the forms of bodies: “Is the spirit itself the form it appears in, without leaving its own spiritual station? Or is that form only in the eye of the person who sees it, as the sky appears blue? Or is the spirit to that form like the soul is to the body—that is, a real image with its own existence, as the soul exists in the body?” Many people overlook this question.[^V09-570] They are satisfied with the strange forms they have seen, and their pleasure in the experience keeps them from asking what it was or how it happened.[^V09-571]

“If they could take on spiritual form within themselves, shape their imagined forms by their own power, and make their natural forms yield to them—like a material that receives the forms created by the soul, as jinn take on different shapes—they would understand how spirits take bodily form.”[^V09-572]

“He who knows that an animal's spiritual form passes into the intermediate world when the animal is killed, or that a plant's form passes there when it is cut, will know that it does not vanish. The spirit remains embodied in an imagined form.[^V09-573]

If we see it afterward, we see it as we see any dead creature, human or otherwise.”[^V09-574][^V09-575]

In chapter 381, Ibn Arabi says: “Know that these ranks can be seen only by the eye of imagination, not the eye of the body, even when they appear before us. When God gives them forms, we imagine them and see them as clearly as we see people with our eyes. We see meanings with inner sight. When God makes many things seem few, or few things seem many, we see this through imagination,[^V09-576] not bodily sight—even though our bodily eyes are involved in both cases. God says, ‘When you met them, He made them seem few to you, and made you seem few to them,’ and, ‘They saw them twice as many as themselves.’ They did not appear twice as numerous to bodily sight. If they had not seen the many as few through imagination, that would have been false, and the one showing them this would have been lying. But if they saw the many as few through imagination, it was true in that world though not by bodily sight. In the same way, whenever you see something as different from what it is in the outer world, you see it through imagination.”

“People who have never learned this knowledge cannot understand it. They do not distinguish between kinds of sight. Know that you can reach it only through a power God gives to some of His servants. Consider how, if the Companions had understood this distinction, they would not have said that Gabriel was Dihya al-Kalbi.”[^V09-577] “They would have said, ‘This is a spiritual being or a meaning that has taken form; otherwise, we see Dihya with our physical eyes.’ They did not understand the matter fully, though they were honest. When they asked who he was, the Prophet told them that it was Gabriel. Then they understood what they had seen.

“Nothing in the world is more easily confused than imagination and bodily sight. If a person is very skilled at this, they may doubt even things they know by direct experience. If they are not skilled, they judge something wrongly and call a form seen in imagination a dark, physical object. If God gives a person a clear power of distinction, they understand the truth.”[^V09-578]

“Many people of God do not pay attention to this distinction.[^V09-579] If a person were always asleep, they would not know that what they see in a dream is imagination. Yet, while awake, they often see things just like the things they see in sleep and do not distinguish between them. Both are real and clearly present in their own way.

“This is a wide subject. People who rely on formal learning do not recognize it, nor do philosophers who think they understand wisdom. They have failed to recognize the height of this rank or its value. Only God knows its worth, along with those He chooses from among the prophets and saints. Knowledge of it is one of the first ranks of prophecy.

“How beautifully God draws the attention of thoughtful people to it when He says, ‘He forms you in the wombs however He wills.’ Some wombs are in imagination. God forms imagined things in them however He wills, through a spiritual joining and a spiritual pregnancy. He opens the meanings in that womb and gives them whatever forms He wills: Islam takes the form of a camel, the Qur'an of butter and honey, steadfastness in religion of a chain, religion of a loose robe, and knowledge of milk.”

In the same chapter, Ibn Arabi also says: “In the world of imagination, God is the place from which a servant's thoughts take form. Every thought the servant has in this world is created by God there, just as God creates the possible things in existence whenever He wills.[^V09-580] In this world of imagination, the servant's will follows God's will. The servant wills only what God wills, though in another sense God creates only what the servant wills. Whatever the servant wills in this world may or may not appear to the bodily senses. But in imagination, the servant's will takes effect, as God's will does.

“God remains with the servant in this place, creating what the servant wants to create. The same is true of the next life, where the person's inward state becomes outward. Whatever they desire takes form. God acts in response to the person's desires in that world, though not through bodily senses in this one. In this world, God follows the servant's will in this inner realm, and in the next life He follows the servant's desire.[^V09-581] But just as the servant's will follows God's will, the servant's desire follows God's desire. God appears in the form through which the servant sees Him, and the servant takes on that form.[^V09-582]

“We have been given powers of will that act in the world of imagination,[^V09-583] and other powers that do not act on the bodily world. This is why things differ—even the divine names differ in rank.[^V09-584] A person's active will can affect the will of others, or it may not affect them. For example, ‘You cannot guide whom you love.’” These are the end of his words.

All that we have explained and quoted makes this clear: the bodily Paradise consists of forms perceived by the soul's imagination, and desired and enjoyed by it. The soul itself is their only material and source. It is also their maker and the immediate cause that brings them into being. A single human soul, together with all the forms it perceives, is like a great world of the soul, greater than this physical world and all that it contains.

All the trees, rivers, buildings, and rooms found there are alive through their own being. Their life is one life: the life of the soul that perceives and creates them. The soul's perception of these forms is the very act that creates them. It does not first perceive them and then create them, or create them and then perceive them. It perceives and creates them together, without one coming before the other.[^V09-585]

Hell is different. It is not purely spiritual. It is clouded and mixed with this world. It is as if this world, driven by force and control, had been moved into the next life. The person in Hell wants what they cannot get, longs for what harms them, does what they hate, chooses what punishes them, and runs from what would keep them company. They say, as God reports, “If only there were between me and you the distance between east and west! What a terrible companion!”

The true nature of Hell and all that is in it is the truth of this world and its desires, appearing to wretched souls as forms that hurt and torment them. These forms burn the body, melt its flesh and fat, change its skin, deform it, and darken its face. All the forms in the next life have their meanings here; “your deeds will return to you.”[^V09-586]

Since only God is the true maker and cause in the next life, there are no opposing causes, barriers, or forces there as there are here. Whoever agrees with God's judgment and joins their will to God's will, letting their own choice dissolve into His choice, has made their action God's action. Such a person lives in lasting joy and great pleasure. The believer must be like this. I have already explained that the servant's will in Paradise is the same as God's will. The reason is that everything comes from God in the most complete order and the highest good.

A person whose heart is sound, whose nature is free of illness, and whose sense of taste has escaped the bitterness of sin accepts God's judgment. They reach the state of being content and of accepting God's will. They want only what God wants; God wants only good, completion, blessing, and joy. Such a person lives in blessing and happiness without end, because they see God's mercy embracing all things. They see in everything the lasting face of Truth, the pure good and absolute beauty that is the source of every good, perfection, and beauty. They rejoice in God and in all things, because everything comes from Him, exists through Him, and returns to Him. They live in a Garden as wide as the heavens and earth. This is why the guardian of Paradise is called Ridwan, “Contentment.” No one enters Paradise or reaches the home of blessing and honor unless they have reached contentment.[^V09-587]

Those who do not follow the path of knowledge and faith, but remain trapped by worldly desire, have always been prisoners of their appetites and attachments. Desire and appetite oppose wisdom and justice. Since the heavens and earth are established through wisdom and justice, the world of being is spoiled for anyone whose nature opposes its order. Woe to the person whose world has turned against them and whose nature fights the order of existence. The Ruler of the world and the heavens takes vengeance on them because they have become an enemy of God and the world. God says, “If the truth had followed their desires, the heavens and earth and everyone in them would have fallen into ruin.”

Such a person is kept from what they desire and what their appetite demands. God says, “A barrier was placed between them and what they desire.”[^V09-588] The ignorant person may wish that the stones in their house were pearls, that they would never grow old, and that their body could remain young and beautiful forever. But God's wisdom requires pearls to be rare and valuable, and soil to be plentiful for farming and building. It also requires people to travel toward God, not to spend all their effort maintaining their bodies. Their bodies must grow strong and firm, with dense flesh, so they can bear hardship and resist being harmed.

The abode of Hell and punishment is not a separate, independent world or dwelling. It is a mixed state and a dependent form, whose source is this world, carried into the Resurrection by the angels of punishment because of sins, wrong actions, and attachment to worldly things. Understand this carefully. It is a subtle truth, and few mystics have seen the real nature of Hell.[^V09-589] Many, however, have seen the true nature of Paradise and what is in it.

What supports this view is that Hell is an image of God's anger, just as Paradise is an image of His mercy. We have already shown that God's mercy is essential and reaches all things,[^V09-590] while anger is a response. Good things come directly from their source; evil things occur as secondary results.[^V09-591] By the same reasoning, Paradise exists in its own right, while the Fire exists as a consequence.

#### A note from a mystic

A mystic says[^V09-592] about God's words, “Protect yourselves from the Fire whose fuel is people and stones”: Fire can be a remedy for some illnesses. There are illnesses that can be cured only by cauterizing with fire. God says of some people, “They will be branded on their foreheads and sides.” God has made fire a remedy in this case, protecting them from a disease worse than fire itself. What disease is greater than grave sins? God may therefore use Hell as a medicine for them in the next life, just as fire is used to cauterize in this life. By entering Hell, they are cured of something worse: God's anger. For this reason they later leave Hell and enter Paradise, just as worldly laws can protect people from punishment in the next life. These are the mystic's words.

This supports what we have said: Hell is not a real, independent home of punishment. It begins when error and disobedience take form in the soul. If Adam's children had not sinned, God would not have created the Fire. Know that the people who suffer are those whose souls were ready for one of the ranks of Paradise, but whose readiness was destroyed by sin. They are like people whose bodies lose the balance and health that suit them. They feel great pain until they return to the balance that was right for them. Or their readiness may be destroyed completely, and their nature may change into one that fits the state that once harmed them. What was illness becomes health, and pain becomes rest.

The same is true of those who enter the Fire for a time because of their bad deeds. If the light of faith remains in the heart, the darkness of sin cannot reach its innermost part or surround it completely. Such a person will leave Hell and recover from its punishment. But if disbelief has made the heart completely black, and the darkness of sin has reached its deepest part, that person will never leave the Fire; they will remain there forever. If you want to understand this further, listen carefully to what follows, and keep it from the wicked and base.

#### Chapter 28 — How the people of Hell remain in Hell

This is a difficult question, and scholars who rely on formal learning disagree about it. Mystics disagree too. Will the punishment of those who truly belong in Hell last without end? Or will they find happiness in their home of misery, so that their pain ends after a set time? They do agree that unbelievers do not leave Hell and remain there forever.[^V09-593] Each of the two homes has its own inhabitants and will be filled.

Know that the principles of philosophy show that forced states do not last forever in a thing's nature. Everything in the natural world has an end it reaches in time, and that end is its good and completion. God created things with an inborn power that preserves the good they already have and makes them seek the completion they still lack. As God says, “He gave each thing its form, then guided it.” So every thing loves existence and longs for the fullness of existence. This is its own end, the goal it seeks and moves toward by its nature. The same is true of the ends that lead to it, until we reach the highest end and the greatest good. Something may be held back by an obstacle or forced away from its goal, but such obstacles are not common and do not last forever. We have already explained that if they did, the order of the world would collapse: things would stop working, goodness would disappear, the heavens and earth would fail, and neither this world nor the next would come into being. That is what those who deny the afterlife assume.

We can see, then, that all things naturally seek the Real and long to meet Him. Hatred and aversion are passing states. Whoever loves meeting God by nature is loved by God in return. Whoever turns away from meeting Him because of an illness that has come over the soul is turned away from in return. God may punish such a person for a time, until they recover and return to their original nature. Or they may grow used to their painful state. Once they give up hope, their pain and punishment end, and they take on a second nature: the nature of unbelievers who have lost hope in God's special mercy. His general mercy, however, reaches all things. God says, “I send My punishment on whom I will, but My mercy embraces all things.” We also have principles showing that Hell, with its pain and evils, lasts for its inhabitants, just as Paradise, with its joy and good, lasts for its inhabitants. But “lasting” means something different in each case.[^V09-594]

You also know that the order of this world depends on people with rough natures and hard, unyielding hearts. If everyone were blessed, afraid of God's punishment, and humble before Him, the world would fall apart. No one would remain to build and maintain it. It needs people with hard, defiant natures, such as Pharaohs and impostors; people who do harm, such as human devils, whose cunning and nature fit that role; and ignorant, animal-like people, such as unbelievers. A sacred report says, “I made Adam's disobedience a cause of the world's being built.” God also says, “If We had wished, We could have given every soul its guidance. But My word has come true: I will fill Hell with jinn and people together.”[^V09-595] A world made up of only one kind of person would go against wisdom. It would leave all other possible kinds unused, without bringing them from possibility into life. Most levels of this world would then have no inhabitants. The world can function only if it includes low and lesser kinds of things, needed here, with people living in darkness and veiled from the truth. The low and hard-hearted find their pleasures there, far from the home of light, honor, and love.

True wisdom therefore requires different levels of readiness, from strong to weak and from clear to clouded. God's unchanging decree has established that both the blessed and the wretched will exist. Since each group exists by God's decree and through the appearance of a divine name, each has its own true end and proper home. When things return to the nature they were made for, they find what suits them and gives them pleasure. But if they are kept away from it for a long time, as God says, “A barrier has been placed between them and what they desire,” they suffer. God appears through all His names and qualities at every level and in every place, as we have explained elsewhere. He is the All-Merciful and the Most Compassionate, but also the Mighty and the Overpowering. A sacred report says, “If you did not sin, God would remove you and bring another people who would sin.”

The Arab mystic Ibn Arabi says in *The Meccan Revelations*: “The people of the two homes enter them: the blessed enter Paradise through God's grace, and the people of Hell enter it through His justice. Their deeds bring them down to those homes, and their intentions keep them there. The pain they suffer as punishment lasts as long as their worldly life spent in unbelief. When that time ends, they find pleasure in the home where they will remain. If they entered Paradise, they would suffer because it does not suit the nature they were given.[^V09-596] They take pleasure in the fire and bitter cold, and in the bites of snakes and scorpions, just as the people of Paradise take pleasure in shade and light and in kissing the beautiful companions of Paradise. Their natures call for these things. A dung beetle is harmed by the smell of roses but enjoys filth. Someone overheated is pained by the smell of musk. Pleasure follows what suits a person's nature, and pain follows what does not.” Ibn Arabi also quotes a mystic who says that everyone in Hell will eventually leave for Paradise. Then no one will be left there; its doors will flap in the wind, and watercress will grow at its bottom. God will create new inhabitants to fill it. Qaysari says in his commentary on *The Bezels of Wisdom*: “Anyone whose eye has been opened by the light of Truth knows that all the world belongs to God. No one has existence, qualities, or power except through Him.[^V09-597] Everyone needs His mercy, for He is the All-Merciful, the Most Compassionate. One who has these qualities would not punish anyone forever. Even the punishment that He gives lasts only long enough to bring people to the completion set for them. Gold and silver are heated in fire to remove what clouds them and lowers their quality. So punishment holds kindness within it. As the saying goes: ‘Your torment is sweetness; Your anger, contentment; Your cutting off, connection; Your injustice, justice.’” He ends there. You may ask: do not these views, which say that the punishment of the people of Hell will end, contradict what I said earlier—that their pain will last?

I answer: there is no contradiction.[^V09-598] The punishment of Hell may never end for Hell as a whole, while the punishment of each person there ends at a certain time. Ibn Arabi says in *The Meccan Revelations*: “One of the basic states God gave people at their creation[^V09-599] is that they worship only Him. They remained true to this nature and to God's oneness. They did not take anything else alongside God and call it God.[^V09-600] Rather, they treated other things as gods because they thought these would bring them close to God. That is why God says, ‘Name them.’[^V09-601] Once they name them, it becomes clear that they worship only God, in the place where they say divinity is found. The oneness they affirmed in the covenant is therefore still true, and their original nature remains with them.”

I say: this is worship rooted in our nature. We have already said that every natural movement and change in things and souls is toward God, through God, and on God's path. By nature, every person is among those traveling toward Him. By choice and desire, a blessed person draws even closer, moving with greater effort and speed along the path they already follow by nature.

But if a person is an incomplete unbeliever, with a heart sealed shut and no power to understand, they are like livestock: they know only animal aims. Their purpose in this life is to guard the world and maintain their bodies. They have no share in the next life. They wander like animals and wild beasts. They are gathered like them, punished like them, judged like them, and given pleasures like theirs. A hypocrite, on the other hand, has turned away from the special nature God gave people and has been shut out from the sky of mercy. Their pain is severe because they have turned away from their own nature and fallen into an abyss through their own actions. Their pain is in proportion to how far they have left that nature and sunk into Hell.

Yet mercy is wide. Pain shows that there is an original self within a person that resists their bad animal habits. Conflict between opposing things does not last forever or happen most of the time. It must end either when one side gives way or when the conflict is resolved. But the human soul itself cannot be destroyed. So either the bad qualities disappear when their causes disappear, and the soul returns to its original nature and enters Paradise—unless its bad qualities include beliefs such as worshipping other gods—or else it changes into a second nature and escapes pain and punishment. This is what philosophers mean when they say that the punishment of someone trapped in a false belief may last forever. Someone whose false belief has become firm and deep, along with stubborn ignorance, cannot return to their original nature. They become dead to this life and to the life of reason, though they remain alive in a lower, lesser kind of life.[^V09-602] This is what God means when He says of them, “They neither die in it nor live”: they do not die like animals, but they do not live the rational, blessed life either.

Ibn Arabi gives these words as evidence that the punishment of those who remain in Hell will end: “Those are the people of the Fire; they will remain in it,” and the Prophet's saying, “No one will remain in the Fire except those who belong there.” Ibn Arabi's reason is that one of the greatest pains a person can suffer is to leave a home they know well. If the people of Hell left it, they would suffer from being far from the place they belong. God made them with a nature suited to that home. I say that this argument is weak. It relies on the words “people” and “belong there,” which can express other kinds of relation, such as being near, being beside something, or having a claim to it.[^V09-603]

Nor do I agree that leaving a familiar place is the greatest pain, unless we mean the place that fits a person's nature. That claim is hard to prove. A better proof comes from God's words, “We have created many jinn and people for Hell.”[^V09-604] If God created someone with the purpose, under His decree, of entering Hell, then entering it must suit their nature and complete their existence. As we said, the end of a thing is its completion. What completes it cannot be painful to it, though it may be painful to someone created for a higher level.

Ibn Arabi says in *The Meccan Revelations*: “The two homes—the home of joy and the home of Hell—are filled. The blessed enter the first through God's grace, and the people of Hell enter the second through God's justice. Their deeds bring them down to these homes; their intentions keep them there. Their pain lasts for a time equal to the length of their worldly life in unbelief. When that time is over, they find pleasure in the home where they will remain, because they would suffer if they entered Paradise, which does not suit their nature. They take pleasure in the fire and bitter cold, and in the bites of snakes and scorpions, just as the people of Paradise enjoy shade and light and the beautiful companions there. Their natures call for these things. A dung beetle is harmed by the scent of roses but enjoys filth; a person with a fever is pained by the scent of musk. Pleasure follows what suits a person's nature, and pain follows what does not.” He also quotes a mystic who says that all the people in Hell will leave for Paradise, until none are left. The gates will then swing in the wind, watercress will grow at the bottom, and God will create new inhabitants to fill it.

Qaysari says in his commentary on *The Bezels of Wisdom*: “Anyone whose eye is opened by the light of Truth knows that the whole world belongs to God. No one has existence, qualities, or power except through Him. All are in need of His mercy, for He is the All-Merciful and Most Compassionate. One with these qualities would not punish anyone forever. Even punishment is only given to bring people to the completion set for them, just as fire melts gold and silver to remove what clouds them and lowers their quality. Punishment therefore holds kindness within it. As the saying goes: ‘Your torment is sweetness; Your anger, contentment; Your cutting off, connection; Your injustice, justice.’”

You may say: reason shows that God gains nothing from obedience and loses nothing through disobedience. Everything happens by His decree, and people are compelled even in their choices. How, then, could He make their punishment last forever? The Prophet says that the last to intercede will be the Most Compassionate. Every verse that speaks of their punishment is true, and the words of mystics do not contradict those verses. A thing's being punishment in one respect does not prevent it from being mercy in another. Glory be to the One whose mercy reaches His friends even while He punishes them in this world, and whose punishment reaches His enemies even while His mercy is wide in the next.

Ibn Arabi adds: “When the Real appeared to people as they made their covenant with Him, He appeared to them in one of the divine forms.[^V09-605] This gave them the courage to worship Him through visible forms. Their original nature remained, because in truth they did not worship those forms. They worshipped them only because they thought those forms would bring them close to God or intercede for them. God reports their words: ‘We worship them only so they may bring us nearer to God,’ and, ‘These are our intercessors with God.’ These two hopes—intercession and seeing God in different forms—are where people will turn in the next life.[^V09-606]

“When a person understands this divine knowledge,[^V09-607] and sees that those people were driven by forces that would eventually lead them back to God, they will pray for them in the darkness and plead for them: ‘Let them enter Your mercy after they have received the punishment they deserve. If they must remain in that home, give them pleasure there, for they too are among all the things Your general mercy embraces.’ It is impossible for God to be limited. He says that His mercy comes before His anger. Anger therefore ends in nothing.[^V09-608] If it has any effect, it is still surrounded by mercy.

“The prophets say that, on the Day of Resurrection, when they are asked to intercede, God is angry with an anger like no anger before it and unlike any that follows it. This is the most hopeful report to rely on in this matter. That day is when people rise from their graves and stand before the Lord of all worlds. God's anger falls on those who deserve it, and that anger commands them to enter the Fire, suffer, and face punishment—whether they worshipped other gods or committed other wrongs. Some leave through the intercession of those who plead for them; others are brought out by the All-Merciful, as the report says. He then admits them to Paradise, unless they are among the people who truly belong in Hell. The command to enter the Fire therefore applies to all who enter it, both those who belong there and those who do not.

“If their punishment lasted forever,[^V09-609] God's anger would then be greater than the anger that commanded them to enter Hell. But the prophets say that God will not be angry like that again. Mercy must therefore embrace everyone. It is enough that the Prophet's words say, ‘As for the people of the Fire who belong there’—not simply ‘the people being punished.’ Those who live in Hell need not suffer there. Its inhabitants and keepers include Malik and the other angels, as well as insects, snakes, and other animals brought back to life on the Day of Resurrection. The Fire will not punish them. The same may be true of those who remain there: ‘They neither die in it nor live.’”

Scripture gives further proof that God says, “My servants,” to all people.[^V09-610] He calls them His own, and He does so only for those already touched by His mercy. So He will not punish them forever, even if they enter Hell. God says: “If the first and last of you, the people and jinn among you, all had the most Godfearing heart, that would add nothing to My kingdom. My servants, if the first and last of you, the people and jinn among you, all had the most wicked heart, that would take nothing away from My kingdom. My servants, if the first and last of you, the people and jinn among you, stood together on one plain[^V09-611] and asked Me, and I gave each one what they asked for, that would take nothing from My kingdom.”

Everyone naturally dislikes what causes pain and asks God to spare them and give them pleasure. A person may ask with words, or simply through their state—as when a hungry baby cries, even before it understands pain in the body or frustration at being kept from the breast.

A person's needs and the states that pass through the heart are beyond counting. I have given you some examples in this chapter. Ibn Arabi ends there. Know that reason and spiritual insight agree on this: the flow of existence, God's creative word and the word “Be,” comes from Him as one undivided whole. It reaches the Footstool,[^V09-612][^V09-613] then divides in two. One part flows toward this world of visible things, and the other toward the next world of the unseen. It is like a great river flowing as one, then dividing into two rivers. One runs to the right, the nobler, brighter, and stronger side; the other to the left, the lesser, dimmer, and weaker side. The blessed are people of the right and belong to the next life. The wretched are people of the left and belong to this world. We have already pointed out that the people of this world are the people of Hell, because Hell's true nature and pain grow out of this world and its desires.

We have also said that Hell is not a separate home with its own independent existence. There are only two homes: the home of natural bodies that come to be and pass away, and the home of spiritual bodies, alive through their own being and joined to spirits. The difference between this world and Hell is that, while the wretched soul still lives through its natural body, this world is its home of pleasure. It enjoys its desires as animals do. But when it leaves this natural life and enters the next life, its strong attachment to worldly things turns those things into pain. What it once called good now becomes evil. This world's desires become snakes and scorpions in the deepest part of its heart; its food and drink become scalding water and Zaqqum. All these things take forms that match what they mean. The hearts and souls of the people there keep burning with envy, anger, pride, and other traits. Their skin and bodies burn with the fire of nature and desire. The snakes and scorpions formed by their bad habits sting and bite them. This continues as long as God wills.

We can now see that there are two homes, and this world and Hell count as one kind of home.

With this in mind, we can explain why mystics speak of God's two feet resting on the Footstool. They use this image for the flow of existence from God into this world and the next.
