They also speak of God's two hands when they mean His qualities of beauty and majesty, or the angels of intellect and soul, because these are channels for His giving and generosity. Their images and ways of speaking agree with scripture and prophetic reports. Ibn Arabi says in chapter 374 of *The Meccan Revelations*: “The two feet came down to the Footstool, and the spiritual word divided there. The Footstool itself appeared through that word, so the word divided in it. The Footstool is the second form of pure, original bodies after the surrounding Throne. It has two shapes within the universal body, and the two feet came down to it. Each rests in a place.[^V09-614] One place is called Hell; the other is called Paradise. There is no other place for the feet to move to. They receive only from their source, the All-Merciful, who is established on the Throne. So they give only mercy.

The end returns to the beginning in accordance with wisdom. But there is a road between beginning and end. Without that road there would be no beginning or end. The command that descends must therefore make a journey[^V09-615] between them, and a journey can bring hardship and suffering. This is why there is suffering in this world, in the next world, and in the world between them. When the journey ends, the traveler lays down the staff of travel,[^V09-616] and finds rest in the lasting home.

You may object: “If so, people should find rest as soon as they enter their lasting home, even if it is Hell. Yet that is not what happens.”

We reply: “You are right, but you have overlooked something. Travelers are of two kinds.[^V09-617] One travels in comfort, served by others, with every desire met, carried in a covered seat, and protected from changes in the weather. This traveler reaches the destination like the people of Paradise. Another walks the road on foot, with little food and few supplies. Even after reaching home, the traveler feels tired and strained for a time, until the hardship passes and rest comes. This is like someone whose home is the Fire. They suffer there, then receive the mercy that reaches all things. Between these two is a third group: they have neither the comfort of the people of Paradise nor the lasting pain of those whose home is Hell. They live between ease and strain. These are the people who leave through the prayers of those who intercede or through the mercy of the Most Compassionate. They have different ranks, depending on how much pain and effort remains for them. It slowly fades in the Fire, and when their time there ends, they leave for Paradise, the home of rest.[^V09-618]

The last of them are people who did no good, except for their faith and their good manners. Yet God's care had already chosen them for Paradise. The people whose home is Hell remain there.[^V09-619] Its doors close and the Fire shuts around them. They give up hope of leaving. Then rest reaches them too, for they no longer fear being taken away, as they did when they saw the mercy of the Most Compassionate bringing people out. God made their nature suited to that home. They would suffer if they left it, as we have explained. When they lose hope, they are glad. This is the first comfort they find there. Once the period of pain has ended, their state is as we described earlier: they find the punishment sweet. It is still called “punishment” because, in the end, it is sweet to the person who experiences it, like scratching an itch. Understand, God willing, the pleasure each home gives its people.[^V09-620]

Do you not see that the Fire itself remains in pain because it lacks something and is not yet full? It stays that way until the Overpowering One places His foot in it. This is one of the two feet we mentioned on the Footstool. The other rests in Paradise. God says, “Give the believers good news that they have a firm place with their Lord.” The name “Lord” rules over them, and the name “Overpowering” rules over the others,[^V09-621] because Hell is a home of power, awe, and majesty, while Paradise is a gentle home of beauty and closeness. The “firm place” is one of the two feet of the Footstool. One is for the Fire and one for Paradise. God cares for both, because both lead to mercy. This does not mean that He does not care, as the ignorant imagine. If He did not care, He would not set punishments for wrongdoing, call Himself angry, or describe Himself as severe in power. These all show His concern for those affected.[^V09-622] It is said that Paradise is prepared for the mindful, and a painful punishment for the wretched.

Each foot rules over its own home and people. The two feet make people richer or poorer, cause them to live or die, and create pairs of male and female. Without them, there would be no worship of other gods.[^V09-623] Each has a home it rules and people it governs. A person who knows God keeps returning to Him and deals with each place as God wants.[^V09-624] The ignorant person does not.” Ibn Arabi ends there.

Qaysari summarizes: “The three main places in the next life that hold all people are Paradise, Hell, and the Heights between them. Each has a ruling name that draws its own people to it, because they belong to it and fill it.[^V09-625] God's promise reaches everyone. In truth, His promise means that He brings each person to the completion meant for them. Paradise is promised to people, but so is Hell. God says, ‘Every soul will come with a driver and a witness.’ As people are drawn to Paradise by the nature that suits it,[^V09-626] with the All-Merciful, the Most Compassionate, and the angels of mercy leading them, so people are drawn to Hell by the nature that suits it, with the Mighty, the Overpowering, Satan, and the angels of punishment leading them. Hell itself is promised to them; it was not promised as punishment.[^V09-627] The threat of punishment, which comes from the name the Avenger, also reaches everyone in one way,[^V09-628] and its effects appear in five groups. The people of Hell include those who worship other gods, unbelievers, hypocrites, and sinful believers. These last include people who know that God is one but do not act on what they know, and those kept from the truth.

“When the power of the Avenger rules over them, they suffer in the Fire, as God says, ‘Its walls will surround them.’ After many years and long ages, they grow used to the Fire and forget the joy of God's approval. They say, ‘It is the same whether we grieve or endure; there is no escape for us.’ Then mercy reaches them, and their pain is lifted. For the person who knows God, the punishment is painful in one way, but sweet in another: even while being punished, they see the Real, and that vision is their greatest joy.[^V09-629] For those who do not know the true joys, their state is also sweet in one way, because, as a report says, the people of Hell play with the Fire. Playing brings some pleasure, even while they are punished for not having the Garden they believed in—the Garden made of their deeds, with its beautiful companions and palaces.

“For people whose nature calls them away from God and toward the Fire, the punishment is painful, though they may actually want it. This is like some unbelievers here who cut their arms and throw themselves from high places. I once saw a man who had driven thick nails through the base of his fingers. He was proud of it, refused to have them removed, and kept them there until he died. For hypocrites, whose nature inclines both to completion and to its opposite, the punishment hurts because they see the completion they cannot reach. But since their pull toward the lesser state is stronger, they accept it. Their pain ends after the Avenger has punished them. Pain becomes pleasure, as when someone first rejects a lowly act, then repeats it until it feels familiar and even takes pride in it.

“Those who worship things other than God are punished because they confined the Real to the thing they worshipped and treated the limitless God as if He were limited. Yet, since the thing they worshipped was in truth the Real appearing in that form, they worshipped no one but God, and in this respect God accepts it. Their punishment therefore becomes pleasure for them.[^V09-630] Unbelievers suffer greatly, but their nature calls for this state, so they do not feel pain in it. A person may call it punishment when they know there are higher ranks and kinds of joy beyond their own. But punishment does not last forever as punishment: it ends through the help of those who intercede, and at last through the help of the Most Compassionate. This is why watercress will grow at the bottom of Hell, and why the report says, ‘My mercy comes before My anger.’”

#### Chapter 29 — What Hell is made of and what form it takes

We have already said that Hell belongs to the same kind of reality as this world. Its source and material are the soul's attachment to worldly things simply because they are worldly. Its form is made up of painful traits, absences, and faults. Absence and defect are negative states, so by themselves they do not act or cause pain. But when the soul experiences them as present, they have a kind of existence for it. In this way they become real evils for the person who has them.[^V09-631] Consider the loss of a connection. It is only the absence of a connection that should be there, yet the sense of touch can feel it as intense pain. It is an absence that is experienced and felt. If an absence can be present in this way, it becomes a real evil. The pain of touch is the experience of something that harms the one who feels it.

A person's direct awareness of a thing is the thing's actual existence as it appears to them. If what they are directly aware of is an absence, that absence, though still an absence, is present as something real. This is a subtle and difficult point.[^V09-632] It also answers a familiar objection: pain is an evil, yet it is an act of awareness, and awareness is something real. This seems to conflict with the philosophers' claim that real evil has no independent being in the world; it is the absence of a thing or of a perfection that belongs to it.

It is not enough to answer that pain is evil only as a side effect. Our direct experience tells us that pain itself is evil for the person who feels it, even if we set aside the lost limb, broken connection, or other defect that caused it. Keep this in mind. The form Hell takes in the next life is the soul's experience of pains that come from absences and defects.[^V09-633] The wretched soul continues to suffer for as long as it remains in a state where it can recognize those defects and feel the lack of what should be there. The pain ends if either the soul changes into a lower nature, or the defect disappears because the soul's state improves and it turns its attention to higher things it once knew but had been kept from by the distractions of the senses. In either case, the punishment ends.

In short, Hell is this world as a worldly state, impressed on the soul as its own condition on the Day of Resurrection. It contains all that is in the heavens and earth in so far as they are flawed and harmful, but not in so far as they are complete and good. In their goodness and completion, they belong to Paradise. While the soul lives here, it sees the things of the world through bodily senses. What those senses show is mixed together: right and wrong, sound and damaged, are hard to tell apart. We see the sun, moon, stars, sky, and earth in a confused way. We assume they last forever[^V09-634] and that their light belongs to them by nature, rather than coming from their true sources. We assume the sky and earth really are as our senses show them: fixed, enduring, high or low, and set in a certain order.

When the Day of Resurrection comes, these things change. What belongs to them is separated from what does not. What is true is separated from what is false, their borrowed light from their original darkness, and what is good from what is corrupt. God says, “God will not leave the believers as you are until He separates the corrupt from the good.” Hell is the original truth of this world, separated from the good and complete things that do not belong to it. When the Resurrection begins and each group settles in its home, each form returns to its own reality. The people of Paradise are governed by what God gives them in the next life. The people of Hell are governed by what God placed in the matter of this world: in the movements of the heavens and the fixed stars, and in the seven planets whose lights are blotted out. They are still stars, but their light is gone in the Resurrection. The sun is still a sun, but its light is darkened, because its light comes from its original sources and truly belongs to them, not to these physical bodies.

Ibn Arabi says in chapter 60 of *The Meccan Revelations*: “The Fire is close in nature to this world.[^V09-635] It is not pure punishment or pure pleasure. That is why God says, ‘They neither die in it nor live.’ The reason is that the divine command placed in the heavens and the movements of the stars remains there, but its effect changes as the forms of the heavens change and the stars lose their light and spread out. Their effects therefore increase or decrease and change in other ways.”

He also says: “Know—may God keep us and you safe—that Hell is one of the greatest created things. It is God's prison in the next life. It is called Hell because it is deep. A deep well is called a *jahnam*. Hell contains burning heat and bitter cold. It has the coldest cold and the greatest heat. From its top to its bottom is a distance of one thousand seven hundred and fifty years. People disagree about whether it was created already or will be created later. They disagree about Paradise too. But for us, the people of spiritual insight and knowledge, both are created and not created. To say that they are created is like saying that someone who builds a house has finished its outer walls. People call it a house, but if they go inside, they see only walls around an open space.”

The builder then makes the rooms, halls, shelters, paths, storerooms, and everything the future residents will need. Hell is a home of burning air. Its fire has no embers; its fuel is human beings, stones worshipped as gods, and jinn. God says, “Its fuel is people and stones,” and, “You and what you worship besides God are fuel for Hell.” He also says, “They will be thrown into it—the misguided, their idols, and the armies of Satan.” The tools in Hell come into being through the deeds of the people and jinn who enter it.

God made Hell through His quality of anger. The pain and suffering felt by those who enter it also come from that anger. But they exist only when people and jinn enter it. If no one were there, Hell itself would feel no pain, and neither would its angels. Hell and its keepers would be immersed in God's mercy, taking pleasure in praising Him without tiring. God says, “Do not exceed the limits I set, or My anger will fall on you,” and, “Anyone on whom My anger falls has fallen into ruin.” Here, anger itself is pain. Someone who claims to follow our path but does not understand this may say that Hell was created from God's power to compel and that the name “Overpowering” appears there. If that were so, Hell would be busy with itself instead of having power over the tyrants. It could not say, “Is there any more?” or “Some of me has consumed the rest.” God comes to it with the mercy that embraces all things and gives it room to claim more and to rule over the tyrants and deniers. People are mistaken about how Hell was made.[^V09-636]

One of the strangest reports about this comes from the Prophet. He was sitting with his companions in the mosque when they heard a great crash and became frightened. He asked, “Do you know what that sound was?” They said, “God and His Messenger know best.” He said, “A stone was thrown from the top of Hell seventy years ago. It has just reached the bottom.” Just as he finished speaking, a hypocrite who was seventy years old died in his house. The Prophet said, “God is greatest.” The companions understood that the stone was that hypocrite. From the day God created him, he had been falling into Hell. When he died, he reached its bottom. God says, “The hypocrites will be in the lowest level of the Fire.” God let the companions hear that sound so they would learn. Consider how remarkable the Prophet's words are and how gently he taught them. Ibn Arabi ends there.

#### A spiritual reminder

You have learned that Paradise is above the seventh heaven in rank, but in its true nature it is within the veils of the heavens and earth, because it belongs to the unseen world, which is the inner side of the visible world. You have also learned that the next life grows out of this world. Know, then, that this world is like a kitchen where food for the people of Paradise is prepared. The warmth of the heavens' movements and the rays of the stars ripen it.[^V09-637] Human deeds are the material from which the food of their souls and bodies in the next life is made.[^V09-638] The more balanced and complete their deeds are, through worldly discipline and bodily effort for God's sake, the better prepared their food, fruit, and drink will be in the next life. It will suit them better and give lasting life greater strength.

A mystic says:[^V09-639] “The heat of the heavens and the rays of the sun and stars are like embers under a cooking pot. They affect the things that grow here and ripen the fruit and minerals, bringing them to maturity. This heat brings benefit, even though it is fire. Whoever understands the next life, the place of Paradise and Hell,[^V09-640] and how the fruit of Paradise ripens to give pleasure to its people, will know where the Fire and Paradise are. The fire beneath the bowl-shaped earth of Paradise ripens its fruit. It warms the underside of Paradise's earth, bringing its food into the right state, just as fire under a cooking pot ripens food. The earth of Paradise is musk,[^V09-641] according to a report, and musk is warm by nature. The trees of Paradise grow in this musk, just as earthly plants grow in manure, which is warm and makes things rot. This is enough to show the benefit of fire. May God protect us from it in both homes.” The mystic ends there.

Although this argument uses examples and comparisons, careful thought shows that it can be proved. Do not take it to mean that Hell is only this physical world beneath the outermost heaven. We have already explained that Hell belongs to the next life, even though its form comes from what this world becomes and its inner reality is hidden here.

The mystic's point is that the ripening of Paradise's fruit and the cooking of its food depend on the heat of this world and the rays of the stars.[^V09-642] A human being comes into being, grows, and reaches full development through changes in its matter. These changes require the body's natural heat, which comes from the movements of the heavens and their light, as we have explained elsewhere.

The same is true of a person's growth in understanding and action. It takes place through bodily movement and thought. Movement requires heat; heat and movement go together and cannot be separated. All movement in this world ultimately comes from the motions of the heavens, especially the outermost heaven. In the same way, all natural heat in bodies comes from the light of the stars, especially the sun. Careful observation shows this.

When matter takes on a higher form, it first has to weaken or break down from its lower form. A seed buried in the ground must lose its hard, lifeless state and begin to decay under the heat before it can take on the form of a plant. The same is true of the changes that happen in a seed and a fetus as it develops through plant and animal life.

The soul's growth also comes after inner breaking and digestion. Bodily movement brings this about in physical acts of worship, and thought brings it about in acts of understanding. All of this depends on the movements of the heavens and the light of the stars. So the knowledge and good deeds that complete the soul—and the life, joy, food, and drink they give it in Paradise—come through the natural heat of this world. That heat belongs to the same source as the fire of Hell, as we have explained more than once.

#### Chapter 30 — Where the pain and punishment of Hell are felt

You have learned that Hell is a mixed state, part of this world and part of the next life. Its matter is the absence and evil found in this world. Its form is the soul's experience of those absences and evils as painful images, because it is attached to and absorbed in this world. So where does punishment take place? It is not simple, like saying it happens only in the body, only in the rational soul, or only in the animal soul.

The limbs and organs of the body are not where pain is felt. They receive the different punishments and pains as forms of completion that they enjoy, because they can be divided and joined, heated and cooled, and changed by other opposites and absences. Every state that comes to a body[^V09-643] is a kind of completion for it.[^V09-644][^V09-645] When a body is divided, its old form ends and two new connected forms appear. The earlier form does not remain alongside the new ones, so it has nothing that can suffer harm.[^V09-646]

This is different for the soul. It can perceive one opposite state together with another that once suited it. If a bodily organ changes from the balanced warmth that gives it strength to a cold or heat that opposes it, the soul perceives both the former state and the new one. The new state is painful because it conflicts with the earlier state that suited the soul. The body and its form do not experience this in the same way. Think carefully about the difference.

Not everyone who is in a place of punishment is being punished.[^V09-647] Some may enjoy what comes to them, such as the keepers of Hell, its guards, and those who work in its furnaces and prisons. The body's limbs and powers also serve as instruments of punishment for the soul that once ruled them and used them as it pleased. They cause pain to that soul when it sees that its own realm and place of control are no longer doing what it wants. Instead, the things sensed through its powers and senses come to it in forms it dislikes.

The rational soul—the spirit from God, the home of wisdom and knowledge—is happy in this life and the next. It has no share in misery, since it does not belong to the realm of evil. But God joined it to the animal soul, which is like a mount. The rational soul's task is to guide it along the straight path. If the animal soul obeys, it becomes a calm, trained mount. If it refuses, it is a wild animal that turns right and left whenever the rider tries to guide it. The rider can train and discipline it until its nature changes and it does what the rider wants.

The animal soul is the source of disobedience, but it has no guilt of its own.[^V09-648] Its acts—desire, anger, and the rest—are natural powers and ways of praising and honoring God. It does not deliberately act against God or break the religious law. It simply follows its nature, which happens not to suit its rider.

You must think carefully about this point to find where pain is felt. The place where pain is felt is a mixture of two things: the human soul joined to the animal soul, before it has become a fully active intellect. It is like a boundary that joins and lies between the two. It is the place where punishment takes form, because it is animal, and the place where pain is felt, because it is rational.

When punishment comes on the Day of Resurrection, it falls on the animal soul because it refused to follow the rational soul along the path of truth. It is like beating an animal that has left the road it should follow. Consider the penalties set for unlawful sex, rape between men, theft, and other acts. These penalties fall on the animal soul, which feels the pain of death, losing a hand, or being struck. The body carries out the penalty, but the pain is felt by the soul that senses and imagines.

The rational soul—the intellectual being that knows pure ideas—never leaves its high place.[^V09-649] It remains in its honor and lasting happiness, because it comes from God's spirit. Most people do not have this fully developed rational soul. But every person has an animal soul. In the blessed, it is sound, obedient, and tame; in the wretched, it is disobedient and wild. The obedient soul will roam freely in the gardens and pastures of Paradise. The disobedient soul will be punished until it submits.

As for the limbs and organs, they have only lasting joy in Hell, just like its keepers. They praise and honor God and always obey whatever happens to them or is done through them, just as they do in this life. A person may imagine that an organ feels pain because they themselves feel pain through it. But that is not so. The person who feels pain receives through the sense organ an image of what they dislike. Consider a sick person who is asleep. Their sense of touch still works, and the wound that hurts when they are awake remains in the body. Yet while asleep they feel no pain. The one who feels pain has turned away from the visible world and entered the world between this one and the next. They no longer know what is happening there. When the sick person wakes and returns to the visible world and the realm of the senses, the aches and pain begin again.

If the person remains in the intermediate world, they may see a frightening dream or painful image and suffer, or they may see a good dream and pleasant image and feel joy. The pleasure or pain follows wherever they go. The same is true of their state in the next life. Pay attention to this point and see clearly what I have explained.

#### Chapter 31 — The gatekeepers of Hell and their number

God says, “Over it are nineteen,” and, “We have made the keepers of the Fire angels. We have made their number only a trial for those who disbelieve.” Know that God has angels who act in bodies by His command, just as He has angels whose knowledge is turned only toward Him, with no concern for bodies. They are the ones referred to in the verses “those who race ahead” and “those who direct affairs.”[^V09-650] These subordinate beings direct affairs in the intermediate realms of darkness and in the forms of the natural world. The natural world appears outwardly as this world, with its many levels, but inwardly it is Hell, with its levels. The word “then” in the verse points to an order of existence: each of these beings is under a holy, separate reality that existed before the souls that govern and the natural things that move. They are like the spirits of the great heavenly world and of the small human world, which contains a pattern of the great one. In the upper, greater world, they are the spirits of the moving planets and the fixed stars, grouped into the twelve signs of the zodiac. Together they make nineteen governors in broad outline, though their details are beyond counting.[^V09-651][^V09-652] Some are governed by others, in keeping with the order of wisdom.

In the small human world, there are likewise nineteen powers that stand at the head of the other powers and natures. They directly govern and act in the lower realms of animal and plant life. Twelve of them are the sources of animal action and perception. Ten serve the five outer senses and five inner senses. Two guide movement: desire draws us toward what suits us, and anger drives away what does not. The other seven are the sources of plant life. Three are the basic powers of nutrition, growth, and reproduction. Four serve nutrition through attraction, retention, digestion, and expulsion. All nineteen help kindle the Fire of Hell, which rises from the heat of nature's Hell and its desires. These fires are hidden from people's sight today. On the Day of Resurrection, they will appear and burn through existence, melt bodies, and tear away the limbs. They will call those who turned away from the truth.

As long as a person is held in this world by inner and outer restraints, whose hidden side is full of Hellfire, they are imprisoned by nature and held captive by the nineteen heavenly governors and the nineteen lower powers. They cannot rise to the lasting life or enter the world of Paradise, the home of spirit and joy. They remain tormented by Hell, scorched by its burning heat, and bound in chains like captives and slaves. God describes their state: “Take him and bind him. Then make him enter the Fire. Then put him in a chain seventy cubits long.” This is because the soul has not escaped the body's prison or completed itself through knowledge and action. It has not become free like the beings that no longer depend on bodies, free from the captivity of desire and attachment. This explains God's words: “He did not believe in God, the Mighty, and did not urge the feeding of the poor.” The first points to neglect of knowledge of God; the second, to neglect of good action.

When the spirit leaves the body at death and passes from this world to the next, which lies within the veils of the heavens and earth as we have said, it moves from prison to the place of confinement, and from desire to the abyss. Here too it was imprisoned and surrounded by Hell, but it did not feel the pain of that prison. While here, it had no close friend and no food except the foul washing of wounds, which only sinners eat. When the veil is lifted, it feels this pain. The torment that was hidden within it in this life becomes outward in the next. The keeper of Hell then hands it over to the nineteen gatekeepers, who arise from the nineteen universal governors. It suffers at their hands, just as it suffered through those powers in this life without knowing it.

But whoever is guided by their Lord and stands firmly on the straight path—the path of God, the Mighty and Praiseworthy—will travel toward the next life with the light of guidance and the steady steps of knowledge and piety. They will reach the home of peace, escape these ruinous punishments, and be freed from the world's slavery and from desires that command wrongdoing. God gives the example of “a man owned by several partners who quarrel with one another, and a man devoted to one man alone. Are the two alike?” Praise belongs to God, but most people do not understand.

#### Chapter 32 — The stages of the journey to God and the way to the true Paradise

The stages that lead to the true Paradise have corresponding depths of distance from God and descent into the real Fire.[^V09-653] One of the people who reached certainty says that a person is first given a particular existence, then a particular knowledge, then a will, then limited power, and then action. Since the return is a return to our original nature, a return to the beginning at the end, the seeker must gradually leave these qualities behind, in the reverse order from the one in which they first appeared.

A seeker who travels toward God by faith and the light of knowledge must first stop claiming action as their own. This is the stage of piety and giving up attachment to the world. God says, “You have no say in the matter,” and, “I do not know what will be done with me or with you; judgment belongs only to God.” The seeker must then give up their own choice. They have no will except in what God and His Messenger permit; their will is absorbed in the will of God and His Messenger. “A believing man or woman has no choice when God and His Messenger have decided a matter.” Everything comes from God in keeping with His decree and measure, in the most complete form and the best order.[^V09-654]

When the seeker reaches this stage and becomes steady in it, they accept God's decree and attain contentment. They are free of worry, grief, and pain, because they see that all things are good, beautiful, and complete. They see that God's mercy embraces everything. The one who knows God sees the lasting face of the Real in everything, and sees all else as passing away, for there is no truly separate reality beside Him. They see absolute goodness and beauty appearing in every thing, and so they rejoice, take delight, and are content.

A person who sees the whole world clearly in the mind sees it arranged in the best way through knowledge of God, His qualities, and His acts. In other words, they know things through their causes and origins, which come from God. This is the way of the truthful: they look at things in the light of the Real and see Him as the witness in everything. God says, “Is it not enough that your Lord is witness over all things?” Before this clear vision, people see things as others do, by the senses or by thought clouded with darkness, which leads to mistakes. For the seeker, the earth becomes another earth, and the earth shines with the light of its Lord. So do the heavens and all that lies between them. The seeker is in a bright, intellectual Paradise as wide as the heavens and earth. This is why the keeper of Paradise is named Riḍwān, “Contentment”: the seeker does not enter Paradise or reach the home of honor and nearness until they reach contentment. A sacred report says, “Whoever is not content with My decree and patient with My trial should worship a lord other than Me and leave My earth and My heaven.” God also says, “God's good pleasure is greater.”

After this stage, the seeker must stop claiming power as their own. They no longer see themselves as having strength or ability apart from God's power, from which no possible thing is excluded. “There is no power or strength except through God, the Most High, the Almighty.” This is the stage of trust in God: “Whoever puts their trust in God, He is enough for them. God will bring His purpose to pass.” It is also the stage of surrender: “I hand my matter over to God.”

After that, the seeker must stop claiming knowledge as their own. Their knowledge fades into God's knowledge, which takes in everything, down to the smallest thing in heaven or earth. God's knowledge of things is part of His very being. The one who knows God looks at things through the light of their Lord, through His knowledge of Himself and of all else. They have no separate knowledge of their own; they look only at the Real, His qualities, and the effects of those qualities. Their knowledge is absorbed in God's knowledge. This is the stage of submission, as in God's words: “Submit fully,” “Peace be upon you. You have become pure, so enter and remain forever,” and, “Peace to you, people of the right.”

Finally, the seeker must stop claiming existence as their own. Their existence, which they once thought belonged to them, fades into the existence of the Real, through whom everything exists and is sustained. They no longer see themselves as existing on their own. This is the station of unity, the highest station and greatest gift: the station of being lost in God's oneness. “These are the ones God has blessed: the prophets, the truthful, the martyrs, and the righteous.” This is the highest stage for those who travel toward God through faith and the light of knowledge. The journey has no further station beyond this.

But those who do not follow the path of those who see God's oneness, whose actions and states follow their own wishes and nature, are as God describes: “If the Real had followed their desires, the heavens, earth, and all who live in them would have fallen into ruin.” They are kept from what their desires call for, barred from what they want, and left under God's anger and the Fire of His wrath. One desire brings them down into the Abyss. They are denied what they love and desire, bound in chains like slaves, and handed over by the keeper of their souls to destruction. This is why the keeper of Hell is called Mālik.

For every stage reached by the people of the Real, they have a corresponding depth in Hell. The depth corresponding to trust in God is abandonment: “If God abandons you, who can help you after Him?” The depth corresponding to submission is humiliation: “Whoever God humiliates will find no one to honor them.” The depth corresponding to nearness and union is being driven away and cursed: “Those whom God curses, and all who curse, will curse them.” The first group gave up the claim that its power, knowledge, and existence belonged to it alone. It gained in return unlimited power, knowledge that comes from God, and everlasting existence. The second group insists that these qualities belong to it in its own right. It therefore falls into boundless helplessness, complete ignorance, and everlasting ruin. That is the great disgrace.

#### Chapter 33 — The Tree of Tuba and the Tree of Zaqqum

God says, “Tuba is theirs, and a beautiful homecoming,” and, “The Tree of Zaqqum is food for the sinner.” It is a tree that grows from the root of Hell—that is, from worldly nature. Its fruit is like the heads of devils. The fruit is where the tree begins to bear and where its fruits come out of their coverings. Here, it stands for the beginnings of false beliefs and bad qualities: the main devilish traits. When each of these traits takes root and grows strong in the soul, it becomes an essential form of a devil, as we have seen when a person's dominant traits become lasting forms within the soul. These traits that give rise to devils are misleading desires, destructive hopes, and blameworthy appetites. They feed and strengthen the souls of those who have lost their way, fill their inner selves, and give rise to Hellfire and painful torment. God says:

“Then you, who have gone astray and denied the truth, will eat from the Tree of Zaqqum and fill your bellies with it.” That is, they fill their souls with blameworthy desires and the sources of inner sickness. These bring many kinds of torment, hardship, and pain in the next life, just as excessive eating can lead to fever, headache, and other aches and pains.

Know that when the human soul reaches its full development in knowledge and action, it becomes like a good tree bearing the fruits of true knowledge and certain insight. Its roots are firm knowledge; its branches are the results that reveal the truths of the unseen and the divine world. This is the soul's growth in its power to know and understand. As for its power to act and bring things about, it reaches a point where whatever it wants and wishes appears before it at once. Its power to bring about the images it wants becomes strong. For the inner self in this world becomes the outward self in the next.

Here, a person imagines and creates whatever they want by means of the imagination. Many images of desired things arise within the mind. But these images are not present to the senses; they exist only in the imagination, with a weak and uncertain kind of existence.[^V09-655] So they do not bring the same pleasure as things present to the senses. The soul is also drawn away by the distractions of the senses. At the Resurrection, however, the soul is free of worldly distractions and the effects of the senses. Its inner life is open and clear; what it knows is directly present, and what was hidden becomes visible. Its pleasure is therefore as strong as the degree to which things are present and real. It is intense because the soul's images now have the standing of things seen directly. The difference between this and worldly imagination is that, in the next life, the soul has the full power to bring the images it wants before its senses.

It lacks this power here because its abilities are divided and its attention is occupied by external things and sensory distractions. The happy person in the next life therefore finds whatever they desire at once. The desire gives rise to an image, and the image appears before them, just as God says, “There they will have whatever their souls desire and whatever delights their eyes. They will remain there forever.” This power is broader and more complete than the power to make something exist outside the senses in this world. In this world, one thing cannot be in two places, and two things cannot occupy the same place; things crowd and get in one another's way. The soul's images in the next life multiply without conflict or crowding. It can bring infinitely many of them into being at once; arguments against infinitely many existing things do not apply there, because there is no sequence among them.[^V09-656]

Nor does one thing seen by the senses distract the soul from another there, because the soul's power is so great. Its pleasures are countless. Since the images that bring pleasure are free of matter, their pleasure is intense and complete, unclouded by defects or opposites.

Once this is clear, we can say that the Tree of Tuba represents the generous, happy soul. A report from the family of the Prophet says that Tuba is a tree whose root is in the house of Ali ibn Abi Talib, and that every believer has a branch of it in their own home. This is the meaning of God's words, “Tuba is theirs, and a beautiful homecoming.” In terms of knowledge, this means that divine knowledge—especially knowledge of the next life that the mind cannot reach by ordinary reasoning alone—comes from the light of the final Prophet and the spiritual authority woven into his message. Its light spreads through the spiritual authority of his greatest successor, reaching those who are ready for guidance, faith, knowledge, and insight. The fruits of divine knowledge and the lights of true understanding have grown and spread in the hearts of this blessed community's knowers through the seed of Ali's spiritual authority and the growth of his guidance. The Prophet said, “I am the city of knowledge, and Ali is its gate.” The Prophet's spiritual parenthood of this community stands in relation to that of the other saints and scholars as Adam's bodily parenthood stands in relation to the rest of humanity. The Prophet also said to Ali, “You and I are the parents of this community.”[^V09-657]

The wise mystic Ibn Arabi says in *The Bezels of Wisdom* that the Tree of Tuba is the source of all the trees of Paradise, just as Adam was the father of all his children. God planted this tree with His own hand, shaped it, and breathed His spirit into it. When God Himself planted the Tree of Tuba and breathed His spirit into it, He adorned it with the ornaments and garments that hang upon it. We are its soil, just as the things on earth are its adornment. The tree gives each fruit in Paradise the very nature that it has. A seed gives the palm tree and all the seeds in its fruit their nature. These are his words.

From this we can understand that the Tree of Tuba means the source of true knowledge and good qualities. They adorn, nourish, and clothe souls that are ready to receive them, just as the things on earth adorn and nourish the people who live there. Since our souls are the soil of this tree and God is the one who plants it, its ornaments must be knowledge, insight, and fine qualities. These are also the soul's food and clothing, for knowledge feeds the soul and piety clothes it.

#### Chapter 34 — How the states and effects of the people of Paradise and Hell continually renew

The people of Hell certainly undergo changes: their skins are replaced,[^V09-658] and their bodies change from one form to another. God says, “Whenever their skins are burned away, We replace them with other skins, so they may taste the punishment.” Their nature is made up of bodily, material powers, because, as we have said, Hell is of the same kind as this world. The actions and reactions of material powers are limited, so they must eventually stop or change.

The changing of bodies and matter[^V09-659] also depends on a circular motion[^V09-660] set in motion by the heavenly bodies around the earthly bodies whose directions differ and which come into being and pass away. What happens to the people of Hell follows God's command and the moving power He placed in the outermost sphere, which compels it to move. The fixed stars move along with the seven planets, whose lights are put out in the next life. They are still stars, but no longer shine or pierce the darkness. Their influence shapes the people of Hell in many forms of suffering and punishment, according to their past deeds and the beginnings of their actions, beliefs, and intentions. This is why people of spiritual knowledge and vision say that the condition of Hell and its people is close to that of this world and its people.[^V09-661]

For this reason, after the people who truly belong in Hell have suffered for their appointed time and their punishment has run its course, they have neither pure pleasure nor pure pain. Their state lies between life and death, as God says, “They neither die in it nor live.” The same is true of people in this world: worldly pleasure is mixed with hardship, and life is touched by death. This is because the effects of the heavenly motions remain within them, and they are not given the strength to escape the rule and influence of nature. So they do not escape the Fire's torment, even as their forms and their way of being change in keeping with changes in the forms of the spheres and stars—their darkening and loss of light. God says, “As for those who are wretched, they will be in the Fire, where they will sigh and groan. They will remain there as long as the heavens and earth endure, except as your Lord wills.”

The people of Paradise, by contrast, do not undergo this kind of change, transformation, coming-to-be, or passing away.[^V09-662] Their way of being rises above nature and its laws. Their movements and actions are of another kind: they involve no strain or fatigue. Their actions are not bodily. They are like a passing thought or an inward glance here, which causes no weariness, exhaustion, or loss of strength.

For them, the heavens and their motions are folded away. They are among the people of the right, and time and space are folded for them. Their time brings together the past and future of our time. Their place holds everything that the heavens and earth can contain. Yet the Paradise of deeds and its pleasures are certainly sensed and perceived. Still, the things sensed there are not physical or material. Their forms are forms of perception, and their real existence is their being perceived. Everything there exists in the soul, free from this world's nature and from matter that changes and passes away.

Even so, forms continue to arise and renew in Paradise. They do not come from material causes, but from powers in the soul and acts of God, in keeping with His words, “Every day He is at work.” It has been established that the changes in the visible world begin in the world of souls. Paradise is the world of souls, so it too contains forms that come into being through the soul.

The wise mystic Ibn Arabi says in Chapter 47 of *The Meccan Revelations*:

“The next life is always creating. In Paradise, people say ‘Be’ to what they want, and it appears before them. They do not wish for anything, or even think of bringing anything into being, without it appearing before them. The same is true of the people of Hell: they cannot have a thought that frightens them with a punishment worse than the one they already suffer without that very punishment coming upon them. The thought itself brings it about. The next life makes things take perceptible form as soon as a thought, wish, intention, or desire arises. All of this can be perceived there. This does not happen in this world: here, not everyone can act through intention alone.” These are his words.

Anyone who knows God's power to create through imagination—and knows what the soul can experience, or rather bring into being by God's permission, through images of enormous bodies and great spheres, still and moving, of many lands and their inhabitants, their states and qualities, all in the blink of an eye—will find this account easy to believe.

## Notes

[^V09-1]: A soul may be complete, intermediate, or deficient in knowledge. In each of those conditions it may also be complete, intermediate, or deficient in conduct. There are therefore nine possible combinations.

    The passage speaks somewhat loosely because it does not name every combination. One omitted case is a soul with intermediate knowledge but deficient conduct; another is a soul complete in knowledge but deficient in conduct. Perhaps their treatment can be inferred. The first is even less likely to join the intellects than a soul intermediate in both respects. If it commits grave wrongs, its condition resembles that of a soul deficient in knowledge and guilty of wrongdoing; otherwise it resembles the intermediate case. The soul complete in knowledge but deficient in conduct nevertheless joins the intellects because it has already become an intellect in actuality.

    There is a more serious difficulty with the wording. The passage begins by speaking only of souls deficient in knowledge. Souls with intermediate knowledge are therefore outside the group first described. How, then, can the author introduce the sound, intermediate soul as one of the cases within that group?

[^V09-2]: The commentator suggests these philosophers may have wanted to explain the promised punishment and everlasting misery by placing it in this world, as if this world itself were the Hell of the miserable.

[^V09-3]: Force does not act permanently or in most cases.

[^V09-4]: The manuscripts the commentator saw give the flood's date as the “middle” of the cycles. He says another source instead gives a particular year count and warns of the flood around the halfway point.

[^V09-5]: A note on the word: it means “near” or “adjacent.”

[^V09-6]: A commentator adds that the author leaves out two other possibilities: that the soul simply perishes, or that it enters the divine realm rather than only the intellectual realm. He treats the first as too plainly false to mention and includes the second under “the world of intellect.”

[^V09-7]: The commentator points out that this proof depends on the soul beginning to exist. A supporter of transmigration who believes souls are eternal could reject that premise.

[^V09-8]: Given that premise, the recipient is ready only for a new outpouring of soul from the chain of intellectual causes, whose nearest source is the Active Intellect.

[^V09-9]: The earlier objection to the movement of a soul into another body was that the arrival of an old soul would prevent a newly created soul from entering. It did not deny that the receiving body was ready for a new soul in the first place. The present objection instead questions that readiness itself.

    One version says that if a human body could receive a newly created soul, the human soul would not possess its three successive levels: plant life, animal life, and properly human life. A soul arriving from another body is supposed to account for those levels. Another version argues that, if plant matter is ready to receive a new soul, human matter must instead receive only a migrating one. Yet, on the account of downward migration, even a plant might receive a migrating soul. The example of the human body therefore cannot establish a general rule for plants.

    There is a further problem. If plant matter deserves a newly given plant soul, the same must be true of human matter as it passes through the stages of development. Otherwise a human body would have two plant souls: the new one due to its development and the one carried over from elsewhere. It cannot skip the plant stage altogether. Once it has passed through the levels of nonliving matter, its preparation for plant life is no different in principle from that of other plant matter. To allow a new soul in one case and deny it in the other would be to make a distinction without a reason. This objection is therefore not difficult to answer, even though al-Ghazali accepts it in his discussion of resurrection.

[^V09-10]: The commentator suspects that the word “that” was added by a copyist. If it is removed, the line more clearly says that gradual development must produce the new soul.

[^V09-11]: The author measures nobility by nearness to God. This supports the claim that human matter, being more balanced than plant matter, is ready for a new human soul. If the opponents instead define nobility as having passed through the earlier levels, that is an unsupported claim; in any case, development through those levels can be continuous.

[^V09-12]: This refers to the common philosophical view that celestial souls do not come from animal or human souls. One commentary reports another view in which perfected earthly souls rise to the heavens and celestial souls later enter the intellectual realm.

[^V09-13]: The commentator suggests another problem for the downward view: if every animal came from a human soul, there must have been long periods without animals or plants. That conflicts with the philosophers' account of the natural kinds and would make human life impossible. The objection is strongest if the world began at a particular time; a view that the world has always existed can instead claim an endless cycle of humans and animals.

[^V09-14]: The numbers argument applies to upward transmigration, because plants greatly outnumber animals and animals greatly outnumber humans. It does not apply in the same way to downward transmigration.

[^V09-15]: In a general kind, the increase of individual beings also means that they occur at more times.

[^V09-16]: The commentator says a cross-reference in his copy points to a page where the same numbered note was mistakenly placed.

[^V09-17]: Essential development cannot be stopped by force while the subject remains in existence, because the subject itself is what is developing. A force may interrupt an accidental change, but not the soul's deep movement in its very being.

[^V09-18]: When the soul first descends from the world of meanings, where there are no images, it begins to take form. What was utterly subtle becomes gradually denser. Coming from the world of divine command, it first appears in delicate images that are pure forms without matter. It then becomes darker and bodily through forms joined to matter. There can be no direct leap from such light to such darkness; an intermediate level must connect them. The same rule holds when the soul rises again. Nature does not jump over its stages.

    A distant comparison may help. Under intense cold, clear air containing no smoke or vapour may become a fine, pale cloud, as explained in discussions of changes of state. A thicker cloud, by contrast, may form from vapour and smoke, while the finer cloud and the light-bearing air pass through it. In the same way, the soul first takes an image as its form, and that image becomes denser until a natural body appears. The illustration is only a comparison, not a claim that the soul is literally made of air or clouds.

[^V09-19]: It is easy to understand why someone absorbed in God pays no attention to the body's outward shadow. But the soul also has a kind of shadow: the form or image through which it appears. Those who truly know God turn away even from this.

    They do not seek the world of images in the life to come and its imagined pleasures, any more than they seek the fading material pleasures of this natural world. Their attention is absorbed in the world of meaning, and beyond it in the Creator of meaning and form, the Lord of the world of command and the world of creation.

    Both worlds of images may follow them like inseparable shadows. But why would they look at those shadows? For them, the worlds are like mirrors. They look through the mirrors, not at the mirrors or at their pleasing images. What they long to see is the meaning behind all meanings, the Light beyond all lights. A report about the Imams says, “Your mention is among those who remember; your names among the names, and your souls among the souls.”

[^V09-20]: This follows from the author's view that matter and form are united in the thing they make. Matter considered simply as potential is one, because there are no distinctions within pure potentiality, just as there are no distinctions within pure actuality considered in itself.

    But matter becomes many when forms give it different kinds of existence. The matter of a body is not the same as matter considered at its most basic level. Matter made into fire is not the same as matter made into water, and the matter of a compound likewise differs according to the form that makes it that kind of compound. This is why matter needs bodily form in order to exist as a body, and a particular natural form in order to belong to one kind rather than another.

[^V09-21]: This point is even clearer on the principle that the knower and what is known become one in the act of knowing.

[^V09-22]: The verse contrasts people who differ only in outward color and shape with people who differ inwardly. The commentator reads it as saying that heedless people inwardly resemble animals, while the learned are unlike them.

[^V09-23]: “First” refers back to the first kind of life described here.

[^V09-24]: In the next life, forms are free of matter and cannot change in the way natural forms do. If they could mix and produce new bodies, that would be a continuation of earthly life, not the afterlife. Movement there is only movement of form.

[^V09-25]: The commentator rejects the argument that an animal's memory of a painful event proves it has a universal idea. A horse might flee from an image in its imagination that resembles a painful image it retained. An animal can repeat the same failed escape many times without reasoning out how to solve the problem; a human being can reason from particular experiences to a general solution.

[^V09-26]: The commentator adds that some spiders spin intricate webs of nested circles linked by straight spokes, resembling diagrams of the celestial spheres. This can leave the observer amazed.

[^V09-27]: Even the bodies of plants and animals are raised to a higher use, as we see when they become food for human beings. The mention of their identities points to something further: insofar as their life is taken up into human life, their souls too share in its rise toward the world of intellect.

    The commentator pictures the human being as a delicate nursing infant. Nature is its mother, place its cradle, time its nurse, and plants and animals the mother's two breasts. A child so delicate cannot digest raw and heavy food. Plants draw nourishment from water mixed with soil, and animals feed on plants and other things; their more solid constitutions can bear what a human being cannot take in directly in the same way.

    Care for human life therefore provides plants and animals as two means of nourishment. Within them, the elements are prepared, ripened, and refined until they can sustain a human body. Something similar happens when a nursing mother takes in food too coarse for her infant. The powers of her body transform it into milk suitable for the child. In this sense, plants and animals are called nourishment for humanity.

[^V09-28]: Some people flee from intellectual realities as someone with a cold flees from the scent of musk. Without knowledge, they cannot long for the world of meaning or reach it. Knowledge is the seed of direct vision.

[^V09-29]: This explains the words attributed here to the prophet Hud. No natural kind is left without guidance: an encompassing reality takes it by the forelock and draws it toward its goal through the very movement of its existence. Ahead of each kind stand the higher beings that govern it, discussed earlier, and above them the divine names of which they are expressions.

    In the language of those who know these realities, animals fall under the names All-Hearing and All-Seeing; the jinn under the names Subtle and All-Aware; the angels under the names Most Holy and All-Pure, and under the names All-Powerful and Compelling. Harmful creatures reflect the names that humble and bring harm; fire, especially the fire of Hell, reflects the names of vengeance and irresistible power. Other kinds are understood in the same way.

    The complete human being, however, is the servant of God under the name *Allah*, the greatest divine name. Such a person is the fullest and noblest display of that name, which stands above the names governing all the other kinds. The Lord of Muhammad, Hud, and the other righteous people is thus the end of the straight path that belongs to humanity, above all to its finest members. “This Qur'an guides to what is most upright.”

[^V09-30]: If the objection is that a soul grows more attached to matter and images as it becomes more miserable, this answer is not enough by itself. The commentator adds that the soul is drawn to images and forms, not to matter merely as matter; such images also exist in the imaginal realm.

[^V09-31]: The manuscripts appear to have a mistaken word here. The commentator suggests the intended meaning is “pleasure” or “taking delight.”

[^V09-32]: The first death is the ending of earthly existence. The first revival is life in the intermediate state after death. The second death is the ending of that intermediate state, and the second revival is life in the final world. It is in that last state that the unhappy enter Hell, after the reckoning, the record of deeds, the weighing of acts, the crossing of the bridge, and the other stages described in revelation. The final life is more intense than the intermediate life, as waking is more intense than sleeping; in turn, the intermediate life is more intense than earthly life. In the intermediate state a person is like a traveler who has left home but keeps looking back.

    Things encountered along the road are half-seen, as though the traveler were neither fully alive nor fully dead. This is the very meaning of an intermediate state: it faces in two directions. Eventually sight becomes sharp and the inner vision firm, and intermediate existence gives way to the final life. This is why the body of the intermediate state is said to differ from the body of the final life. Both the happy and unhappy who still live at the level of forms undergo this change. Those who have risen beyond forms pass, when they awaken, into the world of intellect and meaning. Some changes belong to the blast that overwhelms, and others to the blast that brings fear.

    The verse therefore concerns the stages of existence after death, not natural bodily changes and certainly not the return of a soul into another earthly body.

[^V09-33]: “The same body” means the same in its form and identity, not in its changing material. A body in the next life no longer has the potentiality that belongs to earthly development.

[^V09-34]: The commentator explains bodily identity through a lasting soul and an indistinct basis of matter or form. On another account, the soul's qualities and habits are the basis for the afterlife image that belongs to it.

[^V09-35]: “Young and beardless, around thirty” describes their youthful appearance, while their understanding, strength, and bodily development are complete.

[^V09-36]: Mount Uhud is near Medina; al-Bayda is a mountain near Damascus. The report compares the distance to three days' travel.

[^V09-37]: This recalls the words of revelation: “The life to come is truly the life.” The reason is that the life to come is itself alive and aware. Life and awareness are not extra qualities added to it, as they seem to be in this worldly condition, which resembles sleep. Sleep, after all, is called the brother of death.

    Think of what you see in a dream: a sky above you, an earth beneath you, beautiful images that delight you, or disturbing images that frighten and hurt you. All of them exist as forms in your awareness, yet their presence can be powerful. In a confused dream, imagination works upon forms gathered from waking experience. In a true dream, the inner faculty that brings sensations together may witness forms from higher sources and reflect them to the soul, or the soul may know them directly without such a reflection. In every case, what is experienced belongs to knowledge and life.

[^V09-38]: Pleasure and pain are nothing other than awareness of what suits us or goes against us. What we know immediately is the form present in awareness. So an event outside us is not pleasant or painful simply as an event; it is experienced as pleasant or painful through how it is known.

    Even a distressing image need not cause distress by itself. Much of its power comes from what imagination and judgment make of it. The image of losing family or property makes you grieve because imagination treats the loss of your connection to them as though you had lost your very self, or some quality that truly belongs to you.

    Similarly, we may fear the image of a dead person who, even when alive, had no power to harm us. Our horror of the corpse comes from imagination. The same is true of our dislike of many things we have learned to find repulsive. It is as though we carve out, for ourselves, the very things that frighten, hurt, sadden, and attack us. What trouble we bring upon ourselves!

    Blessed is the person who sees the Lord appearing in both gentleness and severity, and loves Him through all the forms of His names. Such a person finds the greater contentment that comes from God.

[^V09-39]: The word means numbness or a dulling of sensation, a term also used by physicians.

[^V09-40]: This is a difficult objection for anyone who claims that a separate soul can suffer bodily-style pain but denies that its inner powers can exist in an imaginal form. A particular image requires a particular power that can perceive and imagine it.

[^V09-41]: The objection is that there is no special relation to explain why this soul, rather than another, should become attached to this heavenly body, rather than another. Without such a reason, the choice would be arbitrary. It is not being claimed that an earthly soul can have no relation at all to a heavenly body. Someone could answer that even before death the soul is joined to something resembling a heavenly sphere. We have said that the human being contains something corresponding to everything in the world. The very fine vapour within the body, which is the soul's first bodily connection, resembles a heavenly sphere because it is balanced and stands between opposing qualities.

    This likeness is why it can be called a ‘sphere.’ Scripture itself speaks of the heavens as ‘smoke.’ On this comparison, the heavens are like the subtle vapour in the head of the greater human being—the world as a whole—while the vapour in a person's own head is the sphere of the small world that a human being contains.

[^V09-42]: Some philosophers proposed a smoky body as the subject of imagination because the soul's earthly powers depend on subtle bodily fluids, nerves, veins, and related structures.

[^V09-43]: The proposed body would be composite and so subject to decay, though the realm of the afterlife is everlasting. If it were a simple, indestructible body, it would in effect be another celestial sphere, yet would still be finite and too small to hold an unlimited number of souls.

[^V09-44]: The objection is about souls gathering all at once, not entering bodies one after another over time.

[^V09-45]: In the soul's higher state, its power to act returns to its knowledge, somewhat as God's knowledge is active rather than received from things outside Him. Its imagination returns to a power of direct seeing, as happens in dreams—which are said to resemble death. What someone sees, hears, and experiences there can be vivid perception, not merely a faint picture. Here is a fuller account. In this bodily life the soul has ten outward and inward powers. In the world of images there are corresponding powers, since this world receives its reality from that one. But the world of images is wider and is not limited by physical matter, position, or time.

    Each power can therefore contain all ten: sight can do the work of all ten, and so can hearing, and so on. This gives a hundred forms of power. At the level of intellect, where the soul is unified more completely, each of these can again be understood in relation to all ten, making a thousand. These are not a thousand separate organs. One soul, precisely because it is one, contains their powers. For this reason it brings forth its many bodily powers as expressions of what is already in it. That is the point of the claim that the visible powers are all shadows of what lies in the soul's own reality.

    Scripture asks, ‘Have you not seen how your Lord extends the shadow?’

[^V09-46]: The manuscript note says the passage seems to promise a second argument against the objection, but no such second argument appears.

[^V09-47]: Taken literally, this passage and the later words ‘even if they are assumed to be without end’ might suggest that the souls of the deeply unhappy are finite in number. That need not be so. The souls of those nearest to God and the souls of those who have reached the depths of misery may both be without number, not only the many people in between. The world has a beginning, as the stronger argument shows, yet God's giving never comes to an end. His giving is everlasting even though each thing that receives it comes into being. His creative act does not cease, though each created thing begins. God's words do not run out.

    Perhaps Sadra is here allowing, for the sake of argument, the view that the suffering of unbelievers will eventually end because they will acquire a nature suited to the Fire, as a salamander is said to be suited to fire. Their souls would then cease to count as unhappy in that sense. He may also be drawing on the view, soon to be quoted from Suhrawardi, that ignorant and immoral people may be delivered through the Greater Spirit.

[^V09-48]: I see no difficulty in saying that the clear and subtle body of a heavenly sphere can display images belonging to the souls of some of those who have attained happiness. Because it is so clear, forms from the world below can also appear in it. Suhrawardī makes this point in *The Philosophy of Illumination*: everything in the world of elements, he says, is pictured in the heavens just as it appears here, with all its features. Every human being is shown there with all their states, movements, and moments of rest, whether already past or still to come.

    Al-Shīrāzī, explaining another passage by Suhrawardī, says that these pictures of earthly things in the heavenly spheres must recur. If something knows every future event in its proper order, as the souls of the heavenly spheres are said to do, or if all those events are pictured within it, as they are said to be pictured in the heavenly bodies, then the recurrence of those events follows. He accepts the first claim on the basis of the earlier argument, and so accepts the conclusion.

    There is no conflict between the claim that these images exist in their own right and the claim that they appear through other things. On the author's account, imagination is independent of matter, while the subtle spirit associated with the brain provides a way for it to appear. The reality imagined and the act of imagining are one at the appropriate level. Similarly, for Suhrawardī, images seen in mirrors and in imagination belong to the independent world of images. Neither the spirit associated with the brain nor a mirror is their material container. Each is only a place where they become visible.

    An image that exists independently does not *need* a mirror in order to be what it is. Its appearing there follows from the mirror's own nature. In the same way, when the soul, at a level beyond ordinary bodily existence, appears with its imagined forms through a heavenly body, that does not make it the soul of that heavenly body. Images appear in polished mirrors because of the mirrors' properties and because of a relation between the soul and the forms seen. This does not make the mirrors living beings with souls. Nor does it prevent heavenly bodies from remaining under the powers proper to their own natures. Thus no movement of one soul into another natural body is required.

    Just as the heavens can display the greater world of images, they can display the lesser world of images belonging to individual souls. We should not reject the possibility that a being in the visible world reveals one from the unseen world. After all, the soul belongs to a higher order while its body is a visible, physical thing that reveals it. Imagined forms belong to that higher order too, while the subtle bodily spirit can reveal them. The discussion of resurrection will also establish ways in which Paradise and Hell appear in this world.

    The author objects that souls in the next life have no bodies through which they could stand in a spatial relation to the heavens, as an object stands before a mirror. But why could their relation not be like the relation of powers and images to the subtle spirits associated with the brain?

    This is how I understand Suhrawardī's statement in *The Philosophy of Illumination*: some happy souls who have not reached the highest rank, together with people of self-restraint who have freed themselves from bodily attachments, can enter the world of independent images, which appear through certain higher heavenly bodies. They can themselves bring such images into appearance. Food, beautiful sights, pleasing sounds, and whatever they wish become present to them. These forms are more complete than the corresponding forms in our world, because their higher supports are complete while the bodies that support appearances here are limited. These souls remain there because they retain a connection with the higher intervening bodies, which do not decay.

    In Suhrawardī's terminology, an *intervening body* is a natural body. Elsewhere he says that some souls at an intermediate rank may give rise to luminous, independently existing images, displayed through the heavenly spheres, which appear as countless groups of angels. This is also the meaning of an earlier view, attributed by his commentator to Plato and others, about the passage of people who have reached perfection in action. — S. R.

[^V09-49]: In sleep, the soul produces beautiful images, enjoys them, and is affected by them. It also produces frightening images and suffers fear and pain because of them. These images do not appear for no reason.

    In ordinary bodily life, good or poor care, together with the conditions of the body, prepares someone for a particular state of health or illness. One outcome does not occur without something that makes it possible. Nothing comes to be until its causes are in place. Likewise, in waking life, our chosen actions, the body's natural condition, and the bodily spirit prepare the soul to form certain images rather than others. This does not happen through an arbitrary choice without a cause.

    So too in the next life: past actions and settled habits bring the soul, through the order of causes, to produce forms suited to what it has become. It cannot simply choose incompatible forms, however strong its powers become, because effects must be suited to their causes. That is why we are told that our deeds will return to us. As Rumi says, in effect: “If a thorn has wounded you, you planted it yourself; if you wear silk, you spun it yourself.”

    All higher and lower causes are also means by which God's generosity reaches things and through which He acts. “Say: All is from God.” The real existence of created things does not conflict with God's being the true source of existence. In the same way, their real actions do not conflict with His all-embracing power to act. We must hold both truths together, avoiding either extreme.

[^V09-50]: We should not picture descent as a straight line and the return as a separate curve. The circle is taken as the best image because it is simple and whole; its center stands in the same relation to every point on its edge. Each level on the descending side has a counterpart on the ascending side. The realities correspond, though their modes of existence differ. On the way down, there are intellects that are immaterial from the beginning. On the way up, there are souls that become fully intellectual only after being freed from matter and its attachments. Souls begin their existence in connection with bodies, even though they continue in a spiritual state.

    This helps explain the saying that the bearers of the Throne are four at the beginning but eight at the end: the first four are joined by four others brought forth along the path of the complete human being. The verse says, ‘Eight will bear the Throne of your Lord that Day.’ There are distinctions within each half of the circle, yet all share one reality, because existence is one reality with differing degrees. On the descending side, souls come before the world of images in the timeless order; on the ascending side, souls reach that world after living in time. There is a world of images on each side, and below them are the changing natures that exist in time.

[^V09-51]: The first intermediate realm is where the covenant with God is taken; the final one is where people meet the results of keeping or breaking that covenant. Philosophers agree that such forms exist, though they disagree about whether they exist independently or in celestial souls.

[^V09-52]: One might object: a rule of reason cannot cease to apply, so what is impossible must always remain impossible. The reply is that the statement is intended to uphold both reason and revelation. The forms that appear in the afterlife must answer to the forms known in this world, but they do not thereby return to the earthly world. ‘Return in the afterlife’ can mean that worldly forms find their answer there. Or it can mean a return to a world that is directly seen and disclosed, not a return to physical nature as we know it. In the afterlife, forms become fuller, clearer, and more readily known than in the intermediate state after death.

    In my copy of the Journey on the Soul, a disputed word appears only in the margin; it may have been added by a later hand. If it is not original, the sentence can be read in another way: precisely because the afterlife is not this world, a return from one to the other is impossible. They are different states, as the goal differs from the way that leads to it.

[^V09-53]: The comparison between mirror images, dreams, and afterlife images works only if the images are not materially inside the mirror and if the soul is connected to the image so that it is alive.

[^V09-54]: Avicenna points out that people marvel when a magnet pulls iron, yet take for granted the soul's power to move the body—making it rise, descend, walk, run, or fall. The note also quotes Persian verse about how imagination shapes conflict and honor, while our strength comes from an unseen wind.

[^V09-55]: You may ask: how does this account agree with the belief that the Prophet ascended to heaven in his body? I answer that the discussion here is not about prophetic souls or miracles. In the case of Muhammad, the final Prophet, the ascent did not involve his coarse, heavy body *as a coarse body*. His body was subtle.
