A body that can see behind itself as clearly as in front, and that casts no shadow, cannot be dense in the ordinary way. It is subtler even than the outermost heavenly sphere. Yet the body with which he ascended was his outward, actual body.

    Later, in the discussion of bodily resurrection, the author will say that the body raised on the last day is this same body that lived in the world. Bodily ascent and bodily resurrection go together; they are, so to speak, twins nourished at the same breast, and many of the same principles govern them.

    How could a philosopher who knows divine realities say that the Prophet ascended only in spirit, not in body? In one sense, the ascent of the spirit includes that of the body, because the deeper reality contains its outward expression. The spirit's ascent therefore carries with it the ascent of what expresses it. This is even clearer for the body that appears in the world of images. But someone who truly knows these matters should not stop at saying that the body is included in the spirit. That person should also affirm that the Prophet's outward body itself ascended.

    It is useful here to give a brief account of the ascent, though a full account would go beyond the purpose of this note. Many reports describe it, and the Qur'an refers to it in the verse beginning, “Glory be to Him who took His servant by night from the Sacred Mosque.” Gabriel brought the Prophet the creature called Burāq, with a saddle and bridle set with precious jewels and wings like those of birds. When the Prophet first approached to ride, Burāq shied and ran away. Gabriel brought it back, and the Messenger of God mounted it.

    A ladder with steps extending from earth to heaven then appeared. Through it the Prophet ascended, stage by stage, seeing the heavens and what they contain. Later the Burāq was replaced by the *rafraf*, on which he rose still farther. Gabriel reached the limit of his strength, but the Prophet continued to the innermost place of divine majesty, where God revealed to His servant what He revealed and he saw what he saw. The reports supply the remaining details.

    Muslims have differed greatly about the nature of this event. Some, citing reports attributed to ʿĀʾisha and Muʿāwiya, say it took place in a dream. I regard that claim as false. Others say it happened while he was awake, but only in spirit. Others accept that he traveled in his body as far as the Farthest Mosque, as the Qur'an explicitly states, but deny that his body rose beyond heaven. I also reject this last view. The ascent to heaven itself has become a firmly established belief of the religion. A belief established in that way does not depend on our recovering a separate proof for every part of it. Its detailed features, however, are drawn from the reports.

    Most Muslims maintain that the Prophet was awake and traveled bodily from the Sacred Mosque to a place above the heavens. Some spiritual teachers and philosophers also accept that the event happened while he was awake, but understand the body involved as a body of the world of images. They take everything he saw as an image with a real meaning, following accounts such as those of Muḥyī al-Dīn Ibn Arabi on the forms of the afterlife and of Suhrawardī on the world of reflected images. They hold that every meaning can take a form and every higher reality has an outward expression.

    On this interpretation, what people traveling toward God see must be understood through its inner meaning, much as a dream must be interpreted. Traveling from the Sacred Mosque to the Farthest Mosque is a visible form of moving from one spiritual station to another. The prophets' following Muhammad in prayer represents the followers of his community, heirs of the prophets, taking him as their guide. Burāq represents the acts of worship that carry a person forward, as in the saying, “Prayer is the believer's ascent.”

    The saddle and bridle represent a gathered, attentive mind and a heart present in worship. The jewels represent love, sincerity, humility, and reverence. Burāq's shying away represents a passing, bodily or human thought. Gabriel's bringing it back represents the removal of that thought by the universal intellect, which directs the Prophet toward what is right—or by the Prophet's own spiritual reality. The *rafraf* represents the joining of unity and multiplicity, and of spiritual and natural qualities, through taking on qualities that reflect the One of whom it is said, “Nothing is like Him, and He is the All-Hearing, the All-Seeing.”

    Gabriel's visible form, resembling Diḥya al-Kalbī, represents the universal intellect and is an expression of the Holy Spirit. Gabriel's growing weary and being unable to go farther represents the Prophet's superiority even to the angels nearest to God. He passes beyond them to a station that no other being can enter, the station described as “or nearer still.” The Prophet expressed this by saying, “I have a time with God in which neither an angel brought near nor a prophet sent forth can accompany me.” ʿAṭṭār of Nishapur puts it well:

    > When He makes a celebration in solitude with His friend,
    > Gabriel's wings burn; he cannot enter.
    >
    > When the great bird of his soul appears,
    > Even Moses is left startled and small.

    The other details may be interpreted in the same way. But do not mistake this view for the claim that the ascent happened *only in spirit*. Those who say it was purely spiritual do not suppose that the Prophet saw these actual images at all. For them, the ascent is simply the progress of the prophetic spirit through higher levels of understanding. Climbing the heavens means advancing by thought and knowledge; Burāq is a metaphor for obedience; and the saddle, bridle, and jewels stand for keeping the heart free of selfish thoughts and open to angelic and divine inspiration. They are not forms the traveler actually sees.

    A person engaged in prayerful attention and spiritual struggle may, however, really see images of that struggle: perhaps a battlefield on which armies of light pursue armies of darkness. Another person may undergo the same struggle without seeing any such image. This experience does not come to everyone. It depends on the strength of a person's spiritual practice and insight, and on the rank of prophecy or sainthood reached.

    The truth, in my view, is that the Prophet ascended while awake, with his noble outward body, to a place above the heavens, and with his holy spirit to the innermost divine presence, to the station “or nearer still.” Those who deny a bodily ascent raise several objections. The strongest relies on the philosophers' claim that the heavenly spheres cannot be split open and then joined together again.

    Many theologians, including the careful thinker al-Lāhījī in *Gawhar-i Murād*, therefore said that this impossibility applies only to the outermost sphere. The reports speak of the Prophet ascending above the heavens, and the religious accounts count seven heavens, with the Throne and Footstool beyond them. They argued that the reason breaking and rejoining cannot occur is that the outermost sphere sets the bounds of direction. To break it would require direction to be fixed independently of what fixes direction. On this reasoning, the impossibility would apply only to that outermost sphere.

    I am surprised that so skilled a thinker should have said this. A heavenly sphere, in the philosophy under discussion, is a simple body with a fifth kind of nature, whose natural tendency is circular rather than straight movement. If it could receive the tendency toward straight movement, or the associated features of heat, cold, expansion, and contraction, it would no longer be a heavenly sphere. What would then distinguish it from an earthly element? The distinction rests precisely on these two kinds of natural motion. Showing that one proof of impossibility applies only to the sphere that sets the bounds of direction does not remove every other proof. Denying a particular reason is not the same as denying every reason.

    The better answer is that the Prophet's bodily ascent through even the outermost sphere required no breaking at all. His body was so subtle that it could pass through the heavenly bodies without splitting them. I have described only a small part of its subtlety at the beginning of this note.

    Even people who practise the arts of preparing substances know of oils that, when placed on the palm, are said to penetrate to the back of the hand because of their fineness. They also speak of highly refined substances called *spirits*, produced through repeated distillation and rising vapours, which penetrate denser substances called *bodies* as though they pass through them at once.

    Consider a vessel filled with earth. No more earth may fit, yet it can still hold a substantial amount of water. Fill it with water, and there may still be room for air. A skin filled with air may still receive fire, in the account of the elements used here. The same reasoning can be extended, hypothetically, from fire to a heavenly sphere, and from a lower sphere to a higher one. The finer substance can pass through the denser one. The body of the final Prophet, the center around which the spheres turn, is subtler than they are. It is therefore no wonder that it could pass through them without tearing them apart.

    Some say that he ascended in the body of the world of images, but not in his outward body. They would not say this if they fully understood the relation between the two. It is like the relation between an image supported by a surface and a reality that stands by itself. What stands by itself possesses in full the perfections expressed in what depends on something else; it does not lose any of its positive reality.

    Another objection is drawn from the Qur'anic passage in which unbelievers demand that the Prophet make a spring gush from the ground, produce a garden of palms and vines with rivers running through it, cause pieces of the sky to fall, bring God and the angels before them, possess a house of gold, or rise into heaven—and even then they say they will not believe in his ascent until he brings down a book they can read. He is told to reply, “Glory be to my Lord! Am I anything but a human messenger?” Some take this to deny that a human being can ascend bodily.

    But the expression of wonder concerns their demand that he bring God before them, not the possibility of rising into heaven. Bodily ascent is no more opposed to his humanity than having a garden of palms and grapes would be. The same answer applies to their other weak objections. What I shall explain about bodily resurrection will also help us understand bodily ascent. — S. R.

[^V09-56]: As the soul turns toward its higher life, it pays less attention to keeping the body in good condition, and the body gradually deteriorates.

    You may object that many people fear death and care for their bodies more intensely as it approaches. A sacred report even says that God does not hesitate over anything as He does over taking the soul of a believing servant: the servant dislikes death, and God dislikes causing the servant distress.

    The answer is that the turn toward the higher life, and the wish to rely less on the body and its powers, belong to the soul's rational nature and deepest aim. They do not rule out the imagination's fear of bodily death. Imagination and bodily apprehension are, in one respect, other than the soul's rational nature. In short, the wish to leave behind dependence on the body is a wish for completeness and self-sufficiency. Reason naturally seeks this, even when imagination shrinks from dying.

[^V09-57]: “All beings” here means the beings of the natural world, which move toward God in different ways. It does not mean that every soul is a separate intelligence.

[^V09-58]: Among the great mystics, a divine name means the reality of existence considered through one of God's attributes. Consider existence as something that appears in itself and makes other things appear: it is called Light. Consider it as what reveals both itself and everything else: it is called the Knowing One. Consider it as beautiful and good, rejoicing in itself and in its own effects, loving and approving them because they come from it: it is called the Willing, the Loving, and the Content. Consider it as giving existence knowingly and by choice: it is called the Powerful. Other divine names can be explained in the same way. ‘Name’ may also refer to any created thing insofar as it is a sign of some perfection of God.

    With this in mind, the statement that the two groups travel and come to rest ‘in God's name’ means that the wind, the person steering, those who follow, the soul, and the soul's powers all act as signs of God's power. The journey begins through His name as the source that brings things forth, and comes to rest through His name as the final end to which they return. The mystics regard the divine names as the real sources of the kinds of things in the world. Where others see the works of bodily powers, natural forces, souls, and intellects, they see the effects of God's beautiful names.

[^V09-59]: The commentator suggests that the author may have left out another version of the “one soul” view for brevity, or because it was too weak to mention.

[^V09-60]: The argument assumes that each feature must exist separately. The commentator replies that a thing can contain several features in one unified existence.

[^V09-61]: Powers can separate even when they are in the same bodily place, as when one bodily function fails while another remains. But the argument is about distinct souls, not merely distinct powers. Their differences can also come from the different stages and roles of a living thing.

[^V09-62]: The commentator clarifies the grammar of this sentence: it introduces the condition that there must be a shared source for the powers.

[^V09-63]: The objection would work only if “cause” meant “manifestation” in the special sense used by some mystics. That is not the sense used in this argument.

[^V09-64]: The soul is what performs all these actions. Yet the soul is not material, while the actions in question have some connection with matter. It therefore needs different bodily organs, each of which directs the soul's activity towards a particular task.

    Without these organs, its activity would not be directed in this specific way. And without that direction, no particular action would take place: considered simply in itself, an immaterial agent has an equal relation to all material things. This is, in substance, what the author will say near the end of the discussion. Seen this way, the objection raises a genuine point, but its supporters have not explained it with enough care.

[^V09-65]: Individual sense images prepare the soul to know particular things. The soul itself is not a storehouse of separate bodily images.

[^V09-66]: The commentator distinguishes physical multiplicity, such as division into places and shapes, from differences in meaning. Intellectual beings can contain many meanings without being divided into parts.

[^V09-67]: Take anger as an example. It is one reality appearing at different levels. In the bodily world, it may appear as the blood growing hot, the face darkening, and the veins swelling. In the soul, it is an inward state free from those bodily features, moving a person to seek relief or revenge. At the level of intellect, it corresponds to the power to overcome, as shown by the higher lights separate from matter. At the divine level, it points to the absolute power that prevails over all lesser lights, the overwhelming majesty of the Light of Lights before which all multiplicity fades.

    Love, mercy, fear, and similar states can be understood in the same way. This example concerns states of feeling. Knowledge also appears differently at different levels: whiteness is present to sight in one way, to imagination in another, and to intellect in another. The same is true of beings themselves. The human being has a natural, bodily existence, an existence in the world of images, an intellectual existence, and a higher existence; and within the mind it can be understood as this particular sensory image, an image in imagination, a general intellectual concept, or a universal nature applying to all these levels.

[^V09-68]: The commentator extends the comparison: the ten outer and inner senses are present in the imaginal world in many combinations, and in the intellectual world in an even more unified way.

[^V09-69]: Many meanings can be true of one simple being without breaking its unity.

[^V09-70]: In short, what is true of the receiver affects what it receives, and what is true of what is received affects the receiver. The soul brings its own awareness to its powers and their bodily instruments. The body, however, imposes limits and particular conditions on how that awareness appears. Because an organ is limited, it cannot display every quality of the soul.

    An objection may be raised. If the bodily world is divided into parts, should not every power of the single soul appear in every organ, even if only faintly? Why should one organ see and another hear?

    The answer is that division alone does not explain the differences. The organs also have different bodily makeups, materials, positions, and relationships. That is why the soul appears as sight in the eye and as hearing in the ear, even though every part of reality may in one respect contain something of every other part.

    There is a deeper answer. The soul is a great sign of reality, and it shows qualities of both transcendence and likeness. It has levels: meanings free from particular limits, meanings understood in relation to something else, forms free from matter, and forms joined to matter. Some of its powers are one without occupying places; others are divided through position and bodily organs. These differences belong to their respective levels. The human soul therefore has a genuine, reflected unity, not the numerical unity of a thing counted as one part among others.

[^V09-71]: The soul's need for different powers and organs shows that it is at a lower stage than the separate intellect. When the soul no longer needs them, it has become intellect.

[^V09-72]: The claim that the soul cannot be one because it acts both with and without matter is not decisive if existence can grow stronger by stages and the soul can change in its very being.

[^V09-73]: “Fully present knowledge” here means knowledge at the level of the acquired intellect.

[^V09-74]: The argument includes imagination among the powers said to perceive bodily things.

[^V09-75]: This refers to the earlier argument that different powers can be separated.

[^V09-76]: One source can produce one effect when the body's readiness is present, and no effect when it is absent. These are different conditions, not different sources.

[^V09-77]: If the Giver of Forms alone caused every effect and the powers only prepared the body, then active powers would not be needed. The commentator says this would make them useless.

[^V09-78]: The disagreement here concerns demonstration, not merely a contest of opinions. The illuminationist philosopher argues according to his own principles, while the author argues both from his principles and from those of the philosophers in Aristotle’s school. One example is the author's acceptance of substantial forms joined to matter and responsible for things' distinctive effects. The philosopher of illumination, by contrast, speaks of forms separate from matter that act upon it: the governing beings he calls the lords of species.

    The author is right that the unity of God, both in Himself and in His action, does not require us to deny created powers. Powers joined to physical things are instruments through which God acts. The higher beings that are separate from matter are also His instruments, or, in another image, eyes through which His governance is displayed. Scripture itself speaks of God's hosts and describes Him as exalted in rank and Lord of the Throne.

    On the illuminationist account, the single reality does not differ in level, still less in number or kind. Its differences arise from the ways it appears to receivers with different capacities.

    An objection arises: the philosopher of illumination also speaks of powers. What can he mean by them if, throughout the body, only the soul acts? Consider the imagination. For the author, it is the soul appearing as a particular active power, able to perceive images free from matter though not from size. The power that forms bodily shapes, or the power by which a plant grows, is likewise the soul operating in a particular way without leaving its higher level. In the illuminationist account, imagination can instead be understood as the soul or intellect appearing through a receptive power joined to a bodily condition. The same can be said of the other powers. In this sense it is reasonable to say that the illuminationist philosopher did not abolish powers altogether.

[^V09-79]: Even the ancient philosophers discussed here held that imagination can exist apart from the natural body. It is linked to the imaginal world, not simply to a bodily organ.

[^V09-80]: Differences between kinds of living things come from their active sources; differences between individuals come from the matter that receives a form. Matter makes an individual distinct, but it does not create the kind itself.

[^V09-81]: Bodily powers are real natural causes. God is the ultimate source of existence, but these powers act directly within nature.

[^V09-82]: A preparatory cause is something whose presence, and then its passing away, helps make another thing possible. We must distinguish three cases. A positive cause is something on whose presence the result depends. An obstacle is something whose absence is required. A preparatory condition is involved in bringing about the state from which the result can occur. I take the philosopher's remark to be directed at people who multiply the soul's powers and then call them mere preparations for the giver of forms. Their position rejects the view, held by some theologians, that one created act generates the next—for example, that moving a hand directly generates the movement of a key, or that premises generate a conclusion.

    Philosophers instead distinguish the actual giver of existence from the causes that prepare things to receive it. But the word ‘preparatory’ is also used in an everyday, wider sense: a means through which the real source sends its effect. Sadra uses it this way for bodily and immaterial causes, both here and in his book on origin and return. The real giver of existence, he explains, must be free of every unfulfilled possibility, and only God is so free. All other causes are means through which His giving reaches things. A philosopher of this rank certainly knew the difference between a cause in the strict sense and a preparatory condition.

[^V09-83]: In the *Philosophy of Illumination*, Suhrawardi offers a related explanation. Light, he says, gives of itself and has, in its very being, love for what is of its own kind and mastery over what is below it.

    When the light that governs a living body exercises mastery through that body, the power of anger arises. Through its love, the power of desire arises. The governing light also sees particular images and understands a general form that can apply to them: someone sees Zayd and Amr, for example, and grasps the human nature they share. A corresponding power of nourishment appears in the body.

    Likewise, because a complete light can be the source of another light, a power of reproduction appears in its body, bringing about another body with its own governing light. Because light can also increase through the lights that reach it and move from possibility to fulfillment, a power of growth appears in the body, enlarging it in fitting proportions.

    These bodily powers are therefore branches of the governing light. The body is its image or representation. This is a shortened account of Suhrawardi's explanation.

[^V09-84]: The senses may fall quiet in sleep, illness, deep contemplation, a spiritual experience, or death. In these states, a person may perceive through the inward senses.

[^V09-85]: The commentator asks whether there may also be a fourth, divine human level, linked to God's greatest name.

[^V09-86]: The philosopher compares ordinary senses with weak intellects and higher intellect with strong sense. Perception and action always belong to one self, though they appear at different levels.

[^V09-87]: The Prophet's reports about tasting, smelling, seeing, and touch refer to real perceptions, not only abstract spiritual ideas. They take place through the soul's subtle, afterlife senses.

[^V09-88]: The Arabic word means “gathered and folded.” The Prophet's saying that the earth was folded up for him means its distant regions were brought near.

[^V09-89]: The report about the sky groaning points to God's greatness and the great number of angels.

[^V09-90]: The sun makes an object visible, while the soul brings its imagined forms into being. The soul also gives rise to images of things seen outside it.

[^V09-91]: Fine and coarse physical matter do not, by themselves, make something higher or lower in existence. The commentator also warns that some of the chapter's medical claims are open to challenge in light of modern medicine.

[^V09-92]: The commentator distinguishes three kinds of bodily spirit: one associated with the brain, one carried in the arteries and linked to the heart, and one linked to the liver.

[^V09-93]: Turning toward the sky in prayer is, in truth, turning toward God.

[^V09-94]: You may object that there is no last intermediate level. Would this not place an infinite number of things between two boundaries?

    No. The intermediate levels are joined together without separate dividing lines. They are one continuous reality with degrees, not a collection of things that could be counted, still less an infinite number of separately existing things.

    The author gives a similar example elsewhere. Between water and air there is vapour. Between water and vapour we can distinguish thinner water and denser vapour; between vapour and air there are further degrees. All of these changes occur through the movement of substance itself. The same idea helps answer an objection to the rule that a higher possible being comes before a lower one: the ordered intellects are degrees of one reality. That objection and its answer have been explained more fully elsewhere.

[^V09-95]: Another argument for the subtle bodily spirit is that the human being is a small image of the universe and contains counterparts to its different levels.

[^V09-96]: The commentator says that dissection shows the brain and spinal cord as the roots of the nerves, despite the cited claim that no proof establishes this.

[^V09-97]: Writing can communicate more clearly than gesture, but it takes more effort. Gesture is easier, but works only for someone who can see it from the right direction. The mention of the eyes is an example, not the only possible gesture.

[^V09-98]: “Must” and “must not” can refer to duties known through reason or religious teaching. “Good” and “bad” can cover all five kinds of moral judgment: required, recommended, allowed, disliked, or forbidden.

[^V09-99]: Animals share some of these traits with human beings, though they are weaker or less clear. The commentator points to records of animal behavior as evidence.

[^V09-100]: Theoretical reason receives knowledge from what is above it; practical reason acts on what is below it, especially the body.

[^V09-101]: The power also called *practical reason* is a power of understanding. But it deals specifically with particular matters connected with what we do, such as how to use our powers of perception and movement. The other power, theoretical reason, deals with what can be understood apart from such particular actions. This distinction underlies the difference between practical and theoretical wisdom.

    Particular actions here include more than everyday work. They include actions directed toward the life to come, such as justice and kindness done for worthy and firmly established ends. They also include the work of ordinary living, such as building a house so carefully that it serves the purposes for which it was made.

[^V09-102]: The power of desire serves practical reason in a human being, just as it serves the powers of judgment and imagination in an animal. When desire serves practical reason, we call it *will*. When it serves the animal power of judgment, we call it *appetite*.

    Consider visiting someone who is ill. If a person does this for God's sake, the action belongs to practical and theoretical reason. If the aim is to gain a pleasure or benefit imagined by the mind or felt by the senses, it belongs to the animal powers of perception and movement.

[^V09-103]: Suppose we begin with the general rule, “Every good action ought to be done.” Add the statement, “Telling the truth is good.” Together these give us the general conclusion, “Telling the truth ought to be done.” Theoretical reason understands that general judgment.

    When practical reason faces a particular choice, it says, “This act would be truthful. Every truthful act ought to be done. So this act ought to be done.” The judgment now concerns something we can actually do. Practical reason has reached it with help from theoretical reason.

    These are two levels of one intellect, not two wholly separate powers. Theoretical reason understands things we cannot bring about by our own actions. Practical reason understands how we are to act.

[^V09-104]: Practical reason usually begins from common beliefs and experience, though some of its starting points can be self-evident or well tested.

[^V09-105]: The commentator says that careful attention to Avicenna's account shows that there is one judge within us, not several separate selves.

[^V09-106]: The soul remains the underlying source of its powers, and those powers are different levels at which it appears. Each level, considered only in its own particular form, is not the whole soul. As explained earlier, the soul has both multiplicity within unity and unity within multiplicity.

    The author points out that to know the soul properly we should first understand existence itself and its different levels. The strongest explanation is one that moves from cause to effect. This is one way to understand the saying, “Whoever knows himself knows his Lord.” We come to know the laws and qualities of the Lord first, and then, by their light, know the soul. Knowledge of the former becomes the measure of knowledge of the latter. Other interpretations of the saying were discussed earlier.

[^V09-107]: The intellect's early need for the body's powers is part of its development. It does not mean that the intellect always depends on them.

[^V09-108]: Strength and nobility are different qualities. A soul may have strength without nobility, nobility without strength, or both.

[^V09-109]: A person may smell one thing, hear another, and write something else at the same time. They do not confuse these activities, and they can tell their different qualities apart. The common sense that brings perceptions together offers an example, however small, of a being whom no activity distracts from another.

    This is especially clear on the author's account, which holds that the soul actively produces its perceptions of particular things. When something visible is before it, a sound is heard, a smell reaches it, food is tasted, and something is touched, the soul can grasp all these things at once.

    The same applies, at a higher level, to the simple intellect that gives rise to detailed acts of understanding. This is the settled ability to know. It grasps many things in one enduring act, even when no separate images of those things pass through the imagination in sequence. Ask someone who possesses this ability a number of different questions: they can see the answers together before putting them into words one by one.

    Someone might think that the answers were not known until they were separately expressed, and that the knowledge was merely close at hand beforehand, unlike the distant possibility of knowledge in an uneducated person. But that confuses a lack of separate images in the imagination with a lack of intellectual knowledge. It also confuses the absence of many separate forms with the presence of one complete and powerful form of understanding. This unified light makes the different objects of knowledge clear to the intellect itself, even before they become clear to its imagination, its senses, or anyone else.

    A human being is more than imagination and sensation. The intellect is the highest level of the person, and the other powers draw on it. Nor is this knowledge merely vague. Existence is light; the stronger and more unified that light is, the better it can reveal differences within what it knows. Many things can be known more distinctly through one comprehensive act of understanding than through scattered acts. In the same way, all fixed realities are eternally known to God in perfect detail.

[^V09-110]: “The first” and “the second” refer to strength or weakness in the soul's sensory and intellectual levels.

[^V09-111]: Learned virtues are valuable too. The commentator notes that Aristotle calls freedom a “settled quality,” which can suggest a virtue developed through repeated practice.

[^V09-112]: The two virtues are wisdom and freedom. Together they mean readiness to understand many things and to become less attached to matter.

[^V09-113]: Justice is the balance among the soul's four main powers. The power of knowledge means readiness of the theoretical intellect.

[^V09-114]: Too much practical thought becomes cleverness used to gain worldly benefits and avoid harm. Too little neglects practical judgment. The virtue lies between these extremes.

[^V09-115]: Knowledge of the truth is the source of many goods, but it also bears a relation to the wisdom of character. Near the end of the *Metaphysics* of the *Healing*, Avicenna limits wisdom as a character trait to balanced thinking about everyday affairs. It would be better not to limit it in that way, since our thinking also concerns matters of theory.

    This does not mean that examining difficult questions too deeply is a vice. The search for truth is good. The fault lies in shifting endlessly among opinions without reaching sound conclusions because one has neglected the sustained study of real knowledge. Such a person may still discuss difficult ideas with great confidence. The appearance of learning is then like the swelling of a sick body mistaken for healthy strength, or like smoke without fire. Spending time with such people can spread this habit, just as certain company can encourage ignorance or foolishness.

    We must distinguish wisdom as knowledge from wisdom as a trait of character. The first is knowing things as they truly are. We should seek more of it, as in the prayer, “My Lord, increase me in knowledge.” The second concerns sound judgment and moderation in conduct. It is one part of justice and calls for balance.

    Some people confuse this character trait with practical philosophy, the branch of learning distinguished from theoretical philosophy. But practical philosophy is also knowledge, and more of it is desirable. It concerns what lies within our choice: how communities are governed, how households are managed, and how character is improved. This is knowledge *about* conduct. Wisdom as part of justice is the character trait itself, not a field of study.

    Moderation, courage, generosity, and wisdom can each be natural or acquired. Some people are born inclined toward them; others naturally incline in the opposite direction. As Ali said: “I have seen reason in two forms, inborn and learned; learned reason cannot help where inborn reason is absent.” We are not commanded to produce gifts of nature. We are responsible for the virtues we can acquire, so that the soul becomes “light upon light.” Practising good habits, avoiding both excess and deficiency, keeping good company, and avoiding corrupt company strengthen natural virtues. Where a natural tendency is weak, practice can bring it nearer to completion.

[^V09-116]: Some accounts list generosity as a separate root of character. The author includes it under the powers of desire and justice.

[^V09-117]: The named traits—courage, chastity, generosity, and the rest—mean the balanced middle points in ordinary moral language.

[^V09-118]: The Arabic line means that both ends of a matter's proper course are blameworthy.

[^V09-119]: There are many possible combinations: strength only in intellect, imagination, or sense; nobility alone; or different degrees of both strength and nobility.

[^V09-120]: Strength and nobility may be joined. For example, courage from strength needs wisdom to guide it.

[^V09-121]: Cowardice means holding back when one should act; recklessness means acting when one should hold back.

[^V09-122]: Self-sufficiency may come from confidence in one's ability, even if misplaced, or from noble trust in God.

[^V09-123]: “What is present” means what the senses can reach. “What is absent” lies beyond them.

[^V09-124]: The discussion is about both the meanings we understand and the things we perceive. It distinguishes a universal nature from its particular instances.

[^V09-125]: A universal nature exists through its particular instances; it does not have a separate material existence beside them.

[^V09-126]: Individual identity can remain through gradual change. A thing's features may change without breaking its continuity as one individual.

[^V09-127]: Abstraction does not mean removing some features and keeping others. It is a change in the level of existence of the thing perceived.

[^V09-128]: A sense-image is higher than the material object as it is perceived by accident, but lower than an imagined image.

[^V09-129]: The intellectual human being is a universal form, present in many particular people. Its “organs” are forms of knowledge and power, not bodily parts.

[^V09-130]: The person's journey toward perfection is the goal of natural development, while the person remains the same individual.

[^V09-131]: When one natural kind changes into another, neither its former individual nor its former kind remains.

[^V09-132]: There is no number of separate levels here, much less an infinite number. So the objection that an infinity would be enclosed between two limits does not arise. There is no actual division between the levels, as we said of the worlds between body and intellect. Keep this in mind.

    A more distant comparison is the outermost heavenly sphere, which was taken to be one continuous body. Its divisions were made by imagination, not found as separate breaks in it. This concerns substances. For quantities, think of one continuous line, such as the axis of that sphere, or of a line imagined as infinite. Think also of time as one continuous movement from God and towards God. We divide a line by imagined points and time by imagined instants, days, hours, minutes, seconds, and so on. The divisions do not make the underlying continuity a multitude of separate things.

[^V09-133]: Up to this point, the person is usually only a natural human being. The useful test is whether the powers of imagination have become established. Young children do not dream at first, because their imagination is still weak. As that power develops, the images they form become mirrors in which they attend to things they have sensed.

    In a similar way, a person remains at the level of the soul until the power to grasp things through thought develops. The person then becomes a human being at the level of intellect. This is especially true when sensed things and imagined pictures become mirrors through which the mind grasps what it can understand without them. The person has then passed beyond shifting states and reached a steadier condition. As the Qur'an says, “Remain upright, as you have been commanded.”

[^V09-134]: If we define a human being through the parts of a definition, the complete definition is a rational animal. “Animal” alone is not enough, and neither is “rational” alone. Likewise, if we describe the parts that exist in the world, a complete human being consists of soul and body.

    The human being considered at the spiritual level is the soul; the natural human being is the body. But the full human being is the union of the two. It might be better to remove the word “here,” since the author's purpose is to show that becoming free from matter does not mean simply throwing the body away. It means the body's existence becomes subtler and more complete. Perhaps, however, “here” is meant to describe the two as united.

    In any event, this discussion challenges those who suppose that a human being is nothing but the soul.

[^V09-135]: The body grows more subtle and unified as the soul develops. The commentator says this finer body is the imaginal body.

[^V09-136]: The soul's direct, essential care is for the imaginal body, not the dead material body. The latter is only a shell.

[^V09-137]: The afterlife body is related to the soul as light is to its source, though the perfected intellect may no longer attend to its body.

[^V09-138]: More precisely, what the perceiver grasps becomes free from matter along with the perceiver, because the two are united in the act of understanding. At both levels, the earth is changed into another earth: dark, material existence gives way to a brighter existence in the form of an image, more complete and more lasting.

    Since what makes a thing what it is lies in its form, the perfection reached at the higher level completes the lower one. Indeed, that higher perfection is the lower one's own perfection. All forms rise until they no longer depend on matter and join the pure forms of the world of images. This means that their separate character is absorbed and disappears there. Souls likewise grow more complete until they join the intellects. Even animal souls join the higher realities governing their kinds and share in their gathering after death, though only as followers.

[^V09-139]: An ascent from one level to a higher one is a change that makes a thing more complete. In this kind of ascent, the earlier level is not taken off and replaced, as happens in a change from one opposed state to another. What falls away is only its deficiency and limitation. The later level retains the perfection of the earlier one and adds something to it. It is a putting-on followed by another putting-on, rather than a taking-off followed by a putting-on.

    Every power present in the natural human being is present more fully in the human being at the level of the soul, and more fully still in the human being at the level of intellect. As discussed earlier, at the highest level those powers are gathered into one, because it belongs to the realm in which things are united. In this sense, God's hand is with the gathered community.

[^V09-140]: The natural human being senses things through bodily organs. The human being at the level of intellect, being more complete and self-sufficient, knows directly through the self. At that level, immediate awareness serves the role of hearing and sight.

    A natural person who attends to one sensation is drawn away from another. At the intellectual level, one act of awareness does not prevent another: a thing is known together with other things. This is one reason for saying that such a person grasps more things, including universal realities. Some earlier philosophers likewise say that God knows a particular thing “in a universal way.” Their point is that He knows the particular together with every other particular, in one timeless act.

    Since intellectual knowledge is direct presence, its vision reaches higher realities free from matter. Through one act of seeing it grasps what belongs to the world of intellect, and what can be seen in the worlds of image and nature. What a lower power sees is included in that higher seeing. The same principle applies to the other senses.

[^V09-141]: The claim applies to souls generally, including the souls of animals and celestial beings.

[^V09-142]: The commentator thinks a few words may be missing from the source passage.

[^V09-143]: Here, “image” means a likeness or representation, not an object of worship.

[^V09-144]: This seeing takes place through the intellect as it rises toward completion, in the bright side of the heart. There the person does not hear the distancing answer, “You shall not see Me.” The Lord of lords speaks otherwise. A person should therefore become good through the full development of practical reason, which guides action, and excellent through the full development of the intellect that knows. More than that, the mountain of the separate self must be broken down, as the mountain of independent existence is broken down in the earlier image. Only then does the person become fully human in actuality.

    The first human being in the world of intellect is a brilliant, piercing light containing every human state—that is, every human perfection. There is nothing astonishing, for God, in gathering a whole world into one being. Yet those perfections exist there in a higher way. This is an example of a figure of speech that appears to qualify praise but actually increases it, as in the saying, “I am the most eloquent of people—though I come from Quraysh.”

    This is the human being whom Plato described when speaking of the divine forms. He distinguished two meanings of *human*: the visible, perishable human being and an enduring human reality that neither decays nor disappears and that cares for its kind. Plato, however, added something to the definition: he said that this enduring human being uses the body. (In manuscripts of the book on the soul that we have seen, the wording “because he added to its definition” is an error.)

    The definition should have stopped at the shining light containing all human perfections in full actuality. The use of the body and its powers involves need and limitation, whereas an intellect fully developed involves perfection and freedom from need. Plato must therefore have meant that the body is used in a secondary way, through the soul, to bring other things to completion—not because the higher human reality needs the body in order to complete itself.

    The rational soul's use of the body, through the truth and for the truth, follows from its connection with that higher reality. It is as though the soul has already taken off the cloak it still appears to wear. This is the point of the statement that follows: when the animal soul becomes a soul of the lower spiritual world, working with imagined and mathematically conceived forms to grasp general truths, the rational soul in full activity comes into being at a higher level. It then gives that lower spiritual soul a nobler life, belonging to the world of intellect. We have already seen that the earlier mode of being of the universal intellect can also be attributed to the soul; this is the basis for speaking of the soul as ancient in time. The same applies to the intellect's later mode of being. This makes the matter clearer. — S. R.

[^V09-145]: The soul's relation to the body is through a flow of life from the higher level, not by leaving its higher home.

[^V09-146]: The rational soul remains connected to the intellectual world while giving life to the lower soul.

[^V09-147]: The senses at the higher and lower levels have different kinds of objects and different ways of knowing.

[^V09-148]: The rule of possibility requires both the highest and the lowest levels to exist, along with every level between them.

[^V09-149]: The human being is a small-scale image of the wider order of existence: its levels connect without gaps.

[^V09-150]: The author follows the older view that different bodily organs express different inner powers. He links anger with the heart, fear with a movement inward, and appetite with changes in the stomach and liver.

[^V09-151]: The soul is not built from separate powers. The powers we see in the body are different expressions of one soul, in a simpler and more unified way.

[^V09-152]: The soul is the source of the body's powers, and in this account a cause contains the perfection of what it causes in a higher, simpler way.

[^V09-153]: “It disappears with it” refers to the first sensed quality, not to the whole body.

[^V09-154]: One undivided inner power gathers the reports of the separate senses and recognizes them together.

[^V09-155]: The body of the afterlife is imaginal and spiritual, while the body of this life is material. The same soul makes each body the body of one person.

[^V09-156]: Cause and effect are connected in their being, not cut off from one another. The soul's unity reflects the source from which it receives its existence.

[^V09-157]: A person can remain the same through continual change because the soul, the stable source of identity, remains.

[^V09-158]: The author means definitions and descriptions. It is not enough to memorize a definition of the Active Intellect—for example, that it needs no matter either to exist or to act, that it has no unrealized capacity waiting to be fulfilled, and that it stands as a universal source in relation to the individual intellects that are already thinking. A person may know all these features so well that the knowledge has become a settled habit, yet still fall short.

    The soul must itself become independent of the body and its powers, let go of narrow personal aims, rise above ends invented by imagination, and grow fuller in its own existence. It must join that higher intellect and endure through its endurance—or, more truly, through God's endurance. The universal intellect is God's word, power, and will. In short, take on the qualities of spiritual beings, and, in the proper sense, the qualities of God.

[^V09-159]: Perhaps Avicenna was simply not in a state to give an answer when the question was put to him. A person is not always in the same condition. As the Persian verse says:

    > At times I sit upon the highest roof;
    > At times I cannot see the ground beneath my feet.

    The fact that different things receive a power in different ways is one matter. But the author is right that the soul's unity is not the unity of a single item counted alongside others. It is a unity that gathers many powers into itself. We could call it a true unity reflected at a lower level.

    Earlier discussions showed that all eight kinds of acting can be found in the soul. Spiritual teachers also speak of seven subtle levels within a person, beginning with nature. In a complete human being, powers that are scattered through different levels of the world come together. When no power is missing, no corresponding act need be missing either.

[^V09-160]: Plants and animals are singled out because their material parts are continually breaking down and being replaced. This is especially evident in animals. In the medical account being used here, the causes include several kinds of heat: heat linked to the elements, to the body's own constitution, to the heavens, and to bodily and inward movements. The movement of breath and blood outward or inward during anger, joy, fear, sorrow, and similar states is discussed elsewhere.

    But the difficulty disappears if we attend to the unchanging aspect of each thing that points toward God. A thing's existence is what gives it stability, unity, and its own identity. It is like a fixed place for washing beside water that never stops flowing.

[^V09-161]: Perhaps the point is that animals are aware of themselves only together with what they sense or imagine. They do not know themselves in a pure way, free from such images. In that respect their awareness is no higher than that of a person who wrongly imagines the self to be located in space and time. As the verse says:

    > While you imagine yourself in front and behind,
    > You are bound to the body and deprived of the soul.
    > Above, below, before, behind belong to the body;
    > The bright reality of the soul has no direction.

    By “instruments,” the author means the animal power of judgment and the brain. By the “shadows” among which an animal shares awareness, he means the images in its inner powers of perception. It grasps an image in common with other images without clearly distinguishing one from another, rather as a person may recognize something under a general description without seeing what makes it particular.

[^V09-162]: The “shared awareness” among inner images may refer to the inner power that gathers them into one animal experience.

[^V09-163]: The source means “this fear” in particular, rather than fear as a general idea.

[^V09-164]: Fear, love, and anger have no size and cannot be divided into pieces. The commentator asks how such states could belong to an extended body.

[^V09-165]: Calling the soul an “intellect” when it has intellectual forms may name the subject by a quality it has gained.

[^V09-166]: Avicenna argues that separating an attribute from matter shows that the soul itself is free of matter.

[^V09-167]: The claim is not that the body becomes immaterial. Rather, the soul's higher and lower levels remain joined by a real connection.

[^V09-168]: A plant kind can take in more matter as it grows; the commentators explain that one form may extend across several portions of matter.

[^V09-169]: The objection is that a single plant form cannot belong to one fixed portion of matter if the matter beneath it keeps changing.

[^V09-170]: One proposed stable element is the original matter formed from the parents.

[^V09-171]: This is not simply a repetition. The question is whether an original part remains in a plant even though the senses cannot distinguish original parts from later additions. A pause in growth may also matter to Avicenna's argument.

[^V09-172]: The power above the changing natural powers is ultimately God, who sustains time.

[^V09-173]: This refers back to Avicenna's claim that one form determines one portion of matter.

[^V09-174]: A changing thing can remain one through time by continuing without a break. This is a kind of persistence through time.

[^V09-175]: Avicenna may mean that the soul knows basic first principles. The commentator says this alone is too little to explain the soul's full life after death.

[^V09-176]: Do not miss this point. Something that exists at the level of intellect does not have a firm place there until it has become a complete intellectual reality. It reaches that state by acquiring the distinguishing forms that complete its intellectual nature. Knowing the first, basic truths—whether simple ideas or judgments—is like charcoal becoming warm. Gaining further truths through reasoning is like the charcoal changing into fire. The undeveloped soul is like charcoal; a soul that has become intellect in full is like a fire that is nothing but fire. Charcoal does not become fire merely because it has grown hot. In the same way, a human soul does not become an actual intellect merely by possessing the first truths.

    It may have reached its highest development in imagination while remaining only close to becoming an intellect in full. It has not yet become established in the intellectual world. Consider this carefully.

[^V09-177]: Avicenna thought celestial souls become complete by repeatedly knowing higher beings. Sadra adds that they also grow in their own being.

[^V09-178]: This means the simple knowledge of the Necessary Being. The philosophers in Aristotle’s school call God's knowledge “intellect” or “understanding” to distinguish it from the other kinds of knowing: sensation, imagination, and the animal power of judgment.

    Avicenna did not, however, reach the deeper meaning of simple intellect. A reality wholly without parts contains all beings in a higher and more complete way. Its knowledge is direct presence and comes before the things known. Every existence is present through its own presence, and every nature is present through the divine names and qualities from which it necessarily follows.

    Avicenna's most developed claim is that the forms arise from God's own being and depend on Him as effects, not as qualities lodged within a subject. They do not come to Him as our mental representations are said to come to the soul from the Active Intellect, with the help of things perceived in the natural world. In this older account, the senses help prepare us for knowledge: one who lacks a sense lacks the knowledge normally gained through it.

[^V09-179]: The order of causes and effects is not a sequence in time. The commentator says they are all present together in one direct act of knowledge.

[^V09-180]: If God is the source of intellectual forms, He cannot lack the perfection they express.

[^V09-181]: True simplicity means containing every kind of being without parts or any lack.

[^V09-182]: These intellectual forms are not separate beings of that kind. They remain through God's own abiding existence, not merely because He keeps them in existence. They live through His life, not merely because He gives them life. In this sense, whoever sees them sees God. They have none of the matter, movement, or other features that mark ordinary things as separate. Even the rules that govern the distinction between one thing and another fall away at this level.

    Avicenna should have removed separation, independence, and division from these forms by understanding them as knowledge immediately present to God. If this is true of existences that still admit of some distinction, how much more is it true of intellectual forms that are free from matter? The answer cannot be to replace direct knowledge with the very separation that never existed and never will.

[^V09-183]: Sadra's point is that Avicenna did not always give each matter the attention it deserved. He should, in Sadra's view, have devoted more of his time to the highest knowledge and to seeking the truth. That does not mean that mastering many branches of learning is without value. Broad knowledge is one of the highest achievements and helps students who need guidance. Sadra thinks that if Avicenna had devoted most of his attention to knowledge of God, he would have avoided some serious mistakes in philosophy. But we should be careful about the examples offered. When Avicenna does not identify one permanent reality in plants and animals, this need not mean that he has forgotten what existence itself is, or that he thinks its different levels have nothing in common.

    He does say that a cause and its effect are related in the kind of existence they have, and that existence is one reality. His *Discussions* and other writings bear this out. In *The Divine Witnesses*, Sadra himself explains that the Aristotle’s followers' apparent claim that existences differ concerns the essences described by those existences, not existence in its own right.

[^V09-184]: The note explains a few difficult words in Avicenna's letter: “fate,” “claws,” “curtain,” and “flash of light.”

[^V09-185]: Knowledge is one with what is known directly. Even when the object is known through a form, its essence and its existence are not wholly separated in that knowledge: what makes the knowledge of the sun different from knowledge of the moon is also what makes both instances of knowledge alike. In this sense, knowledge of the sun is knowledge shaped by the sun, and knowledge of the moon by the moon. Awareness of existence can be direct or gained through ideas. Direct awareness may be general or detailed. The first is like the holy soul's awareness of its own existence, as understood here and by Suhrawardi. The second is like the awareness of someone who has become absorbed in the reality he knows; the highest case is God's knowledge of Himself.

    Knowing existence through ideas also takes two forms. One studies its different levels by examining the essences of things, their defining features, and the qualities they may or must possess. The other knows existence through general descriptions that truly apply to it: that it overcomes nonexistence, gives things their unity and identity, is the light of reality, and is the life, love, knowledge, and awareness running through all things. Thinkers differ in how fully they understand existence by these paths. Existence is, after all, the subject of the highest philosophy.

[^V09-186]: Pleasure depends on awareness, the power that perceives, and what is perceived. The more complete each is, the greater the pleasure.

[^V09-187]: The First Reality sustains the intellect. When the soul turns inward, it may discover a beauty that was already present within it.

[^V09-188]: The commentator assumes that lack of knowledge is one obstacle, then focuses on the distractions of the body.

[^V09-189]: Learning about intellectual realities prepares the soul for direct awareness of them; death removes the body's distractions.

[^V09-190]: Because the soul is linked with the body, people imagine themselves to occupy a low place in space and to belong to a particular date. Yet the soul in its own nature is free from spatial directions and times. On the view of philosophers who reject separate Platonic forms, even universal intellectual ideas have no location, time, or direction. How much more must this be true of the soul that forms them?

    Intellectual universals and even particular images seem weak to us partly because we treat them as mirrors in which to look at things outside us, rather than attending to the images themselves. If the soul made them its direct objects, they would grow stronger, as they do in the life to come. Those who seek universal knowledge should use particular images to reach universal meanings.

    Without these distractions, the soul could recognize that every universal has a unity that gathers many things together and a luminous presence embracing them. Its finer expressions, and the still finer expressions of those expressions, include the individual things of the worlds of image and nature. They are its lights and rays. In it there is multiplicity within unity and unity within multiplicity.

[^V09-191]: The commentator quotes a Persian verse: while the soul is tied to the body, it cannot clearly see its beloved; when the tie ends, the veil is lifted.

[^V09-192]: The soul's theoretical side reaches pure intellectual truths; its practical side reaches justice and balanced conduct.

[^V09-193]: The expression means “ox hunger.” The foreign term *fān-būlī-mūs* refers to great hunger: one part of the name means something large, like an ox, and another means hunger. In Persian, a very large thing may be likened to an ox.

    The old medical explanation is that extreme cold affects the opening of the stomach. The stomach then loses its sense of touch and no longer notices the feeling that, in that account, black bile produces to signal hunger. Its power to draw food in is also lost, to the point that the person cannot swallow a mouthful. The stomach appears satisfied, while the other organs are desperately hungry because no nourishment reaches them. The same cold is said to spread from the stomach to the organ of taste and make that sense numb. This is a description from the medical theory of the time.

[^V09-194]: The point is not that imagining something strongly enough will make it appear as a real form in the next life. If that were so, everyone who enjoys comfort in this world and imagines fine food, elegant clothing, beautiful spouses, and similar pleasures would meet those very things there. Such an idea is unworthy of the author's careful teaching, as his explanations already make clear and will show again.

    Rather, good actions, the regular practice of them, the qualities of character they establish, and the fitting images through which the religious tradition describes them lead to corresponding forms in the next life. A person may be gathered with beautiful companions and pleasures suited to those actions. The same principle applies to forms of punishment. Greed, and the objects a greedy person constantly imagines, can take the form of ants with which that person is gathered. Someone who harms other Muslims, and whose thoughts are filled with ways of causing harm, may encounter that character as snakes and scorpions.

    The Qur'an says that those who wrongfully consume orphans' property are really consuming fire in their bellies. Here they consume pleasant things by unlawful means; there the same act appears as eating fire.

    Something similar happens in dreams, which resemble death in one respect. Years of plenty may appear as fat cows and years of famine as thin cows. Knowledge can appear as milk, and happiness as greenery. Each action has a meaningful, necessary connection to the form in which it appears. In the next life, people will encounter these forms as realities shaped by what they did and imagined. “People are asleep; when they die, they awaken” and discover the meanings of what they saw in this dream of earthly life. — S. R.

[^V09-195]: Details about speech, hearing, and sight could be added or left out without changing the minimum knowledge required here.

[^V09-196]: Knowing God as simple, complete being and as the source of all forms is a higher level of understanding.

[^V09-197]: Direct knowledge of God's essence, including prior knowledge of all things, is also a higher attainment, not the minimum requirement.

[^V09-198]: Direct union with, or awareness of, intellectual universals is a high attainment. To know the whole order of existence can be to see God.

[^V09-199]: The commentator says Sadra is speaking about himself, and that his arguments and conclusions support his claim.

[^V09-200]: A thing's nature determines what kind of knowledge and form it can receive. Sugar is suited to sweetness, barley matter to barley's form, and a sparrow to the form of a sparrow. The commentator says this fit comes from the thing's own nature, not from an outside choice; “eternity” here does not mean a moment in time.

[^V09-201]: The note points to the second group's desire for status and leadership, and to the way desire and cunning can take control of a person.

[^V09-202]: The “signs” are especially the great signs that lead people to God. The note says that when people turn away from them, they also lose sight of themselves.

[^V09-203]: The first group's barrier is natural, like that of animals without speech; their hearts are sealed by their nature.

[^V09-204]: “Companions of the Fire” means people who stay with or belong to it, as people may be called companions of the world when they live in it and keep to it.

[^V09-205]: “From eternity” refers to the timeless order of things, not to a point in time.

[^V09-206]: Here, “formal knowledge” means knowledge about rules and how to act.

[^V09-207]: The two kinds are misery from being veiled from truth and misery from punishment. They can also be described as not knowing and as holding false beliefs. The first is a lack of knowledge, even before a person sins; the second is made worse by wrongdoing.

[^V09-208]: This points to the way actions and words take on fitting forms in the afterlife.

[^V09-209]: The first group's misery does not come from losing the highest joy, since these souls lack the desire and readiness for higher knowledge. It comes from losing the particular worldly things they have grown used to.

[^V09-210]: The note stresses that false certainty is especially damaging. It says the soul, naturally made for truth, can become filled with errors and lies.

[^V09-211]: The note says these people may already have blessings and worldly power, yet remain veiled from truth because they settle for general religious beliefs instead of seeking deeper understanding.

[^V09-212]: The note gives examples of the deeper aims of religious practices: prayer can bring the soul close to God, fasting can clear the heart, charity can teach generosity, and pilgrimage can loosen worldly ties.

[^V09-213]: Categories and rules can themselves become a barrier. For example, someone may assume that every quality must belong to something else, and so fail to see that the soul can be life and knowledge in its own right. The note calls this kind of learning a major veil.

[^V09-214]: The “first principles” are the starting points of a proof; their arrangement is its structure. The note connects this to the Qur'anic image of a just balance.

[^V09-215]: The paradise described here includes the soul's awareness of God's essence, qualities, and creative acts.

[^V09-216]: Reaching the soul's goal means more than having an external connection to it. In this account, the soul is transformed into what it seeks through its development. The Active Intellect has existence in itself and also a presence through which the soul is completed.

[^V09-217]: The Active Intellect's existence includes the lower beings that depend on it. Its connection to the soul comes later in time, but it is prior in the timeless order. The commentator compares this to an image in a mirror that grows clearer until it no longer depends on the mirror and disappears into its source; the source itself does not change.

[^V09-218]: The Active Intellect can be understood as the storehouse of the soul's knowledge by comparing knowing with forgetting. The note compares this with imagination preserving what the common sense receives, and memory preserving what inner judgment has reached.

[^V09-219]: The Qur'anic verse does not say directly that an angel is the source of knowledge. The commentator says the argument depends on the idea that created things receive from God through a fitting intermediary, and that angels serve as such intermediaries.

[^V09-220]: A universal idea is a general description that can be said of many people. The Active Intellect is more than a general description: it is a real source that contains and supports the individuals who share that nature.

[^V09-221]: To say that the soul and what it understands are united does not mean that their definitions are the same. It means that the intellectual form exists through the soul, and the soul's existence is at once life, intellect, knower, and known.

[^V09-222]: The Anqa's thousand wings and beaks stand for the unending flow of God's gifts. In each prophet, saint, sage, and fully developed person, its two wings represent theoretical and practical understanding, or knowledge and power. Its tongue speaks through them all, as God's tongue. The note quotes Persian verse: “His voice is heard through every throat, / though it comes from Abdullah's own.”

[^V09-223]: Mount Qaf is the mountain of power when the Anqa is viewed as coming from above. It is the Qaf of the heart when the bird is seen appearing within receptive hearts, through God's care.

[^V09-224]: The bird's echo is the actual knowledge of intellects and of a fully developed human being.

[^V09-225]: A physician's treatment may begin with inspiration or instruction from God.

[^V09-226]: The bird's wings represent the powers and natural qualities that give rise to the specific forms of things in the elements and the material world.

[^V09-227]: The bird's “flight” represents the Active Intellect's relation to other intellects, or their ascent and descent as they understand universal truths and connect with it.

[^V09-228]: The bird's knowledge includes the knowledge of all who know God, because they receive it through their connection to it. The people of truth share one essential set of beliefs. This is why the Imam is said to know all languages and the songs of birds.

[^V09-229]: The fire mentioned here is the fire of nature, and the water crossed is the sea of this world, sometimes called the burning sea. This concerns the Anqa. The Simurgh, ruler of the heavenly birds, is different: it represents the universal intellect, or existence flowing through all things as God's light.

    Farid al-Din Attar speaks of the Simurgh in these lines, here given in their sense:

    > At the beginning, the Simurgh showed a wondrous glimpse of itself,
    > Passing over China in the middle of the night.
    > One feather fell there, and the whole world was stirred.
    > Each person took an image from that feather;
    > Whoever saw the image was filled with longing.
    > All the works of creation shine by its splendour;
    > Every image is an image from its feather.
    > If the feather's image had not appeared,
    > All this commotion would never have arisen.
    > That feather is now in the picture-house of China:
    > This is the meaning of “Seek knowledge, even in China.”

    The bird appearing in China stands for the universal intellect. China stands for the universal heavenly soul, at the farthest eastern reach of the spiritual world. Night is the descending order of existence, the reality expressed by the Night of Power. The fallen feather is a reflection or shadow of the intellect; the kingdoms are the turning heavens; and the image each person receives is the particular intellect.

    Attar also says that we have a king beyond Mount Qaf, named the Simurgh, king of birds: “He is close to us, and we are far from him.” Those lines point more clearly to existence flowing through all things.

[^V09-230]: Here, “what is present to the soul” means its good or bad traits and habits.

[^V09-231]: One version of the text reads “clustered fruit” here.

[^V09-232]: Avicenna compares the soul's connection to this body to a mirror's connection to images: the body lets familiar images appear to the soul.

[^V09-233]: Avicenna holds that matter and form always go together. He does not accept forms that exist independently of matter.

[^V09-234]: The note describes the old medical view that the soul is connected to the body through subtle vapors and the body's vital spirit, which flows through the arteries, nerves, and veins, especially in the heart, brain, and liver.

[^V09-235]: Seeing happens when an image appears in the inner sense called the *phantasia*, which works like a two-sided mirror. An image may come from the outer senses or arise within. The inner sense receives it, and imagination keeps it. The images coming from within may be reflections of spiritual realities or forms assembled by imagination from what it has stored.

[^V09-236]: The note argues that an image gives pleasure because it appears to the soul, not because it exists in matter outside us. A beautiful image in matter exists for that matter, not for us. If a child dies or leaves, the outside event does not by itself cause our pain; the image we form of it does. The note alludes to the story of Khidr and Moses.

[^V09-237]: The “market” is the good habits formed by repeating good actions. Each habit is a real counterpart to a particular subtle form.

[^V09-238]: The next life's images come into being through direct creation, while things here are formed from matter.

[^V09-239]: The note refers to the creation of likenesses and forms in the next life, and to Qur'anic statements about things we do not know. These, the author argues, point beyond the materialist account.

[^V09-240]: The peaceful soul returns to God; other souls draw near to Him. For those who are far from Him, nearness reveals His power and majesty.

[^V09-241]: The author repeats an old medical account in which semen forms from vapors rising through the body and gathering at its extremities. The note says this account was used to explain why cutting veins behind the ears was thought to affect fertility.

[^V09-242]: These are rhetorical arguments about God's power, not strict demonstrations.

[^V09-243]: The question of how parts join or separate is obvious and needs no proof. Materialists ascribe it to natural powers, while Ash'arite theologians ascribe it to God alone. The author argues that natural powers are themselves fields of divine action; the real issue is the goal toward which beings move and the realm to which they return.

[^V09-244]: The next realm is not beside this world; it stands above it in the order of existence. Until we leave the veils of the natural body, we are not yet in the afterlife. The note compares this transition to a chick emerging from its egg.

[^V09-245]: If nothing continually sustained composite things, their parts would separate as the elements moved toward their natural places. Earlier, the author argued that God must hold them together against those tendencies.

[^V09-246]: “Bodies” here includes both natural bodies and the imaginal bodies of the afterlife. Natural bodies are like graves or coverings for imaginal forms and souls. A Persian couplet says that when Israfil blows his trumpet, the dead souls rise.

[^V09-247]: Animals can be raised by following human beings in two ways: all the things attached to a person may follow them, or perfected animal forms may return to the imaginal realm while the soul returns to a luminous, intellectual form. In either case, they return to God in their own way.

[^V09-248]: This means that they follow the human journey in its vertical order of stages.

[^V09-249]: The verses include those in *The Event*, “Is the One who created the heavens and earth not able to create others like them?”, and “Who will give life to these bones when they have decayed? Say: The One who brought them into being the first time.” The note says the first creation came directly from the active cause, without pre-existing matter, and so can resurrection.

[^V09-250]: The author will take up these objections one at a time and answer them later.

[^V09-251]: That is, more fitting to God's own nature.

[^V09-252]: The whole natural world is created. Matter, time, and space do not themselves depend on earlier matter, time, or space; every kind of thing born within the world is created.

[^V09-253]: The imaginal realm is timelessly prior to the material world; the intellectual realm is prior in its own order and in timeless rank. For those nearest to God, it is the highest goal.

[^V09-254]: The imaginal realm comes later only in relation to our own existence.

[^V09-255]: This differs from the view of some religious scholars, who say that paradise and hell will not be created until the final resurrection.

[^V09-256]: There are two blasts: one puts out a fire and the other lights one. Our natural bodies are like pieces of wood burning with a fire that is God's light. At the great resurrection this fire is extinguished all at once, while other pieces of wood—pure forms belonging to the next life—are lit by the souls to which they belong. Those forms become the bodies of that life.

    Look at the order of things from beginning to end. At one time the lights that govern souls are joined to material bodies; at another they are joined to the pure forms of the next life. In a limited way, this resembles our present experience: while awake, our souls are attached to natural bodies, but in sleep they appear through bodies of images.
