Scripture says that God takes souls at death, and also those that have not died during their sleep; He keeps those for whom death is decreed and sends the others back.

    To understand the point, consider where human beings—and indeed all animals—come from. Their bodily material goes back to the elements. Those elements have no life, knowledge, power, or other perfection of their own. Such qualities arrive in them as gifts held for a time, and one day those gifts must be returned. While looking at a living body in all its splendour, remember the same materials when they are lifeless and disfigured. That contrast shows where its passing bodily life begins.

[^V09-257]: Naturalists recognize passive and natural powers but miss the active causes that act through them. They also fail to recognize imaginal souls and separate intellects. Materialists who appeal to time as the cause of life, death, union, and separation likewise fail to recognize the immaterial self within them or God beyond them.

[^V09-258]: The author calls the view that affirms both kinds of resurrection the soundest. A human being is body and soul, and each has its fitting completion and reward. A purely spiritual afterlife would leave most people without a suitable goal; a purely bodily afterlife would fail to account for the highest souls.

[^V09-259]: “This body” means the imaginal body of the next life. The same person persists through the form, not through the same matter. The soul gives the body its individuality, and its own individuality is grounded in its real existence and unity.

[^V09-260]: This objection assumes that a person is identified by the body's matter and is restored by gathering the same material pieces. If identity belongs to the body's form, material from one body can become part of another body without the person's form transferring. Food becomes part of the eater's body, but the food's form does not. Every form remains itself for its own time; it does not turn into another form. A body, as a body, does not become food for something else. Matter is called the matter of a body only because it is joined to that body's form.

[^V09-261]: The note rejects the idea that a person's identity lies in their matter. It belongs to their form. A person has an inner form—the soul—and an outer bodily form. Both remain in the next life, so the person's identity is preserved.

[^V09-262]: The point is whether the human being survives, not whether an abstract idea of “body” survives. A body as a general kind exists only through real, particular bodies. The note discusses the claim that a body's continuity may remain the same even as it is divided, but rejects this as incomplete: the body's basic form and connection come before its other kinds of continuity.

[^V09-263]: This objection applies even if there are souls besides the one that belonged to the body. If an infinite number of souls existed, they would crowd and block one another, since a limited world cannot hold an unlimited number. Theologians who say that souls are finite do not face this particular problem.

[^V09-264]: A proverb says: “Look to the goals; leave the beginnings.”

[^V09-265]: According to Plato's followers, every kind of thing, even water, earth, air, fire, gold, silver, and other minerals, has an intellectual form. This makes all beings copies of those forms. The note says it is a serious weakness that they do not allow for a closer, subtle version of these forms in the world between matter and intellect.

[^V09-266]: The difference between the two interpretations is clear. In the first, the intellectual realities are the same kind of thing as their subtle counterparts, differing only in how broad or limited they are. In the second, they are inner states and qualities of the soul, different in meaning from outside things, like the difference between greenness and flowing water. The first view treats them as real forms; the second treats them as results or meanings that these forms bring about.

[^V09-267]: The note says this argument completes the account of pleasure, not pain. An effect like poison may become a noble, bright intellectual experience when it is understood. The intellectual world is a world of beauty, truth, and clarity; this reading returns to the first interpretation, though the commentator notes that it has problems.

[^V09-268]: The commentator asks how a bodily effect, such as touch, could become an intellectual experience while remaining a touch. This could make sense only if “touch” means intellectual awareness, and the intellectual form is like the bodily form of touch.

[^V09-269]: The note says the lover had many beloved things: women, children, gold, silver, fine horses, and other worldly goods.

[^V09-270]: “Imaginal” here does not mean a weak picture that has no reality. The author is careful to say that the afterlife forms are not merely in human imagination. They have real existence in the imaginal world.

[^V09-271]: This is especially clear in the view that mirror images belong to the imaginal world. An afterlife image is like a mirror image, but it exists on its own, without a mirror, and is alive through its connection to the soul.

[^V09-272]: In sleep and similar states, the soul's power to perceive is also its power to create. The sky, ground, people, and other things seen in a dream are made by the soul without matter and are directly present to it.

[^V09-273]: The word here is a broken plural. The note says the soul's world can include many levels: the two worlds, their joined whole, and a still wider realm beyond them.

[^V09-274]: In *The Healing*, Avicenna says that the true religious law has already explained bodily resurrection, while he will explain spiritual resurrection himself.

[^V09-275]: This view is better because it does not reduce a human being to bones, muscles, veins, and flesh. It allows for an immaterial spirit as well. But its supporters have not fully explained what the returning bodies are, what they are like, or where they exist, and they have not explained the final state of the higher souls.

[^V09-276]: The Torah mostly contains laws and teachings about conduct, with little about God's oneness, names, qualities, or the afterlife. The Qur'an treats these subjects in much greater detail. The note suggests that the Torah's time in paradise may refer to the period when believers enjoy bodily pleasures before moving into a spiritual life. The Gospel's account may describe the spiritual afterlife of the righteous and those close to God.

[^V09-277]: Each person returns alone, as an individual apart from matter and its attachments. For some, this means being alone apart from their nature and its qualities as well. A Persian verse says: “Yesterday, the day before, and today—all four become one when you become one.”

[^V09-278]: This refers to souls who have completed their practical development but not their intellectual development. They reach an intermediate world, unlike those who have completed both forms of growth and hold the highest rank.

[^V09-279]: Trees, rivers, rooms, the sky, the earth, the speaker, and the listener seen in dreams or sudden visions are all forms of awareness, strong or weak.

[^V09-280]: The first two principles show that what returns after death is the individual's own particular existence. Since existence is what makes a person this person, identity remains even when many features and forms change. Distinguishing features may change as different expressions of one continuing individual.

[^V09-281]: The third principle works together with the fourth. Strength and weakness, and the gradual movement from one to the other, are not separate kinds of existence. The same existence can grow from something hardly noticeable into a simple intellect and an active intellect. The more complete stage remains the very existence that was less complete, just as the dust can become, by stages, the Lord of all worlds.

[^V09-282]: Saying that a lower level of existence is “base” does not mean existence is evil. Existence is good and noble. Here, “base” means a lower and less complete level, not the absence of existence.

[^V09-283]: The unity of one continuous motion is its personal unity. It is one as a whole, although it has points and stages along the distance it covers. Its continuity is simple; change and multiplicity come from its relation to these points and stages.

[^V09-284]: Some of these principles are necessary for the argument, while others help establish it. This one is especially important. “Form” here includes a thing's kind, bodily form, arrangement, or whatever makes it actual, such as the soul that makes an animal an animal.

[^V09-285]: If the composite is what it is through its form and final difference, one might wonder why definitions of composites list many broader kinds and differences. The author distinguishes two tasks: showing how one thing contains many parts, and showing how many parts form one thing. Detailed definitions serve the first task. His account serves the second: once a thing is complete, the features named in its definition follow from its form rather than existing separately from it.

[^V09-286]: This returns to the earlier claim that a defining feature is a way of existing. It also refers to the author's choice among four accounts of how a thing's inner conceptual parts are combined.

[^V09-287]: “This” refers to the meaning of Avicenna's claim. The note asks how defining something by features that follow from it can compare with defining it by the parts and causes that make it what it is. The reply is that features that do not come later than the thing in existence disclose its inner reality. On the view that existence is primary, all essences are drawn from existence itself; essence and existence are united rather than joined as separate parts.

[^V09-288]: This, too, is a necessary principle. The soul is one, yet it contains many powers and has a kind of unity through them, just as it has multiplicity within its unity. It is a wonder among beings, and its unity a wonder among kinds of unity: it is one while containing all multiplicities and worlds. “The greater world is folded within you.” Everything sensed, imagined, supposed, or understood—including pleasures and pains, actions and traits, and particular and general meanings—is a state or aspect of the soul. The many things are really concepts drawn from the levels of its existence. The soul has a reflected, dependent unity; God's unity is real and not numerical. The note ends with a Persian verse: “How did the shadow stretch? It is the pattern of the saints, showing the light of God's sun.”

[^V09-289]: The soul's rising and descending through these levels does not mean it moves from one physical place to another. In its simple intellective reality it is in the world beyond nature and in timeless duration; in its natural body it is in the material world and in time.

[^V09-290]: The soul and intellect are therefore distinct kinds of substance within the five traditional categories of substance.

[^V09-291]: Saying that individuation comes from existence does not by itself explain the body. The body is individuated through the soul, while the soul is individuated through its existence. Not all philosophers held that individuation comes from existence, but many accepted that the body is individuated through the soul; this helps explain how the resurrected body and the body in this life can retain one identity.

[^V09-292]: The answer also relies on the claim that the soul preserves the body's identity.

[^V09-293]: The imaginative power works together with the common sense, which perceives. Imagination stores and preserves the forms it perceives.

[^V09-294]: The point is that different forms can arise from the active cause rather than from differences in the matter receiving them. For example, images of a person, horse, or tree reflected in water differ because of the reflecting causes, even though the water is one kind of matter. Likewise, kinds of things can arise from different aspects of their active causes. In some philosophical accounts, intellectual beings are the active causes of natural kinds; in others, those kinds arise from divine ideas. Each form is a creative divine word: “Be!” The things corresponding to those forms exist through God's necessary existence, while created forms exist through possible existence.

[^V09-295]: “The power that forms images” means the common sense considered from its inward side. As explained earlier, it is like a mirror with two faces: it perceives forms from imagination and forms assembled by the image-making power, while imagination preserves them. “Image-making power” can also mean imagination or, more commonly, one of the four principal plant powers.

[^V09-296]: Imagined forms can serve as mirrors through which we look at something else. When the mind attends to the sun outside us—its brightness, eclipse, or other features—the imagined image is only a reflected appearance of that physical sun. When the image itself becomes the object of awareness, however, it is a real being in its own right, more complete because it is free of matter. It is like an immaterial sun: a universal intellectual reality that contains all its limited, passing instances. The universal concept of the sun is a light that lets the intellect know all the individual suns. While the soul is busy with individual things and its body and powers draw its attention to desirable objects, the universal concept may seem weak. But its weakness is not part of its own nature. The same is true of an imaginal form.

[^V09-297]: Unlike the intellectual mode, which is universal and free of matter, the individual mode consists of measured imaginal forms. In the primordial world, these are the forms of the covenant.

[^V09-298]: “Clay” can mean a thing's essence, the settled qualities that shape the rational soul, or the matter of the body. For the prophets, the virtuous and their followers, these meanings refer to their complete human nature, their good qualities and habits, and the balanced bodily mixture formed in pure lineages and wombs. For hypocrites and their followers, the contrasts are a merely potential humanity, blameworthy qualities and habits, and an unbalanced bodily mixture.

[^V09-299]: This means whether their bodies are imaginal or physical.

[^V09-300]: That is, when the person's substantial development, together with the changing qualities that follow it, reaches its full course during worldly life.

[^V09-301]: The word for “nurse” is Persian. The commentator explains the image: nature is the mother; plant and animal life are its two breasts. Because the human body is delicate, the elements cannot nourish it without being changed by nature and, at times, by human craft. Just as a baby needs its mother's milk rather than adult food, the bodily human being receives nourishment through plant and animal life. The spiritual human being also needs knowledge from two sources—the theoretical and practical intellect—as nourishment for growth and completion.

[^V09-302]: The commentator suggests that the text may have read, “You already know…” without labeling the passage “the eleventh principle.”

[^V09-303]: “Imaginal” here means a real, distinct form in the world of images, not merely a separate body made of different matter. The author says that existence, individuality, soul, and form are truly what they are, even as they become stronger and more complete. A stronger stage is the same individual as the weaker stage; it does not become a second individual or kind. In the next life, only limitations and defects are removed. Pure capacity, which is not yet an actual feature of a person, falls away. Otherwise, the form would remain worldly rather than becoming an afterlife form.

[^V09-304]: In the language of revelation, resurrection means arrival at one's final ends; in the language of philosophy, it means reaching those ends. “Resurrection” is used in scripture for arrival at ends beyond what the senses can perceive. The soul's imaginal form and change of meaning are part of this movement.

[^V09-305]: The final end gathers together the ends of all things. God has beautiful names that guide each kind and its proper end; all the levels of the worlds express His power and knowledge. The ends of people devoted to inner meaning and of people devoted to outward form are both with Him: He has a place of welcome for some and a place of punishment for others.

[^V09-306]: The spiritually advanced have already reached their end through the strength of their awareness of God. Their simple intellect and universal divine soul belong to the world beyond nature even while their bodies remain in the material world. Their true time is timeless duration, and what is fixed in relation to what is fixed belongs to eternity. When the body's covering is removed, this state appears fully. This is like the report that Ali said, “By the Lord of the Kaaba, I have won,” and also said, “If the veil were lifted, my certainty would not increase.” The same applies to the passage about driving the guilty onward.

[^V09-307]: If “animals” means nonhuman animals, their resurrection is dependent on the human one; paradise contains birds and other creatures that souls desire. If it means people who are like sheep and gazelles in their ignorance, then their gathering is direct. Likewise, “stars” may mean low-growing plants, and “trees” plants with trunks; or both may refer to people whose life has remained at the level of plant life, occupied only with taking in and expelling, and deeply attached to plants.

[^V09-308]: The shape of food does not matter to nourishment. Bread becomes part of the person who eats it, but it first changes through several stages into pure blood. Its roundness, length, or other shape does not become part of the eater. If a form itself became food, it would no longer be that form while serving as food.

[^V09-309]: The increase in size comes through expansion: the amount grows without adding more basic material parts. This is what the commentator calls true expansion.

[^V09-310]: The first answer is based on proof. The second is only a debate within the assumption that the resurrected body must be a natural body of finite size. Even if the spiritually advanced are no longer connected to bodies, the number of other souls is still unlimited. God's giving is without end, whether for those brought near, those on the right, those on the left, or those between them. The infinite cannot be measured by the finite.

[^V09-311]: A complete world contains everything necessary to its existence and its consequences. If it were made from the matter of this natural world, it would compete with other forms for that matter and depend on them, so it would not be complete. If it occupied a place and position here, it would be surrounded by the world's places and directions, and depend on them. It would not be self-sufficient. The next life is not alongside this world in the same time sequence; its mode is timeless. Those who ask when the Hour will come do not understand the answer “Its knowledge is with my Lord,” because they have not entered the divine presence. The final ends of the worlds are within this world in depth, as stations of truth before the Sovereign, especially in the presence of God and the realm of His names and attributes. The next world contains forms of everything, including places, times, positions, and directions, but they belong to its own mode of existence.

[^V09-312]: This is like the verse, “My Lord, send me back, so that I may do good.” Another way a person can speak with God is by becoming a stronger expression of His qualities and receiving His creative words more fully.

[^V09-313]: The motion and change that produce the soul are those in which each later stage gathers the perfections of the earlier one and adds something more. It is not the mere exchange of one bodily garment for another, nor a downward movement of matter. Matter has descended to its lowest form; how could the rational soul remain naturally connected to it so that it prevented a new soul from arising, as the argument claims?

[^V09-314]: “Like a heavenly body” refers to its delicacy, clarity, luminosity, and balanced mixture. When opposites are balanced, each is present in a way that resembles its absence. Because of these similarities, one term can stand for the other: the life-giving spirit may be called a heaven, and the heavens may be called vapor or smoke, as in the verse: “Then He turned toward the heaven while it was smoke.”

[^V09-315]: The soul is what makes a person an individual. If old Zayd is the same as young Zayd because the soul preserves their identity, he is also the same person as the fetus. The resurrected person is therefore the very one who obeyed or disobeyed.

[^V09-316]: The commentator says that resurrection restores the human form a person had in the first life, whereas transmigration would give the soul an animal form.

[^V09-317]: If the soul alone individuates a person, as that account says, but resurrection restores the first individual while transmigration makes another, the account seems to require an additional feature—such as the new and old forms matching—to explain identity.

[^V09-318]: The same soul also remains through transmigration, so on this account it would preserve individuality there as well.

[^V09-319]: The commentator says this is especially strange for two reasons: Ghazali says the returning body shares no parts with the first body, even though he distinguishes resurrection as the return of the first person; and he allows a kind of transmigration while strongly condemning it elsewhere.

[^V09-320]: This is the view of those who believe in transmigration: after a soul leaves its body, it enters, for example, an embryo in an animal's womb.

[^V09-321]: Here, “this” refers to the alleged impossibility of transmigration.

[^V09-322]: The idea that a heavenly body becomes the setting for a soul's imagination does not mean the soul becomes its soul. The commentator says that Ghazali's reading of Avicenna suggests uncertainty about transmigration because he thinks the heavenly body becomes the soul's own body.

[^V09-323]: If a soul were inside the body as an accident is inside its subject, it would not be independent enough to have a relation to two possible bodies. A soul free of matter can have such relations without being contained in either body.

[^V09-324]: The source wording is difficult. The commentator proposes a corrected reading: if the suited, returning soul has received what it is due, then no new soul should flow from the Giver of Forms. “What it is due” is the object of “receives”; “from among the souls” means some of them; the source of the receiving is the soul itself. The final clause gives the result if that condition holds.

[^V09-325]: That is, a heavenly body reveals or gives form to familiar images and desires of the soul.

[^V09-326]: Transmigration does not mean that a soul leaving a body at death moves into another already living body, since then two souls would inhabit one body. It means that a soul moves into matter ready for it, such as an animal embryo in a womb. How could Avicenna allow a human soul to move into a living heavenly body, which already has both an individual soul and a universal intellective soul? Some philosophers did teach a different system: after long ages, the souls of the heavens complete their development and join the intellects; perfected earthly souls rise to take their place, and another group rises later. That system is a form of transmigration.

[^V09-327]: The claim that a specific readiness fits each soul does not solve the problem. Dust and ash are not ready for a rational human soul. Nature must first bring matter through the perfections of lower kinds before it can rise to a higher kind. Each form requires suitable matter, readiness, and whatever brings about that readiness. Semen cannot receive the form of an embryo until it first takes on the form of a clinging substance; likewise, the matter of an embryo must first pass through its earlier stages. Though matter is what receives each form, the conditions that make it ready are not simply matter itself. Scattered earthly parts could not receive a soul separated from the body without first becoming ready through the forms closer to that soul.

[^V09-328]: The commentator notes the author's use of a phrase that seems to refer to all pleasures. It is a rhetorical claim, not a sound proof. Removing pain is a kind of absence, and absence cannot itself produce pleasure. The confusion comes from treating something that accompanies pleasure as if it were pleasure itself. Looking at a beautiful face without first being separated from it, hearing a lovely voice, smelling a fragrance, tasting sweetness, or touching a soft body can all be positive pleasures, not relief from pain. God takes joy in His own being, even though religious language about God is carefully defined; He has neither absence nor pain. The same is true of immaterial intellects joined to God.

[^V09-329]: Both replies are weak. The first does not answer why reward or pain should be given if God is not affected by them. The second assumes the conclusion: if the objection stands, there is no reason for a promise or warning whose truth needs to be shown.

[^V09-330]: The afterlife soul is self-sufficient and draws everything it chooses from its own inner reality. The one who gives joy and the one who receives it, or the one who causes fear and the one who feels it, are within the same soul; nothing comes from outside to change it. The commentator compares this with the philosophers' account of God's knowledge: it does not affect God, because it does not come from something outside Him. Our knowledge, by contrast, receives forms from outside, through the Active Intellect and the preparation of our awareness.

[^V09-331]: Worldly things that people grow attached to pass away; those who have not grown close to what lasts will not find it familiar. One might object that their memories of familiar things remain, so they can still enjoy them. The reply gives two reasons they cannot: the horror of the afterlife overwhelms them, and worldly pleasures themselves turn into harm. Wrongfully consuming an orphan's wealth becomes like consuming fire; forbidden food becomes like bitter, poisonous plants. As in the scriptural dream where years of famine and plenty appear as lean and fat cows, a person who devoured orphans' wealth unjustly will perceive themselves as eating fire.

[^V09-332]: The ordinary, untrained person relies mainly on sense, imagination, and instinct. Without the strength of an example, they would not be reassured. Even if reason were present, without examples suited to it, it would argue with reason and resist understanding abstract realities.

[^V09-333]: The person in question believed what the Prophet brought, did their best, and sincerely committed to an interpretation. Ghazali also says that the things promised in revelation can exist in five ways: in themselves, intellectually, in imagination, through the senses, or by likeness. Anyone who accepts one of these ways is not to be declared an unbeliever. Only someone who denies all meaning and rejects the promise entirely is an unbeliever.

[^V09-334]: This includes rules for trade, marriage, inheritance, and related matters.

[^V09-335]: The commentator says that the verse's phrase “at an appointed time” answers the objection: it names a timeless, eternal day, not a day within ordinary time. A thing in time is not known in itself; it is known through an image that makes it present to awareness. The commentator compares this with the verse, “We send it down only in a measured amount.”

[^V09-336]: “Returns to God and is gathered to Him” means that the person is annihilated in God and lives through Him: outwardly they have their form in this world, but their inner meaning is with God, where the Hour is known. In their bodily aspect they remain here; in spirit they are in the world beyond nature or in the divine realm. Likewise, their natural body is in time while their spirit is in a higher timeless duration, and their inner meaning is in eternity. Whatever appears there is the form of what exists here; knowledge is the seed of direct vision. The note quotes Persian verses meaning: “Many ask about the Day of Judgment as if they were already there,” and “Whoever has not seen the Beloved's face directly today is like a child waiting for tomorrow.”

[^V09-337]: “On the face of the earth” can mean the body's ground or the ground of material readiness. It may also mean that as long as someone remains here, there is still a knower and something known, and the Hour has not yet come for them.

[^V09-338]: This phrase echoes the Qur'anic “in a seat of truth, with a Sovereign who is all-powerful.” The divine presence is a fitting replacement for standing by the body and clinging to the earth.

[^V09-339]: The commentator lists five meanings of “grave.” First, the body is the grave of the soul, as in the Persian saying, “The souls of the dead are in the grave of the body.” Second, it is the familiar physical grave where the body is buried. Third, it is the imaginal body described in the main text. Fourth, for the soul, it is the set of conditions surrounding it: worldly distractions press in on the soul, sins and ignorance darken it, and knowledge is light. Fifth, for souls free of matter, it is the world of pure intellect. The graves and shrines of these souls are the places the nearest angels visit and surround.

[^V09-340]: The author answers an objection to spiritual resurrection: if the powers that perceive individual things depend on matter, they perish with the body, while the intellect that survives cannot perceive particulars. He replies that these powers exist in an imaginal, nonmaterial way. They remain with the soul and continue to imagine, understand, and sense particular things.

[^V09-341]: These forms are shadows of the soul's bright or dark qualities from this life: its good works and knowledge, or the worldly distractions and narrow limits that weighed it down.

[^V09-342]: The forms of the next life are the same as the imaginal forms of the intermediate state in their basic nature. They differ in strength, weakness, completeness, and attention. In the intermediate state, a person is still turned toward this world; in the next life, this world is forgotten and attention turns fully toward the forms of the afterlife. The intermediate state is like a dream, and this life is like a dream within a dream.

[^V09-343]: Desire appears as a pig, anger as a wild beast, and deceit as a demon. A person ruled by these natural urges is like someone who serves them and carries out their orders. God says, “Do not worship Satan,” and asks, “Have you seen the one who takes their own desire as their god?”

[^V09-344]: The reference is to the first level of the power of imagination, which leads back to what philosophers call the material intellect. The account must explain both what remains after death and what is still only a capacity. The author identifies it with imagination because this is the final form reached by the natural powers in this life and the first basis for the forms of the next.

[^V09-345]: Even if matter and form were only joined together rather than united as one thing, they would still need a shared nature and a close fit. This point does not depend on their being one.

[^V09-346]: Here, smells and sounds are examples of forms that air receives; the pronouns in the Arabic refer to air. But air does not receive colors, so “lights” may have been intended instead of “colors.” “Rays” does not mean that the material basis for receiving forms is itself a ray; the author's count of three also points to this reading.

[^V09-347]: Here, “matter” means any underlying basis for something, not only the material that carries an added quality.

[^V09-348]: The author says that those who notice this will see that imagined and understood forms are more noble, complete, and powerful because their basis is finer. Many people think they are weaker and less important, especially when they treat mental images as mere copies of physical things. If they saw those forms as real in themselves, they would judge the opposite—above all of lasting, universal thoughts free of material coverings and limits.

[^V09-349]: “Inner causes” here means a person's habits and character traits.

[^V09-350]: The first verse looks at each person's own place and the preserved core within them. The verses about God's rule look instead at the dropping away of relationships and ranks. Both point to different levels of God's active power: even when created things act, God is the source of their action. All existence is His light and gift; good is in His hand, while evil does not come from Him.

[^V09-351]: The commentator has already listed several meanings of “grave.” One is the set of habits and deeds, bright or dark, that surround the soul after death. The “grave” and “place of sleep” in the question, “Who woke us from our place of sleep?” are the shadows and forms that enclose the soul between death and Resurrection. These forms grow stronger and more complete as the soul turns away from this world and toward the next, whether in kindness or severity. Resurrection is this waking and full development. The intermediate state is like sleep; resurrection is waking from weak forms and taking on stronger ones.

[^V09-352]: In reason and in the word's ordinary sense, we are already gathered to God: our existence is hidden and erased beneath His. In the religious sense, this gathering is still to come. It occurs when we leave behind our natural bodies, take on pure forms of the intermediate world, and are gathered through them toward God's expressions of kindness or severity. It is the return of meaning and form to their source.

[^V09-353]: The commentator explains the seventy-cubit chain through the ties that hold a person back from God. He says that human existence has seven subtle levels, each with ten parts. These are the ten portions from which Adam’s nature was formed: one from the elements and nine from the nine heavenly spheres. Love, for example, is linked to Venus and anger to Mars. The elemental portion at each level refers to earthly qualities and the ability to be affected. In counting the seventy ties, he says to count the final effects and leave out their starting points.

    If each of the seventy ties is seen as reflecting a thousand of God’s beautiful names, they make seventy thousand veils of light and darkness, as in a well-known report. Their existence is light, while their limited nature is darkness. For the unhappy, the seventy ties remain only potential and never become a path to God. Even when they become active in a person’s nature and soul, they are like fire if they do not lead to God. Since the person does not see or reflect God’s names, these ties have no living connection with Him; together they form the chain seventy cubits long.

    The commentator then gives another way to count these ties. He divides people into seven groups linked to the seven planets: rulers and commanders to the Sun; soldiers to Mars; clerks and accountants to Mercury; scholars and advisers to Jupiter; farmers and people who live in caves or mountains to Saturn; musicians, lovers of adornment, women, and noblewomen to Venus; and travelers and merchants to the Moon. Each group depends on things that support bodily life, such as money. The commentator lists ten kinds of attachment, based on the astrologer’s houses: money; siblings and relatives; property, parents, servants, and animals; children; spouses and partners; young people; misfortunes; people with authority over us; friends; and enemies and large animals. He leaves out the first house because it stands for the self, not an attachment, and the ninth because it stands for knowledge and virtues, which perfect the soul rather than tie it down. Seven groups multiplied by ten attachments make seventy ties that keep the soul from reaching God.

    The seventy thousand veils arise because each tie contains a thousand particular concerns. The commentator compares them with the many tasks Adam needed after coming down to earth to obtain even a bite of food, according to a report. Bright veils include family and knowledge when they are not directed toward God; dark veils include money, property, animals, and similar worldly things.

[^V09-354]: “Their pairs” means a person's habits and the forms those habits take. The Qur'an also uses “pairs” for things joined in different ways: existence and essence, general kind and the feature that sets a kind apart, matter and form, and male and female.

[^V09-355]: The “wild beasts” include the animal traits that follow from a person's habits. They may also include real animals, though those are gathered only as a secondary part of the event.

[^V09-356]: This is an animal form without physical matter: the imaginal body, the soul's first bodily link in this life. The life-giving spirit is its second link, and the physical body that covers it is the third.

[^V09-357]: These inner senses perceive things of the next life. Through them, people trained in spiritual practice perceive what others cannot, as do the prophets. The author earlier cited reports of the Prophet seeing the earth's east and west and hearing the sky groan. A mystic puts it this way: “Beyond these five senses are five more, like pure gold beside copper. The health of these senses comes from a healthy body; the health of those comes as the body fades. Seek the first kind of health from a doctor, and the other from the beloved.”

[^V09-358]: If a person's habits are good, their forms are smooth-faced and youthful; if their habits are bad, their forms are dark and blue.

[^V09-359]: Someone might object that images held in imagination cannot perish because they are free of matter, even if they still have size and shape. The author has already shown that imagination and the inward sense that brings together what the outer senses perceive do not depend on matter. The forms of what worldly people love would therefore remain with them, since a thing is what it is because of its form, not its matter. The commentator says this question was answered earlier when the author discussed cause and effect.

[^V09-360]: “The sun” means the sun of Reality. The world is like a mirage that seems like water but disappears when a person reaches it. A mystic compares worldly people to those who watch the waves and forget the sea, clinging to one wave or bubble even though it will soon break or vanish. Those who turn toward God are like people absorbed in the sea itself. Its greatness keeps them from being distracted by waves and bubbles, except as signs of its works and appearances. The sea is the lasting reality beneath them. The mystic says: “When the court of Truth appears, the traveler who trusted in illusion will be ashamed.”

[^V09-361]: If someone worships to gain pleasure or escape Hell, they love themselves and turn toward their own reward, not toward God. Early jurists therefore required worship to be directed purely to God. The commentator says this kind of devotion is possible only for those close to God, and cites the saying, “My bliss and My paradise.”

[^V09-362]: Plato's view should not be read as saying that each personal soul existed earlier in a separate, incomplete form. A higher reality, such as the Active Intellect, exists before the physical world, and the soul exists within it at a higher level: the source contains the branch, and the fuller reality contains its more limited expression. Something is what it is through its fullness, not its lack.

[^V09-363]: This means perception through the senses of the intermediate and next worlds—in general, perception through the soul's inner images. The author says this can be known through reason and religious reports, and also through direct experience.

[^V09-364]: The reference is to dreams, spiritual unveilings, sudden glimpses, and similar experiences.

[^V09-365]: Ibn Arabi seems to mean the natural body. Sadra later says Ibn Arabi relied only on personal experience, which would be impossible if taken literally: it would lead to contradictions, such as the soul's destruction or its moving from one body to another. A thing is itself through its form, and an individual is distinct through the soul and its existence. Ibn Arabi was a religious mystic who guarded Muslim belief, so what he says agrees with the teachings and reports of the faith.

[^V09-366]: The form is not inside the intermediate world as water is inside a cup. To say a mental form is “in the mind” means it is one level of the mind or soul. In the same way, an outer form is one level of the world outside us.

[^V09-367]: This means the natural body that has become a corpse: the form seen at the time of questioning. Earlier reports about the soul's long separation from worldly life and its strong turn toward the next life help explain this point.

[^V09-368]: The questioning itself is one kind of punishment.

[^V09-369]: This refers to the verse, “On the day the Shin is uncovered and they are called to bow down.”

[^V09-370]: This refers to a report, quoted earlier in the discussion of al-Ghazali, that paradise has a market where forms are offered.

[^V09-371]: As explained earlier, a human being can take different forms in the spiritual world, just as jinn can appear in different forms in this world. The commentator alludes to the well-known poem: “My heart has become capable of every form.”

[^V09-372]: The commentator calls the still-open possibilities of the divine decree “spiritual,” and the settled, merciful commands of God's realm “lordly.” The former still include the possibility of taking different shapes; in the latter, those possibilities have been gathered into a settled outcome.

[^V09-373]: The Prophet is reported to have said, “Before you there were a thousand thousand worlds and a thousand thousand Adams. Your world is the last of the worlds, and your Adam is the last of the Adams.”

[^V09-374]: When the Prophet was asked whether he and his followers would also come to the Fire, he replied, “Yes, we passed through it while it was still cool.” They had not fallen into the traps and ties of worldly life. For them, God makes the Fire “cool and safe”; it is like light, not something that burns them or drowns them in its flames.

[^V09-375]: The two reports do not conflict. “Every child is born with a sound nature” refers to the spirit, which comes from a pure, protected world. “The fortunate person is fortunate in the womb, and the unhappy person is unhappy in the womb” refers to the body's nature and temperament, which may be sound or unsound. The commentator also relates the first report to the bright spirit and the second to the soul's formation in the body's four sources and the way its nature is shaped by good or bad habits. Finally, the first concerns the spirit's pure beginning, while the second concerns what is known from the beginning, in the “Mother of the Book” and the “Mother of Pens,” as in another report: “The fortunate person has been fortunate from eternity, and the unhappy person unhappy from eternity.”

[^V09-376]: This natural worship means following the commands and limits built into creation, just as religious worship means following God's revealed commands and limits. Both aim to bring a person closer to God. Religious worship also keeps people away from Hell; natural worship draws them away from matter and the lowest levels of nature. Whoever is shaped by spiritual qualities is more open to God's mercy; whoever is ruled by bodily nature moves farther from it. This is why the world is called cursed, along with those who are wholly caught up in it—not those whose bodies are here while their hearts have risen above it. The complete person's simple intellect belongs to the highest, lasting realm, not to time or place, even though their body remains in both. The awake mind knows that references to dates, places, and positions describe only the body's life; the simple intellect is free of them.

[^V09-377]: The body, as a whole shaped and made one by the soul, does not simply perish. The soul gives it its identity, individuality, and unity. The scattered elements of the body, considered apart from the soul, are not the body itself; they remain much as they were before and after death.

[^V09-378]: The first statement concerns the soul's original natural power; the second concerns the power it develops over its life. In those who reach full maturity, there is no body in the fullest sense to decay at death. At most, a faint body remains, like a shadow that the soul no longer attends to because it is absorbed in what lies above it.

[^V09-379]: This refers to God's words: “When Moses came at Our appointed time and his Lord spoke to him, he said, ‘My Lord, show Yourself to me, so that I may look upon You.’” The soul's “appointed time” is its growth toward a meeting with God over the course of life. God's “speech” is the true speaking of the soul through God, as well as its receiving the levels of practical and theoretical understanding that prepare it to meet Him. God's appearance in the worlds of mercy and power draws out the soul's corresponding qualities. Death is therefore both a natural end and the way to a real meeting with God.

[^V09-380]: If we set aside the sphere's soul and consider its body alone as matter, little more than a weak extension remains. This extension can be split and broken, like other extended things. As explained earlier, the body of a heavenly sphere is not a separate, strong material thing; its strength comes from the soul that gives it life and movement.

[^V09-381]: God has placed in the soul a love of lasting life—the life of the Living One who never passes away. This world offers only a borrowed life, mixed with loss and decay. Souls love it because it is a faint reflection of lasting life, and they hate its opposite. But the reality is far greater than its shadow, especially a shadow that has fallen so far from its source.

[^V09-382]: The goal is for the soul to be gathered in a complete state, not an incomplete one. This requires it to be given enough time to grow.

[^V09-383]: The answer is contained in the question. Most people do not know that death leads to the life described here. Even if they hear it, it may remain a passing thought rather than firm knowledge. So they still fear death.

[^V09-384]: The soul is so subtle that it takes on the form of whatever it attends to. At first, it is closely tied to the body and its balanced condition. It is distressed when that balance breaks or the body is torn apart. This is why it feels pain at death, even though its higher nature is not destroyed.

[^V09-385]: The first purpose—pain and fear that protect the body—applies to ordinary people. The second purpose concerns those who reach the next stage of life. The intermediate world depends on imagination, which everyone has, but its images must become firm. Every being needs time to grow and mature; young children, for example, do not dream in their earliest years. Pain warns of danger before the body is fully formed. Without the ability to feel pain, an animal would lack touch, and without touch it would not be an animal.

[^V09-386]: A thing must reach its end by changing and becoming something new, as an animal soul on the human path becomes a rational soul, and as reason becomes fully active and acquired. The soul's return to God means that it loses its separate self and is erased in Him. Reaching the final end is another way of describing resurrection and reward. A divine philosopher speaks of this return in both ways: every thing has an end it must reach, including the ends built into nature.

[^V09-387]: These intellects have a real inner connection because, strictly speaking, they have no separate essence of their own, let alone matter as a separate base. The rules that make things separate from God depend on matter, motion, or what follows from them. None of those apply to the intellects. They belong to the realm of God's own being.

[^V09-388]: This refers to the question, “For what purpose is it?” The purpose meant here is the final end.

[^V09-389]: In *The Divine Things*, the author raises a problem about the principle that the nobler being must come before the lower. It seems there could be another being between God and the First Effect, nobler than that effect but lower than God, and then another between these, without end. This would trap an infinite number of beings between two fixed limits. The author answers that the intellects are like one being with many levels, like one soul with many subtle powers. They are all faces of God and His light, joined to Him in a real, inner connection. There is no actual infinity of separate beings here, only infinitely many ranks within one connected reality.

[^V09-390]: This proof uses direct awareness. It has two forms: unity with what is known, and the way a cause is present in what it causes. Here, “unity” means losing the separate self in God.

[^V09-391]: The act of revealing is the same as what is revealed, because the intellect loses its separate self in this account. It has no independent essence; it exists only through its link to God and belongs to God's own realm.

[^V09-392]: The intellect is not merely like an image in a mirror. It is the image itself, and the mirror is only a comparison. As we have seen, it has no separate essence of its own, so there is no actual mirror here.

[^V09-393]: The author proves unity in two ways: first, by showing that what is understood in itself is united with the intellect that understands it; second, by showing that knowledge is united with the one who knows.

[^V09-394]: “It exists through God” means that it depends on Him for its existence. The question “For what cause?” can refer to different causes. Here it means the cause that brings something into being. Earlier, when the author said that a thing's fullness is its end, he meant its purpose. If the text instead reads “standing in God,” that can mean that God gives it existence. This makes it more separate from Him. But if what depends on God is itself not made by another, its connection to God is also not made. The author assumes that these intellects exist necessarily through God, since their necessity and lasting life come from Him.

[^V09-395]: The author leaves out the souls of the heavenly spheres. Their universal souls are included under rational souls, and their particular souls—the ones spread through the bodies of the spheres—are included under human imaginal souls, since their return follows the same pattern.

[^V09-396]: A soul reaches a state of purity by gaining the right qualities in this life. The next life is not a place to earn those qualities; it is where what a person has done comes into view. A soul may be drawn toward the Active Intellect by an angel's help, or by the help of a complete human being. The note's phrase “because of the strength of longing” means that the soul longs strongly to be drawn toward it.

[^V09-397]: Every animal has a form and a meaning: a body and a soul. Each develops through inner change during its life in this world. God gives each being a set time to develop. Once the animal soul has completed that development, it leaves the material body, takes on its imaginal form, and can exist without the natural body. It then joins the other souls of its kind while each remains distinct. The intellect that rules the kind is a broad unity, not a small, countable group; its light is wide enough to hold all their souls and imaginal forms.

[^V09-398]: We call this a return in its own right. Other kinds of return happen through something else.

[^V09-399]: This refers to those who say that the soul first arises from the body, then continues after death through inner change, or even before that change. Plato says the soul exists as one in the higher world, not as many separate individuals. Ibn Arabi agrees and calls this shared state the “Presence of Unity.” Others say that the soul exists in this general state at the same time as its separate life in the body. These include some Aristotelians and many religious thinkers who say that the soul is free of matter in its essence but begins with the body.

[^V09-400]: This is a comparison between the greater world and the smaller world of the human body.

[^V09-401]: The spirits were not present as separate beings in the unfolding world. They existed together in God's eternal knowledge, distinct there because God's knowledge includes every detail. God's knowledge is complete in one undivided act and at the same time knows everything in detail. The general state means this undivided existence; the detailed state means the many meanings and forms of things, which some call the “fixed essences.”

[^V09-402]: This applies to written letters if we mean the letters that a natural pen is ready to write. But it does not explain the letters that exist in the universe. Those were hidden in the existence of the Higher Pen, as a poem says: “We were exalted letters, not marks left on the mountain peaks. I am you in knowledge, and you are us; we are all in Him. Ask the one who has arrived.” In God's pure knowledge, I, you, and all things exist together as one, not as separate meanings or essences. Their power comes from the strength and completeness of this unity, and God's power acts through it.

[^V09-403]: This means the Universal Intellect within the Universal Soul, or the Universal Spirit within the detailed forms. That reading better suits the context. A Persian poem says: “When His power breathed upon the pen, thousands of forms were drawn on the tablet of nonexistence.” Here, “nonexistence” means the essences of forms, which exist only in the mind.

[^V09-404]: Simple forms are like separate letters; the spirits of compound bodies are like letters joined into words.

[^V09-405]: A Persian poet says: “I always live in the writer's hand; He keeps me between two fingers. My own being has no movement in it; He moves me at every moment.” The two fingers mean the qualities of beauty and majesty, or gentleness and force.

[^V09-406]: This follows the reading “forms” instead of “Trumpet.” If read as “Trumpet,” it still fits the author's point. The spiritual blowing and fragrant gift from God is the spirit that joins these forms, as God says, “I breathed into him of My spirit.”

[^V09-407]: The better view is that the spirits arise from the lower side of the forms, not their higher side. The verse “Then We brought it into a new creation” also suggests that the spirits emerge through a real inner change, not by being made out of material that was already present, as people who look only at physical nature may think.

[^V09-408]: The “book of the soul” means the soul's inner record, written through its habits and qualities.

[^V09-409]: In short, there are three views. Two say the soul becomes free of the body's form but still keeps the feature that made it different from other souls. The third says the soul always remains joined to some form, whether in this world, the intermediate world, or the next.

[^V09-410]: The soul comes before the body in its own nature and in a timeless sense. It gives the body its individual identity. It is also the body's true distinguishing feature, the cause of its special effects. Without a distinguishing feature there can be no kind; without a kind there can be no particular being. We have already shown that a body cannot choose one special form over another for no reason. The body is not first an empty, identical thing that later receives any form. The mistake comes from confusing what exists outside the mind with what we understand in thought.

[^V09-411]: Ibn Arabi does not deny that the body follows the soul, whether we mean the usual human body or the slow-witted person in the form they take in the intermediate world.

[^V09-412]: “The same body” means the body whose form makes it the body it is, and whose individual identity comes from its soul and existence. The commentator also wants to protect religious teaching and the beliefs of ordinary Muslims. As the author has said, the well-known teaching is that on the Day of Resurrection this is the same body and the same soul. The difference lies in the qualities of each state: worldly, intermediate, or next-life.

[^V09-413]: The plant has something like touch-awareness. If it truly had the sense of touch, it would be an animal, since touch distinguishes animals. It would also move by its own choice. But nature rises from lower to higher in order, so the plant cannot have full touch-awareness.

[^V09-414]: God gives the plant a set time to develop its form and soul. The author wrote earlier about particular animal souls in a way that also applies here.

[^V09-415]: These trees are not separate from the heavenly trees; they are contained within them. They also include trees formed from human habits and actions. In paradise, the ground is ready for planting, and its plants are words of praise to God.

[^V09-416]: The Lote Tree is described in Qur'anic commentary as standing to the right of the Throne, above the seventh heaven. The knowledge of every angel ends there, and what rises to heaven or descends from above passes through it. The tree is covered by what covers it. The commentator reads it as the Great Intermediate World, the end of the journey of perfected souls and their actions and knowledge, and the highest level of God's names. Angels gathering around it like ravens means that those who travel toward God reach their end there. There is no village beyond Abadan. On this reading, the Lote Tree means a blessed state of wonder, as in the Prophet's prayer, “Lord, increase me in wonder of You,” and Ibn al-Farid's line: “I think the limit of waking and drunkenness is my ascent toward her; my home lies at the farthest point of the Lote Tree.” The word for the tree is also linked to an illness of the head called *sudar*.

[^V09-417]: I repeat this point because the evidence that existence is the same as these qualities, and that their levels follow the levels of existence, can be seen by looking at the human soul. This is even clearer when we look at intellect and what lies above it. The soul is knowledge, will, and love through its very being. Its being is at once awareness of itself and its powers, present knowledge of itself, life, light, and the ability to act through all its powers. The same is true, even more fully, of intellect and what lies above it. In extended bodies, however, completeness in every quality is also a lack in every quality.

[^V09-418]: The author does not mean that the forms that flow into matter are themselves gathered after death, since they belong to this worldly life. He means that they are gathered in the same way as simple beings are.

[^V09-419]: The difference from the first principle is only what blocks the form from rising higher. At the first level, the obstacles are distance in space, duration in time, and darkness of matter. At the later level, the obstacle is conflict between forms. What brings the forms together also differs: at first, existence itself; later, balance and a well-ordered mixture.

[^V09-420]: These are imaginal forms, also called suspended images.

[^V09-421]: The phrase means that the Illuminationists say these forms exist; it does not mean they say the forms are directly connected to the separate intellects. The same is true of their view about separate intellects: they do not say there is no soul-form between them and the natural bodies. There must be a connecting link.

[^V09-422]: They are called “soul-forms” because they are images in the separate imagination of the Universal Soul, much as images in a person's imagination are soul-forms for that person. They are images, but together they also form knowledge, perception, and life. The next life, as the report says, “is the true life.”

[^V09-423]: The “matter of intellect” here means imaginal forms, in line with the author's own account. The “matter of soul” means sensible forms. Each is called matter because it is joined to its form, changes into it, and becomes it.

[^V09-424]: This refers to the wonders that the images of the intellectual world contain.

[^V09-425]: This means that each being longs for a real inner union with its source, and for direct knowledge of it. This is the state the author elsewhere calls “all things in all things,” since no veil separates beings beyond matter. The commentator reads the desire to rise in this way because fully complete beings do not feel lack or longing. The passage also concerns the intellects of rising human souls, as the author's next words show.

[^V09-426]: These forms are not dark and made of matter. They exist more completely and clearly than physical forms. Nor are they merely imaginary; they can be seen. To be seen means to take a form that the shared sense can perceive, whether that form comes from inside or outside. The outward senses are only pathways that lead to this shared power of perception, like five rivers flowing into one pool.

[^V09-427]: The proof that the visible world reveals these hidden forms is that natural processes ripen bodies and plants and change them. Nature itself is like a fire with three flames. Imagine what happens when it changes and appears in another world, with its chains, shackles, scorpions, and snakes.

[^V09-428]: Matter, decay, and passing away belong only to this world. If their features were carried into another world, there would not be more than one kind of world. In brief, fully formed beings return to intellect; extended things that hold together or flow away return to the pit of matter; and matter returns to destruction, because lack rules over it. As we said earlier, powers and unrealized possibilities return to matter, while complete forms return to the source of complete forms. This does not contradict the truth that all things return to God, because even the weakest existence returns to Him, and judgments follow what is strongest in each thing.

[^V09-429]: Just as matter and the pit of nonexistence are the end of physical things considered only as bodies, and of people who reject nature's commands, Hell is the end of souls that reject God's moral and religious commands.

[^V09-430]: In contrast, wise and intellectual ends belong to the long upward path. Their starting points are mistaken ideas suited only to false arguments, empty hopes, and desires opposed to true rational aims. Here, “tyrant” means a specific power of desire or a particular craving. These goals move further into nonexistence as they become more limited and fade away, as we see when trivial aims of the past vanish. A wise person says, “How sorry I am for what I neglected in my duty to God.”

[^V09-431]: What happens to all happens to each one. As God says, “Your creation and your rising are only like the creation and rising of one soul.” After natural forms are complete, the first blowing brings them death and decay. A second blowing brings fear and collapse to the souls that have completed their development; it puts out their light. In the next blowing, their fire and greater light are kindled: the souls receive imaginal forms through God's breath. So one form dies and another lives. The author will explain this later.

[^V09-432]: This is the imaginal earth, lit by its Lord's light—the divine light that guides the soul. It is like many pieces of wood and wicks lit by a great fire. When the author says that this may mean the earth we know, he means any earth, simple or compound, including bodies. He says “after it has been taken into God's hand” because the verse appears in the context of Resurrection, after the blowing that brings death and before the books of deeds are placed and the prophets and witnesses are brought forward. The earth can also mean the fullest capacity to receive existence, and God's complete light and unity.

[^V09-433]: This interpretation leads to another, and the verses are connected in the chapter of *The Groups*. But the verse about God's hand comes before the others, so the commentator turns to explain it here.

[^V09-434]: We have already seen that only a weak trace like matter remains at the edge of the heavenly body's form. Its resistance to outside change comes from its soul, not its body. This supports the idea that the heavens are folded up.

[^V09-435]: The fire and other forms exist hidden within matter and then appear from it. Matter contains all forms only as possibilities; a form is what makes a thing actual, while matter is only what makes it possible.

[^V09-436]: This is the imaginal form that corresponds to the natural form.

[^V09-437]: This supports the author's claim that these forms have life in their own right.

[^V09-438]: In all five places, “this fire” means the soul-form. “The fire in the higher world” means the first, intellectual fire.

[^V09-439]: Up to the phrase “plants of the earth,” the philosopher gives examples that are not true as they sound and cannot be used as proof. If he means the compound earth, then it includes plant life and minerals, which may have an active inner form. But his goal is to prove an active form within the simple earth, even though earth should receive action rather than act. So words like “growth” and “sprouting” should mean the earth's cooling, drying, and holding forms together. The earth's inner soul-form guides these actions.

[^V09-440]: I must say that Ibn Arabi's insight is very deep, and deserves to be written in gold, not ink. “Life” can mean the source of perception and action, as when we say a living thing is aware and active. This is the life usually attributed to spirits. It belongs to spirits naturally, but to bodies only through something else. “Life” can also mean simple, all-pervading existence. We have often called this “the life that flows through everything.” In that sense, it belongs to all things that exist, including bodies. How could it not, when a body exists, and existence is life in each thing according to its level?

    When great philosophers call bodies decaying, dead, or dark, they mean the first kind of life. It comes to bodies from spirits, so they have one life of their own and another received from outside. It is strange that the author says this here, when he repeatedly teaches in this book and elsewhere that life, knowledge, and will follow from existence or are the same as it. I have often shown this rule by looking at the rational soul: its existence is its knowledge of itself, its life, light, will, and love of itself. It is also its power to take on many states through its powers and images. For the author and other knowers, the soul is existence itself.

    When Ibn Arabi says that stones and crafted vessels have forms of life, he means existence, though it is a passing existence received from another. His statement that bodies remember and praise God is also true. The author establishes this in his early discussion of general principles, and I have explained it in my notes. When we set aside the differences between things, their praise is included in God's own praise, as the verse says, “There is nothing that does not praise Him.” That is, each thing praises God through its own existence.

[^V09-441]: If “speech” means human speech, then animals do not have it either. But if it means each thing's own kind of speech, then everything speaks and is guided to what it was made for. Every existence is a word. Seen as coming from God, it is God's word; seen as belonging to its own nature, it is that nature's word. It describes God's qualities and displays His names. Praise describes the praised one's qualities and gifts, and the existence that flows through all things praises itself in this way.

[^V09-442]: Ibn Arabi's wording keeps the different levels in view, even if one level is only a shadow of a light or a branch of a source.

[^V09-443]: The verses about all things praising God show that existence, wherever it is, has life, knowledge, awareness, and will. Ibn Arabi himself says that God is with us wherever we are.

[^V09-444]: The author could say that bodies are dead and dark compared with the animal life that enables awareness and action. But they are not dead compared with the deeper life that belongs to existence and flows through all things. “We made every living thing from water” means that life arises from the life of existence. Even matter, which is lower than bodies, is alive in this sense because it exists. The author's statement that bodies are mixed with absence already shows that they have existence.

    If relative death and darkness made them unable to be alive, aware, or praising in any sense, then they would also be dead in relation to the highest life: knowledge of God, His qualities, and His actions. People who know God would then be alive, while others were dead. How could the praise of all things show that all things have life? We do not deny the life and praise of imaginal bodies or other forms. But life has three levels: general, special, and most special, as we have explained.

[^V09-445]: This interpretation changes Ibn Arabi's meaning. He clearly says that life belongs to bodies in their own right, not that they receive it from outside.

[^V09-446]: The root meaning of “the Hour” may be “striving” or “moving quickly,” as some dictionaries suggest. One may explain the word by saying that “the Hour” comes from this root. The Qur'an uses it for the brief moment when the Resurrection begins, a short time in which a great event takes place. The author says the word also points to the fact that Resurrection is hidden within the world of nature and is part of the soul's long upward journey. This is why people see it at different distances: God says they think it is far away, while He sees it as near. It may be called a “day,” as in the verse that describes a day lasting fifty thousand years; it may be called an “hour”; or it may be even shorter, “like the blink of an eye, or shorter still.” The return to God is like the beginning of creation.

[^V09-447]: What happens to all happens to each one. As the verse says, “Your creation and your rising are only like the creation and rising of one soul.” Each soul reaches its end and becomes independent of the body and its powers. In the same way, all things move toward their ends and return to their source, until they reach the final end. The smaller hours are included in the Great Hour, but sense and imagination cannot grasp the whole of this movement. Even the intellect can know it only in a general way: everything moves toward its end through an endless chain of inner change. The time of one limited change is limited, but the time of endlessly many changes has no end. Compared with the Source of all sources, however, it is like the blink of an eye, or even shorter.

[^V09-448]: Light and forms may be imagined as a cone, with its point at the world beyond matter and its broad base at the material world—or the other way around. If we focus on simplicity and unity, the point belongs to the world beyond matter, where things are most simple and united, while the material world is divided and mixed. If we focus on the amount of existence, the world beyond matter is wider, and the material world is the narrow point.

[^V09-449]: This is a kind of metaphor used with verbs. For example, “the situation spoke” means that it showed or pointed to something. The Qur'an's phrase “they bought error in exchange for guidance” means that they chose error instead of guidance.

[^V09-450]: This comment corrects al-Razi's claim that the blowing does not happen twice. The first blowing causes death in the lower world; this matches Ibn Arabi's account of two blowings. The author then explains that the first ends natural forms and the second strengthens the link between spirits and simple imaginal forms. The first form dies; the second is used and brought to life.

[^V09-451]: A report says that a sound is made in the horn as many times as there are creatures. This points to the meaning just described.

[^V09-452]: “The eye” here means either the form itself and its inner reality, or the sight of that imaginal form. It can be called the “eye” because the senses in that world are joined; each sense does the work of all the others.

[^V09-453]: Rather, they are people of God. They are not subject to changing worlds or time, because they are not the spheres and bodies made of elements that come into being and pass away. In their inner reality, they are simple intellects and spirits sent free of place, time, and direction. Their home is the world of divine power; their time is the highest, rightward realm; their direction is a spiritual ascent through light. They rise like the angels who are near to God. A Persian poem says: “When I became empty of myself, I knew nothing but God. I disappeared from myself and remained through the Real; my garment of being was torn open at once.”

[^V09-454]: The great Resurrection belongs to an upward path through levels of being, not to a point on the ordinary timeline. That is why the Prophet was taught to answer questions about its timing by saying, “Its knowledge is with my Lord.” Anyone who tries to find its time by looking ahead along ordinary time is making the same mistake as someone who looks backward in time to find the eternal Source. It is like mistaking a swelling for something substantial. Thinkers find this difficult, let alone people who rely mainly on imagination. The author says that the Day of the great Resurrection is to the small resurrections of each breath as a day is to its hours, or a year to its days. Mawlana says, in effect: “Who would ask when the great Gathering will come, while it is already gathering all things?”

[^V09-455]: The signs of the great Resurrection have counterparts in the small one. When the earth shakes on that day, the body's ground shakes at death and its structure trembles. When the earth and mountains are lifted and crushed, the body's bones are crushed. When the stars are darkened, the lights of the body's powers go out. When the sun is folded up and the moon darkens, the spirit of the heart—the sun of the small world—comes to an end, and the light of the spirit in the brain, which depends on the heart, fades. When all creatures are gathered to God, the soul's many powers are gathered into one simple spiritual self.

[^V09-456]: This refers to God's fullest revelation of Himself through His names “the One,” “the Unique,” and “the All-Subduing.” Individual natures disappear, and all existence returns to God; the link between existence and separate natures falls away. Before the great Resurrection, beings appear as many and are linked to their individual natures. At the Resurrection, even separate intellects lose that distinction, because every trace of otherness is overcome. In this account, the natural world is the world of what is separate from God.

[^V09-457]: They exist in a fuller and brighter way, as explained earlier.

[^V09-458]: This upward return is a rise in the order of being, not movement through space. The soul rises as it is uncovered and freed from its coverings. The author says that the words “Resurrection” and “gathering” are fully fitting only when the soul is completely free of matter, as Ali said at that moment, “By the Lord of the Kaaba, I have won.” Yet the truth of Resurrection is already present. The Resurrection of ordinary people, whose goals and rewards still take bodily form, is not the same as the Resurrection of those who have reached the truth and attained the highest qualities they could receive from God. The author alludes to the end of Avicenna's *The Healing*, where the fully realized person is described as a human being shaped by divine qualities.

[^V09-459]: This means the soul of a heavenly sphere, which is like an image of imagination. The author says “power” because imagining and the power to imagine are one.

[^V09-460]: The scattered lights we see here will then become stronger, more unified lights of the spiritual world.

[^V09-461]: This is like the way the First Source appears to those who know it.

[^V09-462]: The author means especially good habits and beautiful forms. The “Garden of Qualities” and “Garden of the Essence” are familiar ideas to those who have trained themselves in good character and reached spiritual insight. Two Persian poems make the point:
    >
    > Whoever has not seen the Beloved's face today
    > is like a child on the road, still waiting for tomorrow.
    >
    > They have put off our promised meeting until tomorrow;
    > I hope tomorrow never comes between us.

[^V09-463]: This is like the state of certain saints, called the Abdal, whose shadowed being is said to have become luminous, along with their senses. One of them says, “I see only Your face and hear only Your voice.” Husayn ibn Ali says, “Blind is the eye that does not see You.”

[^V09-464]: This refers to people whose intermediate world remains inactive. May God protect us from that.

[^V09-465]: The problem comes from judging by outward appearances. The author cites a Persian poem:
    >
    > Many have been led astray by outward form;
    > they aimed at the form and missed God.
    > For this reason the world has gone astray,
    > and few know God's true friends.
    > They say, “They are human, just like us;
    > we all sleep and eat.”
    > Do not judge the pure by yourself:
    > a lion and milk may share the same written name, but they are not the same.

[^V09-466]: Every natural being worships through its created nature by obeying the commands and limits built into it. Human beings also have worship that comes through religious duties. Here, a soul's “condition” means its states, character, knowledge, and understanding. The religious motive is the intention to draw near to God. The human being is the traveler; the path is what moves within the traveler.

[^V09-467]: The perfect human being reflects the all-encompassing divine name, God. They may also reflect names such as the All-Hearing, the All-Seeing, the Knower, the Overpowering, the One who leads astray, or the One who lowers. But they are servants of God as a whole, not servants of only one of these names.

[^V09-468]: To stray from one's original nature may mean falling short of the highest rank without necessarily falling into Hell. This reading includes the people of the right, since God divides people into three groups: those brought near, those on the right, and those on the left.

[^V09-469]: The path has two lanes: one develops theoretical understanding, the other practical reason. The first is finer than a hair; the second sharper than a sword.

[^V09-470]: There are four stages: clearing and polishing, cleansing away, adorning, and passing beyond the self. The final act is witnessing God. At that stage, a person sees every kind of knowledge, power, quality, and existence as absorbed in God's knowing, power, and being.

[^V09-471]: These are people of high resolve who cut off their ties to the world all at once. A Persian verse says: “Take one step beyond yourself; the next lies in the Friend's lane.”
