[^V09-472]: This applies if the goal is the garden of God's pure essence, in the light of that essence. Otherwise, the journey within God is itself the garden of His qualities. How, then, could there be a journey from God?

[^V09-473]: They do not possess the root; how could they possess the branch—the path that leads to it?

[^V09-474]: “Tyrants” here means the tyrants among them.

[^V09-475]: The seven groups may refer to the seven planets, the teachings of the seven prophets of resolve, or the seven subtle powers of the human being. God's outpouring and light never stop or fade.

[^V09-476]: They accepted Islam outwardly, through attachment, but did not live it fully. Islam means surrender and steadfastness, as in God's command, “Stand firm, as you have been commanded.” The bridge of mercy keeps them from being consumed by the fire of their nature before they have crossed.

[^V09-477]: These are people who sin openly, unlike those who keep their faults hidden.

[^V09-478]: People may fast to obey God, to clear the heart—for hunger is a cloud that rains wisdom—to understand the poor, or to take on the quality of the Self-Sufficient, who does not eat. People may give alms to obey, to develop generosity, to show compassion, or to take on the quality of the Provider, who meets needs. The light of an act depends on the intention behind it.

[^V09-479]: Other kinds of knowledge are tools, not ends in themselves. Good deeds polish the mirror of the heart. A sacred report says, “I created the creatures so that I might be known.”

[^V09-480]: The author brings together the path's two sides: theoretical understanding and practical action, each with its own stages. Though spiritual in its meaning, the path also has a visible form in the afterlife, like a bridge over a deep pit of Fire.

[^V09-481]: The three-branched shadow stands for the dark state of material nature. It is spread out in space, with length, width, and depth, and its parts are separated from one another. It cannot protect the soul from the fire of desire; nor can it escape the burning that comes from being spread out in time.

[^V09-482]: A traditional prayer addressed to the Imams calls them “the greatest path and the most upright way.”

[^V09-483]: The path, the traveler, the distance, and the movement are one. So too are the starting point of the movement and the point it reaches.

[^V09-484]: Movement here is cyclical: action follows knowledge, and knowledge is built on earlier action and knowledge. To put the foot on the head means acting according to the light of one's knowledge; to put the head on the foot means building new understanding on the results of one's knowledge, which itself rests on earlier understanding.

[^V09-485]: Knowledge is included among deeds, since it is an act of the heart. The record contains three kinds of forms: states and settled traits, which are both effects and sources of deeds; corresponding images that appear in the soul; and conscious representations of the deeds. Even now, despite all the veils and distractions, a person can look back and see their deeds imprinted in the book of the self. How much clearer will they be when the veil is lifted?

[^V09-486]: The agents that record different kinds of things include the Active Intellect for universal forms, souls embedded in matter, suspended images, divine forms, and subtle ties corresponding to individual things.

[^V09-487]: A good deed leads to another good deed until goodness becomes a settled trait.

[^V09-488]: Good traits form ranks of angels—beautiful youths and everlasting boys, or good influences and choices in this life. Every thought and action points toward more good. Lights descend on them from every side. A Persian verse says: “The light of lights, from right and left, / has cast a collar around my head and neck.” The same applies, in reverse, to darkness and the powers that display it.

[^V09-489]: Saying that the angel and devil have “one substance” points to the unity of what is named. Scripture also speaks of punishing angels. The angelic trait has a side that displays God's overpowering power.

[^V09-490]: These forms are like souls embedded in matter and like universal souls and intellects. They exist in a partial form at the lower level, and in a universal form at the higher level. The lower forms are held within the higher ones.

[^V09-491]: The three forms are the settled traits produced by deeds, the bodily images that represent them, and the conscious images of the deeds.

[^V09-492]: “The unbelieving devil” means the form of a trait created by repeated sin. Repeated sins may blacken the heart until a believer turns away from faith. The author also suggests that the perfect human being is the root from which the rest of creation branches, having been created from what remained of that person's clay. The human being's plant and animal powers are likewise roots from which plants and animals branch. The same applies to character: love and power in the perfect human are roots from which other forms of love and power branch. The same is true of the perfect human being's protection from error in knowledge and action, and of the corresponding qualities in angels.

[^V09-493]: One might object: if the human soul itself causes reward and punishment, how can God be the one who rewards and punishes, as scripture and the shared teaching of believers say? The answer is that God is the giver of all existence, light, and darkness. Nothing acts independently of Him. Intellects, souls, and powers are channels through which His action is specified. Whether existence appears through mercy or through power, it is God's outpouring, directly or through one or more intermediaries. The effects of the soul exist through God, but their particular forms are specified through the soul.

[^V09-494]: This is like a dream, which is called “the little death.” The sky above you, the ground beneath you, the person who speaks to you, and whatever pleases or hurts you are all objects of your knowledge. Knowledge that acts is the same as power.

[^V09-495]: The “bird” may mean the record of a person's deeds, which is the person themselves. It may also refer to their good or bad fortune. People often use a bird as a sign of what is to come, as in the expression “the crow of separation.”

[^V09-496]: The phrase points to the words “We will bring you into a form you do not know.” The implication comes from the verb “bring into being.”

[^V09-497]: The word “total” comes from an accountant's way of adding: “This is two, this is three, this is five; the total is ten.” Here, the total or summary means the settled traits, the separate entries are the repeated deeds and words of this life, and the book that counts them all is the human soul.

[^V09-498]: For those brought near to God, this is clear. They become simple, universal intellects and spirits free of matter, time, place, and other veils. All forms are gathered into their being. A report says that Ali recited the whole Qur'an at once, meaning the Qur'an in every sense: written or created, in the world or within the self. This all-encompassing awareness is a lasting rank for those brought near.

    Those on the right have not completed their theoretical understanding, and those on the left have not done so either. For them, seeing everything in one moment is not a lasting rank; their spiritual freedom is only partial and temporary. They have simple awareness, not awareness that reflects on itself. The state of those brought near is like that of people continually absorbed in Joseph's beauty; the state of those on the right and left is like that of the women who were struck by his beauty for a time. A settled trait is itself a simple state that contains the actions flowing from it. To see that trait is to see all those actions at once.

    There is a difficulty about bodily resurrection. Scripture and tradition describe changes and successive events in the afterlife: skins are replaced, and the people of Paradise eat, drink, move, and enjoy one another in sequence. The same applies, in a way suited to them, to the people of Hell. Yet potentiality and material matter belong to this world, not the next. This world is the field of sowing; the next is the harvest. If so, all that follows from a person's deeds and traits should appear at once. This seems to support the idea that everyone sees everything in a single instant. But neither reason nor revelation supports that as a general rank, and many philosophers who deny that separate things can be known by immaterial beings cannot explain it. How could it apply to those on the right, let alone those on the left? If it did, they would all have the highest rank, though they lack the knowledge and standing required for it. God's acts are not arbitrary. The claim makes sense for the active intellect and universal intellects, but the difficulty does not concern them.

    The truth is that afterlife forms are pure images, with extension but no material bearer. They depend on God for their existence. Their parts cannot occupy the same place or moment, not because matter prevents them, but because this belongs to their own nature. In this world, too, extended parts cannot occupy the same place, and successive moments cannot occur together. This explains the forms perceived. As for the one who perceives them, even someone on the right has not brought their theoretical intellect—which gives true spiritual freedom and breadth—from potentiality into full activity. Their existence is therefore still limited in its second nature. The rational soul is subtle, but it takes the form of what it turns toward. Having spent its life joined to extended images and passing particulars, it follows their pattern: it experiences its desires, meals, and other pleasures one after another. It does not have endlessly many meals or encounters, but it may have many, one after another. This is how the author resolves the difficulty.

[^V09-499]: We learn sound logic by joining with the Active Intellect and by following God's messengers. Logic is the measure of the sciences.

[^V09-500]: “The balance” here means the general measure that includes the five kinds. The author's answer shows that the Qur'an contains a measure of knowledge; the separate question of where it uses each of the five kinds is answered in detail in *The Keys to the Unseen*.

[^V09-501]: Philosophers commonly call Aristotle the First Teacher, since he established logic, and al-Farabi the Second Teacher, since he brought it from Greek into Arabic. The author instead says that God is the first teacher, Gabriel the second, and the Messenger the third. This is the inward chain of teaching through spiritual beings; outwardly, the teaching comes through receiving the Qur'an.

[^V09-502]: The three forms of the measure of equality are the first, second, and third figures of the syllogism. The author does not count a fourth, since it is less natural. The measure of entailment is the conditional syllogism; the measure of contradiction is the disjunctive syllogism. He gives Qur'anic examples of each. For example, “He is the One who begins creation, then brings it back, and that is easier for Him” compares the easier return with the first creation. “I do not declare myself innocent; the soul urges one to evil” reasons from the soul to a general rule. Abraham says of a setting star, “I do not love those that set,” contrasting it with his Lord, who does not set. Another passage reasons that God is not rightly understood by those who say He revealed nothing to any human, since Moses was human and received a revealed Book. The conditional form appears in “If there were other gods in heaven and earth besides God, both would be ruined”; the disjunctive form in “Either we or you are rightly guided, or plainly in error.” The author notes that scripture and religious law use many concepts without their formal technical names. Their presence in the Qur'an does not make them innovations. He asks God to grant fairness and protect people from injustice.

[^V09-503]: The author's comparison is polemical. He says that Hanbalites and others rely only on the senses, imagination, and estimation, while a pure philosopher relies only on reason. These would be like the senses and reason within one human being if each were active on its own. Neither side alone is complete. The people who bring the faculties together are, in the author's view, the successful, rightly guided, and glad.

[^V09-504]: A person should compare the two worlds by using sound reason and accept that every natural form in this world has a corresponding imaginal form in the next.

[^V09-505]: This refers to those who interpret Qur'anic passages about the beginning and the return as referring only to spirits and abstract meanings. For them, “form” refers only to material forms.

[^V09-506]: This is the world of intellects that exist through God's sustaining power. It contains pure meanings, with no attachment or form—neither an imaginal form nor a natural one.

[^V09-507]: The scales of knowledge are heavy when there is more to weigh by the five measures, especially when the measure is demonstrative proof. The scales of deeds are heavy when the pan of good deeds outweighs the pan of bad deeds. The author is not speaking loosely about “heavy” and “light.”

[^V09-508]: Those on the spiritual path also take account of themselves, watch over their actions, and weigh their good and bad deeds each night. If their bad deeds outweigh the good, they make up for them through repentance, returning to God, remembrance, and worship. If the two sides are equal, they add night worship to tip the balance toward good. If their good deeds outweigh the bad, they compare them with God's countless blessings, both outward and inward, and return in humility, asking forgiveness and protection through His generosity. If they reflect, they will see that even their acts of worship came through God's help and guidance, and are among His greatest gifts. God says, “Do not claim that you have done God a favor by accepting Islam. Rather, God has favored you by guiding you to faith.”

[^V09-509]: The full verse says: “Do not drive away those who call on their Lord morning and evening, seeking His face. You are not responsible for their account, nor are they responsible for yours; if you drive them away, you will be among the wrongdoers.”

[^V09-510]: The word translated “unjust” can also mean “one counted among the unjust,” as in words for a blacksmith, date seller, or grocer.

[^V09-511]: “Powers that act” does not mean the intellects called the lords of species, since they too pass away. It means the beautiful names of God, which mystics see as the powers that nurture things. The visible forms disappear into the One who appears through them, just as animals return to the names “All-Hearing,” “All-Seeing,” and “All-Knowing,” angels to “All-Pure” and “All-Holy,” and the human being to the name God.

[^V09-512]: This means that all natural forms change, along the full course of their development, into forms of the intermediate world. Those forms belong to the level of souls, just as souls belong to the level of intellects. The universal divine souls are, in this account, the same as the intellects. All the goals of natural things are contained in the highest goal, whose unity is a true oneness, not a count of separate things. All things stand there through God, the Sustainer, not through material receivers. This change is not only a change in how someone looks at things when they set aside ordinary relationships. Reaching that view can itself be a spiritual station while a person is alive, and a necessary step toward the highest goal. “Whoever is blind here will be blind in the next life.” Knowledge is the seed of seeing.

[^V09-513]: This means that God owns everything, because everything there exists through His existence. This is the meaning of the verse, “Suddenly they will all be standing, looking on.”

[^V09-514]: When everything returns to God's realm and all existence returns to the Source of being, the stars have no light or visible presence of their own. Light and appearance belong to existence; individual natures are dark and hidden. This does not mean they are a positive force of darkness. It means they have no independent light.

[^V09-515]: This is an example. The bodies of the people of Hell also take forms suited to their souls; some are raised as apes or pigs. The bodies of the people of Paradise are young, smooth-skinned, and beardless. This is different from this world, where a body may be dark while the soul is full of light, as in the case of Bilal, or the reverse.

[^V09-516]: If you want to see an example of that flat plain, dear friend, return all the guiding lights and particular forms to God. Give back the borrowed ornaments and garments to those they belong to; act with justice and give each person their right. Then you will see everything returning upward to God. Only the bare plain remains, stripped of the water that gives life to spirits and bodies, smooth like a clean page with none of its strange forms or patterns. Yet even that page does not remain on its own: God takes it too. “The earth, all of it, will be in His grasp on the Day of Resurrection.” Since there are levels of affirming God's oneness—oneness in actions, qualities, and being—the mountains pass through three states, as the Qur'an describes: loose sand, scattered wool, and dust blown away. Their sandy matter does not remain; all is finally lost, and dominion and form belong to God.

[^V09-517]: This is because that state brings opposites together.

[^V09-518]: The earth—especially the earth of our bodies—moves and develops in its very being until it reaches its goals and brings out what it has held within it. Its hidden capacities appear. It is then brought to the darkness beneath the gathering place. Once the forms and lights return to God, what remains is bodily form, marked by distance in space, passage through time, and the darkness of matter. These are all forms of absence, not presence. This happens at the gathering of the body's powers and souls toward God.

[^V09-519]: “The awakened plain” means the Resurrection. Its movement is one event for all, along the full course of time, like one call and one blast. Yet across the breadth of that event there are countless cries and blasts. It is one arrival, a single unity that contains countless arrivals, each with its own goal. All bow before the Living and Sustaining One.

[^V09-520]: Hell is said to return to this region because nature itself is like fire, and attachment to it means being burned by fire. The author sets its limit at the sphere of the stars because the region from earth up to that sphere is where the powers and senses operate. They reach it through the stars, not beyond it to the outermost heaven. Whatever these powers perceive as many separate things, tied to matter and to limited forms, becomes fuel for the Fire. This is why the gates of Hell are said later to match the seven powers of perception.

[^V09-521]: A scale must suit what it weighs. We do not weigh gold and silver with a scale for wheat and barley, or the reverse. A person with many deeds needs a large scale; one with few deeds needs a small one.

[^V09-522]: The point is not that ledgers of deeds are literally put on scales and weighed, as some theologians say. Rather, the marks of good and bad deeds on the two sides of the active soul are compared. Every meaning has a form, so visible scales are set up there to represent this weighing, as a container holds what is put into it. The scales are rightly set up, and the records rightly spread open.

[^V09-523]: Other things do have opposites: truth and falsehood, giving and withholding, kindness and harm, and so on. They may take turns in the same person. Lasting certainty does not share its place with its opposite.

[^V09-524]: “Records” is the plural of “record,” meaning a book. A report tells of a person who had never done a good deed, but once said sincerely, “There is no god but God.” On the Day of Resurrection, ninety-nine books of human deeds are set against this one statement. Each book is as large as the distance between east and west, yet the statement outweighs them all. The report appears to compare one side with the other. The author adds that the statement also equals and overcomes all the records.

[^V09-525]: God says, “On that Day eight will carry the Throne of your Lord.” At the beginning of the world, four carried it; on the Day of Resurrection, four more are added. The Throne means the all-spreading existence. God's coming means the appearance of divine unity in the universal divine soul, and the earth shining means the heart lit by God's light. This is the inward meaning. In the outward image, that Throne resembles the House of God, which God has never entered physically. A complete mystic says, “Since the House of my heart was built, God has never left it for even an instant. Since this visible House was built—the Kaaba—He has never set foot in it.”

[^V09-526]: “The uncovering of the shin” is a figure of speech for something becoming visible, like the Arabic saying that war “uncovers its shin.” I also wonder whether the word could be read as “His cupbearer,” referring to the cupbearer of the Pool of Abundance. A report says that no one knows Ali ibn Abi Talib's true nature except God and Muhammad. “They will be called to bow down” may mean that the Imam and those who follow him are called to bow to the One God. God has made this meaning clear to me through His generosity.

[^V09-527]: Hell appears in this form because a camel is fierce. Some say it appears like a dark buffalo, since the blackness of cattle is linked with death. Hell is the home of death, and darkness brings death.

[^V09-528]: It would fit better to say that Israfil is the source of life, while Gabriel is the source of knowledge. Perhaps “Yahya” here means the life of knowledge, as Ali said, “People are dead; people of knowledge are alive.”

[^V09-529]: This resembles what happens in this world: God seals their hearts and hearing, and covers their sight. The parts of Hell press into one another as worms do in the world. The pressure grows because each person carries the burdens of others; they do not only have their own troubles, but take on the troubles of those around them. These burdens press on the soul and make it small. “Its lowest becomes its highest” means that elements become offspring, and offspring become elements. They eat one another: one form turns into another, as minerals become plants, plants become animals, and animals become human beings. The stronger forms attack and master the weaker, as a lion attacks sheep and the fire of material nature overcomes weaker powers.

[^V09-530]: Bodily fire causes pain in the body; spiritual fire causes worry, grief, and regret in the soul, like the fire of separation and remorse. Ali says in a prayer, “My Lord, suppose I could bear Your punishment; how could I bear being separated from You?” In a divine revelation, God says, “How great is the sorrow for what I neglected in my duty to God!”

[^V09-531]: The Arabic word here means a public display, like lining up an army for review. In Persian, it is called *san didan*, “to review the army.”

[^V09-532]: This means the universal souls and universal intellects.

[^V09-533]: “They did not believe in God, the Great” refers to the complete unbeliever, not the hypocrite. The unbeliever's denial of God, association of partners with Him, and outright rejection are different from the hypocrisy of outward belief and inward unbelief. If the verse instead read “there was no faith in him,” and the unstated subject of “enters” referred to the hypocrite, then the next words about hypocrites would simply explain that.

[^V09-534]: “He thought” here means “he was certain,” as in God's words, “They were certain there was no refuge except with Him.”

[^V09-535]: This means a person who denies that a Creator exists. Such a person goes too far in denial, while a person who associates partners with God goes too far in the other direction.

[^V09-536]: In the second interpretation, “the Heights” is the name of an idea or state; in the first, it is the name of a place.

[^V09-537]: It is better to understand “they have not entered it” as describing the people of Paradise, not the people on the Heights. The people of the Heights call to the people of Paradise while the latter have not yet entered it, saying, “Peace be upon you.”

[^V09-538]: Someone might say this report is unclear. It could explain “the path”—which would support the author's point—or “those whom You have blessed,” which would not. We answer that it explains “the path”: the phrase “we are the straight path” refers to Ali and his descendants. The verse itself names the blessed as prophets, the truthful, witnesses, and the righteous. Their straight path is Muhammad and his descendants.

[^V09-539]: Paradise is not made from opposing elements that break apart, and one opposite does not enter another and make it sick or destroy it.

[^V09-540]: This especially refers to the higher spiritual realm along the upward path. The simple, complete intellects there belong to the divine realm. Whoever sees them has, in a sense, seen God. They are beyond death, aging, and similar changes.

[^V09-541]: As you can see in this world, people are never satisfied. Their desire and greed grow day by day. In Hell, the changing of skins means that their nature continues to change through an inner movement of being. They do not die because they still have animal life, but they do not truly live because they lack intellectual life. “People are dead, but people of knowledge are alive.” They ask Malik to let them die, but death has already been killed by the blade named Yahya.

[^V09-542]: They are familiar with the Fire, so they feel pain only when they leave it and must face what is unfamiliar. This is like people in this world who do not feel the heat of nature while they are busy with it, but feel pain when they turn away or think about death and what comes after.

[^V09-543]: The author has already quoted this from Muhyi al-Din ibn al-Arabi earlier in this book.

[^V09-544]: This Garden exists as one universal reality, with a single, all-gathering existence, though it contains many forms through separate, particular natures and shapes. Earlier, the author discussed the prior existence of spirits. This account does not require the usual Aristotelian theories.

[^V09-545]: The upward course extends to God, the final goal. We have explained that an action's appointed term depends on its nature: it may be long or short. Here the end depends on God's power and His words, which never run out or perish.

[^V09-546]: This means the point where the soul's return becomes visible. In fact, its return to God begins before unavoidable death, as it grows from incompleteness toward completion.

[^V09-547]: “Pair” may mean two things together, or it may refer to one of the two, as when one shoe is called a pair of shoes. The two Gardens here are one sensory Garden and one spiritual Garden.

[^V09-548]: “Every evening and morning” means all through the night, from evening to morning, so it does not conflict with the report that the spirits rise from the Garden at dawn. The report can also be read inwardly. The west points to the fading of separate, unreal forms and lights in the highest realm. The sweet water is the all-spreading existence. The spirits leave their bodies—or the forms surrounding them—and move toward the highest goal. Their being in that high realm at night refers to the many separate lights going out, as in the report, “God created creation in darkness, then sprinkled His light upon them.” The Garden's fruits are the sight of God's beauty and majesty. The spirits meeting and recognizing one another means that their souls become one; as a Persian verse says, “The souls of God's lions are joined as one.” Dawn is the return of their imagined existence after it had disappeared, and their waking after the joy of union. Their flight between heaven and earth is their state in the intermediate world, between disappearance and return—the state called absence, or the middle station. “When the sun rises, they return to their graves” may be a copying error for “they deliberately tend to their images,” meaning that forms descend from the world of meaning. The Fire in the east is the flowing fire of nature, with its three branches, at the start of the upward path. The spirits of unbelievers live there when they return to nature. Their night is nature's darkness. At dawn, they are drawn a little toward the light and briefly receive mercy, as people in this life sometimes do. They then hurry to a valley whose heat is less than their night in nature, though greater than any fire in this world. In the evening they return to the Fire—their lower station and the demands of their fiery traits.

[^V09-549]: The author is listing reports that name worldly places as locations of Paradise or Hell. Abu Basir's report says nothing about birds. It may mean that the spirits are in this world, in a Garden shaped like bodies in Paradise. Or it may mean the reverse: in Paradise, they are in a Garden shaped like bodies in this world. Another possibility is that in the intermediate world they are in a Garden shaped like bodies on the Day of Resurrection.

[^V09-550]: This means that the spirits attached to the unbelievers' brains and hearts go there. If the original word is “their chests,” the meaning is clear. If it says “their remains,” it may be a figure of speech for the residue left behind.

[^V09-551]: Paradise is folded up within the soul's being, and its existence is suspended from the number of the sun's rays and similar things, as a sage says.

[^V09-552]: This and similar reports refer to the two wings of knowledge and action, or theoretical and practical understanding.

[^V09-553]: Paradise and Hell are pure forms without matter. The same applies to the reports that follow.

[^V09-554]: The reporter may be unsure which way around the saying goes: “beneath the sea is a Fire,” or “beneath the Fire is a sea.” The report means that a Muslim should not enter the world or take it as their home except as a means to the next life—as a fighter for God's cause, or as one who makes the outward or inward pilgrimage. Beneath the sea of nature is the Fire of matter; beneath the Fire of nature is the sea of matter. The Fire is marked by distance in space and passage through time; the sea is matter, mixed and set ablaze. The author will later explain the sea as nature.

[^V09-555]: This refers to the universal intellect and the heart of the perfect human being. The universal reality of Paradise—the spirit of the world and the place where the All-Merciful is established—is the all-spreading existence called God's wide mercy. It may also mean the Garden of the divine essence and qualities. The Greatest Throne here is not the outermost heavenly sphere. That sphere is only a visible sign of Paradise, not its universal image.

[^V09-556]: This refers to the root from which the lote tree grows. The lote tree and the tree of Zaqqum point in opposite directions. The lote tree's branches and leaves rise upward, while the branches of Zaqqum descend. The particular powers of perception operate in these two directions. The forms they take in are the source of reaching Paradise or Hell. They are themselves among the levels of those two places. The deeds and knowledge of the good rise to the highest realm: “Good words rise to Him.”

[^V09-557]: Someone might object that imagination only stores images; it does not perceive them. The author replies that by “imagination” he means the common sense, the inner power that gathers what the senses perceive. He calls it imagination because he is speaking about its inward side, which perceives images held in memory. This avoids confusing it with the outward senses.

[^V09-558]: The Fire does not enter or burn the heart's actual spiritual core. Only a kind of fire—such as the fire of nature or the fire of bad deeds—can send its flame toward a simple intellect without entering it. The heart and that fire belong to different realms. This fire may interrupt the forms of the intermediate world, but it cannot damage the heart's essential being.

[^V09-559]: “Openings” means small windows or passages from one level to another. A bad deed leads to another bad deed, just as a good deed leads to another good deed.

[^V09-560]: This applies when we mean these senses as tied to the present body and separate from the senses of the next life. If we mean the senses in a broader way, or together with the spirit that uses them, they are not separate from their afterlife forms.

[^V09-561]: The author described examples of the Prophet's spiritual senses earlier, in his discussion of the soul.

[^V09-562]: This means the inner soul and its settled qualities of knowledge and action.

[^V09-563]: Since the worlds reflect one another, ten senses exist in your imaginal body. That world is free of matter and more complete than this one, so each of its senses can do the work of all the others. Ten senses multiplied by ten give one hundred powers; these, joined to the ten powers of the intellect, give a thousand.

[^V09-564]: The soul first comes into being through the body, but it remains after the body. Through its inner movement, it changes from a natural, bodily form to an imaginal body. The soul itself is the lasting root held in common by both.

[^V09-565]: The image grows stronger step by step until it becomes a form of the next life.

[^V09-566]: This means the body that had appeared as an earthly body.

[^V09-567]: This is true in terms of being and existence, though not in terms of the kind of thing or its definition. This is the way of the mystics who learn through direct experience.

[^V09-568]: The inner state takes the place of the outward form.

[^V09-569]: The “divine likeness” is that God is always bringing about something new, while imagination changes from one thought to another at every moment.

[^V09-570]: This refers to a spirit appearing to a student in the form of a teacher after the teacher has died, or another spirit taking the teacher's form to explain something or warn the student about a wrong path. Is the spirit itself taking an imaginal form without leaving its spiritual station? Or is the form only in the eye of the person who sees it, like the sky's blue color? Or is the image real, with its own existence, while the spirit is to it as a soul is to a body? The author holds that the first answer is true.

[^V09-571]: Some spiritual travelers are satisfied with these unusual visions. Their delight in seeing them keeps them from asking what they are or how they happen. There are different ranks: doing the work, knowing what the work is and why it happens, and bringing the two together. Few reach the last rank.

[^V09-572]: If their spiritual power were strong enough to control the images their imagination creates, while their natural form yielded to them like material to a maker, they would understand how spirits take on different forms, as jinn are said to do.

[^V09-573]: This means that the natural body may decay, while the spirit remains and takes an imaginal form. This shows that spirits really do take form, both for spiritual travelers and for others.

[^V09-574]: If a person sees the dead animal or plant afterward, they see its imaginal form, just as they see any dead person or creature.

[^V09-575]: This is Ibn Arabi's answer to the person who understands this point. Since Ibn Arabi was deeply immersed in affirming God's oneness, “you” here refers to the person he is describing, just as “you know” can mean “that person knows.”

[^V09-576]: What appears to be extra—such as a crowd seeming small or large—is seen through imagination. The ordinary eye may see what is actually there, while imagination shows a different number. The cause is the soul's connection to a divine power and to the spiritual reality of Muhammad. The exact number appears to the eye; the changed number appears through imagination.

[^V09-577]: Gabriel has a true spiritual reality: the Active Intellect. The Prophet said, “I saw him, and he filled the whole horizon.” Gabriel also has a particular image, free of matter but limited and dim, like a person of the Prophet's time. Dihya al-Kalbi was the man whose appearance matched that image. The Companions thought the figure was Dihya, but the image was only a form through which Gabriel's reality came down from the world of intellects to the Prophet.

[^V09-578]: If a person's imagination is very strong, they may think that what they see through imagination is a physical thing, and may even doubt things known by direct experience. If they do not have that power, they may put a thing in the wrong category: they may call a form seen by imagination a dark physical object, when it is a bright image of a deeper truth. A person given clear judgment can distinguish the two.

[^V09-579]: Some copies read “they do not make a distinction,” meaning that many people of God do not separate what they see through imagination from what they see through the senses. If they always slept, they would call their visions imagination. Yet when awake they may see similar things and call them physical, even though both kinds are real and clearly present in their own way.

[^V09-580]: By saying that God is the “place” where thoughts take form, Ibn Arabi may mean the source from which they come. Or he may mean that God's active existence and will are where a servant's thoughts take on particular forms. These particular forms are qualities of existence.

[^V09-581]: In this world the servant's will follows God's will; the servant's commands also follow God's commands. The mystic Ibn Arabi describes it as the servant having no separate will left, so God's will guides their choices. A report tells of a ship caught in a storm. A passenger who was close to God prayed, “For a long time I have given up what I want for what You want. For one hour, give up what You want for what I want.” The sea became calm. Ibn Arabi's point is that God watches over the servant so that what the servant wants can come into being in this inner realm.

[^V09-582]: The images a servant forms within themselves match the outward images through which God appears. The soul takes on the color of the forms it sees.

[^V09-583]: Ibn Arabi says elsewhere in *The Meccan Revelations* that every person creates in imagination forms that exist only there. A person of spiritual insight can, through focused will, create a form that also exists outside the place where they are thinking of it. The author discussed this in the first volume, in the section on existence in the mind.

[^V09-584]: Divine names and their forms also differ in rank: one may be great, greater, or greater still. A Persian verse says, “This movement first arose from the beauty that has existed from eternity.”

[^V09-585]: The forms of this world were the main goals for the people of Hell. The imaginal and intellectual forms they had here were ways of seeing the natural forms and passing particulars around them. This does not mean that the material forms of this world literally survive. The author says “were” because, in this account, those forms were their worldly aims.

[^V09-586]: The meanings of all the forms in the next life already exist in our actions here: “Your deeds will return to you.” Since all things there arise through God's will and purpose, accept His commands here and let go of your own desires, so that what appears in the next life is pleasing to you.

[^V09-587]: Worshippers strive for God to be pleased with them. Those who know God strive to be pleased with what He decides and ordains. They say, “Contentment is God's greatest gate.”

[^V09-588]: An ignorant person may wish that the stones of their house were pearls, that they never grew old, or that they stayed forever young and beautiful. God's wisdom requires pearls to remain rare and costly, while soil is plentiful for farming and building. It also requires people to travel toward God instead of spending all their effort maintaining the body. A person needs a strong body with firm flesh to bear hardship and avoid being harmed.

[^V09-589]: Many mystics say that punishment will end. In their view, evil is made up of defects and absences, whose source is error, disobedience, and attachment to the world. The world itself is not the existing things, which are good and full of light. It is the limits, defects, decay, and imagined relations of those things.

[^V09-590]: Even things people dislike are under God's wide mercy. Nothing in God's kingdom exists unless it is touched by His mercy and will; otherwise, it would not exist. If we consider a thing only by its meaning or outward form, it returns to nonbeing.

[^V09-591]: Evil can come about through any agent, as in God's words, “Whoever does evil will be repaid for it,” and, “Whoever does an atom's weight of evil will see it.”

[^V09-592]: These are the words of Muhyi al-Din ibn al-Arabi in chapter 87 of *The Meccan Revelations*.

[^V09-593]: The commentator disagrees. Some say that “forever” can mean a very long time, and some mystics say that the people of Hell will leave for Paradise. The author dismisses this as unusual. The commentator says that scripture and basic religious teaching establish that unbelievers do not leave Hell, though endless punishment is not itself a required belief. In the commentator's view, the punishment lasts and unbelievers do not leave. The author's argument that forced states do not last forever does not apply, because their dark nature has become part of what they are. Their first nature remains, and a second nature has become fixed through habit.

[^V09-594]: Pleasure lasts for each individual, while suffering lasts as a kind. The people who suffer continue to exist as one person after another. The author explains this later.

[^V09-595]: The verse means, “If We had wished, We would have guided them.” But God does not wait for some future state; He is the source of all that is already real. Rather, His guidance comes in different ways so that His many beautiful names can appear. Each name rules over a kind of being, and there are as many paths to God as there are breaths among people.

[^V09-596]: This is like an ignorant person who feels uneasy in a gathering of scholars because their learning does not suit him. Fire, too, is unlike light.

[^V09-597]: In their own view, people have existence and attribute it to themselves. The oneness of God means that we remove these separate claims. When people claim existence for themselves, they also claim qualities such as knowledge, power, and will, and they claim to act. Creating depends on existing. Existence and action are attributed both to God and to created things; this is neither total compulsion nor complete independence, but a middle way.

[^V09-598]: The verses use plural forms, as in “the people of the Fire will remain in it,” and “whoever remains in the Fire will be given scalding water that tears their insides.”

[^V09-599]: This is the root of perfection and light in good qualities and states: the nature God gave people. The verses ask, “Can there be any doubt about God, the Creator of the heavens and earth?” and say that if people are asked who created them, they answer, “God.” They recognize that the true source worthy of worship is the perfect Being: living, knowing, powerful, willing, hearing, seeing, and speaking. This belief leads them back to good and happiness.

[^V09-600]: God says, “They are only names that you and your fathers have given them, for which God has sent down no authority.” A Persian verse says, “The parts of existence all took the name of the Beloved; the name I bear is mine, but all the rest is His.”

[^V09-601]: If people call idols “intercessors” or “those who bring us close to God,” it becomes clear that they are not treating the objects themselves as true gods. If they call them wood, gold, or stone, the point is clearer still. A true god must be living, knowing, powerful, and willing, not dead, ignorant, helpless, or forced. The worshippers themselves have these qualities, so how could they worship something less complete? In fact, they worship the true existence appearing in those forms. Existence is life, knowledge, awareness, power, will, light, love, and other perfections. Remember what we explained about the rational soul.

[^V09-602]: A false belief can become a lasting part of the rational soul and change its nature. It then becomes dark and unable to return to the life of the intellect. It still has animal life, which is lower. This applies to deeply rooted false belief in a stubborn person. Ordinary ignorance, simple ignorance, and other bad habits can still disappear; they do not change the soul in this way.

[^V09-603]: Strictly speaking, “people who belong there” cannot mean only people who happen to be nearby. A person must also have a bond with that place. We do not call a neighbor “one of the family,” or everyone who lives in the world “one of the world's own.” We call people “God's people” when they love Him and feel close to Him, and “the Prophet's companions” when they love and support him.

[^V09-604]: The commentator says that the word “for” in “We created many for Hell” states the purpose of their creation, not only the result that later follows.

[^V09-605]: This refers to the covenant in which God asked, “Am I not your Lord?” The commentator questions the idea that the covenant took place through a visible form. The fixed realities of possible things had no existence of their own; they appeared through God's existence and knowledge. Their very state said, “Praise, greatness, and existence belong to You.” This was the clearest answer to God's question. Since every meaning has a form and every reality has a subtle counterpart, the covenant described here is its true meaning, while the images and words are its symbolic form.

[^V09-606]: Believing that other beings are intercessors and signs that lead to God is a belief filled with light, like life-giving water that puts out fire. This original nature draws people back to it, as God says, “As He began you, so you will return.”

[^V09-607]: This means knowledge given by God, knowledge from the highest source, or divine wisdom. It also means knowing that God's knowledge brings things into being; it does not merely receive information from them.

[^V09-608]: Anger has no lasting object. As explained earlier, anything that exists is under God's will. If God rejected it completely, it would not exist.

[^V09-609]: An effect needs its cause not only to begin, but to continue. Each punishment needs anger to continue with it. The first anger may bring someone into Hell, but it does not by itself make the punishment last forever, since anger is a passing state.

[^V09-610]: This refers to God's words, “My servants who have wronged themselves, do not despair of God's mercy. God forgives all sins,” and to the sacred report quoted here, which also addresses everyone as “My servants.”

[^V09-611]: This points to the plain where people gather for judgment. A report says that all people will be gathered in one open place.

[^V09-612]: This is the all-embracing flow of existence, God's creative word “Be.” It is one at the beginning and remains one at the end.

[^V09-613]: We have said before that the world of forms reached by the body's senses—from the Footstool down to the center of the earth—and the forms of Hell in the next life are joined to the forms of this world. In this sense, they seem not to belong to the next life. What remains there are the forms of Paradise and forms seen through the soul's clear inner sight. The world of meaning is one, because matter, time, place, and the other things that divide bodies are absent there. This oneness remains until it takes form in two worlds of images—one of form, one of matter. They branch out like a person whose two feet come down from one body. This image of the Footstool stands for the universal soul, which includes all souls, both blessed and wretched. The two feet branch out from it because spirit and body are joined.

[^V09-614]: The Shaykh's words—that the two feet rest in Paradise and Hell—do not conflict with the author's claim that one flow of existence goes to this world and the other to the next. As explained, the forms of Hell belong with the forms of this world. The area from the Footstool to the earth's center shows forms of Paradise when the senses are guided by the intellect and lit by it. It shows forms of Hell when the senses are used without the intellect's guidance. The Shaykh means that both feet come from God, the Giver of peace, and bring peace to those in both homes. They begin with the All-Merciful and end in mercy.

[^V09-615]: This alludes to God's words: “God created seven heavens, and of the earth the same number. His command descends between them.”

[^V09-616]: The Persian word means “to set the staff down and rest.” It is a way of speaking about reaching the end of a journey and resting there.

[^V09-617]: The commentator says Ibn Arabi calls them two groups, though he describes three, because he divides them by whether or not they enjoy complete comfort.

[^V09-618]: The hardship lasts longer than in the first example, but it eventually ends. These people then live in their home, Paradise.

[^V09-619]: The phrase “the people of this other home” begins the discussion of those who truly belong in Hell.

[^V09-620]: The Arabic word means “to be full” or “to be filled.” Hell's pain ends when its fire and fuel increase and fill it. Its pain came from being incomplete.

[^V09-621]: The mystics say that every being is ruled by one of God's names. The people of Paradise are under gentle names such as “Lord,” while the people of Hell are under “Overpowering.” The word for “Overpowering” can also mean “one who restores what is broken,” as in the prayer, “O Healer of broken bones.”

[^V09-622]: This answers the idea that God does not care because people are only a handful of dust. If they were so insignificant, He would not punish their crimes, become angry, or act with such force. These are signs that He does care.

[^V09-623]: This means pairing and sharing. God says, “We created every pair,” such as matter and form, soul and spirit, body and soul, good and evil, light and darkness, and many other pairs.

[^V09-624]: A person who knows God does not think badly of Him or judge the people of any home in a way that conflicts with God's judgment and will.

[^V09-625]: The mystics add “servant of” to a divine name for each being that shows that name. Someone who shows knowledge may be called “servant of the All-Knowing”; someone who shows patience, “servant of the Patient”; and one who gives gifts, “servant of the Giver,” even if their given name is Zayd, Amr, or Bakr. Paradise is ruled by the Gentle, Hell by the Overpowering, and the Heights between them by both. The Heights are where a person's good and bad deeds are equal. God also has names made up of more than one name, each with its own effects, such as “the Living, the Self-Sustaining,” “the All-Merciful, the Most Compassionate,” and “the Most High, the Great.”

[^V09-626]: The pull comes from an inner fit between the person and the place; it is not foreign to their nature. God's name guides them. His power and greatness shine out, and His majesty is also beauty.

[^V09-627]: The commentator thinks the word means Hell itself, not a promise of punishment. The usual word for a threat of punishment has a different ending.

[^V09-628]: God's threat, like His promise, reaches everyone in some way. Even Paradise has hardships on the way to it, as the report says, “Paradise is surrounded by hardships.”

[^V09-629]: It is like being bitten by someone you love, or drinking sweet rosewater.

[^V09-630]: A mixed thing is never pure, and what is limited is not the unlimited. But this does not mean that anything is truly separate from God. There is no second reality beside existence itself. Whatever we suppose to be a second reality is still that same existence. God says He does not forgive worshipping another god alongside Him. This means that no second reality can stand beside pure existence. Pure existence includes everything of its own kind and leaves out what is foreign to it: a thing's definition and its absence. Yet when the mind, imagination, or senses point to those things, they too have some kind of existence. The word “forgive” can mean “cover,” as when a crowd covers the ground. So the verse means that real existence cannot be covered or surrounded by a second reality. It includes every other distinct thing, whether great or small, but it does not allow a second god.

[^V09-631]: This raises a question. Direct awareness is said to be the very thing known as it exists outside us. If the thing known is an absence, then awareness would be an absence too, and an absence cannot cause anything. But if, as the author says, it has a kind of existence, how can it be a real evil when existence is good? The answer is that direct awareness does not make pure nothingness present. We cannot picture pure nothingness, such as a complete empty space or the removal of a drawing from a board. What we directly experience is a real state opposite to the thing that is missing. The afterlife images of direct awareness are distorted forms of worldly deeds. The badness of those deeds came from the absences that accompanied them.

[^V09-632]: We explained this earlier in the section on the divine world, quoting a respected authority. The notes there may help here.

[^V09-633]: The opening of this chapter says that Hell's true form is the original truth of this world. The ugly forms created when deeds take shape—as painful habits and images—are not its true form, because they actively exist.

[^V09-634]: The lasting light belongs to God, not to the sun, moon, or stars themselves. A Persian verse says, “How many times has this stream changed its water, while the moon and stars still shine in its reflection.”

[^V09-635]: Worldly pleasure is mixed with decay and passing away. Its joy carries sorrow; its antidote is poison; its cure is illness; its pleasure is pain. Its forms are constantly breaking apart, and its meetings are only brief contacts. There are many other flaws like these.

[^V09-636]: If Hell came from the divine name “Overpowering,” rather than from pain, absence, and defect, it would already be complete through God's power. It would not be hungry and thirsty, cry “Is there any more?” or consume itself. It would not be so narrow that God had to place His foot in it. Instead, God brings it wide mercy, giving it more room to ask for more and to act against the tyrants. The Fire's need shows that people are mistaken about how it was made.

[^V09-637]: This means that worldly deeds prepare the food of the next life.

[^V09-638]: Deeds create lasting qualities in the soul, and these qualities give rise to forms suited to them—both the food and the form of the person who eats it. Good deeds may make people appear beautiful and youthful in the next life; bad deeds may give them ugly forms, like monkeys or pigs.

[^V09-639]: This is the careful mystic Ibn Arabi, in chapter 87 of *The Meccan Revelations*, in his discussion of protecting oneself from the Fire.

[^V09-640]: He means that the lower world of images is set beneath the high world visible to us. The stars, like glowing embers, affect the high world of Paradise just as fire under a pot affects what is above it. In this way, the heat from the stars also works below them.

[^V09-641]: His point is that fire is useful both to those in Hell and to the people of Paradise. Its own good for those in Hell has been explained earlier. For Paradise, it ripens its fruit, reshapes the people who live there, gives the ground its scent of musk, and strengthens the roots of its trees.

[^V09-642]: Heat is a distant cause of these changes. The nearer causes are good habits in the soul and the afterlife changes and improvements that follow from them. The shaping and purification of the soul come from its love of God, its journey toward Him, and its quick progress on that path.

[^V09-643]: This means the body considered simply as matter. In that sense, it has no touch and cannot feel. But as limbs and organs, bodies can feel pain because they have touch.

[^V09-644]: “Enjoy completion” means “become more complete.” Body considered as matter has no sense of touch, so it cannot feel pain or pleasure.

[^V09-645]: These are only secondary qualities of a body. For example, heat is not the completion of water as a kind of body, and cold is not the completion of the heart or liver. Heat and cold are additional qualities for them. A body's division into parts may not be a completion either, since it breaks the body's basic connected form. This may follow from the view that bodies are made of indivisible parts or that their basic extension is both body and matter.

[^V09-646]: A body considered as matter may lose its connection without pain, because it has no touch. But losing that connection is not a completion for it. It removes what gives its nature unity and leads to its breaking apart. How could a lack of connection be a completion?

[^V09-647]: The limbs and organs serve the mixed human soul, joined to the animal soul. That is where pain and punishment are felt, as in the case of Hell's keepers.

[^V09-648]: Saying that the animal soul is not guilty does not mean that it cannot feel pain. Pleasure and pain are the awareness of what suits or harms a being. The animal soul senses particular things that suit or harm it, and each power takes pleasure in what suits it and feels pain at what does not. The sense of touch, for example, enjoys balanced warmth and smoothness, but feels pain from harsh heat or cold and roughness. Taste enjoys sweetness and feels pain at bitterness; the other senses work in the same way. Still, though animals can feel pain, they are not punished by God for it. Their souls feel pain, but not as punishment. They do not have the grief, regret, and inner torment that belong to a higher soul. Those deeper pains belong to the human soul where it is joined to the animal soul.

[^V09-649]: The rational soul belongs to the highest spiritual realm, and its time is eternal. It is the simple intellect, the spirit of God in the world of meanings. How could the dust of the natural world or its opposites reach it? A Persian verse says: “If you keep measuring yourself as front and back, you are bound to the body and cut off from the soul. Above and below, front and back, are words for the body; the bright soul is beyond direction. Open your sight with the King's pure light, or you will think like someone short-sighted.”

[^V09-650]: Here the author means the angels who act in the world, the spirits attached to things, and the natures impressed in them.

[^V09-651]: This explains in detail what he means by “the spirits.”

[^V09-652]: The fixed stars are beyond number, like grains of sand and drops of sea water, and so are their spirits, or perhaps they are even more numerous. But when they are grouped according to the twelve signs of the zodiac, then together with the spirits of the seven moving planets they make nineteen governors.

[^V09-653]: This means the Paradise of God's essence and the Paradise of His qualities. The author restates part of his account of spiritual resurrection in a different way, while also giving the passage a fitting end. A strong opening and a strong ending are both valued, as we see in the Qur'an's chapters.

[^V09-654]: At this stage, the seeker no longer follows personal desire. They want only what God wills and commands, and dislike only what He dislikes and forbids. God's commands express His active will; His prohibitions express, in a secondary way, what He rejects. When personal desire and purpose disappear, so do the needs they create. The seeker becomes rich through God's richness. A Persian verse says: “Everyone has some desire in mind; my desire is to have no desire.”

[^V09-655]: The weak and uncertain existence of these images is mainly due to the way they serve, in this life, as mirrors in which we see material things. We do not usually attend to them for their own sake. If we did, they would be stronger and more complete, because they are simpler and more lasting. These images show the soul material things; they exist for you, not for matter or for the external object. Without them, you would know nothing of what is outside. People sometimes say that imagined coins, sweets, and similar things are useless. That is true only when they are trying to encourage someone to work for real things. In fact, these images are real creations made by you through a power God gives you. They are also real things outside matter, because they exist in the soul, and the soul is outside matter. An image of something that exists outside the mind is itself a real thing. As explained in discussions of mental existence, what exists in the mind is called “mental” in comparison with material things outside the soul. Considered on its own, without that comparison, it is an external reality. If our worldly interests did not keep us focused on material things, our mental images would not merely reflect those things or be weak. When the soul has few distractions and desires, these images become so strong that they can be mistaken for material things. This is why Ibn Arabi said, “There is no greater confusion in all existence than mistaking imagination for sense perception.”

[^V09-656]: There is no sequence among these images. They are infinite in number, not spread without end along a single dimension. We quoted a passage from Illuminationist philosophy in our notes on forms that supports this point.

[^V09-657]: The commentator takes this as Ali saying: “Although I am Adam's son in outward form, my inner meaning bears witness to my spiritual fatherhood.”

[^V09-658]: In their own view, the people of Hell undergo a change of identity, because they believe they are nothing but their skins, natural powers, and outward forms. Their souls seem to be absorbed into their bodies. The people of truth are the reverse: their bodies are absorbed into their souls, especially those with simple intellectual understanding. This is why a report says that a believer's body does not decay.

[^V09-659]: This explains how the heavenly spheres and their forces govern people in this world: people are immersed in bodily life, nature, and what follows from them. Their mixtures, compounds, and tastes all depend on the influence of the heavenly spheres. This is the world in which these effects become visible.

[^V09-660]: This means in their first creation, according to what their earlier deeds call for; and in their continued existence in the next life. They remain stars, but no longer shine or pierce the darkness.

[^V09-661]: Just as the true Paradise is close to the realm of divine power and the universal intellects, the Paradise of God's qualities is close to the divine realm.

[^V09-662]: This renewal is like the changes of this world, because the people of Paradise have left the world and its ways. We explained earlier that renewal and expansion belong to the way these separate beings exist.
